# Introduction

Presented below is abstract algebra at an undergrad level, covering most of the material usually thought in Abstract Algebra I and the first half of Abstract Algebra II curricula at the Math undergrad level. Namely, we discuss the following topics, starting from first principles:
1. Group theory
2. Ring theory
3. Field theory
4. Galois theory

The discussions all build towards the fundamental theorem of Galois theory and the insolvability of the quinitic, and towards the fundamental theorem of Algebra. Other highlights include the first three isomorphism theorems, some useful counting techniques that deal with symmetry, classification of finite groups of prime order, and others.

The only real prerequisite here is linear algebra and elementary real analysis, the latter of which we use to prove the fundamental theorem of algebra, and the former of which we use throughout in many examples, and in proofs that deal with field extensions and Galois extensions. Elementary number theory knowledge also comes in handy, since we use modular arithmetic quite a lot and use some results that deal with primes.

Finally, it should be noted that these notes are based on many different resources, most significant of which are:
1. Prof. James Cook's [Abstract Algebra I](http://www.supermath.info/AbstractAlgebra.html) taught at Liberty University.
2. Prof. James Cook's [Abstract Algebra II](https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLBY4G2o7DhF3yvl74tXVgdwukieahxRhG), also taught at Liberty U.
3. Dr. Gadi Aleksandrowicz's writing on [abstract algebra](https://gadial.net/categories/abstract%20algebra/) in his blog (Hebrew).
4. Abstract Algebra: Theory and Application by Prof. Thomas W. Judson, available freely at [LibreTexts](https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Abstract_and_Geometric_Algebra/Abstract_Algebra%3A_Theory_and_Applications_(Judson)).
5. [Contemporary Abstract Algebra](https://www.amazon.com/Contemporary-Abstract-Algebra-Textbooks-Mathematics-dp-1032778911/dp/1032778911) by Prof. Joseph Gallian.

# A Historical Overview

Consider the following problem: *what number, when multiplied by itself and then divided by 5, gives twice the number?*

When faced with such a word problem, one trained in algebra immediately produces the following equation: $\frac{x^{2}}{5}=2x$, which they then solve approximately as follows:

$$
\begin{gathered}
\frac{x^{2}}{5}=2x \iff\\
&x^{2}=10x \iff\\
&&x(x-10)=0 \iff\\
&&&x=0 \text{ or } x =10
\end{gathered}
$$

One then declares the solution to be either $0$ or $10$. This process - of taking a word problem and then phrasing it entirely in terms of symbols and airthmetic operations expressed using those symbols - is an *abstraction* of the problem to the realm of algebra, and has not always been the approach used to tackle such problems. Even though the term *algebra* dates back to the 9th century when it was coined by Al-Khwarizmi, it was not until over 700 years later that algebra in its symbolic form that is thought today become the primary form of algebra. This fully symbolic algebra was presented by Viete in 1591, and later expanded by Descarte in 1637.

Once fully symbolic algebra has been formalized,  mathematicians could forgo word problems and instead focus on solving symbolic equations, also known as algebraic equations or *polynomial equations*. Indeed in those centuries before the development of the theories which would later be unified under the umbrella term of abstract algebra, algebra itself would be defined as *the study of polynomials*.

Solving algebraic equations was done either through direct manipulation of the equation (as we did in our first example) or using explicit formulas that produce the solution to algebraic equations of a given form. The formula for the solutions of a second degree polynomial, known as the *quadratic formula*, is perhaps the second most well known mathematical formula, second only to Pythagoras' theorem

$$
ax^2+bx+c = 0 \iff x_{1, 2} = \frac{-b\pm \sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}
$$
Given that $a \neq 0$ (but if $a=0$ then this is not a quadratic equation, so this isn't a limitation). Deriving this formula is rather straightforward. One way to derive it is presented below:

$$
\begin{gathered}
ax^{2}+bx + c = 0 \\
x^{2}+\frac{b}{a}x + \frac{c}{a} = 0 \\
x^{2} + \frac{b}{a}x = -\frac{c}{a} \\
x^{2} + \frac{b}{a}x + \frac{b^{2}}{4a^{2}} = -\frac{c}{a} + \frac{b^{2}}{4a^{2}} \\
(x+\frac{b}{2a})^{2} = \frac{b^{2}-4ac}{4a^{2}} \\
x + \frac{b}{2a} = \pm \frac{\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}\\
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}
\end{gathered}
$$

Notice that this derivation was performed via algebraic manipulations of the first equation. Each transition is justified from the properties of $\mathbb{R}$ (suppose this is a polynomial over the reals) and from the fact that certain operations - such as multiplication by a non-zero value and addition - maintain the equality. We intentionally do not dissect each transition with proper rigor since we reserve such discussion for the proper introduction of groups and fields.

Turns out that via algebraic manipulation, one can also derive a formula for the solution of a cubic polynomial - polynomial of degree $3$. In fact, there exists one for quartic polynomials as well - these have degree $4$. Attempting to devise a similar method for degree $5$ (quintic) polyonmials remained an open problem in algebra. The problem was eventually solved by the young Evariste Galois in the 19th century, who used a new algebraic structure called *groups* to reason about polynomials. Galois eventually concluded that no such formula exists. This was one of the first steps in the development of *group theory*. Around the same time, first steps towards *ring theory* and *field theory* were also made. These three theories form the pillars of *abstract algebra* or *modern algebra*. Abstract algebra is to algebra as algebra is to word problems - *an abstraction*. Instead of discussing a concrete algebraic equation, we consider algebraic *structures* such as groups, rings and fields, that satisfy certain *axioms*, and from those we produce powerful results which we can later apply to a particular class of algebraic problems.

It is important to note that these structures are defined in such a way that the results derived apply to domains outside of what might be consider classical algebra. For example, the symmetries of a geometric shape which are geometric in nature turn out to be a *group*, and thus also algebraic in nature.

Coming up with the right abstractions is difficult - too abstract and you cannot draw interesting conclusions, too specific and you lose generality. To find the right abstraction, you first have to consider many different *instantiations* and draw similarities between them, concocting the right abstractions after a long and iterative process. This is an important fact in any field of mathematics: take for example the topic of continuity. Discussions on continuity usually start with a definition, followed by a list of results. The definition for a continuous function in topology is "a function $f: A \to B$ is *continuous* if for every open set in the image of $f$, its pre-image is also open". At first, the definition seems arbitrary and not particularly interesting, and it is only through seeing the results on continuous functions in a topological space that the reader becomes convinced that it is indeed a useful (in the sense that it generalizes key properties of many useful results) and good (in the sense that it makes derivation of those results relatively simple and clean) definition, but to come up with a good definition one must first have a list of results, thus the mathematical process is oftentimes presented in reversed order.

All of this is to say that, while our discussion will follow the usual form of *definition -> results -> new definition -> results* etc, it is important to remember that the definitions are exactly the necessary abstractions for the derivation of the results that follow them, i.e. historically results *precede* definitions. However, once we have good abstractions, we can shift our focus to the study of the abstractions themselves, producing powerful new results.

# Groups

Introductory courses in abstract algebra usually start with groups, however I personally prefer to start with monoids.

>[!definition] monoid
>
>A *monoid* is a structure $(S, \cdot)$ where $S$ is a set and $\cdot$ is a binary operation on the set (usually referred to as multiplication or addition), such that it satisfies the following properties:
>1. (Identity) $\exists e \in S$ s.t. $\forall s \in S$, $e\cdot s = s \cdot e = s$.
>2. (Associative) $\forall a, b, c \in s$, $a \cdot (b \cdot c) = (a \cdot b) \cdot c$


Note that here we use multiplicative notation, but it is also customary to consider additive notation, then we say that $(S, +)$ is a monoid if it has an identity $e$ s.t. $\forall s \in S, e+s=s+e=s$ and write associativity as $a+(b+c)=(a+b)+c$.

>[!proposition] uniqueness of the identity
>
>Given a monoid $(S, \cdot)$, its identity element is unqiue.


**Proof.** Take $e_{1}, e_{2} \in S$ s.t. both are identities, not necessarily distinct (since $(S, \cdot)$ is a monoid there must exist an identity), then $e_{2} = e_{1} \cdot e_{2} = e_{1}$ since both are left and right identities, so $e_{1}=e_{2}$, so the identity element is unqiue. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example]
>
>$(\mathbb{Z}, \cdot)$ where $\cdot$ is the usual multiplication operation on $\mathbb{Z}$ is a monoid.

**Proof.** We know that $1$ is the multiplicative identity on $\mathbb{Z}$, so we have an identity. Next, we know that multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ is associative. We conclude that $\mathbb{Z}$ is a monoid under $\cdot_{\mathbb{Z}}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

By requiring a third property from the structure, we devise a group:

>[!definition] group
>
>A *group* is a *monoid* $(G, \cdot)$ such that:
>1. (Inverse) $\forall a \in G \exists a^{-1} \in G$ s.t. $a^{-1} \cdot a = e$. 

>[!proposition] group multiplication is bijective
>
>Given a group $(G, \cdot)$, then the *partial mapping* $\cdot_{a}: x \mapsto a \cdot x$ is a bijection for some $a \in G$.


**Proof.** Let $h=\cdot_{a}$. Take any $y \in \text{domain}(h)=G$. Since $G$ is a group, $a^{-1} \in G$ and $a^{-1} y \in G$ since $G$ is closed under multiplication, and $h(a^{-1}y)=a (a^{-1} y)$ by definition, which gives $y$, so $h$ is surjective.

Take $x_{1}, x_{2} \in G$ s.t. $h(x_{1})=h(x_{2})$, i.e. $ax_{1} = ax_{2}$. Since $a \in G$ and $G$ is a group then $a^{-1} \in G$, so pre-multiplying both sides by $a^{-1}$ (this preserves the equality by the substitution principle which we discussed at length in [Introduction to Formal Logic](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/logic.pdf), we get $a^{-1}(ax_{1} )= a^{-1}(ax_{2})$, and by associativity we eventually get $x_{1}=x_{2}$, so $h$ is *injective*.

We conclude that $h$ is bijective.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] properties of the inverse
>
>1. The inverse is both a left inverse (as defined above) and a right inverse.
>2. The inverse is unique.
>3. The inverse is an involution, i.e. $(a^{-1})^{-1} = a$.
>4. $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$ (socks-shoes property)


**Proof.** 

$1:\quad$Let $a \in G$ and let $a^{-1} \in G$ be inverse of $a$, so $a^{-1}a = e$. Now consider $aa^{-1}$ multiplied by itself, i.e. $(aa^{-1})(aa^{-1})$, by associativity we have $(aa^{-1})(aa^{-1})=a(a^{-1}a)a^{-1}=aea^{-1}=aa^{-1}$, so we have $(aa^{-1})(aa^{-1})=aa^{-1}$, so $aa^{-1}$ acts as the identity element on $aa^{-1}$, and by proposition 2 (uniqueness of the identity) we have $aa^{-1}=e$, so by definition $a^{-1}$ is the right inverse of $a$. This shows that left inverse implies right inverse. To show right inverse implies left inverse, we follow a similar argument and write $(a^{-1}a)(a^{-1}a)=a^{-1}(aa^{-1})a=a^{-1}ea=a^{-1}a$. As a result we can speak of *an inverse* in both cases and we don't need to specify if we are referring to a right inverse or a left inverse.

$2:\quad$Let $a \in G$ and let $x, y \in G$ be inverses of $a$ not necessarily distinct, then from $(1)$ we know that $ax=e=ay$. By proposition 5 (group multiplication is bijective) it follows that $x=y$.

$3:\quad$Consider $a^{-1}a=e$, then $a^{-1}$ is the left inverse of $a$ which exists by the group axioms. Similarly by definition, $a$ is the right inverse of $a^{-1}$, and by $(1)$ this implies that $(a^{-1}){-1}=a$.

$4:\quad$Let $ab \in G$. Consider $(ab)(b^{-1}a^{-1})$. From associativity, $(ab)(b^{-1}a^{-1})=a(bb^{-1})a^{-1}=aea^{-1}=aa^{-1}=e$, so $b^{-1}a^{-1}$ is an inverse of $ab$, and from uniqueness of the inverse $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>$(G, \cdot)$ is a group if and only if $G$ has a left identity and left inverse for every element under $\cdot$ and $\cdot$ is associative on $G$.


**Proof.** $\implies$is immediate from the definition of a group. $\impliedby$We will show that a left identity implies a right identity given an associative operator with a left inverse. Let $e$ be the left identity on $G$, then $\forall a \in G, ea=a$. Now consider $aea=a(ea)=aa$ and also $aea=(ae)a$ so we have $(ae)a=aa$. Taking the left inverse $a^{-1}$ and post-multiplying both sides we get $(ae)aa^{-1}=aaa^{-1}$,  ans by proposition 6 (properties of the inverse) we know that $a^{-1}$ is also a right inverse, so $aa^{-1}=e$, so $(ae)aa^{-1}=ae=aaa^{-1}=a$, so $ae=a$, so $e$ is also a right identity. We see that $(G, \cdot)$ has an identity in $e$ (both left and right), an inverse for every element, and has the associative property by supposition, so it is a group. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] groups
>
>1. From the properties of $(\mathbb{Z}, \cdot)$, we know that $1 \in \mathbb{Z}$ is the identity element, that $\cdot$ is associative, and that $\forall a \in \mathbb{Z}$, its reciprocal $a^{-1} \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $a^{-1}a=1$, so $(\mathbb{Z}, \cdot)$ satisfies all group axioms, so we conclude it to be a group.
>2. Let $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ be the set of all $n \times n$ matrices with scalars from $\mathbb{R}$ such that $\forall A \in \text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$, $A$ is non-singular (i.e. $|A| \neq 0$). From linear algebra we know that $|AB|=|A| \cdot |B|$ so it follows that if $|A| \neq 0$ and $|B| \neq 0$ then $|AB| \neq 0$ so $AB \in \text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ for every $A, B \in \text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ so it is closed under matrix multiplication, i.e. matrix multiplication can be thought of as a binary operator on the elements of $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$. We also know that $|A|$ if and only if $A$ is invertible, i.e. $\exists A^{-1} \in \text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ s.t. $A^{-1}A=I$, where $I$ is the identity $n \times n$ matrix and multiplication is the usual definition for multiplication of matrices, so we have a left inverse. We also know that for every matrix $A$,  $IA=AI=A$, so $I$ is the *identity* element of matrix multiplication. Similarly, we know that $A(BC)=(AB)C$ (if multiplication on those matrices is defined, which is always the case if $A, B, C$ all have the same dimensions, which is the case in $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$), so we have associativity. Thus we conclude that $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ is a group under the usual matrix multiplication operation.

It is important to note that even though $(a=e) \implies (a=a^{-1})$, the converse is not true, i.e. $(a = a^{-1}) \centernot \implies (a=e)$. As an example, consider the subset of [involutory matrices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Involutory_matrix) of $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$, clearly it is not just $I_{n}$, yet all such matrices satisfy $A=A^{-1}$.

>[!theorem] cancellation law
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $a, b, c \in G$, then $(ab=ac) \implies (b=c)$ and $(ba=ca) \implies (b=a)$.


**Proof.** Suppose $ab=ac$, then pre-multiply by $a^{-1}$ and from associativity we have $a^{-1}ab=(a^{-1}a)b=eb=b$ and also $a^{-1}ac=(a^{-1}a)c=c$, so $b=c$. To show $(ba = ca) \implies (b=a)$ we post multiply each side with $a^{-1}$ and get $b=a$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] abelian group
>
>A group is *abelian* or *commutative* if it also satisfies the following property:
>1. (commutative) $\forall x, y \in G$, $x \cdot y = y \cdot x$.

>[!proposition]
>
>Let $G$ be a group. If $\forall a, b, c \in G$, $(ab=ca) \implies (b=c)$, then $G$ is abelian.


**Proof.**  Let $a, b \in G$ and substitute $a$ for $a^{-1}$, $b$ for $ab$ and $c$ for $ba$ in $(ab=ca) \implies (b=c)$, then we get $(a^{-1}(ab)=(ba)a^{-1}) \implies (ab=ba)$. Consider $a^{-1}(ab)=(ba)a^{-1}$, by  associativity we have $(a^{-1}a)b=b(aa^{-1})$, so $b=b$, so the LHS of the implication chain is true, so by MP we have $ab=ba$, so by definition $G$ is abelian. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] abelian and non-abelian groups
>
>1. $(\mathbb{Z}, \cdot)$ is an abelian group since $\cdot$ is commutative.
>2. $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})$ is *not* a group under matrix multiplication since generally matrix multiplication does not commute.

## Power Rules

Let $G$ be a group and consider some $a \in G$, and assume multiplicative notation. Since $G$ is a group and $a \in G$, then $a\cdot a \in G$, and similarly $(a \cdot a) \cdot a \in G$, etc. Also from associativity, it follows that we can lose the parenthesis and write $(a \cdot a) \cdot a = a \cdot a \cdot a$. We can keep playing this game and multiply $a \cdot a$ with itself, and get $a \cdot a \cdot a \cdot a$, or $a \cdot a \cdot a$ with $a$ and get $a \cdot a \cdot a \cdot a$. This form of writing is rather tedious, so we introduce power notation:

>[!definition] power notation
>
>Let $G$ be a group and let $a \in G$, then $a^{n}$ for $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is defined recursively as follows:
>1. $a^{0}=e_{G}$
>2. $a^{n+1}=a^{n}a$
>3. $a^{-n}=(a^{-1})^{n}$


From this definition, it quickly follows that $a^{-n+1}=a^{-n}a$:

>[!proposition]
>
>Let $a \in G$ a group and let $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then $a^{-n+1}=a^{-n}a$.


**Proof.** Let $G$ be a group, $a \in G$ and $n \in \mathbb{N}$. By definition $a^{-n}=(a^{-1})^{n}$. Consider two cases:
1. $0 \lt n$, then $a^{-n}=(a^{-1})^{n}=(a^{-1})^{n-1}a^{-1}$, post-multiplying both sides by $a$ and using the associative property of the group operation we have $a^{-n}a=(a^{-1})^{n}a=(a^{-1})^{n-1}(a^{-1}a)=(a^{-1})^{n-1}$, again by definition we have $(a^{-1})^{n-1}=a^{-n+1}$, so $a^{-n}a=a^{-n+1}$.
2. $0 = n$, then $a^{-n+1}=a^{-0+1}=a$, and also $a^{-n}=a^{-0}=a^{0}=e$, so $a^{-n+1}=a=ea=a^{0}a=a^{-n}a$,

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It can be shown that all regular power rules apply:

>[!theorem] exponent laws (power rules)
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $a, b \in G$, $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$, then:
>1. $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m+n}=a^{n}a^{m}$
>2. $(a^{-1})^{n}=(a^{n})^{-1}$
>3. $(a^{m})^{n}=a^{mn}=(a^{n})^{m}$
>4. $(ab)^{-n}=(b^{-1}a^{-1})^{n}$, and if $G$ is abelian then $(ab)^{n}=a^{n}b^{n}$. This condition may be relaxed to requiring only that $a, b$ commute under the group operation of $G$.


**Proof.** 

$1.$ Suppose $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m+n}$, since addition in $\mathbb{Z}$ commutes then $a^{m+n}=a^{n+m}$ and by supposition $a^{n}a^{m}=a^{n+m}$ so $a^{n}a^{m}=a^{m}a^{n}$, which takes care of the second part of this proposition. To show $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m+n}$, consider the following:

Suppose $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$, then we prove by induction on $n$. If $n=0$ then $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m}a^{0}=a^{m}e=a^{m}=a^{m+n}$, similarly if $n=1$ then $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m}a^{1}=a^{m}a$ which by definition is $a^{m+1}=a^{m+n}$ so the theorem holds. Suppose it holds for some $n$ and consider $n+1$, then $a^{m}a^{n+1}=a^{m}a^{n}a^{1}$, so by induction hypothesis $a^{m}a^{n}a^{1}=a^{m+n}a^{1}$, and by definition $a^{m+n}a^{1}=a^{m+n+1}$, which completes the proof by induction for the case where $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$.

Suppose $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$. If $0 \leq m, n$ then this reduces to the case we have just proven, so either both $m, n \lt 0$ or one is negative and the other is non-negative. Consider two cases:
1. $m, n \lt 0$, then $\exists m', n' \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $m=-m', n=-n'$, so $a^{m}=a^{-m'}$ and $a^{n}=a^{-n'}$, so by definition 13 (power notation) we have $a^{n}=(a^{-1})^{n'}, a^{m}=(a^{-1})^{m'}$. Let $b=a^{-1}$, then $a^{m}a^{n}=b^{m'}b^{n'}$ with $m', n' \in \mathbb{N}$ which reduces to the previous case, so $a^{m}a^{n}=b^{m'+n'}=(a^{-1})^{m'+n'}$ which by definition is $a^{-m'-n'}$, which is just $a^{m+n}$, which proves the theorem for the case when $m, n \lt 0$.
2. One of $(m, n)$ is negative and the other is not, w.l.o.g suppose $m \lt 0$ and $n \leq 0$, then we can proceed by induction on $n$ as we did for the case when $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$ using proposition 14 .

So in all cases for $m, n$ integers, we find that $a^{m}a^{n}=a^{m+n}$, which completes the proof.

$2.$ Let $n \in \mathbb{Z}$. If $0 \leq n$, then we prove by induction on $n$. For $n=0$ we have $(a^{-1})^{0}=e=e^{-1}=(a^{0})^{-1}$. Suppose by induction the proposition holds for some $n$, and consider $n+1$, then $a^{n+1}$ is by definition $a^{n}a$, and by proposition 6 (properties of the inverse) we have $(a^{n+1})^{-1}=(a^{n}a)^{-1}=a^{-1}(a^{n})^{-1}$. By the induction hypothesis $(a^{-1})^{n}=(a^{n})^{-1}$, so $a^{-1}(a^{n})^{-1}=a^{-1}(a^{-1})^{n}$, which by definition is $(a^{-1})^{n+1}$, so $(a^{n+1})^{-1}=(a^{-1})^{n+1}$, which completes the proof by induction for $0 \leq n$. 

Suppose then that $n \lt 0$, then $(a^{-1})^{n}=((a^{-1})^{-1})^{|n|}$ by definition. Write $a^{-1}=b$, then we have $(b^{-1})^{|n|}=(b^{|n|})^{-1}$ (by the previous case), but $b^{|n|}=(a^{-1})^{|n|}$ which by definition is $a^{n}$ (since $n \lt 0$), so $(a^{-1})^{n}=(b^{-1})^{|n|} = (b^{|n|})^{-1} = (a^{n})^{-1}$.

$3.$ As with the previous proposition we first handle the second part. Suppose $(a^{m})^{n}=a^{mn}$, then since multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ commutes $a^{mn}=a^{nm}$, and by supposition $(a^{n})^{m}=a^{nm}$, so $(a^{n})^{m}=(a^{m})^{n}$. We now turn our attention to the first part.

Suppose $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we prove by induction on $n$. If $n=0$ then $(a^{m})^{0}=e=a^{0}=a^{mn}$, if $n=1$ then $(a^{m})^{n}=(a^{m})^{1}=a^{m}=a^{mn}$. Suppose the proposition is true for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and consider $n+1$. By definition $(a^{m})^{n+1}=(a^{m})^{n}a^{m}$, by the induction hypothesis $(a^{m})^{n}=a^{mn}$ so $(a^{m})^{n}a^{m}=a^{mn}a^{m}$, and by proposition $(1)$ we have $a^{mn}a^{m}=a^{mn+m}=a^{m(n+1)}$, which completes the proof.

Now suppose $n \notin \mathbb{N}$, then $n \lt 0$. If both $m, n$ are negative, then $a^{m}=a^{-|m|}$ and $a^{n}=a^{-|n|}$. By definition, $a^{m}=a^{-|m|}=(a^{-1})^{|m|}$. By proposition $(2)$, $(a^{-1})^{|m|}=(a^{|m|})^{-1}$, so $(a^{m})^{n}=((a^{|m|})^{-1})^{-|n|}$, which by definition is $(((a^{|m|})^{-1})^{-1})^{|n|}$, which simplifies to $(a^{|m|})^{|n|}$. Since $0 \leq |m|, |n|$ we can use the result from the inductive proof and get $(a^{m})^{n}=(a^{|m|})^{|n|}=a^{|m| |n|}$, but since $m, n \lt 0$ then $mn=|m| |n|$ so $(a^{m})^{n}=a^{mn}$.

Finally, suppose $n \lt 0$ and $0 \leq m$, then we write $(a^{m})^{n}=(a^{m})^{-|n|}=((a^{m})^{-1})^{|n|}=(*)$. Using proposition $(2)$ again, we get $(*)= (a^{-m})^{|n|}=((a^{-1})^{m})^{|n|}$, and now the result for natural exponents applies and we get $((a^{-1})^{m})^{|n|}=(a^{-1})^{m|n|}$, which by definition is $a^{-m|n|}=a^{m \cdot (-|n|)}=a^{mn}$.

$4.$ Let $n \in \mathbb{Z}$. If $0 \leq n$ then by definition $(ab)^{-n}=((ab)^{-1})^{n}$, and by proposition 6 (properties of the inverse) we know that $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$, so $(ab)^{-n}=(b^{-1}a^{-1})^{n}$. Otherwise, $n \lt 0$ so we use the fact that $((ab)^{-1})^{-1}=ab$ and write $a^{-n}=(((ab)^{-1})^{-1})^{-n}=(*)$ and since $n \lt 0$ then $0 \lt -n$ so by definition $(*)=((ab)^{-1})^{n}$, and finally we substitute $(ab)^{-1}$ with $b^{-1}a^{-1}$ and get $(ab)^{-n}=(b^{-1}a^{-1})^{n}$.

We turn our attention to the second part of the proposition. Suppose $G$ is abeliean and $n \in \mathbb{Z}$. If $0 \leq n$, we proceed by induction. For $n=0$ we have $(ab)^{0}=e=ee=a^{0}b^{0}$, similarly for $n=1$ we have $(ab)^{1}=ab=a^{1}b^{1}$. Suppose the proposition is true for some $n$ and consider $n+1$. By definition, $(ab)^{n+1}=(ab)^{n}(ab)$ and since $G$ is abelian $ab=ba$ so $(ab)^{n}(ab)=(ab)^{n}(ba)$, now from the induction hypothesis and associativity we have $(ab)^{n}=a^{n}b^{n}ba=a^{n}b^{n+1}a$, and by commutativity $(a^{n}b^{n+1})a=a(a^{n}b^{n+1})$ which by associativity is equivalent to $(aa^{n})b^{n+1}=a^{n+1}b^{n+1}$, so the proposition holds for $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Suppose then that $n \lt 0$, then $ab^{n}=(b^{-1}a^{-1})^{-n}$ by the first part of the proposition, with $-n \in \mathbb{N}$, so by the inductive argument we have $(b^{-1}a^{-1})^{-n}=(b^{-1})^{-n}(a^{-1})^{-n}$, and by definition we have $(b^{-1})^{-n}=b^{n}$ (since $n \lt 0$) and also $(a^{-1})^{-n}=a^{n}$, so $ab^{n}=b^{n}a^{n}$, and finally by the commutative property we have $ab^{n}=a^{n}b^{n}$, which completes the proof for the second part of the proposition.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that in this section, we assumed the group operation is multiplicative, but the results obviously hold for additive group operation with a change of notation.

>[!definition] additive notation
>
>Let $G$ be a group with an additive operation $+$, then $n \cdot a$ (also juxtaposed as $na$) for $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is defined recursively as:
>1. $0 \cdot a=e$
>2. $(n+1) \cdot a = n \cdot a + a$
>3. $(-n) \cdot a = n \cdot (-a)$


The proof for theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) is identical, with a change in notation (for example, $a^{m}a^{n}$ becomes $ma \cdot na$).

## Group Morphisms

Group morphisms are mappings between two groups that preserve the group structure.

>[!definition] group homomorphism
>
>A *group homomrphism* is a function $\varphi: G \to H$ where $(G, \cdot)$ and $(H, \times)$ are groups, such that $\forall a, b \in G, \varphi(a \cdot b)=\varphi(a) \times \varphi(b)$.

>[!example]
>
>Recall that $\mathbb{Q}$ is usually constructed as the quotient set of $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^{+}$ under the equivalence relation $\langle a, b \rangle \sim \langle c, d \rangle \iff a \cdot d=b \cdot c$ (where multiplication on the RHS is multiplication as usually defined on $\mathbb{Z}$). Consider a function $E: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q}$ given by $E: z \mapsto \langle z, 1 \rangle$. We have seen in [Introduction to Set Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/intro_set_theory.pdf) that this function satisfies $E(z+_{\mathbb{Z}} w)=E(z)+_{\mathbb{Q}} E(w)$, so $E$ is a group homomorphism from $(\mathbb{Z}, +_{\mathbb{Z}})$ and $(\mathbb{Q}, +_{\mathbb{Q}})$.

>[!proposition] group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse
>
>Let $e_{G}, e_{H}$ be the identity elements of $G, H$ respectively, and let $\varphi: G \to H$ be a group homomorphism, then:
>1. $\varphi(e_{G})=e_{H}$
>2. $\forall a \in G, \varphi(a^{-1})=(\varphi(a))^{-1}$


**Proof.** $1:\quad$Since $\varphi$ is a group homomorphism then $\varphi(e_{G}e_{G})=\varphi(e_{G})\varphi(e_{G})$ , now since $e_{G}e_{G}=e_{G}$ we have $\varphi(e_{G})=\varphi(e_{G})\varphi(e_{G})$, so by definition $\varphi(e_{G})$ is the identity of $H$, and by uniqueness we have $\varphi(e_{G})=e_{H}$.

$2:\quad$We have $\varphi(aa^{-1})=\varphi(a)\varphi(a^{-1})=e$, so by proposition 6 (properties of the inverse) we have that $\varphi(a^{-1})$ is the inverse of $\varphi(a)$, i.e. $\varphi(a^{-1})=(\varphi(a))^{-1}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

More generally, a homomorphism (or *morphism* for short) is a structure-preserving mapping, e.g. a ring homomorphism would be a function $\varphi: R \to T$ where both $R, T$ are rings, such that it preserves the ring structure. We will present concrete examples when we define rings. We have already seen such examples in [Introduction to Set Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/intro_set_theory.pdf).

##  Groups and Order

>[!definition] order of a group
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then the number of elements in the group, i.e. $|G|$, is called the *order of the group*. If $G$ is not finite, then we write $|G|=\infty$.

If $G$ is a finite group, then it must be that $\forall a \in G \exists z \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $a^{z}=e$, since otherwise the group would not be finite. For example in the finite group $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$ whose elements are $0, 1, 2$ and the group operation is addition $\mod 3$ (so we use additive notation), we have $e_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}=0$ and $3 \cdot 1 = 3 \equiv 0 \mod 3$, and $3 \cdot 2 = 6 \equiv 0 \mod 3$.
A formal argument for the general cause is given below:

>[!proposition] order of elements in finite group is finite
>
>Let $G$ be a finite group and $e \in G$ be the identity element, then $\forall a \in G \exists z \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $a^{z}=e$.


**Proof.** Let $G$ be a finite group and let $|G|=n$, then $G$ has exactly $n$ distinct elements. Consider the sequence $(a^{0}, a^{1}, \dots, a^{n})$. Since each element $a^{i}$ is given by $a^{i}=a^{i-1}a$ with $a^{0}=e$, it follows that all the elements in the sequence are also members of $G$, so it must be that not all are unique, since $G$ has exactly $n$ elements, so $\exists m, k \in \mathbb{N}$, $m, k \in [0, n], m \neq k$ s.t. $a^{m}=a^{k}$. Suppose w.l.o.g $k \lt m$, then post multiply both sides by $a^{-k}$ (by power rules $a^{-k}=(a^{-1})^{k}$ and since $a \in G$ and $G$ is a group, $a^{-1} \in G$ and also all its powers since it is closed under the group operation) yields $a^{m}a^{-k}=a^{k}a^{-k}$, which by exponent laws is equivalent to $a^{m-k}=a^{k-k}=a^{0}=e$, and since $k \lt m$ it follows that $0 \lt m-k$, so $m-k \in \mathbb{N}$. Denote $z=m-k$, then $a^{z}=e$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

This motivates defining the *order of an element*.

>[!definition] order of an element
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $a \in G$, then the *order of $a$*, denoted $|a|$, is the *least positive integer* $n$ s.t. $a^{n}=e$. If there isn't such $n$, then we say $|a|=\infty$.


>[!example] order of $2$ in $\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{6}$
>
>Recall that $\mathbb{Z}_{6}^{*}$ consists of the elements $\{1, 5\}$ (the positive integers up to $6$ that are co-prime w.r.t $6$). We will find the order of each element:
>1. $1$ has order $1$ since $1^{1}=1$, and $1$ is the multiplicative identity. In general for every group $G$, $|e_{G}|=1$.
>2. $[5^{2}]=[25]=[4 \cdot 6 + 1]=[1]$, so $5$ has order $2$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{6}^{*}$.

By proposition 21 (order of elements in finite group is finite) and by the well-ordering principle on $\mathbb{N}$ it follows that the order of an element is well-defined for finite groups.

>[!theorem]
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $a \in G$. Then:
>1. If $|a|=\infty$ then $\forall i, j \in \mathbb{Z}$, $a^{i}=a^{j} \iff i=j$
>2. If $|a|=n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then $\forall i, j \in \mathbb{Z}$, $a^{i}=a^{j} \iff n | (i-j)$


**Proof.** 

$1.$ $\impliedby$ Suppose $i=j$, then $a^{i}=a^{j}$. 

$\implies$Suppose by contradiction $\exists i, j \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $a^{i}=a^{j}$ and $i \neq j$, then $i-j \neq 0$. Since $G$ is a group then $a^{-j} \in G$, and multiplying both sides by $a^{-j}$ we have $a^{i}a^{-j}=a^{j}a^{-j}$, which by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) simplifies to $a^{i-j}=a^{j-j}=a^{0}=e$, and also $(a^{i-j})^{-1}=a^{j-i}=e$, so by definition 22 (order of an element)  $|a| \leq |i-j|$, but we assume $|a|=\infty$, a contradiction, so $i=j$.

$2$. $\impliedby$Suppose $n|(i-j)$, then $\exists m \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $i-j=mn$, so $a^{i-j}=a^{mn}=(a^{n})^{m}=e^{m}=e$. Multiplying both sides by $a^{j}$ we have $a^{i-j}a^{j}=ea^{j}$, i.e. $a^{i}=a^{j}$.

$\implies$ Suppose $a^{i}=a^{j}$, and multiply both sides by $a^{-j}$, so we have $a^{i-j}=a^{0}=e$. Now by the division algorithm we know that $\exists!r, q \in \mathbb{Z}, q \in [0, n)$ s.t. $i-j=rn+q$. Substituting for $i-j$ we have $e=a^{i-j}=a^{rn+q}=(a^{n})^{r}a^{q}=a^{q}$, so $a^{q}=e$ and also $0 \leq q \lt n$, but since $|a| = n$ it cannot be that $0 \lt q$, so it must be that $q=0$, so $i-j=rn$, so by definition $n|(i-j)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $a^{k}=e$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, then $n|k$.


**Proof.** Recall $a^{0}=e$. By theorem 24  $a^{k}=a^{0} \implies n | (k-0)$, so $n|k$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Cayley Tables

In this section, we consider groups of finite order.

>[!definition] trivial group
>
>The *trivial group* is a group which consists of a set with one element, denoted $e$, and a binary operation s.t. $e \cdot e = e$, i.e. it is the set that consists only of the identity element. We denote this group $\mathbb{Z}_{1}$. It has order $1$.


Verifying that it is indeed a group is trivial: $e$ is the identity element, $e$ is its own inverse and $e \cdot e = e$ so $e \cdot (e \cdot e) = e \cdot e = (e \cdot e) \cdot e$, so it is associative, so $\mathbb{Z}_{1}$ is a group. It is also vacuously abelian.

Consider a set of two elements $\{a, b \}$. If we wish to create a group from this set, we need to choose one of the elements as the identity element, so we have a set $\{e, a\}$ where $e$ is the identity under a binary operation we haven't yet fully defined, however we only know that $e \cdot e = e, a \cdot e = e \cdot a = a$ (properties of the identity), so all that remains is to determine $a \cdot a$. Since $a \cdot a$ should be $\in \{e, a \}$, it can be either $e$ or $a$. Suppose $aa=a$,then the function $x \mapsto ax$ is given by $e \mapsto a, a \mapsto a$, i.e. its range is $\{a\}$, which is $\neq \{e, a \}$, so it is not surjective, contradicting that proposition 5 (group multiplication is bijective), so it cannot be the case that $aa=a$ if $(\{e, a \}, \cdot)$ is a group, then it must be that $aa=e$, and indeed we can verify that given the set $\{e, a\}$ and the multiplication operation $\cdot$ given explicitly by $ee=e, ae=a, ea=a, aa=e$,  the structure $(\{e, a\}, \cdot)$ is indeed an abelian group and is the only group on two elements (up to an isomorphism).

Before we discuss groups of order three, we should introduce a nice way to visualize how elements of a finite group behave under the group operation: Cayley table.

>[!definition] Cayley table
>
>A Cayley table is a two dimensional table similar to a multiplication table from elementary school, where each row and each column corresponds to an element of a finite group, and every $(i, j)$ cell where $i$ is the row and $j$ is the column corresponds to the result of multiplying the element represented by row $i$ with the element represented by row $j$.
>
>More concretely, if $G$ is a group of order $n$, then consider an ordering $G=\{a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}\}$ on its elements, then the Cayley table of $G$ consists of a two-dimensional table $T$ where the cell at $T_{i, j}=a_{i}a_{j}$.

>[!example] Cayley table of finite group on two elements
>
>Let $G=\{e, a\}$, i.e. $G$ is the finite group on two elements, then as described above we have $ee=e, ae=a, ea=a, aa=e$, so the Cayley table of $G$ is given by
>
>| $\cdot$ | e | a |
>| -- | -- | -- |
>| e | e | a |
>| a | a | e |
>
>Since $G$ is a group of order $2$ and we found that there ought the be exactly one such group (up to a relabeling), and $\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}$ is a group of order $2$, then we find that $G$ and $\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}$ are essentially the same, i.e. they are *isomorphic*: $\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+} \cong G$. We make this connection more formal later.

Cayley tables make in extremely easy to tell whether a group is abelian: the table is symmetric along the diagonal if and only if the group is abelian.

By proposition 5 (group multiplication is bijective), it is apparent that if $G$ is a group and $T(G)$ is its corresponding Cayley table, then each row and each column in $T(G)$ should have each element of $G$ appearing exactly once. Also, if we maintain that the first row and first column represent the identity element $e$, then clearly the value of each cell is already predetermined, since $ea=ae=a$ for every $a \in G$.

This motivates a Soduko-like approach to constructing the Cayley table of a group, however caution is warranted in this case: this restriction leaves too many DOFs - it does not capture the associative property of a group, hence it risks thinking of non-group structures as groups since their Cayley table seems to be valid, in the sense that each element appears exactly once along each row and each column. The reader should beware of such cases, and always verify the associative property is satisfied.

As an example of using a Cayley table to understand how a group looks like under the group operation, consider a finite group on $3$ elements $e, a, b$. First, we fill the template for its Cayley table:

| $\cdot$ | e   | a   | b   |
| ------- | --- | --- | --- |
| e       | e   | a   | b   |
| a       | a   |     |     |
| b       | b   |     |     |

The following restrictions are:
1. Of $aa, ab$, one should be $b$ and the other $e$ by bijectivity of left multiplication by $a$
2. Of $ba, bb$ one should be $a$ and the other $e$.

Suppose $aa=e$, but now we have $ab=b$ and $eb=b$ both in the last column, which violates the restriction that every element appear once, so this case is impossible, so it must be that $aa=b$, and then also $ab=e$.

Likewise, suppose $ba=a$, then $ba=a$ and two rows above it on the same column we have $ea=a$, which is not allowed for the same reason, so it must be that $ba=e$, and then also $bb=a$.

So, filling out the table, we find that there exists a unique group of order $3$ up to an isomorphism, and its Cayley table looks like this:

| $\cdot$ | e   | a   | b   |
| ------- | --- | --- | --- |
| e       | e   | a   | b   |
| a       | a   | b   | e   |
| b       | b   | e   | a   |

It is evident that this group is *non-abelian*, since the table is not symmetric along the diagonal. The reader should also practice caution and verify that multiplication as described in this table is indeed associative on the elements of $G$.

Consider $|G|=4$. Playing the same game, we find that two different Cayley tables representing valid groups, however for $|G|=5$ we again find only one such table. So, there appears that there are two truly different finite groups of order $4$, and again a single (up to isomorphism) finite group of order $5$.

We spend a great deal of time later in order to create the machinery needed to classify finite groups, but for now the reader should wonder why it is the case that for $|G|=2, 3, 5$, all of which are prime, we find that there really is only one such group (up to an isomorphism), while for $|G|=4$, a composite number, this is not the case.

## Subgroups

Consider the group $(\mathbb{Z}, +)$. Now consider the subset of $\mathbb{Z}$ that consists of only the even integers $\{2z | z \in \mathbb{Z} \}$ or equivalently via coset notation (which we will formally define soon) $2\mathbb{Z}$. Now consider the structure $(2\mathbb{Z}, +)$. Let us verify that it is indeed a group:

1. First we must verify $2\mathbb{Z}$ is closed under $+$: let $a, b \in 2\mathbb{Z}$, so $a=2z, b=2w$ for some $z, w \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $a+b=2(z+w)$, and since $z+w \in \mathbb{Z}$ we have $2(z+w) \in 2\mathbb{Z}$, so it is closed under addition. This is the same claim as *the sum of evens is even*.
2. Identity: For $z=0, 2z=0$ so $0 \in 2 \mathbb{Z}$, and $2z+0=2z=0+2z$, so $0$ is the identity in $2\mathbb{Z}$.
3. Associativity: Since $+$ is associative on $\mathbb{Z}$ and $2\mathbb{Z} \subset \mathbb{Z}$, $+$ is also associative on $2\mathbb{Z}$.
4. Inverse: If $a \in 2 \mathbb{Z}$ then $a=2z$ for some integer $z$, and also $2(-z) \in 2\mathbb{Z}$ by definition and $2(-z)=-2z$, now by adding the two we get $2z+(-2z)=0$, so $\forall a \in 2 \mathbb{Z}$ we can find an inverse in $2\mathbb{Z}$.

We then conclude that $(2\mathbb{Z}, +)$ is a group, and call it a *subgroup* of $(\mathbb{Z}, +)$.

>[!definition] subgroup
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $H \subseteq G$. If $H$ is a group under the group operation of $G$, then $H$ is called a *subgroup* of $G$, denoted $H \leqslant G$.

Note that in our example we found that $0$, the additive identity of $(\mathbb{Z}, +)$, is also the additive identity of $(2\mathbb{Z}, +)$. This is always the case.

>[!proposition] identity and inverse elements of a subgroup
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $H \leqslant G$, then 
>1. $e_{H}=e_{G}=e$ (subgroup shares group's identity)
>2. $\forall a \in H, a^{-1}_{G}=a^{-1}_{H}=a^{-1}$ (inverse of subgroup element is the inverse of the element in the group)

**Proof.** 

$1.$ Suppose by contradiction $e_{H} \neq e_{G}$, and let $a \in H$. Since $e_{H}$ is the identity on $H$ we have $e_{H}a=a$ and also $e_{G}a=a$ when considered in $G$, so $e_{H}a=a=e_{G}a$, so $e_{H}a=e_{G}a$, so by theorem 9 (cancellation law) we have $e_{H}=e_{G}$, so we can write $e=e_{H}=e_{G}$.

$2.$ Since $a^{-1}_{G}a=e_{G}$ and $a^{-1}_{H}a=e_{H}$, and by $1$ $e_{G}=e_{H}=e$ so $a^{-1}_{G}a=a^{-1}_{H}a$, so again by theorem 9 (cancellation law) $a^{-1}_{G}=a^{-1}_{H}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now present two theorems for testing if a structure is a subgroup.

>[!theorem] two-step subgroup test
>
>If $(G, \cdot)$ is a group and $H \subseteq G$, then $(H, \cdot)$ is a group if and only if:
>1. $H$ is not empty
>2. $H$ is closed under the group operation $\cdot$
>3. $\forall a \in H, a^{-1} \in H$


**Proof.** $\implies$ is trivial. $\impliedby$Suppose $H$ is a non-empty subset of $G$. Since $H$ is closed under the group operation, we can consider $\cdot$ restricted to $H$ as a binary operator on $H$, so it remains to show that $(H, \cdot)$ is associative, has an identity and an inverse for every element:

1. Associativity follows directly from the associative property of $(G, \cdot)$ since both share the same operation.
2. Inverse is immediate from $(3)$
3. For identity, consider some $a \in H$ ($H$ is nonempty so there is always an element $a$). From $(3)$, $a^{-1} \in H$, so $aa^{-1} \in H$ from $(2)$, but $aa^{-1}=e$, so $e \in H$, hence identity.

So $H \leqslant G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] one-step subgroup test
>
>If $(G, \cdot)$ is a group and $H \subseteq G$, then $(H, \cdot)$ is a group if and only if:
>1. $H$ is not empty
>2. $\forall a, b \in H, ab^{-1} \in H$.
>
>More compactly, we say that $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ if and only if it is nonempty and closed under division (if we interpret the group operation in the multiplicative sense. If we interpret it in the additive sense, then $H$ must be closed under subtraction).


**Proof.** 

$\implies$Suppose $(H, \cdot)$ is a group, then $e \in H$ so $H$ is nonempty. Also, $\forall a, b \in H, b^{-1} \in H$ by the inverse property, and so $ab^{-1} \in H$ since $H$ is closed under the group operation.

$\impliedby$Suppose $H$ is a nonempty subset of $G$.
1. (Identity) Take $a, b \in H$ s.t. $a=b$, then by $(2)$ $ab^{-1} \in H$, but $ab^{-1}=aa^{-1}=e$, so $e \in H$.
2. (Inverse) Take $a \in H$, then by $(2)$ $ea^{-1} \in H$ but $ea^{-1}=a^{-1}$ so $a^{-1} \in H$.
3. (Closure) Take $a, b \in H$, since $b=(b^{-1})^{-1}$ then by $(2)$ with $c=b^{-1}$ we have $ac^{-1} \in H$, but $ac^{-1}=a(b^{-1})^{-1}=ab$, so $ab \in H$, so $H$ is closed under $\cdot$.
4. (Associative) Follows from the properties of $\cdot: G^{2} \to G$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example]
>
>$\text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ is defined as $\{A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} | \det A = 1 \}$, i.e. the set of all $n \times n$ matrices with determinant $1$. We will show that $\text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R}) \leqslant \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ using theorem 32 (one-step subgroup test). First, note that it is a subset of $\text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ since $\det A =1 \implies \det A \neq 0$. Next, we note that $\det I_{n}=1$ so $I_{n} \in \text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ so it is nonempty. Now take $A, B \in \text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$, then from linear algebra we know that $B$ is invertible since it has non-zero determinant and also we know that $\det B^{-1}= (\det B)^{-1}=1^{-1}=1$. Now consider $AB^{-1}$, from linear algebra we know $\det AB^{-1} = \det A \det B^{-1} = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$, so $AB^{-1} \in \text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$, so by the one-step subgroup test $\text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R}) \leqslant \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$.

>[!proposition] intersection of subgroups is a subgroup
>
> If $G$ is a group and $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1}, \dots, H_{n}\}$ is a set of subgroups of $G$, then $\cap_{H_{i} \in \mathcal{H}}H_{i}$ is a subgroup of $G$.


**Proof.** Denote the intersection $A$. Since $\forall H_{i} \in \mathcal{H} (e \in H_{i})$, then $e \in A$ and in particular $A$ is nonempty. Let $a, b \in A$, then $\forall H_{i} \in \mathcal{H} (a \in H_{i} \land b \in H_{i})$, and since each $H_{i}$ is a group it follows that $b^{-1}$ is in all of $H_{i}$ so $b^{-1} \in A$. Similarly, $ab$ is in all of $H_{i}$ so $ab \in A$, so $A$ is a nonempty subset of $G$, is closed under the group operation and has the inverse property, so by theorem 31 (two-step subgroup test) $A \leqslant G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We proceed with a couple of definitions which are examples of subgroups:

>[!definition] center of a group
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then $Z(G)=\{x \in G | \forall g \in G (xg=gx)\}$ is called the *center* of $G$.


>[!definition] centralizer of an element
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then $C(a)=\{x \in G | xa=ax \}$ is called the *centralizer* of $a$.


>[!proposition]
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then:
>1. $Z(G) \leqslant G$ and is abelian
>2. $C(a) \leqslant G$
>3. $Z(G) = \cap_{a \in G}C(a)$


**Proof.** 

$1.$ Let $Z(G)$ be the center of $G$ and let $g \in G$. 

a. (non-empty) Consider the identity $e$ of $G$. Since $eg=ge$, then by definition $e \in Z(G)$, so $Z(G)$ is nonempty. 

b. (inverse) Let $b \in Z(G)$, then $\forall g \in G (bg=gb)$, and in particular $b(g^{-1})=(g^{-1})b$ since $g^{-1} \in G$. Taking the inverse of each side, we have $(b(g^{-1}))^{-1}=(g^{-1})b$, which by properties of the inverse is $(g^{-1})^{-1}b^{-1}=b^{-1}(g^{-1})^{-1}$, which is just $gb^{-1}=b^{-1}g$, but since this is true $\forall g \in G$ then by definition $b^{-1} \in Z(G)$.

c. (closure under group operation) Let $a, b \in Z(G)$ and let $x \in G$, then $ax=xa$ and $bx=xb$, so $(ax)b=(xa)b$, and $(ax)b=a(xb)=a(bx)=(ab)x$, so $(ab)x=(xa)b=x(ab)$, so $ab \in Z(G)$, so $Z(G)$ is closed under the group opreation.

So by theorem 31 (two-step subgroup test), $Z(G) \leqslant G$.

Now we show $Z(G)$ is abelian. Let $a, b \in Z(G)$, then since $a, b \in G$ it follows that for $g=b$, $ab=ba$ by definition of the center of a group, so by definition $Z(G)$ is abelian.

$2.$ Let $a \in G$ and let $C(a)$ be the centralizer of $a$ in $G$.The proof is analogous to $(1)$, only now we fix the group element under consideration.

$3$. We prove by mutual inclusion: Let $x \in Z(G)$, then $\forall a \in G, xa=ax$, so by definition of $C(a)$ we have $\forall a \in G (x \in C(a))$, so $Z(G) \subseteq \cap_{a \in G}C(a)$. Now let $x \in \cap_{a \in G}C(a)$, so again by definition we have that $xa=ax$ for each $C(a)$, so $\forall a \in G (xa=ax)$, so by definition $x \in Z(G)$, so $\cap_{a \in G}C(a) \subseteq Z(G)$. Thus we conclude by mutual inclusion that $Z(G)=\cap_{a \in G}C(a)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Finite Subgroup

A finite subgroup is a subgroup that is also finite. We can refine our one-step subgroup test for the case of a finite group as follows:

>[!theorem] one-step subgroup test for finite group
>
>Let $G$ be a finite group and $H \subseteq G$, then $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ if and only if:
>1. $H$ is nonempty
>2. $\forall a, b \in H, ab \in H$ (i.e. $H$ is closed under the group operation)


**Proof.** $\implies$ is immediate. $\impliedby$ Suppose $H$ is a nonempty subset of $G$ closed under the group operation of $G$. Since $G$ is finite then $\forall a \in G$ the order of $a$ is finite, so $\exists n \in \mathbb{N}^{+}$ s.t. $a^{n}=e$, so since $H$ is closed under multiplication we have $a^{n}=e \in H$. Similarly, since $0 \lt n$ then $a^{n-1} \in H$, and since $a^{n-1}=a^{n}a^{-1}$ by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) and $a^{n}=e$ it follows that $a^{n-1}=a^{-1} \in H$, so $\forall a \in H \exists a^{-1} \in H$ s.t. $aa^{-1}=e=a^{-1}a$. Now let $a, b \in H$, then $b^{-1} \in H$, so $ab^{-1} \in H$ since $H$ is closed under multiplication, so by theorem 32 (one-step subgroup test), $H \leqslant G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Cyclic Groups

Let $G$ be a multiplicative group and let $a \in G$, and consider the set $\langle a \rangle = \{\dots, a^{-2}, a^{-1}, a^{0}, a^{1}, a^{2}, \dots\}$, or more formally the set $\langle a \rangle = \{a^{z} | z \in \mathbb{Z} \}$. Since $G$ is a group and $a \in G$, then we can use the one-step subgroup test to verify that it is indeed a group.

1. (nonempty) trivially it is not empty since $a \in \langle a \rangle$
2. Take $a^{x}, a^{y} \in \langle a \rangle$, so $a^{-y} \in \langle a \rangle$ since $-y \in \mathbb{Z}$, and also $a^{x-y} \in \langle a \rangle$ since $x-y \in \mathbb{Z}$, and by exponent laws $a^{x-y}=a^{x}a^{-y}$, so by the one-step subgroup test, $\langle a \rangle$ is a subgroup of $G$.

We call $\langle a \rangle$ the subgroup *generated by* $a$:

>[!definition] generated subgroup
>
>Given an element $a$ in a group $G$, the subgroup *generated by* $a$, denoted $\langle a \rangle$, is given by $\langle a \rangle = \{ a^{z} | z \in \mathbb{Z} \}$.


We proved earlier that $\langle a \rangle \leqslant G$. Note that if $\langle a \rangle =G$ then it would still be a group, so we sometimes think of $\langle a \rangle$ simply as the group generated by $a$. Formally we cannot use a subgroup test to show that such $\langle a \rangle$ is indeed a group without showing it is a subset of the elements of another group with the same operation, but by definition we impose that the elements of the generated group of $a$ comply with exponent laws, so trivially $\langle a \rangle$ satisfies all group axioms. We call a group generated by a single element a *cyclic group*:

>[!definition] cyclic group
>
>If $G$ is a group and it is generated by a single element $a \in G$, i.e. $\langle a \rangle = G$, then $G$ is a *cyclic group*. We call this $a$ a *generator* of $G$.

>[!proposition] properties of a cyclic group
>
>1. $\langle a \rangle$ is abelian
>2. $|\langle a \rangle| = |a|$ and if $|a|$ is finite and $|a|=n$, then $\langle a \rangle = \{e, a, a^{2}, \dots, a^{n-1}\}$.


**Proof.** $1$. Let $a^{i}, a^{j} \in \langle a \rangle$, then $a^{i}a^{j}=a^{i+j}=a^{j+i}=a^{j}a^{j}$, so the group operation is commutative on the elements of the group, so it is abelian.

$2$. If $|a|=n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (i.e. $|a|$ is finite), then by writing all exponents $z$ in terms of remainder form w.r.t. $n$, we have $z=qn+r$ for $q, r \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $r \in [0, n)$, so $a^{z}=a^{qn+r}=(a^{n})^{q}a^{r}=a^{r}$, and since there are only $n$ options for $r$ then by counting there are exactly $n$ distinct values for $a$, and they are the same as $\{e, a, a^{2}, \dots, a^{n-1}\}$. 

Otherwise if $|a|=\infty$, then suppose by contradiction $\langle a \rangle$ is finite and consider the product of all elements of $\langle a \rangle$ with non-negative exponents, denote it $\pi$. Since $\langle a \rangle$ is a group then $\pi \in \langle a \rangle$, and since we suppose $\langle a \rangle$ is finite then $\pi=a^{k}$ for some finite positive integer $k$, so it must be that $a^{k}=a^{j}$ for some $a^{j} \in \langle a \rangle$ (since $\pi=a^{k} \in \langle a \rangle$), but it also must be that $k \neq j$ and in fact that $j \lt k$ by construction, but by theorem 24  since $|a|=\infty$ it must be that $j=k$, a contradiction, so $|a|=\infty$ implies that $\langle a \rangle$ is also infinite.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that not all finite groups are cyclic groups, and not all cyclic groups are finite. We consider a few examples below:

>[!example] infinite cyclic group
>
>Consider $(\mathbb{Z}, +)$. Clearly $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z}, z = z \cdot 1$, so clearly $\mathbb{Z}=\langle 1 \rangle$ (note here that we use additive notation instead of multiplicative notation), so it is a cyclic group, however $\mathbb{Z}$ is also infinite.

>[!example] generators of a group
>
>Consider the group of modular multiplication with modulus $9$, i.e. $\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{9}$. From number theory, we know that the elements of this group are the coprime positive integers w.r.t $9$ lesser than $9$, i.e. $\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{9}=\{1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8\}$. Consider the generated subgroups of the elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$:
>1. $\langle 1 \rangle = \{1\}$, so it is not a generator of the group
>2. $\langle 2 \rangle = \{2, 4, 8, 7, 5,  1 \}=\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$ so $2$ is a generator of the group 
>3. $\langle 4 \rangle=\{4, 7, 1 \}$ so it is not a generator 
>4. $\langle 5 \rangle=\{5, 7, 8, 4, 2, 1 \}=\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$ so it is a generator of the group
>5. $\langle 7 \rangle = \{7, 4, 1 \}$ so it is not a generator
>6. $\langle 8 \rangle = \{8, 1\}$ so it is not a generator
>
>We conclude that the group is generated by $2, 5$ (mod 9), i.e. $\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}=\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 5 \rangle$, so it is a cyclic group. From this example we see that the same group can have multiple distinct generators.


>[!example] finite non-cyclic group
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}=\{1, 3, 5, 7\}$.
>1. $\langle 1 \rangle = \{1\}$
>2. $\langle 3 \rangle = \{3, 1\}$
>3. $\langle 5 \rangle = \{5, 1\}$
>4. $\langle 7 \rangle = \{7, 1 \}$
>
>The group has no generator, so it is not cyclic.


While not every group is cyclic, it is true that every group is the union of its cyclic subgroups. In example 44 (finite non-cyclic group), while none of the elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}$ is a generator of the group, it is easy to demonstrate that $\cup_{a \in \mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}} \langle a \rangle = \mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}$. We demonstrate this is true in general:

>[!theorem]
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then $G$ is the union of its cyclic subgroups, i.e.
>$$
>G = \cup_{a \in G}\langle a \rangle
>$$

**Proof.** Trivially true since $\forall a \in G \exists \langle a \rangle \leqslant G$ s.t. $a \in \langle a \rangle$, so $a$ is in the union of the cyclic subgroups of $G$, so the union of all these subgroups consists of all elements of $G$, so $G \subseteq \cup_{a \in G} \langle a \rangle$. Conversly, if $b \in \cup_{a \in G}\langle a \rangle$, then $b=a^{k}$ for some $a \in G, k \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $b \in G$ since $G$ is closed under the group operation, so $\cup_{a \in G}\langle a \rangle \subseteq G$, so we conclude the two are the same by mutual inclusion. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We have found that in $\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$, $\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 5 \rangle$. We turn our attention to sufficient and necessary conditions for the equality of two cyclic groups.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $G$ is a group and $a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=n$, and $k \in \mathbb{N}$, then:
>1. $\langle a^{k} \rangle = \langle a^{\text{gcd}(n, k)} \rangle$
>2. $|a^{k}| = \frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, k)}$


**Proof.**  For brevity, denote $d=\text{gcd}(n, k)$.

$1$. Trivially both are groups with the same operation since both are generated by an element of $G$, so it remains to show that they have the same elements. We prove this by mutual inclusion.

$\langle a^{k} \rangle \subseteq \langle a^{d}\rangle$: Let $(a^{k})^{m} \in \langle a^{k} \rangle$. Since $d|k$ we have $k=td$ for some integer $t$. By exponent laws, $(a^{k})^{m}=a^{km}=a^{(td)m}=a(^{d})^{tm} \in \langle a^{d} \rangle$.

$\langle a^{d} \rangle \subseteq \langle a^{k} \rangle$: Since $d=\text{gcd}(n, k)$ then by Bezout's identity $\exists x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $xn+yk=d$, so $a^{d}=a^{xn+yk}=(a^{n})^{x}(a^{k})^{y}$. Since $|a|=n$, then $a^{d}=(a^{k})^{y}$. Now let $(a^{d})^{m} \in \langle a^{d} \rangle$, then $(a^{d})^{m}=((a^{k})^{y})^{m}=(a^{k})^{ym} \in \langle a^{d} \rangle$.

Thus by mutual inclusion $\langle a^{k} \rangle = \langle a^{d} \rangle$.

$2$. From $(1)$ and proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group) it follows that $|a^{k}|=|a^{d}|$. First we show that $(a^{d})^{\frac{n}{d}}=e$, which is trivially true by power rules (note that $\frac{n}{d}$ is an integer since $d$ is a divisor of $n$, so this operation is well defined using definition 13 (power notation) ) since $(a^{d})^{\frac{n}{d}}=a^{\frac{dn}{d}}=a^{n}$, and since $|a|=n$ then $a^{n}=e$, so it must be that $|a^{d}| \leq \frac{n}{d}$.

Suppose by contradiction $\exists m \in \mathbb{N}, 0 \lt m \lt \frac{n}{d}$ (if no such $m$ exists, i.e. if $\frac{n}{d}=1$, the $n=d$ and the proposition is trivially true) s.t. $(a^{d})^{m}=e$, then $a^{dm}=e$ and also $dm \lt n$, but then $|a| \leq dm \lt n$, but we assumed $|a|=n$, a contradiction, so $|a^{d}|=\frac{n}{d}$, so $|a^{k}|=\frac{n}{d}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Consider example 43 (generators of a group) again. We have seen that $\langle 4 \rangle = \langle 7 \rangle$ and also that $\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 5 \rangle$. Note that $4^{2}\equiv_{\mod 9} 7$ and that $2^{5} \equiv_{\mod_{9}} 5$, so we have $\langle 4 \rangle = \langle 4^{2} \rangle$, and also $\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 2^{5} \rangle$, and as for the orders of $2, 4$ we have $|2|=6$ and $|4|=3$. These are not coincidences:
1. For $\langle 4 \rangle = \langle 4^{2} \rangle$, observe that $|4|=3$ and $\text{gcd}(3, 2)=1$, so $\langle 4^{2} \rangle = \langle 4^{\text{gcd}(3, 2)} \rangle=\langle 4^{1} \rangle = \langle 4 \rangle$ by the theorem.
2. For $\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 2^{5} \rangle$, observe that $|2|=6$ and $\text{gcd}(6, 5)=1$, so again $\langle 2^{5} \rangle=\langle 2^{\text{gcd}(6, 5)} \rangle = \langle 2 \rangle$.

We continue our survey with a few corollaries of theorem 46  :

>[!corollary]
>
>If $|a|=n$, then $\langle a^{i} \rangle = \langle a^{j} \rangle \iff \text{gcd}(n, i) = \text{gcd}(n, j)$.


**Proof.**  $\implies$ Suppose $\langle a^{i} \rangle = \langle a^{j} \rangle$. By theorem 46  $|a^{i}|=\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, i)}$ and $|a^{j}|=\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, j)}$, and by proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group) $|a^{i}| = |\langle a^{i} \rangle| = |\langle a^{j} \rangle | = |a^{j}|$, so $\text{gcd}(n, i)=\text{gcd}(n, j)$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $\text{gcd}(n, i) = \text{gcd}(n, j)$, then by theorem 46  we know that $\langle a^{i} \rangle = \langle a^{\text{gcd}(n, i)} \rangle$ and $\langle a^{j} \rangle = \langle a^{\text{gcd}(n, j)} \rangle$, and since we suppose the two $\text{gcd}$s are equal then by transitivity $\langle a^{i} \rangle = \langle a^{j} \rangle$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $|a|=n$ then $\langle a \rangle = \langle a^{k}\rangle \iff \text{gcd}(n, k)=1$.


**Proof.**  Identify that this is simply an instantiation of corollary 47  with $i=1$ and $j=k$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We have seen corollary 48  in our example when we say that $\langle 2 \rangle = \langle 2^{5} \rangle$ (since $|2|=6$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$).

>[!corollary]
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ (i.e. the group of modular addition with modulus $n$), then $k \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}$ is a *generator* of the group if and only if $\text{gcd}(n, k)=1$.

**Proof.** Observe that $1$ is trivially a generator of the group and $|1|=n$, so the proposition can be restated as $\langle 1 \rangle = \langle k \rangle \iff \text{gcd}(n, k)=1$, which is exactly corollary 48  expressed in additive notation ($k=1 \cdot k$). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Consider the case where $|a|$ is prime, then by definition $\forall k\in \mathbb{N}, k \lt |a|$, $\text{gcd}(k, |a|)=1$, so by corollary 48  every element of $\langle a \rangle$ is a generator of the group:

>[!corollary]
>
>If $|a|=p$ where $p$ is prime, then $\forall n \in \mathbb{N}^{+}, \langle a \rangle = \langle a^{n} \rangle$.


>[!example]
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{+}$ which is the group of integers under modular addition modulus $7$, and has $7$ elements: $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$ so it has order $7$, which is prime, then by the theorem every non-identity element of $\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{+}$ is a generator of the group (since $\langle 1 \rangle = \mathbb{Z}_{7}^{+}$ and so each element is given by $n \cdot 1$). We see this in action below:
>1. $\langle 1 \rangle = \{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$
>2. $\langle 2 \rangle = \{0, 2, 4, 6, 1, 3, 5\}$
>3. $\langle 3 \rangle = \{0, 3, 6, 2, 5, 1, 4\}$
>4. $\langle 4 \rangle = \{0, 4, 1, 5, 2, 6, 3\}$
>5. $\langle 5 \rangle = \{0, 5, 3, 1, 6, 4, 2\}$
>6. $\langle 6 \rangle = \{0, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 \}$

In the next theorem, we complete our classification of cyclic groups:

>[!theorem] fundamental theorem of cyclic groups
>
>Let $G=\langle a \rangle$ and $H \subseteq G$, then:
>1. $H \leqslant G \iff$ $H$ is cyclic
>2. $H \leqslant G \implies |H| \space \Big\vert \space |G|$ (if $G$ is finite)
>3. $k \space \Big \vert \space |G| \implies \exists! H \leqslant G (|H| = k)$, and this $H$ is $\langle a^{\frac{|G|}{k}} \rangle$.


**Proof.** We denote $n=|G|=|\langle a \rangle| = |a|$.

$1$. $\implies$ Suppose $H \leqslant G$. If $H=\{e\}$ then $H$ is the trivial subgroup of $G$ and it is cyclic since $\langle e \rangle = \{e\}$, so the proposition holds. If $H \neq \{e \}$, then since $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ it is nonempty and $g \in G$ s.t. $g \neq e$ and $g \in H$, and since $H$ is a group then $g^{-1} \in H$. Since $G=\langle a \rangle$, then $\exists k \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $g=a^{k}$, so $a^{k} \in H$ and $a^{-k} \in H$, and also $k \neq 0$ (otherwise $g=a^{0}=e$), so $a^{|k|} \in H$, so the subset $A=\{n \in \mathbb{N} | a^{n} \in H \land n \neq 0\}$ is a non-empty set of natural numbers, so by the well-ordering principle of $\mathbb{N}$ it has a least element, denote it $m$, then $a^{m} \in H$. Since $H$ is closed under multiplication then trivially $\langle a^{m} \rangle \subseteq H$.

Let $h' \in H$, then $\exists t \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $a^{t}=h'$. By the division algorithm $\exists! q, r \in \mathbb{Z}, r \in [0, m)$ s.t. $t=qm+r$, so $a^{t}=a^{qm+r}=a^{qm}a^{r}$. Since $a^{m} \in H$ and $H$ is closed under the group operation and under the inverse then also $a^{-qm} \in H$, and by supposition $a^{t}=a^{qm}a^{r} \in H$, so $a^{-qm}a^{qm}a^{r}=a^{r} \in H$. If $r \neq 0$, then we have that $r \in A$ and also $r \lt m$, but we denoted the least element of $A$ as $m$, so it must be that $m \leq r$, a contradiction, so $r=0$, so $t=qm$, so $a^{t}=a^{qm}=(a^{m})^{q}$, so $a^{t} \in \langle a^{m} \rangle$, so $H \subseteq \langle a^{m} \rangle$.

By mutual inclusion we conclude $\langle a ^{m} \rangle = H$, so $H$ is a cyclic group with generator $a^{m}$.

$\impliedby$By definition 39 (generated subgroup) we know that if $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ and $H$ is generated by an element then $H$ is a (cyclic) subgroup of $G$.

$2.$ Suppose $H \leqslant G$, then by $(1)$ $H$ is cyclic, so $H=\langle a^{m} \rangle$ for some $m$. By theorem 46 , we have $|a^{m}|=\frac{|G|}{\text{gcd}(|G|, m)} \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $|G|= k |a^{m}|$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $|a^{m}| \space \big \vert \space |G|$. By proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group), $|a^{m}|=|\langle a^{m} \rangle | = |H|$, so $|H| \space \big \vert \space |G|$.

$3.$ **Existence**: Denote $m = \frac{|G|}{k}$. Since $a \in G$ then $\langle a^{m} \rangle \leqslant G$ since it is a cyclic group generated by $a^{m}$. Also, $(a^{m})^{k}=a^{|G|}=e$ since $|G|=|\langle a \rangle|=|a|$, so $|a^{m}| \leq k$. For any $t \in \mathbb{N}^{+}$ s.t. $t \lt k$, it follows that $0 \lt mt \lt |G|$, and if $(a^{m})^{t}=e$ then $a^{mt}=e$ so $|a| \leq mt \lt |G| = |a|$, a contradiction, so it follows that $\langle a ^{m} \rangle$ is a subgroup of $G$ with order $k$.

**Uniqueness**: Suppose $H' \leqslant G$ s.t. $|H'|=k$. Since $G$ is cyclic with generator $a$, then by $(1)$ it follows that $H'$ is also cyclic with some generator $a^{t} \in G$, and by supposition $|H|=|H'|=k$, i.e. $|\langle a^{t} \rangle| = |\langle a^{m} \rangle|$, By theorem 46 , we know that $|\langle a^{t} \rangle| = \frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, t)}$ and similarly for $\langle a^{m}\rangle$, so $k=\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, t)}=\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n, m)}$, so $\text{gcd}(n, t)=\text{gcd}(n, m)$.  Also by theorem 46 , $\langle a^{m} \rangle = \langle a^{gcd(n, m)} \rangle$, $\langle a^{t} \rangle = \langle a^{\text{gcd}(n, t)}\rangle$, and as we have shown $\text{gcd}(n, t)=\text{gcd}(n, m)$, so $\langle a^{m} \rangle = \langle a^{t} \rangle$, so $H'=H$, so $H$ is the unique subgroup of order $k$ in $G$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Recall Euler's totient function $\phi: \mathbb{N}^{+} \to \mathbb{N}^{+}$, where $\phi(x)$ is the amount of numbers which are coprime w.r.t. $x$ and also lesser than $x$. For example, $\phi(4)=2$ since out of all positive integers preceding $4$ only $1, 3$ are coprime w.r.t. $4$, so $\phi(4)=|\{1, 3\}|=2$. Similarly, if $x$ is prime then $\phi(x)=x-1$. We discuss other explicit formulas and relations regarding Euler's totient function in number theory. Here, we observe the following corollary to the FTCG:

>[!corollary]
>
>If $G$ is cyclic and $d$ is a positive divisor of $|G|$, then $\phi(d)$ gives the number of distinct elements of order $d$ in $G$, where $\phi$ is Euler's totient function.


**Proof.** Since $G$ is cyclic then it has a generator $a \in G$ s.t. $G=\langle a \rangle$. Denote $|G|=n$, and suppose $d \in \mathbb{N}^{+}, d | n$. By proposition $(3)$ of theorem 52 (fundamental theorem of cyclic groups) it follows that for some $b \in G$, $H = \langle b \rangle$ is the unique subgroup of $G$ with order $d$ (in fact the theorem also tells us which elements generate this subgroup, but that is of no interest to us here). Suppose for some $c \in G$, $|c|=d$, then $|\langle c \rangle|=d$ so by uniqueness, $\langle c \rangle = \langle b \rangle$, so all elements of order $d$ are generators of $b$.

Since $\langle c \rangle = \langle b \rangle$, then in particular $c \in \langle b \rangle$, so $c=b^{k}$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $\langle b^{k} \rangle = \langle b \rangle$, which by corollary 48  is true iff $\text{gcd}(|b|=d, k)=1$, i.e. if $(k, d)$ are coprime, and by definition there are exactly $\phi(d)$ positive integers less than $d$ which are coprime w.r.t. $d$, so $\phi(d)$ is the number of distinct elements of order $d$ in $G$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example]
>
>Let $k \in \mathbb{N}$ and $n=21k$, and consider $\mathbb{Z}_{21k}^{+}$ i.e. the cyclic group on modular addition modulus $21k$, then since $|\mathbb{Z}_{21k}^{+}|=21k$ and $21 | 21k=n$, by corollary 53  we know that there are exactly $\phi(21)=|\{1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 18, 20\}|=12$ elements of order $21$ in the group, regardless of $k$. In particular, $\mathbb{Z}_{903}^{+}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_{21}^{+}$ both have exactly $12$ distinct elements of order $21$.

We now present a more general corollary which is true for any group, not just cyclic groups:

>[!corollary]
>
>If $G$ is a finite group, then $\forall d \in \mathbb{N}^{+}$ the number of distinct elements of order $d$ is divisible by $\phi(d)$. 


**Proof.** Recall that $\forall a \in G, \langle a \rangle \leqslant G$ and also $|\langle a \rangle| = |a|$ by proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group). If $\forall a \in G (|a| \neq d)$, then $G$ has $0$ distinct elements of order $d$ and indeed $\phi(d) | 0$. Otherwise, $\exists a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=d$, so $\langle a \rangle = d$, and since $d|d$ then by corollary 53  there are exactly $\phi(d)$ distinct elements of order $d$ in $\langle a \rangle$. Now, if $\exists b \in G$ s.t. $b \notin \langle a \rangle$ and $|b|=d$, then we apply the theorem again and count another $\phi(d)$ distinct elements of order $d$ in $\langle b \rangle$, so we now have $2\phi(d)$ distinct elements of order $d$ in $G$. Since $G$ is finite, eventually we will exhaust all elements of $G$, and each element either belongs to one of the groups we have already considered, or it doesn't have order $d$, or it belongs to a disjoint cyclic subgroup we haven't yet considered, which adds another $\phi(d)$ elements of order $d$, so it must be that the number of distinct elements of $d$ is divisible by $\phi(d)$ since we increment our counter in increments of $\phi(d)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Recall example 43 (generators of a group), in which we considered the cyclic subgroups of $\mathbb{Z}_{9}^{*}$. From corollary 55  we expect to identify $k\phi(3)=k|\{1, 2\}|=2k$ elements of order $2$ in the group for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, and indeed the only elements of order $2$ in the group were $(4, 7)$, and $2=2 \cdot 1$ with $k=1$.

Using all the properties of cyclic groups we have found so far, we can check if a group is not cyclic:

Recall our example of $\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}$ (example 44 (finite non-cyclic group)), which we have demonstrated to be non-cyclic by considering all cyclic subgroups of the group and demonstrating that none of the subgroups is the group itself. In the case of $\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}$ this isn't too difficult since we only need to check the cyclic subgroups formed by $4$ distinct elements and we know that the order of these groups will be at most $4$, so the task is manageable. But, given a sufficiently large group, enumerating all cyclic subgroups of the group becomes unmanageable. Instead, we an use results accumulated so far in their contrapositive form to show that a group is non-cyclic.

By corollary 53 , if $G$ is cyclic and $n=|G|$ and $d|n$ for some positive integer $d$, then $G$ has exactly $\phi(d)$ distinct elements with order $d$.  Consider the contrapositive: if $G$ is group and $n=|G|$ and $d|n$ for some positive integer $d$, and $G$ doesn't have exactly $\phi(d)$ distinct elements of order $d$, then $G$ is non-cyclic. Let $d=2$ and consider our example of $\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{*}$ which has order $\phi(8)=4$. Clearly $2|4$ so we expect to find exactly $2$ distinct elements with order $2$, however by checking the cyclic subgroups formed by $3, 5, 7$ we find that each element has order $2$ so in total we have at least $3$ elements of order $2$ (in fact we have exactly $3$ such elements, since $|1|$ (where $1$ is the identity element) is always $1$), so $G$ is non-cyclic.

For a more impressive example, consider $\mathbb{Z}_{24}^{*}=\{1, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23\}$ which demonstrably has order $8$. Since $2|8$ we expect that $\mathbb{Z}_{24}^{*}$ will have exactly $2$ elements with order $2$, however $\langle 5 \rangle = \{1, 5\}$ (since $5^{2}=25=24+1$), $\langle 7 \rangle = \{1, 7\}$ (since $7^{2}=49=2\cdot 24 + 1$), $\langle 11\rangle = \{1, 11\}$  (since $11^{2}=121=5 \cdot 24 + 1$), so $\mathbb{Z}_{24}^{*}$ has at least $3$ cyclic subgroups with order $2$, so $\mathbb{Z}_{24}^{*}$ is not cyclic. Note how we only needed to check the first $3$ non-trivial subgroups of the group.

Another important result which will become useful to us much later in our discussion of the $n^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity is the following, which follows mostly from corollary 53 :

>[!theorem] sufficient condition for finite group to be cyclic
>
>If $G$ is a finite group and $|G|=n$, and $\forall d \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $d \mid n$ we have that $|\{a \in G | a^{d}=e \}| \leq d$, then $G$ is cyclic.


**Proof.** Let $G$ be finite with $|G|=n$. Let $d \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $d \mid n$. From theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem) which we see in the future, it follows that $\forall a \in G, |a| \mid |G|$, hence at least for some $d \mid n$ we have that $\exists a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=G$.

Let $G_{d}=\{a \in G \space | \space |a|=d\}$ i.e. the subset of $G$ of all elements of order $d$. There are two cases: either $G_{d}$ is empty, or it is not. In the case where $G_{d}$ is not empty, then let $b \in G_{d}$. Since $|b|=d$, then $\langle b \rangle=d$. Also, since $\forall c \in \langle b \rangle$, $c^{d}=e$ (by theorem 52 (fundamental theorem of cyclic groups) we have $|\langle c \rangle| \mid |\langle b \rangle|=d$), so $\langle b \rangle \subseteq \{a \in G| a^{d}=e\}$, but by supposition $|\{a \in G| a^{d}=e\}| \leq d$, yet $|\langle b \rangle|=d$, so for every $b, c \in G_{d}$ we have that $\langle b \rangle = \langle c \rangle = \{a \in G| a^{d}=e\}$, i.e. it is the set of generators of the cyclic subgroup of $G$ of order $d$. By corollary 53 , we know that there are exactly $\phi(d)$ such elements, so $|G_{d}|=\phi(d)$ or $G_{d}=\emptyset$.

Let $d_{1}, d_{2} \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $d_{1} \mid n, d_{2} \mid n$, and also $d_{1} \neq d_{2}$ then $G_{d_{1}} \cap G_{d_{2}} = \emptyset$ since otherwise we would have some element in $G$ with order $d_{1}$ and also $d_{2}$ with the two not being equal, which is nonsense, yet every element of $G$ has order at most $n$ since otherwise we would have a cyclic subgroup of $G$ of order greater than that of $G$ which is also nonsense, so it must be that all these $G_{d}$ for $d \mid n$ form a partition on $G$, so $n=|G|=\sum_{d \mid n}|G_{d}| \leq \sum_{d \mid n}\phi(d)$.

Applying the same argument to a cyclic group of $n$ elements, we find that $\sum_{d \mid n}\phi(d)=n$ by corollary 53 , and since $\phi(d)$ is non-negative, it must be that each $G_{d}$ is nonempty for every $d \mid n$ and in particular $G_{n}$ is nonempty (since $n \mid n$), but $G_{n}$ is the set of all generators of cyclic subgroups of $G$ with order $n$, yet $|G|=n$, so $G$ is cyclic.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Permutation and Cycles

>[!definition] permutation
>
>A bijection $\phi$ from a set $A$ to itself is called a *permutation* of $A$.


>[!definition] symmetric group
>
>Let $A$ be a set with finite cardinality $n$, i.e. $|A|=n$. The set of all permutations of $A$ is called the *symmetric group* of $A$, and is denoted $\text{Sym}(A)$.


Note that while their names are similar, the symmetry group of a structure and the symmetric group of a set *are not* the same. Recall that the symmetry group is an automorphism (i.e. an isomorphism from the structure to itself), for example the symmetry group of a regular rectangle $\square ABCD$, which is the dihedral group $D_{8}$, does not consist of a mapping $\phi$ s.t. $A \mapsto A, B \mapsto B, C \mapsto D, D \mapsto C$, since even though it is a bijection it does not preserve the geometric structure of the rectangle (intuitively there is no rigid motion from $\square ABCD$ to $\square ABDC$). 

>[!proposition]
>
>$\text{Sym}(A)$ is a group under function composition.


**Proof.** Recall that the composition of two bijections is also a bijection, so the composition of permutations is a permutation, so permutations are closed under composition. Next, function composition is associative, the identity map $\text{id}_{A}: a \mapsto a$ is a bijection on $A$ so it is a permutation of $A$,  all bijections are invertible and the inverse of a bijection is also a bijection so $\forall \phi \in \text{Sym}(A), \phi^{-1} \in \text{Sym}(A)$, so the structure $(\text{Sym}(A), \circ)$ has an associative operator and satisfies the identity and inverse properties, so it is a group. (Here we recalled our knowledge of bijections which we gathered in [Introduction to Set Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/intro_set_theory.pdf)). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>For a finite $A$, the order of $\text{Sym}(A)$ is $|A| !$, where $|A|$ is the cardinality of $A$ and $!$ is the factorial defined recursively as $0!=1, (n+1)!=n! \cdot (n+1)$.

**Proof.** We prove by a combinatorical argument (the reader may later refine it using induction). Let $\phi \in \text{Sym}(A)$ and consider an ordering $a_{1}, \dots, a_{|A|}$ on the elements of $A$ (which we suppose is finite). Since $\phi$ is a permutation then $\phi(a_{1})=a_{j}$ for some $j \in [1, |A|]$, and there are $|A|$ such options (if $|A|=1$ then we are done). Since $\phi$ is a bijection, then $\phi(a_{2}) \neq \phi(a_{1})$, so $\phi$ has $|A|-1$ options  to send $a_{2}$ to, so in total we have $|A| \cdot (|A| - 1)$ options so far, and for $a_{3}$ we would have $|A|-2$ etc, until we reach $a_{|A|}$ which will only have one possible element it could map to in all possible permutations we have considered, so we have exactly $\prod_{i=k}^{|A|}k$ possible permutations, which is exactly $|A|!$.  Recall that the order of a group is the number of elements in the group, so $|\text{Sym}(A)|=|A|!$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] $\text{Sym}(A)$ non-abelian if $2 \lt |A|$
>
>If $2 \lt |A|$, then $\text{Sym}(A)$ is non-abelian.


**Proof.** Let $a, b, c \in A$ all distinct, then there exists $\sigma, \tau \in \text{Sym}(A)$ s.t. $\sigma(a)=b, \sigma(b)=a, \tau(b)=c, \tau(c)=b$, and all other elements of $A$ are fixed by $\sigma, \tau$. So, $(\sigma \circ \tau)(c)=\sigma(\tau(c))=\sigma(b)=a$, yet $(\tau \circ \sigma)(c)=\tau(\sigma(c))=\tau(c)=b$, but $a \neq b$, so $\sigma \circ \tau \neq \tau \circ \sigma$, so $\text{Sym}(A$) is non-abelian. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] symmetric group of degree $n$
>
>The symmetric group of degree $n$, denoted $S_{n}$, is the symmetric group on the set $\{1, 2, \dots, n\}$, i.e. $S_{n}=\text{Sym}(\{1, 2, \dots, n\})$.

Some writes call $S_{n}$ *the symmetric group on $n$ letters*, which appeals to our combinatorical intuition of the structure of $S_{n}$. 

>[!proposition]
>
>If $X$ is a set with $n$ elements, then $\text{Sym}(X) \cong S_{n}$.

**Proof.** Since $|X|=N=|\{1, \dots, n\}|$, we can find a bijection $\phi: X \to N$. Consider $\sigma \in \text{Sym}(X)$. Since $\sigma: X \to X$ is a bijection, then $\psi = \phi \circ \sigma \circ \phi^{-1}: N \to N$ is a bijection as the composition of bijections, so $\psi \in S_{n}$. 

Consider a mapping: $\omega: \text{Sym}(X)\to S_{n}, \sigma \mapsto \phi \circ \sigma \circ \phi^{-1}$, then

$$
\omega(\sigma_{1})\circ \omega(\sigma_{2})=(\phi \circ \sigma_{1} \circ \phi^{-1}) \circ (\phi \circ \sigma_{2} \circ \phi^{-1}) = \phi (\sigma_{1} \circ \sigma_{2}) \circ \phi^{-1} = \omega(\sigma_{1}\circ \sigma_{2})
$$

So $\omega$ is a homomorphism. It remains to show it is bijective:
1. (injective) Suppose $\omega(\sigma_{1})=\omega(\sigma_{2})$, then $\phi \circ \sigma_{1} \circ \phi^{-1}=\phi \circ \sigma_{2} \circ \phi^{-1}$. Via left-composition with $\phi^{-1}$ and right-composition with $\phi$, we get $\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}$.
2. (surjective) Suppose $\psi \in S_{n}$, so $\phi^{-1} \circ \psi \circ \phi: X \to X \in \text{Sym}(X)$ (composition of bijections is a bijection). It can be verified that $\omega(\phi^{-1}\circ \psi \circ \phi)=\psi$, so $\omega$ is surjective.

We conclude $\omega$ is an isomorphism, so $\text{Sym}(X) \cong S_{n}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We will focus on $S_{n}$ for now. Later when we discuss group morphisms in depth we will see that all our results trivially extend to any symmetric group isomorphic to $S_{n}$ as we will find that all "interesting" group properties are preserved under an isomorphism. 

>[!proposition]
>
>The dihedral group of order $2n$, $D_{2n}$, is isomorphic to a subgroup of the symmetric group of degree $n$, $S_{n}$.


**Proof.**  Recall from our discussion on [Rigid motions & symmetry](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/rigid_motions.pdf) that each element $\phi \in D_{2n}$ is uniquely defined by its action on the $n$ vertices $V_{n}=\{v_{1}, \dots, v_{n}\}$ of a regular $n$-gon (in fact, each element is uniquely defined by its action on a pair of adjacent vertices, which is a stronger proposition), thus each $\phi$ can be represented as some permutation on $V_{n}$, i.e. in a sense $\phi \in \text{Sym}(V_{n})$.
