More rigorously, for each symmetry $\phi$, consider $\phi$ restricted to $V_{n}$, i.e. $\phi|_{V_{n}}$, then $\phi|_{V_{n}} \in \text{Sym}(V_{n})$, and given a mapping $\eta: D_{2n} \to \text{Sym}(V_{n}), \phi \mapsto \phi|_{V_{n}}$ we identify that $\eta$ is *injective* (since otherwise $\phi|_{V_{n}}=\phi'|_{V_{n}}$ for some $\phi, \phi', \phi \neq \phi'$, but since each symmetry is determined by its action on the vertices, then it must bet that $\phi=\phi'$, a contradiction, so $\eta$ is injective). Consider $\eta'$, which is $\eta$ with the co-restriction to $\text{Im}(\eta) \subseteq \text{Sym}(V_{n})$,  clearly this $\eta'$ is surjective as well as injective, so it is a bijection.

Observe that $\eta$ is trivially a homomorphism (in fact, it is a *monomorphism* since it is an injective homomorphism) since $\forall \phi, \psi \in D_{2n}$ we have $\eta(\phi \circ \psi)=(\phi \circ \psi)|_{V_{n}}= \phi|_{V_{n}} \circ \psi|_{V_{n}}=\eta(\phi) \circ \eta(\psi)$), so $\eta$ is a group homomorphism, so $\eta'$ is a group isomorphism, so $D_{2n} \cong \text{Im}(\eta) \leqslant \text{Sym}(V_{n})$, and as we mentioned without proof $\text{Sym}(V_{n}) \cong S_{n}$ (the proof is trivial and follows from the natural isomorphism $v_{i} \mapsto i$), and as we will later see isomorphism is an equivalence relation and subgroups are mapped to subgroups, so $D_{2n} \cong S_{D} \leqslant S_{n}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>$D_{6} \cong S_{3}$, and it is the only dihedral group with $2 \lt n$ s.t. $D_{2n} \cong S_{n}$.

**Proof.** By proposition 64  we know that $D_{6}$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of $S_{3}$. Trivially group order is preserved under an isomorphism (since an isomorphism is injective & surjective, so both the domain and codomain must have the same cardinality), but we know $|D_{6}|=6$ and also $|S_{3}|=3!=6$, and the only subgroup of $S_{3}$ of order $6$ is $S_{3}$, so $D_{6} \cong S_{3}$. For any other $n$ s.t. $3 \lt n$, we have a dihedral group with order $2n$ and a symmetric group of order $n!$, and since $3 \lt n$ then $n! = (n-1)! n \gt 2n$, so $|D_{2n}| \lt |S_{n}|$, so it cannot be that the two groups are isomorphic for $n$ larger than $3$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Cycle Notation

Consider a set $N=\{1, \dots, n\}$ and a permutation $\phi \in S_{n}$. One way to write $\phi$ is via explicitly specifying the image of every element of the set under $\phi$ as follows: $\phi: 1 \mapsto 2, 2 \mapsto 3, 3 \mapsto 4, \dots, n-1 \mapsto n, n \mapsto 1$. Identify that this $\phi$ corresponds to a single rotation $r \in D_{2n}$.

The notation we presented has two major downsides:
1. It is not immediately obvious what the action of $\phi$ on $N$ is, even though our example is very elementary. Consider, for example, a permutation s.t. it takes every $3$ consecutive numbers and exchanges the first and last (i.e. rotates them around the middle number), i.e. if $N=\{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$, then $\phi': 1 \mapsto 3, 2 \mapsto 2, 3 \mapsto 1, 4 \mapsto 6, 5 \mapsto 5, 6 \mapsto 4$. It is even harder to see the pattern here.
2. It has a lot of visual noise coming from all the $\mapsto$ symbols and all the commas.

As an alternative, consider the following notation, called *array notation* or *two-line notation*, applied to the same rotation permutation $\phi$:

$$
\phi=\begin{bmatrix}
1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\
2 & 3 & 4 & 1
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Where $\phi_{1i}$ is the input and $\phi_{2i}$ is the output of $\phi$ for that input, for example $\phi(2)$ is $3$, which is the element right under $2$. This notation makes the action of the permutation on the group more obvious, however the cyclic nature of $\phi$ is not immediately obvious (in the sense that it acts on the elements as if they were all vertices of a cycle, and each vertex is sent to the following vertex by $\phi$). For our second example, this becomes even more apparent:

$$
\phi'=\begin{bmatrix}
1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 \\
3 & 2 & 1 & 6 & 5 & 4
\end{bmatrix}
$$

It is not immediately obvious from this presentation that $\phi'$ acts on $N$ as if its elements belong to two *disjoint* cycles, one formed by $123$ and one formed by $456$. To convince yourself the cycles are disjoint, observe that $(\phi')^{2}=\text{id}_{N}$ and that elements in the subset $1, 2, 3$ remain in the same subset under both $\phi', (\phi')^{2}$ and likewise for elements of $4, 5, 6$, so the cycles are disjoint.

This observation - that permutations can also be thought of as *cycles*, leads to the introduction of *cycle notations*. In cycle notation, we write permutations as a *composition of cycles*, for example we would write the rotation $\phi$ mentioned earlier as $\phi=(1234)$, and interpret it as follows: for every element $x$ in the domain, $\phi(x)$ is the element to the right of $x$ in the cycle, or if $x=4$ then $\phi(x)=1$, hence the name *cycle*. If an element does not appear in the cycle, then it is fixed by it, for example $(123)$ fixes $4$ since $4 \notin (123)$.

Consider another permutation in cycle notation $\psi=(1 \space 2 \space 3)(4 \space 5 \space 6)$, where $(1 \space 2 \space 3)$ and $(4 \space 5 \space 6)$ are again *cycles*. We interpret it as follows:

1. To calculate $\psi(i)$, we go from right to left and in each cycle. 
2. If we never find $i$, then $\psi(i)$ fixes $i$, i.e. $\psi(i)=i$.
3. Otherwise, we find $i$ in some cycle $\alpha$, and then $\alpha(i)$ is the element to the right of $i$ in $\alpha$, and if $i$ the rightmost element in $\alpha$ then $\alpha(i)$ is given by the leftmost element of $\alpha$. More explicitly, if $\alpha=(\alpha_{0}\dots \alpha_{n-1})$, then $\alpha(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{(i+1)\space \% \space n)}$, and we set $i=\alpha(\alpha_{i})$.

For example, pick $i=3$. Going from right to left, we have $(4\space 5 \space 6)$, which fixes $3$ since $3$ is not in the cycle, then we have $(1 \space 2 \space 3)$, so $\psi(3)=(1 \space 2 \space 3)(3)=1$. Writing $\psi$ in two-line notation, we get:

$$
\psi = \begin{bmatrix} 
1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\
2 & 3 & 1 & 5 & 6 & 4
\end{bmatrix}
$$
Which has a lot more noise and makes it harder to detect that $\psi$ is in fact composed of two cycles, $(1\space 2\space 3)$ and $(4 \space 5 \space 6)$.

While this notation takes some time to get used to, it has clear benefits:
1. It makes the cyclic nature of the permutation immediately apparent, especially if $\phi'$ is expressed as a composition of disjoint cycles (more on that later).
2. It is very concise.

Representing $\text{id}_{N}$ in cycle notation is also trivial: on the one hand, we can explicitly write $\text{id}_{N}=(1)(2)(3)(4)$ (for $N=\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$) (check that this really is the identity permutation on $4$ elements), but we prefer to use a shorthand instead and write $\text{id}_{N}=()$ or $\text{id}_{N}=(1)$.

We can now express $\phi'$ in cycle notation, as follows: $\phi'=(1\space 3)(4\space 6)$. In this representation, it is immediately obvious that $\phi'$ fixes $2, 5$ and that $1, 3$ and $4, 6$ form a cycle.

>[!definition] cycle notation
>
>Let $\phi$ be a permutation of a set $A$, then $\phi$ in *cycle notation* is given by a composition of *cycles* $\phi=\alpha \circ \beta \circ \dots \circ \omega$, where $\alpha=(\alpha_{0}\dots\alpha_{n_{\alpha}-1}), \beta=(\beta_{0} \dots \beta_{n_{\beta}-1}), \dots, \omega=(\omega_{0} \dots \omega_{n_{\omega}-1})$,  s.t. each cycle $c$  is a *permutation* of $A$ s.t. $\forall x \in A$, if $x=c_{i}$ for some $c_{i}$ in the cycle then $c(x)=c(c_{i})=c(c_{(i+1) \% n_{c}})$, and if $x$ is not in the cycle then it is fixed by $c$, i.e. $c(x)=x$.


>[!definition] disjoint cycle
>
>Two cycles $\alpha, \beta$ are *disjoint* if $\forall x ((\alpha(x) \neq x) \implies  (\beta(x)=x))$ as well as the symmetrical statement where $\alpha, \beta$ are exchanged (i.e. $\forall x ((\beta(x) \neq x) \implies (\alpha(x)=x))$, or informally - two cycles are disjoint if they do not share an element.

We immediately start with a proposition on disjoint cycles, the proof for which is best read after reading the few examples that follow it:

>[!proposition]
>
>If $\alpha, \beta$ are disjoint cycles, then $\alpha \circ \beta = \beta \circ \alpha$, i.e. disjoint cycles *commute*.


**Proof.** Consider some set $X$ and let $x \in X$. There are two cases:
1. $x$ is not fixed by $\alpha$, so $\alpha(x)=y$ s.t. $y \in X, y \neq x$. Since $\alpha, \beta$ are disjoint then $x$ must be fixed by $\beta$, so $(\alpha \circ \beta)(x)=\alpha(\beta(x)) =\alpha(x)=y$. Similarly, since we suppose $\alpha(x)=y$ so it must be that $y$ is also not fixed by $\alpha$ and belongs to the cycle $\alpha$, so again $\beta$ must fix $y$, so $(\beta \circ \alpha)(x)=\beta(\alpha(x))=\beta(y)=y$, so $(\alpha \circ \beta)(x)=(\beta \circ \alpha)(x)$.
2. $x$ is fixed by $\alpha$, so $(\beta \circ \alpha)(x)=\beta(\alpha(x))=\beta(x)$. If $x$ is also fixed by $\beta$ then trivially $(\beta \circ \alpha)(x)=\beta(x)=x=\alpha(x)=\alpha(\beta(x))=(\alpha \circ \beta)(x)$. If $x$ is not fixed by $\beta$, then $\beta(x)$ is fixed by $\alpha$ due to the same argument from the previous case, so $(\alpha \circ \beta)(x)=\alpha(\beta(x))=\beta(x)=(\beta \circ \alpha)(x)$.

So we conclude $\alpha \circ \beta = \beta \circ \alpha$. We note that trivially this argument extends inductively to a composition of $n$ disjoint cycles $\alpha_{1} \circ \dots \circ \alpha_{n}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

In the next example we demonstrate how to calculate the image of an element under a permutation expressed in cycle notation, as well as how to simplify a permutation in cycle notation:

>[!example] using cycle notation
>
>Consider $\phi=(1 \space 2)(2 \space 3)(5 \space 6 \space 1 \space 2)(2 \space 3)$. We wish to work out $\phi(1)$. Going from right to left, we see that $(2 \space 3)$ fixes $1$, then $(5 \space 6 \space 1 \space 2)$ takes $1$ to $2$, then $(2 \space 3)$ takes $2$ to $3$, then $(1 \space 2)$ again fixes $3$, so we conclude $\phi(1)=3$. Now consider $\phi(3)$, we have $(2 \space 3)$ maps $3$ to $2$, then $(5 \space 6 \space 1 \space 2)$ maps $2$ to $5$, which remains fixed by the next two cycles, so $\phi(3)=5$. For $\phi(5)$, the first cycle (from right to left) fixes $5$, the second sends $5$ to $6$, which is then fixed by the rest of the cycle, so $\phi(5)=6$. For $\phi(6)$, we have that the first cycle fixes $6$, the second sends it to $1$, the third fixes $1$, and finally the fourth sends $1$ to $2$, so we have $\phi(6)=2$. Finally for $\phi(2)$, the first cycle sends $2$ to $3$, the second fixes $3$, the third sends $3$ to $2$ and the fourth sends $2$ to $1$, so we have $\phi(2)=1$, note that we have exhausted all values effected by the cycles, and deduced that $\phi: 1 \to 3, 3 \to 5, 5 \to 6, 6 \to 2, 2 \to 1$, so again in cycle notation we have $\phi=(1 \space 3 \space 5 \space 6 \space 2)$, so $\phi$ consists of a single cycle, and we have $(1 \space 2)(2 \space 3)(5 \space 6 \space 1 \space 2)(2 \space 3)=(1 \space 3 \space 5 \space 6 \space 2)$.


Note that $(1 \space 2 \space 3)=(3 \space 1 \space 2) = (2 \space 3 \space 1)$ (verify that this is indeed the case!)

>[!exercise] simplifying permutations
>
>The reader should follow the process described in example 69 (using cycle notation) and show that:
>
>1. $(1 \space 2 \space 3)(1 \space 2 \space 3)=(1 \space 3 \space 2)$
>2. $(1 \space 2 \space 3)(1 \space 3 \space 2) = (1)=\text{id}$
>3. $(1 \space 3 \space 2)(1 \space 2 \space 3)=(1)=\text{id}$
>
>And conclude that $(1 \space 2 \space 3)$ has order $3$.

>[!proposition] order of a cycle
>
>If $\alpha=(\alpha_{0} \dots \alpha_{n})$ where $\alpha_{0}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ are all distinct, then:
>1. $\alpha^{k}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{(i+k) \% n}$ for $k \in \mathbb{N}$
>2. $|\alpha|=n$


**Proof.**  

$1.$ We prove by induction on $k$. For $k=0$ we have $\alpha^{0}=\text{id}$ and indeed $\text{id}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{(i+0) \% n}$. Suppose the proposition is true for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $\alpha^{k}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{(i+k)\% n}$. By theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)), $\alpha^{k+1}=\alpha \circ \alpha^{k}$, so $\alpha^{k+1}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha(\alpha^{k}(\alpha_{i}))$, which by the induction hypothesis is $\alpha(\alpha_{(i+k) \% n})$, which by definition is $\alpha_{(i+(k+1)) \% n}$, which completes the proof by induction.

$2.$ By $(1)$ we have $\alpha^{n}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{(i+n) \% n}=\alpha_{i}$ for every $\alpha_{i}$ and also trivially $\alpha^{n}(x)=x$ for every $x$ not in the cycle, so $|\alpha| \leq n$. If $|\alpha|=k \lt n$, then $\alpha^{k}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}$, which by $(1)$ is the same as $\alpha_{(i+k) \% n}=\alpha_{i}$, so $n|k$ thus $n \leq k$, but also $k \lt n$, a contradiction, so $|\alpha|=n$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It is not always the case that a permutation in cycle notation can be simplified to a single cycle. For example, $(1 \space 2 \space 3)(4 \space 5 \space 6)$ is already expressed as two disjoint cycles, and cannot be simplified further. It is however always the case that a permutation can be expressed as the composition of disjoint cycles.

>[!theorem] disjoint form
>
>Every permutation $\phi$ on a finite set can be written as a composition of disjoint cycles.


**Proof.** We present a constructive proof which generalizes the process described in example 69 (using cycle notation). Let $X$ be a finite set s.t. $|X|=n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and $\phi$ a permutation on $X$. Consider the following algorithm:

1. Set $X'=X$, $C=\emptyset$ (where $C$ is a set of cycles. This can also be an array, but here we emphasize that order doesn't matter according to proposition 68  since all cycles in $C$ will be disjoint).
2. While $X' \neq \emptyset$:
	1. Pick some $x \in X'$, and remove it from $X'$ (i.e. set $X'=X' \setminus \{x\}$)
	2. Set $y=\phi(x)$
	3. If $y=x$, then $\phi$ fixes $x$, then
		1. go back to $2$
	4. Otherwise, set $\alpha = (x)$ ($\alpha$ will represent a cycle, so order of succession matters)
	5. While $y \neq x$:
		1. Remove $y$ from $X'$
		2. Append $y$ to the end of $\alpha$.
		3. Set $y=\phi(y)$
	6. Add $\alpha$ to $C$
3. $C$ is the set of disjoint cycles whose composition is $\phi$

Below is a Python implementation of the algorithm:

```python
def decompose(domain: list[int], permutation: Callable[[int], int]) -> list[list[int]]:
    cycles: list[list[int]] = []
    working_set = domain.copy()

    while len(working_set) != 0:
        x = working_set.pop()
        y = permutation(x)
        if x == y:
            # x is fixed by permutation, skip.
            continue

        # Start tracking a new cycle.
        cycle = [x]
        cycles.append(cycle)

        # Cycles must eventually wrap around assuming this
        # is a permutation.
        while y != x:
            cycle.append(y)
            # If the permutation is valid, then every element
            # of the domain should appear exactly once
            # in the image, so it cannot be that `remove` throws.
            working_set.remove(y)
            y = permutation(y)

    return cycles
```

**Correctness**: Suppose $x \in X$, then $\phi(x) \in X$ as well since $\phi$ is a permutation. If $\phi(x)=x$ then $\phi$ fixes $x$, and in cycle notation we may omit cycles of length $1$, so our omission (in step $2.3.1$) is justified. Otherwise, $\phi(x) \neq x$, so we start tracking a cycle $\alpha$, setting $y=\phi(x)$ initially and then setting $y$ to be its successor in each iteration (step $2.7.3$). There are $2$ cases:

1. If $y \notin \alpha$, i.e. it is not a value already in the cycle, then we add $y$ to the cycle (step $2.7.2$) and continue to the next value (step $2.7.3$).
2. If $y \in \alpha$, then either $y=x$ or $y \neq x$, but if $y \neq x$ then it must be that $y$ was already the image of some other element in $\alpha$ (since $x$ is the only element of $\alpha$ that is not added due to being the image of another element in $\alpha$), but then either $y$ has two preimages so $\phi$ is not injective, but $\phi$ is a permutation so it is bijective and in particular injective, a contradiction, so if $y \in \alpha$ then $y = x$, which is the condition we check in $2.7$.

Since $X$ is finite and each $y$ is either $x$ or not already in $\alpha$, then $2.7$ can repeat at most $|X'|$ times, so it must eventually terminate. Similarly, $2$ must already eventually terminate since it can repeat at most $|X|$ times, so the algorithm always terminates.

It remains to show that the algorithm produces disjoint cycles, and then their composition is indeed $\phi$. Suppose the cycles are not disjoint, then we have some $\alpha, \beta \in C$ s.t. $\exists x \in \alpha, x \in \beta$, and suppose w.l.o.g. that $\alpha$ was completed before $\beta$, but then $x$ must have been removed from $X'$ either in $2.1$ or $2.7.1$, so it cannot have been in $X'$ while constructing $\beta$, so it cannot be that $x \in \beta$, so it must be that the cycles are disjoint. 

Finally, suppose by contradiction that $\phi$ is not equivalent to the composition of the cycles, which we denote $\psi$. In other words, $\exists x \in X$ s.t. $\phi(x) \neq \psi(x)$. Since $\psi$ is a composition of disjoint cycles then either $\psi(x)=x$ or $\psi(x)=\alpha(x)$ for some cycle $\alpha \in \psi$, but $\alpha(x)$ is simply $\phi(x)$ by construction, so this case leads to a contradiction, so it must be that $\psi(x)=x$ and also $\phi(x) \neq x$, but again $\psi(x)=x$ if and only if $\phi(x)=x$ (step $2.3.1$), so this is also impossible, so we conclude $\phi=\psi$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Transpositions

>[!definition] 2-cycle (transposition)
>
>A *2-cycle* is a cycle of length $2$, i.e. a cycle that consists of $2$ elements. It is also called a *transposition*. Likewise, an *$n$-cycle* is a cycle of length $n$.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $\alpha=(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{2} \space \dots \space \alpha_{n})$ is a cycle, then $\alpha$ can be written as a composition of $n-1$ $2$-cycles as $\alpha = (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n}) \circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n-1}) \circ \dots \circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{2})$


**Proof.** Denote the decomposed form of $\alpha$ into transpostions $\alpha'$. Let $X$ be a set. We prove by induction on $n$. If $n \lt 2$ then $\alpha$ is the identity so the theorem is vacuously true (since $(\alpha_{1})=\text{id}=()$ which is a composition of $1-1=0$ transpositions). If $n=2$, then $\alpha$ is already written in terms of $2-1=1$ transpositions.

Suppose by induction the theorem holds for some $n$, i.e. that $(\alpha_{1} \space \dots \space \alpha_{n-1} \space \alpha_{n})=(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n})\circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n-1}) \circ \dots \circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{2})=\alpha'$ and consider $n+1$. We wish to show by the induction hypothesis that $(\alpha_{1} \space \dots \space \alpha_{n} \space \alpha_{n+1})=(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n+1}) \circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n}) \circ \dots \circ (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n+1}) \circ \alpha'=\alpha''$ (the final transition is simply a notation).

Let $x \in X$, there are three cases:
1. $x \notin \alpha$, in which case $x$ is fixed by $\alpha$ and also $x$ not in any of the cycles that compose $\alpha''$ so it is also fixed by $\alpha'$, so $\alpha(x)=\alpha''(x)$.
2. $x \in \alpha$, in which case we identify that the difference between the $n$-cycle and the $n+1$-cycle is that in the $n+1$-cycle, $\alpha_{n} \mapsto \alpha_{n+1}, \alpha_{n+1} \mapsto \alpha_{1}$ whereas in the $n$-cycle, $\alpha_{n} \mapsto \alpha_{1}$ and $\alpha_{n+1} \mapsto \alpha_{n+1}$ (as it is not a part of the cycle), so if $x \notin \{\alpha_{n}, \alpha_{n+1}\}$ then $\alpha$ and $\alpha''$, agree. Suppose then that $x=\alpha_{n}$, so $(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n+1}) \circ \alpha')(\alpha_{n})=(\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{n+1})(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{n+1}$, so $\alpha''$ and $\alpha$ agree on $x=\alpha_{n}$. Similarly, for $x=\alpha_{n+1}$, we have $\alpha''=(\alpha_{1}  \space \alpha_{n+1})(\alpha'(\alpha_{n+1}))=\alpha_{1}$, so again $\alpha''$ and $\alpha$ agree.

We conclude that $\alpha=\alpha''$ in the $n+1$ case, which completes the proof by induction.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] permutations as a composition of transpositions
>
>Every permutation can be written as a composition of $2$-cycles.


**Proof.** Immediate from theorem 74  given a permutation expressed in cycle notation, which by theorem 72 (disjoint form) we know that every permutation can be.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
We proceed to a survey of the arithmetic properties of transpositions.

>[!theorem] transposition arithmetics
>
>$\forall a, b, c, d$ s.t. all $a, b, c, d$ are different:
>1. $(a \space b)=(b \space a)$
>2. $(a \space b)(c \space d)=(c \space d)(a \space b)$
>3. $(b \space c)(a \space b)=(a \space b)(a \space c)$
>4. $(a \space c)(a \space b)=(a \space b)(b \space c)$


**Proof.** $1$ is immediate from definition 66 (cycle notation). $2$ is a special case of proposition 68 .

$3$. Observe that $\phi=(b \space c)(a \space b)$ is not written in disjoint from, so we follow the process described in theorem 72 (disjoint form) and demonstrated in example 69 (using cycle notation) and observe the following: first, $\phi(c)=(b \space c)(a \space b)(c)=(b \space c)(c)=b$, and also $\phi(b)=(b \space c)(a \space b)(b)=(b \space c)(a)=a$, and finally $\phi(a)=c$ (we know this is the case without direct calculation since a permutation is bijective and we have already found the preimage of the rest of $a, b, c$), so $\phi=(c \space b \space a)=(a \space c \space b)$, which by theorem 74  is equivalent to $(a \space b)(a \space c)$, so $(b \space c)(a \space b)=(a \space b)(a \space c)$.

$4$. Observe that by $(1)$, $(a \space c)(a \space b)=(a \space c)(b \space a)$, which is simply $(3)$ with the substitutions $b \mapsto a, c \mapsto c$ and $a \mapsto b$, so $(a \space c)(b \space a)=(b \space a)(b \space c)$, which again by $(1)$ is $(a \space b)(b \space c)$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>The identity permutation on $2 \leq n$ elements can be written as a product (composition) of transpositions, and the product always involves an *even* number of transpositions.


**Proof.** The first part of the proposition is simply restating corollary 75 (permutations as a composition of transpositions). Suppose $\text{id}$ is written as a product of $m$ transpositions. If $m=0$, then the theorem is vacuously true. Otherwise, we have $\text{id}=(a_{0} \space a_{1})(a_{2} \space a_{3})\dots(a_{2m-1} \space a_{2m})$ for some $a_{0}, \dots, a_{2m}$. Denote $\alpha_{i}=(a_{i} \space a_{i+1})$, then $\text{id}=\alpha_{0} \alpha_{1} \dots \alpha_{m}$. 

Suppose by contradiction that it is possible for $m$ to be $1$, i.e. $\text{id}=(a_{0} \space a_{1})$, but then clearly it is not the identity since $a_{0} \neq a_{1}$ (otherwise it is not a valid cycle) yet $(a_{0} \space a_{1})(a_{1})=a_{0}$, so it cannot be that $m=1$.

Consider a transposition $\alpha_{i}$ for some $0 \lt i$ (if $m \neq 0$ then by the argument from the previous paragraph we know $1 \lt m$, so there must exist at least one such transposition), then $\text{id}=\alpha_{0} \dots \alpha_{i} \dots$. Consider the product $\alpha_{i-1} \alpha_{i}$ and $a \in \alpha_{i}=(a \space b)$. There are three cases:

1. $\alpha_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}$, in which case $\alpha_{i-1} \alpha_{i}=\text{id}$ so the two cancel out
2. $a \in \alpha_{i-1}=(a \space x)$, in which case by theorem 76 (transposition arithmetics) we have $(a \space x)(a \space b)=(a \space b)(b \space x)=\alpha_{i} \circ (b \space x)$.
3. $a \notin \alpha_{i-1}$, in which case again by theorem 76 (transposition arithmetics) we can rewrite $\alpha_{i-1}\alpha_{i}$ as $\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i-1}'$ s.t. $a \notin \alpha_{i-1}'$ as well.

Thus in all cases where $\alpha_{i-1} \neq \alpha_{i}$, we can move $\alpha_{i}$ to the left while also rewriting the permutation $\dots \alpha_{i-1} \alpha_{i} \dots$ as $\dots \alpha_{i} \alpha_{i-1}' \dots$ s.t. $a \in \alpha_{i}$ and $a \notin \alpha_{i-1}'$. By repeating this process, either $\alpha_{i}$ is eventually cancelled out, or the permutation becomes $\alpha_{i} \circ  \dots \circ \alpha_{i+1} \circ \dots$, where $\forall k \in [0, i-1], a \notin \alpha_{k}$.

Pick $i=m$, then by the previous argument either $\exists \alpha_{k} \in [0, m-1]$ s.t. $\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{m}$, or we can rewrite the identity as $\text{id}=\alpha_{m} \circ \dots$  s.t. $\alpha_{m}$ is the only transposition with $a \in \alpha_{m}$, so $\text{id}(a)=\alpha_{m}(a)=b$ where $b$ is the other element in the transposition $\alpha_{m}$, but this is impossible since $\text{id}$ is the identity so it must be that $\text{id}(a)=a$, so it must be that $\exists k \neq m$ s.t. $\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{m}$. By applying this argument to all permutations (from the rightmost to the leftmost), it is evident that $\forall \alpha_{i} \exists \alpha_{k}$ s.t. $\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{k}$ and $i \neq k$, so it must be that $m$ is even (since every transposition must be cancelled out by some other transposition, so they must appear in $\text{id}$ in pairs).

The proof can trivially be expressed more formally using an inductive argument.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>Every permutation $\phi$ is the product of either an *even* number of transpositions, or an *odd* number of transpositions, i.e. $\phi$ cannot be represented as the product of both an odd and an even number of transpositions.

**Proof.** Let $\phi \in \text{Sym}(A)$ for some $A$. Take two representations of $\phi$ in terms of $2$-cycles, not necessarily distinct (we have shown that such representation must exist). So for some $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ and $\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{m}$ we have:

$$
\phi=\underbrace{(\alpha_{1}\space \alpha_{2}) \dots (\alpha_{n-1} \space \alpha_{n})}_{\psi} = \underbrace{(\beta_{1} \space \beta_{2}) \dots (\beta_{m-1}\space \beta_{m})}_{\tau}
$$

Consider $\tau$. It is the product of $m-1$ $2$-cycles. Each $2$-cycle is also a permutation so it is an element of $\text{Sym}(A)$, so by a previous result $\tau^{-1}=((\beta_{1}\space\beta_{2})\dots (\beta_{m-1} \space \beta_{m}))^{-1}=(\beta_{m-1} \space \beta_{m})^{-1} \dots (\beta_{1} \space \beta_{2})^{-1}=(*)$. By proposition 71 (order of a cycle) the order of every $2$-cycle is $2$, so each $2$-cycle is its own inverse, so $(*)=(\beta_{m-1} \space \beta_{m}) \dots (\beta_{1} \space \beta_{2})$.

Now consider $\tau^{-1} \circ \psi$. Since $\tau=\psi=\phi$ then $\tau^{-1} \circ \psi = \psi^{-1} \circ \psi=\text{id}$. Multiplying the two explicitly according to $(*)$, we get:

$$
\tau^{-1} \circ \psi = (\beta_{m-1}\beta_{m}) \dots (\beta_{1} \beta_{2}) (\alpha_{1} \space \alpha_{2}) \dots (\alpha_{n-1} \alpha_{n})=\text{id}
$$

So we have the identity permutation expressed as a product of $2$-cycles, so by theorem 77  it must be that there is an even number of $2$-cycles in this expression, but the number of $2$-cycles in this expression is exactly the sum of the number of two cycles whose product is $\psi$ and the sum of the number of two cycles whose product is $\tau$, so we have two numbers that add up to an even number, which is only true if the parity of both numbers is the same (i.e. either both are even or both are odd), which completes the proof.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
This results justifies the following definition:

>[!definition] parity of a permutation
>
>A permutation is *even* if it can be written as the product of an even number of $2$-cycles, otherwise it is *odd*. The *sign* of a permutation $\sigma$, denoted $\text{sgn}(\sigma)$, is defined to be $1$ if $\sigma$ is even and $-1$ if $\sigma$ is odd.


Equipped with this result, we can derive the first useful result in our discussion of transpositions:

>[!theorem] order of product of disjoint cycles (Ruffini)
>
>If $\phi=\gamma_{1} \gamma_{2} \dots \gamma_{n}$, where $\gamma_{1}, \gamma_{2}, \dots, \gamma_{n}$ are all disjoint cycles with length $m_{1}, m_{2}, \dots, m_{n}$ respectively, then $|\phi|=\text{lcm}(m_{1}, m_{2}, \dots, m_{n})$. 


**Proof.** Denote $m=\text{lcm}(m_{1}, m_{2}, \dots, m_{n})$ and consider $\phi^{m}=(\gamma_{1} \gamma_{2} \dots \gamma_{n})^{m}$. Since all cycles are disjoint, then by proposition 68  they all commute, so by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) we have $(\gamma_{1} \gamma_{2} \dots \gamma_{n})^{m}=\gamma_{1}^{m}\gamma_{2}^{m} \dots \gamma_{n}^{m}$. Since $\forall i \in \mathbb{N} \cap [1, n], m_{i} | m$ (since $m$ is their least common-multiple), and since each $m_{i}$ is the length of a cycle $\gamma_{i}$ which by proposition 71 (order of a cycle) is equal to the order of $\gamma_{i}$, then each terms can be written as $\gamma_{i}^{z_{i} m_{i}}=(\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}})^{z_{i}}=\text{id}^{z_{i}}=\text{id}$ for some $z_{i} \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $\phi^{m}=\text{id}$, so $|\phi| \leq m$.

Suppose $\phi^{k}=\text{id}$ for some $k$, so by a similar argument $\prod_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}^{k}=\text{id}$, but then each $\gamma_{i}^{k}=\text{id}$ since the cycles are disjoint, so it must be that $k$ is a common-multiple of $m_{1}, \dots, m_{n}$, so $m \leq k$, so we conclude $|\phi|=m$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
We see the theorem in action in the next example:

>[!example] finding the order of a permutation
>
>Consider $\phi=(1 \space 2 \space 5 \space 3)(1 \space 5 \space 3)(1 \space 4 \space 7 \space 5)$. First, we rewrite it as the product of disjoint cycles following the algorithm outlined in theorem 72 (disjoint form): Start with $1$.
>: $\phi(1)=4, \phi(4)=7, \phi(7)=1$, which completes a cycle. Next, pick $5$. $\phi(5)=3, \phi(3)=2, \phi(2)=5$, which completes the second cycle. There are no more elements to consider, so we conclude $\phi=(1 \space 4 \space 7)(5 \space 3 \space 2)$, the order of both cycles is $3$, so their least common-multiple is $3$, so $|\phi|=3$. As another example, take $(1 \space 2 \space 3)(6 \space 9)(7 \space 33 \space 72 \space 49)$, which is already the product of disjoint cycles with lengths $3, 2, 4$, so the order of this permutation is $\text{lcm}(3, 2, 4)=12$.


We provide a sufficient conditions of $S_{n}$ the symmetric group on $n$ elements, to be $S_{n}$:

>[!theorem]
>
>If $p$ is prime and $H \leqslant S_{p}$, and also $H$ consists of a transposition and a $p$-cycle, then $H=S_{p}$. 


**Proof.** w.l.o.g suppose the transposition is $\sigma=(1 \space 2)$, and let $\tau$ be the $p$-cycle, so $\tau^{p}=(1)$. If $p=2$ then we are done since $\sigma$ generates $S_{p}$, so suppose $3 \leq p$. By proposition 71 (order of a cycle), $\exists n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $1 \leq n \lt p$ and $\tau^{n}=(1 \space 2 \space i_{3} \dots \space i_{p})$ where $(i_{3}, \dots, i_{p}$ are the other elements of the set of $p$ letters other than $1, \space 2$ (which we also denote $i_{1}=1, i_{2}=2$) in no particular order. The reason such $\tau^{n}$ exists is because, if we look at the distance from $1$ to $2$ in $\tau$ (which both must belong to the cycle $\tau$ since it consists of all elements of $S_{p}$ by supposition) and denote it $n$, then proposition 71 (order of a cycle) tells us that $\tau^{n}$ maps $1$ to $2$, and primality of $p$ guarantees that this too will be a $p$-cycle.

Now, note that $\sigma \circ \tau^{n}=(1 \space 2) \circ (1 \space 2 \space i_{3} \dots \space i_{p})=(2 \space i_{3} \dots \space i_{p})$, which we denote by $\psi$. Note that $\omega_{k}=\psi^{k} \circ (1 \space 2) \circ \psi^{-k}$ for $k \in \mathbb{N}, 1 \lt k \lt p-2$, behaves on the elements of $S_{n}$ as follows:
1. Since $1 \notin \psi$, then $1 \notin \psi^{k}$, so $\psi^{-k}$ fixes $1$, then $(1 \space 2)$ sends $1$ to $2$, which is then sent by $\psi^{k}$ to $i_{k+2}$ (since we suppose $1 \lt k$ then all these indices are greater than $3$), so $\omega_{k}(1)= i_{k+2}$.
2. For any other element, there are two cases: either the element is sent to $2$ by $\psi^{-k}$, and then sent to $1$ by $(1 \space 2)$ and fixed by $\psi^{k}$, or it is not sent to $2$, in which case it is not in $(1 \space 2)$ since $1 \notin \psi$, so then $\omega_{k}$ on this element reduces to $\psi^{k} \circ \psi^{-k}=\psi^{k-k}=(1)$ the identity. For $i_{k+2}$, we have $\psi^{-k}(i_{k+2})=i_{2}$ which is $2$, so $\omega_{k}(i_{k+2})=1$.

We have $\omega_{k}=(1 \space i_{k+2})$ for $1 \lt k \lt p-2$ i.e. we have all transpositions of one of the $p$ letters and $1$. Since $\omega$ is given by a composition of $\psi, \sigma$, then $\omega_{k} \in H$ since $H$ is a subgroup of $S_{n}$ by supposition. Similarly, $(1 \space j)(1 \space i)(1 \space j) \in H$, but $(1 \space j)(1 \space i)(1 \space j)=(i \space j)$ by theorem 76 (transposition arithmetics), so we have all transpositions of $S_{p}$ in $H$. By corollary 75 (permutations as a composition of transpositions), we can write every $\varphi \in S_{p}$ as a composition of transpositions, so $S_{p} \subseteq H$, but by supposition $H \leqslant S_{p}$, hence $S_{p}=H$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Alternating Group

Suppose $\sigma, \tau \in S_{n}$ and both have even parity, then $\sigma \circ \tau$ clearly also has even parity. Also, since $n$ is finite, then $S_{n}$ is finite, hence by the one-step subgroup test, the set of all even permutations on $n$ elements is a subgroup of $S_{n}$. We call this the *alternating group* on $n$ elements:

>[!definition] alternating group $A_{n}$
>
>Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then the *alternating group* on $n$ elements, denoted $A_{n}$, is the group of all even permutations on $n$.


As we have argued briefly above, $A_{n} \leqslant S_{n}$.

>[!lemma] every even permutation decomposes to 3-cycles
>
>If $\sigma \in S_{n}$ is an even permutation, then $\sigma$ can be written as a composition of $3$-cycles.


**Proof.** Let $\sigma$ be an even permutation, then by definition it decomposes to an even number of transpositions. It suffices to show that for $\sigma$ composed of $2$ transpositions, we have that $\sigma$ is expressible as a $3$-cycle, then by induction this fact naturally extends to any even permutation on $n$ letters.

Suppose $\sigma=(a \space b)(c \space d)$. If $(a \space b)=(c \space d)$ (i.e. $\{a, b\}=\{c, d\}$ in no particular order), then we have $\sigma=(a \space b)^{2}=(1)$ the identity by proposition 71 (order of a cycle), which is exactly the composition of zero $3$-cycles so the proposition is vacuously true.

Suppose $(a \space b) \neq (c \space d)$. Since $(a \space b)=(b \space a)$ and $(c \space d)=(d \space c)$, there are really only two cases to consider w.l.o.g: $a=c$ or $a \neq c$.

Suppose $a = c$, then $(a \space b)(c \space d)=(a \space b)(a \space d)$, in which case by our proof for theorem 76 (transposition arithmetics) we have that $(a \space b)(a \space d)=(a \space d \space b)$, which is a $3$-cycle, so the proposition holds.

Suppose $a \neq c$, then $(a \space b)(c \space d)$ is the composition of two disjoint transpositions. Recall that in our proof of theorem 82  we have seen that $(a \space b)(a \space c)(a \space b)=(b \space c)$, hence we can write $(c \space d)$ as $(c \space d)=(a \space c)(a \space d)(a \space c)$, so we have $(a \space b)(c \space d)=(a \space b)(a \space c)(a \space d)(a \space c)$, which we can also write as $((a \space b)(a \space c)) \circ ((a \space d)(a \space c))$, i.e. as a composition of $2$ permutations which themselves are the composition of two non-disjoint transpositions. As we have argued in the previous case, each can be written as a single $3$-cycle, so $(a \space b)(c \space d)=(a \space c \space b)(a \space c \space d)$, so the proposition holds.

Completing the proof via induction on the general case is left as an exercise.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $A_{n}$ is generated by $3$-cycles
>
>$A_{n}$ is generated by $3$-cycles from $S_{n}$.
>


**Proof.** By lemma 84 (every even permutation decomposes to 3-cycles) and by definition 83 (alternating group $A_{n}$). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We will collect a few more results on the alternating group on $n$ letters later, once we introduce normal and simple groups. Finally, when we discuss solvability by radicals in the context of Galois theory, we will use these results to demonstrate that there is no general formula for finding the roots of a general quintic over the complex domain (we show a stronger result, but it requires definitions we haven't intrdouced yet, so for now we state this weakened version as a hint for what's to come).

# Group Homomorphism
 
This section extends greatly on the introduction of the types of group morphisms in [[#^2e2207]]. Let $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ be a homomorphism.

>[!proposition]
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then $\text{Ker}(\phi) \leqslant G_{1}$ (where $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{x \in G_{1} | \phi(x)=e_{G_{2}}\}$) and $\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant G_{2}$.


**Proof.** 

$\text{Ker}(\phi) \leqslant G_{1}$: Recall by proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse) that $\phi$ maps the identity of $G_{1}$ to the identity of $G_{2}$, so $e_{1} \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is a nonempty subset of $G_{1}$. Consider $a, b \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$. By the same proposition, $\phi(b^{-1})=\phi(b)^{-1}$, and since $b \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$ then $\phi(b)^{-1}=e_{2}^{-1}=e_{2}$. Consider $ab^{-1}$, since $\phi$ is a homomorphism then $\phi(ab^{-1})=\phi(a) \phi(b^{-1})$, and $\phi(a)=e_{2}$ since $a \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$ and we have just shown $\phi(b^{-1})=e$, so $\phi(ab^{-1})=e_{2} \cdot e_{2} = e_{2}$, so $ab^{-1} \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so by theorem 32 (one-step subgroup test) we have $\text{Ker}(\phi) \leqslant G_{1}$.

$\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant G_{2}$: Again since $\phi(e_{1})=e_{2}$ then $e_{2} \in \text{Im}(\phi)$ so it is a nonempty subset of $G_{2}$. Let $a, b \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, then $\exists x, y \in G_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=a, \phi(y)=b$. Consider $xy^{-1} \in G_{1}$, since $\phi$ is a homomorphism we have $\phi(xy^{-1})=\phi(x)\phi(y^{-1})=a \phi(y)^{-1}=ab^{-1} \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, so by theorem 32 (one-step subgroup test) $\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant G_{2}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>Let $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ be a homomorphism, then $\phi$ is injective if and only if $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$.


**Proof.**  $\implies$Suppose $\phi$ is injective. We know $\phi(e_{1})=e_{2}$, so $e_{1} \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$. Since $\phi$ is injective, every image has a single preimage, so $\nexists g \in G_{1}, g \neq e_{1} \land \phi(g)=e_{2}$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$. Suppose by contradiction $\phi$ is not injective, i.e. $\exists a, b \in G_{1}$ s.t. $a \neq b$ and $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$. Observe that $\phi(a), \phi(b) \in G_{2}$ which is a group, so $\phi(b)^{-1} \in G_{2}$, so we can multiply both sides by $\phi(b)^{-1}$ and get $\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1}=e_{2}$. By proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse), $\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1}=\phi(ab^{-1})$, so $\phi(ab^{-1})=e_{2}$, so $ab^{-1} \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, but then $ab^{-1}=e_{1}$, so by definition $a^{-1}=b^{-1}$, so $a=b$, a contradiction, so $\phi$ is injective.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] group homomorphism commutes with group operation
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism and $a \in G_{1}$, then $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z}, \phi(a^{z})=\phi(a)^{z}$.


**Proof.** Suppose the proposition holds $\forall 0 \leq z$, then if $z \lt 0$ we recall by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) that $a^{z}=(a^{-z})^{-1}$, and by proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse) we know that $\phi(a^{z})=\phi((a^{-z})^{-1})=\phi(a^{-z})^{-1}$. Since $z \lt 0$ then $0 \lt -z$, so for $\phi(a^{-z})$ the proposition holds and we have $\phi(a^{-z})^{-1}=(\phi(a)^{-z})^{-1}$, which by exponents laws is $\phi(a)^{(-z)\cdot (-1)}=\phi(a)^{z}$, so if the proposition holds $\forall 0 \leq z$ then it holds $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z}$.

We now prove the proposition holds $\forall 0 \leq z$ by induction. For $z=0$ and $z=1$ the proposition trivially holds. Suppose it holds for some $z$, and consider $a^{z+1}$, which by definition is $a^{z}a$, so $\phi(a^{z+1})=\phi(a^{z}a)$, which since $\phi$ is a homomorphism, is the same as $\phi(a^{z})\phi(a)$. Applying the induction hypothesis, we know $\phi(a^{z})=\phi(a)^{z}$, so $\phi(a^{z})\phi(a)=\phi(a)^{z}\phi(a)=\phi(a)^{z+1}$, which completes the proof by induction.

We conclude the proposition is true.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] Group homomorphism preserves subgroups
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then:
>1. If $H_{1} \leqslant G_{1}$ then $\phi(H_{1}) \leqslant G_{2}$.
>2. If $H_{2} \leqslant G_{2}$ then $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \leqslant G_{1}$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. Let $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ be a group homomorphism and $H_{1} \leqslant G_{1}$, and consider $\phi|_{H_{1}}$. Denote $H_{2}=\text{Im}(\phi|_{H_{1}})$, we will prove by theorem 32 (one-step subgroup test) that $H_{2} \leqslant G_{2}$. First, since $e_{1} \in H_{1}$ and $\phi(e_{1})=e_{2}$, then $e_{2} \in H_{2}$ so $H_{2}$ is nonempty. Consider some $a, b \in H_{2}$, then $\exists x, y \in H_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=a, \phi(y)=b$. Consider $xy^{-1} \in H_{1}$ (it is an element of the set since $H_{1}$ is a group), then by proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse) $\phi(xy^{-1})=\phi(x)\phi(y)^{-1}=ab^{-1}$, so $ab^{-1} \in \text{Im}(\phi|_{H_{1}})=H_{2}$, so $H_{2} \leqslant G_{2}$.

$2$. Suppose $H_{2} \leqslant G_{2}$, then $e_{2} \in H_{2}$, and by proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse) $\phi(e_{1})=e_{2}$ so $e_{1} \in \phi^{-1}(H_{2})$, so $\phi^{-1}(H_{2})$ is non-empty. We proceed  by the one-step subgroup test. Denote $H_{1}=\phi^{-1}(H_{2})$ and let $a, b \in H_{1}$, then $\phi(ab^{-1})=\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1}$. Since $a, b \in H_{1}$ then $\phi(a), \phi(b) \in H_{2}$, so either $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$, in which case $\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1}=\phi(a)\phi(a)^{-1}=e \in H_{2}$, or $\phi(a) \neq \phi(b)$, in which case since $H_{2}$ is a group, $\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1} \in H_{2}$, in both cases $\phi(ab^{-1}) \in H_{2}$, so by definition $ab^{-1} \in \phi^{-1}(H_{2})=H_{1}$, so by the one-step subgroup test $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \leqslant G_{1}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Group Isomorphism

Isomorphisms are bijective homomorphisms. Intuitively, when two structures are *isomorphic* we understand that they are essentially *the same* under different guises, i.e. they have the same *structure*. A natural question then is, what does it mean for two groups to have the same *structure*? We have defined certain properties of a group that make groups inherently different from one another, such as whether or not the group is *cyclic* or *abelian*, and the *order* of the group of elements of the group. We have also considered important subgroups, such as the *center* of a group and the *centralizers* of the group. When we say that two groups have the same *structure*, we expect to find a *bijective mapping* that maintains the group structure and keeps these properties as group invariants under the mapping. This is what it means for two groups to be *isomorphic*. All of these follow naturally as a result of supposing that there exists some isomorphism $\phi$ between the two groups, as we will demonstrate in the next theorem:

>[!theorem] properties of a group isomorphism
>
>Let $G_{1}, G_{2}$ be groups and $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ be a group isomorphism, then:
>1. $|G_{1}|=|G_{2}|$.
>2. $\phi(Z(G_{1}))=Z(G_{2})$, i.e. $\phi$ maps the center of $G_{1}$ to the center of $G_{2}$.
>3. $\forall a \in G_{1}, \phi(C(a))=C(\phi(a))$, i.e. $\phi$ maps the centralizer of $a$ in $G_{1}$ to the centralizer of $\phi(a)$ in $G_{2}$.
>4. $\phi(\langle a \rangle)=\langle \phi(a) \rangle$, i.e. $\phi$ maps cyclic subgroups to cyclic subgroups and generators to generators.
>5. $\forall a \in G_{1}, |a|=|\phi(a)|$, i.e. $\phi$ preserves order.
>6. $G_{1}$ is cyclic $\iff$ $G_{2}$ is cyclic.
>7. $G_{1}$ is abelian $\iff$ $G_{2}$ is abelian.
>8. Let $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x$ a variable in $G_{1}$, and $b \in G_{1}$, then $x^{k}=b$ has exactly the same number of solutions as $x^{k}=\phi(b)$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. Trivially true since $\phi$ is a bijection so $G_{1}, G_{2}$ have the same number of elements, so $|G_{1}|=|G_{2}|$.

$2$. Recall definition 35 (center of a group) - $Z(G_{1})=\{g \in G_{1} | \forall x \in G_{1} (xg=gx)\}$. Let $y \in G_{2}$. Since $\phi$ is an isomorphism it is bijective and in particular surjective, i.e. $\forall y \in G_{2} \exists x \in G_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=y$. Let $g \in Z(G_{1})$, so $xg=gx$, so $\phi(xg)=\phi(gx)$, and since $\phi$ is a group isomorphism we have $\phi(xg)=\phi(x)\phi(g)=y\cdot \phi(g)$ and $\phi(gx)=\phi(g)\phi(x)=\phi(g) \circ y$, which is true $\forall y \in G_{2} \forall g \in Z(G_{1})$, so $\phi(g) \in Z(G_{2})$, so $\phi(Z(G_{1})) \subseteq Z(G_{2})$. Since $\phi^{-1}$ is also an isomorphism by [[#^7c54e6]] then by an identical argument we have $\phi^{-1}(Z(G_{2})) \subseteq Z(G_{1})$, so $Z(G_{2}) \subseteq \phi(Z(G_{1}))$, so by mutual inclusion $\phi(Z(G_{1}))=Z(G_{2})$.

$3.$ Let $a \in G_{1}$. Recall definition 36 (centralizer of an element) - $C(a)=\{g \in G_{1} | ag=ga\}$. Take some $g \in C(a)$, then $\phi(a)\phi(g)=\phi(ag)=\phi(ga)=\phi(g)\phi(a)$, so $\phi(g) \in C(\phi(a))$, so $\phi(C(a)) \subseteq C(\phi(a))$. Symmetrically for $\phi^{-1}$, we find that $\phi^{-1}(C(\phi(a))) \subseteq C(a)$, so we conclude $\phi(C(a))=C(\phi(a))$.

$4$. Let $a \in G_{1}$ and consider $a^{k}$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, then by proposition 88 (group homomorphism commutes with group operation) it follows that $\phi(a^{k})=\phi(a)^{k}$, and since $\langle a \rangle = \{a^{k} |k \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ then $\phi(\langle a \rangle)=\{\phi(a)^{k} | k \in \mathbb{Z}\}$, which is exactly $\langle \phi(x) \rangle$.

$5.$ By $(1), (4)$ it follows that $|\langle a \rangle| = |\langle \phi(a) \rangle|$, and by proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group) $|a|=|\langle a\rangle|$, $|\phi(a)|=|\langle \phi(a) \rangle|$, so $|a|=|\phi(a)|$.

$6$. Immediately from $(4)$.

$7$. $\implies$ Suppose $G_{1}$ is abelian, and let $a, b \in G_{2}$. Since $\phi$ is an isomorphism it is in particular surjective so $\exists x, y \in G_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=a, \phi(y)=b$. Since $G_{1}$ is abelian, $xy=yx$, so $\phi(xy)=\phi(yx)$, and since $\phi$ is a homomorphism $ab=\phi(x)\phi(y)=\phi(xy)=\phi(yx)=\phi(y)\phi(x)=ba$, so $G_{2}$ is abelian. $\impliedby$ Follows the same argument with $\phi^{-1}: G_{2} \to G_{1}$.

$8$. Suppose $x_{0}^{k}=b$ for some $x_{0}$, then $\phi(x_{0}^{k})=\phi(b)$, but $\phi(x_{0}^{k})=\phi(x_{0})^{k}$ by proposition 88 (group homomorphism commutes with group operation), so $\phi(x_{0})^{k}=\phi(b)$, so $\phi(x_{0})$ is a solution for $x^{k}=\phi(b)$. Conversely, suppose $y_{k}^{x}=\phi(b)$ for some $y_{0}$, then $\phi^{-1}(y_{0}^{k})=\phi^{-1}(\phi(b))$, so $\phi^{-1}(y_{0})^{k}=b$, so $\phi^{-1}(y_{0})$ is a solution for $x^{k}=b$. Thus, every solution for $x^{k}=b$ is also a solution for $x^{k}=\phi(b)$, so the number of solutions to both equations is the same.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Since so many of the group properties are invariant under isomorphism, then we have many ways of determining that two groups are not isomorphism.

>[!example]
>
>We will show $(\mathbb{R}, \times) \not \cong (\mathbb{C}, \times)$ (it is left as an exercise for the reader to verify the two structure are groups, with the usual definitions for multiplication in either field). Consider the equation $x^{4}=1$ in $\mathbb{R}$, then we have $x^{4}-1=(x^{2}-1)(x^{2}+1)=(x-1)(x+1)(x^{2}+1)=0$, and the only solutions to this equation in $\mathbb{R}$ are $\pm 1$, i.e. there are $2$ solutions. If $(\mathbb{R}, \times) \cong (\mathbb{C}, \times)$, then for every isomorphism $\phi: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{C}$, $1 \mapsto 1$, so by the final proposition of theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism) we expect to find $2$ solutions in $\mathbb{C}$ as well, however in $\mathbb{C}$ the expression can be written as $(x-1)(x+1)(x-i)(x+i)=0$, and the solutions to this equation are $\pm 1, \pm i$, i.e. there are $4$ solutions compared to $2$ in $\mathbb{R}$, so $(\mathbb{R}, \times) \not \cong (\mathbb{C}, \times)$.

We now turn our attention to automorphisms, which are isomorphisms from a structure to itself. Let $G$ be a group, then we denote the set of automorphisms of $G$ as $\text{Aut}(G)$.

>[!proposition]
>
>$$
>\text{Aut}(G) \leqslant \text{Sym}(G)
>$$


**Proof.** $\text{id}_{G}$ is an automorphism so $\text{Aut}(G)$ is nonempty. We proceed according to the one-step subgroup test. Let $\phi, \psi \in \text{Aut}(G)$, then $\phi \circ \psi^{-1}: G \to G$ is also an isomorphism since the inverse of an isomorphism is also an isomorphism ([[#^7c54e6]]) and isomorphisms form an equivalence relation ([[#^1ea7eb]]), and clearly it is an isomorphism from $G$ to itself, so $\phi \circ \psi^{-1} \in \text{Aut}(G)$, so $\text{Aut}(G) \leqslant \text{Sym}(G)$ by the one-step subgroup test. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We turn our attention to a particular subgroup of automorphisms. We begin with an example. Let $M \in \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$, then $M^{-1}$ exists. Now let $X \in \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ and consider a mapping $\phi: X \mapsto M^{-1}XM$. Recall from linear algebra that we call this *conjugation* by the matrix $M$. Now observe that $\det (M^{-1}XM)=\det(M^{-1}\det(X) \det(M)=\det(M)^{-1} \det(M) \det(X) = \det(X) \neq 0$, so $M^{-1}XM  \in \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$, so $\phi$ a mapping from $\text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ to itself. Likewise, $\phi(MXM^{-1})=X$, so $\phi$ is surjective and also if $\phi(X)=\phi(Y)$ then trivially $X=Y$ so it is also injective, so $\phi$ is bijective, and $\phi(XY)=M^{-1}XYM=M^{-1}XMM^{-1}YM=\phi(X)\phi(Y)$ so it is a homomorphism, so we conclude $\phi$ is an automorphism. We call automorphisms of this form *inner automorphisms*.

>[!definition] inner automorphism
>
>Let $G$ be a group. An *inner automorphism* $\phi_{g}: G \to G$ is an automorphism of the form $\phi_{g}: x \mapsto gxg^{-1}$ for some $g \in G$. We call $\phi_{g}$ *conjugation by $g$*.
>The set of all inner automorphisms of a group $G$ is denoted $\text{Inn}(G)$.

>[!proposition]
>
>Let $\phi_{g}$ be an inner automorphism $x \mapsto gxg^{-1}$, then $\phi_{g}^{-1}=\phi_{g^{-1}}$, i.e. $\phi_{g}^{-1}(x)=g^{-1}xg$.


**Proof.** Since $\phi_{g}$ is an automorphism then it is an isomorphism so it is a bijection so its inverse exists and is unique. Observe via direct substitution that $g(g^{-1}xg)g^{-1}=(gg^{-1})x(gg^{-1})$ from associativity, which is just $e \cdot x \cdot e = x = \text{id}(x)$, so $\phi^{-1}_{g}(x) = g^{-1}xg$, which is just $\phi_{g^{-1}}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>$$
>\text{Inn}(G) \leqslant \text{Aut}(G)
>$$

**Proof.** Observe that $\text{id}_{G}(x)=e\cdot x \cdot e^{-1}$ so $\text{id}_{G} \in \text{Inn}(G)$ so it is nonempty. We proceed by the one-step subgroup test. Let $\phi, \psi \in \text{Inn}(G)$, then $\exists g, h \in G$ s.t. $\phi(x)=gxg^{-1}$ and $\psi(x)=hxh^{-1}$. By proposition 94 , $\psi^{-1}(x)=h^{-1}xh$. Consider $(\phi \circ \psi^{-1})(x)=\phi(\psi^{-1}(x))=g(h^{-1}xh)g^{-1}=(gh^{-1})x(hg^{-1})$. Observe that $(gh^{-1})^{-1}=hg^{-1}$, so $(\phi \circ \psi^{-1})(x)=(gh^{-1})x(gh^{-1})^{-1}$, which is exactly the inner automorphism of $gh^{-1}$, so $\phi \circ \psi^{-1} \in \text{Inn}(G)$, so $\text{Inn}(G) \leqslant \text{Aut}(G)$ by the subgroup test <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$, i.e. the group of modular addition $\mod n$. Since the group is cyclic (with generator $1$), then every subgroup of it is also cyclic, and we know by a previous result that for an element $k \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}$ to generate it, it must satisfy $\text{gcd}(n, k)=1$, i.e. $(n, k)$ must be coprime, and the amount of such elements is also exactly $|\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}|$, i.e. the order of the group of modular multiplication $\mod n$. This observation motivates the following theorem:

>[!theorem]
>
>$$
>\text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}) \cong \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}
>$$

**Proof.** Let $\psi \in \text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+})$ and let $k \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$, then $\psi(k)=\psi(k \cdot 1)=k \psi(1)$ (here we used proposition 88 (group homomorphism commutes with group operation) in additive notation), so $\psi$ is uniquely determined by where it maps $1 \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$. By theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism), since $\langle 1 \rangle$ is a cyclic subgroup of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ (in fact, it is the same group), then $\psi$ maps $1$ to a generator of a cyclic subgroup in $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ of same order, i.e. it must map $1$ to another generator of the group. As we recalled above, the only elements that satisfy this property are those which are coprime w.r.t. $n$, the amount of which is exactly $\phi(n)$ where $\phi$ is Euler's totient function evaluated at $n$, and also $|\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}|$.

Consider a mapping $\tau: \text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}) \to \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}$ given by $\tau(\psi)=\psi(1)$. As we reasoned above, $\psi(1)$ is coprime w.r.t $n$, and the elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}$ are exactly the numbers which are coprime w.r.t. $n$ (and less than $n$), so the codomain is valid, and trivially the function is a bijection. Consider $\psi, \eta \in \text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+})$. By proposition 92  we know that $\text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+})$ is a group, so $\tau(\psi \circ \eta)$ is well-defined, but also $(\psi \circ \eta)(1)=\psi(\eta(1))$, and since $\psi$ is an automorphism and $\eta(1) \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ then by the same reasoning as above $\psi(\eta(1))=\psi(1 \cdot \eta(1)) = \psi(1) \eta(1)$, and by definition of $\tau$, $\tau(\psi) \tau(\eta)=\psi(1)\eta(1)$, so we have $\tau(\psi \circ \eta)=\tau(\psi)\tau(\eta)$, so $\tau$ is a homomorphism and also bijective, so it is an isomorphism, so $\text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}) \cong \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Intuitively speaking, the theorem implies that we can think of the automorphisms of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ as elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{*}$.

# Cosets

>[!definition] coset
>
>Let $H \subseteq G$ where $G$ is a group with multiplicative notation, and let $a \in G$.
>1. $\{ah | h \in H\}$ is called the *left coset* of $H$ with representative $a$, denoted $aH$.
>2. $\{ha|h \in H\}$ is called the *right coset* of $H$ with representative $a$, denoted $Ha$.

Cosets let us perform a group operation where one of the operands is fixed on the entire set. For example, $2\mathbb{Z}$ is the set of all even integers (which we also know to be a group), and $1+\mathbb{R}$ is simply the real line again, and also $1+\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}+1$. We see that sometimes the left and right cosets with same representative are the same, and sometimes they are equal to the set itself, i.e. there is a natural bijection from $H$ to $aH$ or $Ha$ in that case. Also of note is that sometimes the representative is not a member of the coset, for example $2+3\mathbb{N}^{+}$ is a left coset of $3\mathbb{N}^{+}$ with is a subset (but not a subgroup!) of $(\mathbb{Z}, +)$, yet $2$ is not a member of that coset since $2=2+3x$ has no solutions in $\mathbb{N}^{+}$. When $H \leqslant G$, then trivially $a$ is a member of all $a$-cosets:

>[!proposition]
>
>Let $G$ be a group with $a \in G$. If $H \leqslant G$, then $a \in aH$ and $a \in Ha$.


**Proof.** Since $H \leqslant G$ then $e \in H$, so $a=ae \in aH$ and $a=ea \in Ha$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now suppose that $H \leqslant G$ and show that all "nice" properties we expect are preserved.

>[!theorem] properties of a coset
>
>Let $H \leqslant G$ and $a, b \in G$, then:
>1. $aH=H \iff a \in H$
>2. $aH=bH$ or $aH \cap bH = \emptyset$
>3. $aH=bH \iff a^{-1}b \in H$
>4. $|aH|=|bH|$ and $|Ha|=|Hb|$.
>5. $aH=Ha \iff H = aHa^{-1}$
>6. $aH \leqslant G \iff a \in H$.


**Proof.** 

$1.$ $\implies$ Suppose $aH=H$. By proposition 98 , $H \leqslant G \implies a \in aH$, so $a \in H$. $\impliedby$ Suppose $a \in H$, so $a^{-1} \in H$ since $H$ is a group. Let $h \in H$, then $a^{-1}h \in H$, so $a(a^{-1}h)=h$ and also $a(a^{-1}h) \in aH$, so $H \subseteq aH$. Now let $ah \in aH$, since $a \in H$ and $h \in H$ and $H$ is a group then trivially $ah \in H$, so $aH \subseteq H$, so $H=aH$.

$2$. Suppose $aH \neq bH$ and suppose by contradiction $aH \cap bH \neq \emptyset$, i.e. $\exists k$ s.t. $k=ah$ and $k=bg$ for some $h, g \in H$. Let $bt \in bH$ for some $t \in H$, so $g^{-1}t \in H$, so $a(hg^{-1}t) \in aH$, but $a(hg^{-1}t)=(ah)(g^{-1}t)=b(gg^{-1})t=bt$, so $bt \in aH$, so $bH \subseteq aH$. By a symmetrical argument we find $aH \subseteq bH$, thus $aH=bH$, a contradiction, so either $aH=bH$ or $aH \cap bH =\emptyset$.

$3$. $\implies$ Suppose $aH=bH$. $b \in bH$ by proposition 98 , so $b \in aH$, so $b=ah$ for some $h \in H$, and the only solution to the equation is $h=a^{-1}b$, so $a^{-1}b \in H$. $\impliedby$ Suppose $a^{-1}b \in H$, so $a(a^{-1}b)=b \in aH$, and by proposition 98  $b \in bH$, so $b \in aH \cap bH$, so by $(2)$, $aH=bH$.

$4$. By definition $aH=\{ah | h \in H\}$. Consider a mapping $\phi: H \to aH, h \mapsto ah$. Evidently it is surjective. Suppose $\phi(h)=\phi(g)$, then $ah=ag$, then since $a \in G$ and $G$ is a group, by theorem 9 (cancellation law) $h=g$, so $\phi$ is injective, so it is a bijection, so $|H|=|aH|$. Since $aH$ is any left coset of $H$, then also $|H|=|bH|$, so $|aH|=|bH|$. By a similar argument we find that $\phi: H \to Ha, h \mapsto ha$ is a bijection, so we have $|aH|=|Ha|$ and $|Ha|=|Hb|$.

$5$. First, notice that, in general $aHb$ is well defined even without parenthesis (i.e. $(aH)b=a(Hb)$, so the expression $aHb$ is well defined), this follows directly from the associativity of $G$.

$\implies$ Suppose $aH=Ha$. Let $aha^{-1} \in aHa^{-1}$. Since $aH=Ha$ then $ah=ga$ for some $g \in H$, so $aha^{-1}=gaa^{-1}=g \in H$, so $aHa^{-1} \subseteq H$. Conversely, let $h \in H$. Solving $h=aga^{-1}$ in $G$, we have $g=a^{-1}ha$, and again since $Ha=aH$ then $\exists k \in H$ s.t. $ha=ak$, so $a^{-1}ha=a^{-1}ak$, so $g=k \in H$, so $aga^{-1} \in aHa^{-1}$, but $aga^{-1}=h$, so $H \subseteq aHa^{-1}$, so $H=aHa^{-1}$. $\impliedby$ Suppose $H=aHa^{-1}$, then by definition $aHa^{-1}=\{aha^{-1} | h \in H\}$ and also $Ha=\{ha | h \in H\}$, and since $aHa^{-1}=H$ we can write $h \in H$ as $aga^{-1}$ for some $g \in H$, so $Ha=\{(aga^{-1})a | g \in H\}=\{ag | g \in H\}=aH$ by definition, so $Ha=aH$. 

$6$. $\implies$ Suppose $aH \leqslant G$, then $e \in aH$, so $ah=e$ for some $H$, so $h=a^{-1}$, so $a^{-1} \in H$, and since $H \leqslant G$ it follows that $a \in H$. $\impliedby$ Suppose $a \in H$, then by $(1)$, $aH=H$, and by supposition $H \leqslant G$, so $aH \leqslant G$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] Lagrange's theorem
>
>Let $G$ be a finite group and let $H \leqslant G$, then $|H| \space \big | \space |G|$.


**Proof.** Suppose $H \leqslant G$. If $H=G$ then the theorem is trivially true, so suppose $H \neq G$, then $G \setminus H$ is nonempty, so take some $a_{1} \in G \setminus H$, and consider $a_{1}H$. By theorem 99 (properties of a coset), $|a_{1}H|=|eH|=|H|$. Now consider $G \setminus a_{1}H$, it is also nonempty since $|a_{1}H|=|H| \lt |G|$, so let $a_{2} \in G \setminus a_{1}H$. Consider $a_{2}H$, by the previous theorem we also know $|a_{2}H|=|a_{1}H|=|H|$, and also since $a_{2} \notin a_{1}H$ and yet $a_{2}=a_{2}e \in a_{2}H$ it follows that $a_{1}H \neq a_{2}H$, so by the theorem they are disjoint. Now, either $a_{1}H \cup a_{2}H = G$, or the union is a proper subset of $G$, in which case we consider some $a_{3} \in G \setminus \cup_{i=1}^{2}a_{i}H$. Again $|a_{3}H|=|H|$. If $G \setminus \cup_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}H = \emptyset$, then since all left cosets are disjoint, we have $|G|=\sum_{i=1}^{3}|a_{i}H|=3|H|$, so $|H| \space \big | \space |G|$. Otherwise, we proceed as above. Since $G$ is finite and all cosets are disjoint, eventually this process will terminate, and it is always the case that $\sum_{i=1}^{n}|a_{i}H|=n|H|$, so $|H| \space \big | \space |G|$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem) generalizes a a result we have already proven for cyclic groups in theorem 52 (fundamental theorem of cyclic groups).

>[!corollary]
>
>If $H \leqslant G$ and $G$ is a finite group, then $G$ has exactly $\frac{|G|}{|H|}$ distinct left $H$-cosets.


**Proof.** In the proof of Lagrange's theorem we have seen that for some finite sequence $(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})$, we have $G=\cup_{i=1}^{n} a_{i}H$, such that $\forall a_{i}, a_{j}(i \neq j \implies (a_{i}H \cap a_{j}H = \emptyset))$. Consider some $b \in G$ s.t. $b \notin \{a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}\}$, and consider $bH$. For every $i$, either $a_{i}H$ and $bH$ are disjoint, or they are the same coset (by theorem 99 (properties of a coset)), and since $G$ is covered by $\mathcal{aH}=\{a_{i}H | a_{i} \in \{a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}\}\}$ then for some $a_{i}$ it must be that $a_{i}H=bH$, and only for that $a_{i}$ since for every other $a_{j}$ we have $a_{i}H \cap a_{j}H = \emptyset$, so every left $H$-coset is an element of $\mathcal{aH}$, so $G$ has exactly $|\mathcal{aH}|=n=\frac{|G|}{|H|}$ distinct left $H$-cosets. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Lagrange's theorem motivates the following definition:

>[!definition] index of a subgroup
>
>Let $H \leqslant G$, then the *index* of $H$ in $G$, denoted $[G:H]$, is the number of disjoint left $H$-cosets in $G$. 


By theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem), the index of a subgroup is well defined.

>[!corollary]
>
>If $G$ is a group of prime order, then it is *cyclic*.


**Proof.** Let $G$ be a group of prime order, then the only divisors of $|G|$ are itself and $1$, which is the order of the trivial cyclic subgroup of $G$. Let $a \in G$ s.t. $a \neq e$ (there must exist such element since otherwise $|G|=1$ which is not prime), and consider $\langle a \rangle$, it is a subgroup of $G$ so by theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem), $|\langle a \rangle| \space \big | \space |G|$, so $|\langle a \rangle|$ is either $1$ or $|G|$, but by choice $a \neq e$ so $|\langle a \rangle| \neq 1$, so $|\langle a \rangle|=|G|$, so $\langle a \rangle = G$, so $G$ is *cyclic*. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

>[!proposition]
>
>If $G$ is a finite group and $a \in G$, then $a^{|G|}=e_{G}$.

**Proof.** Consider $\langle a \rangle \leqslant G$. By theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem), $|a|=|\langle a \rangle|$ divides $|G|$, so $|G|=n|a|$, so $a^{|G|}=a^{n|a|}=(a^{|a})^{n}=e_{G}^{n}=e_{G}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] every group of composite order has proper nontrivial cyclic subgroup
>
>If $G$ is a group and $|G|$ is composite (i.e. not prime nor $1$), then $\exists H \leqslant G$ s.t. $H$ is proper nontrivial, i.e. $H \neq G$ and also $H \neq \{e\}$. Furthermore, we can find such $H$ that is cyclic.


**Proof.** Consider $a \in G$ s.t. $a$ is not the identity of $G$. If $\langle a \rangle \neq G$ then since $\langle a \rangle \leqslant G$ we are done. Otherwise, write $G=nd$ where $(n, d)$ are coprime and consider $\langle a \rangle, \langle a^{d} \rangle$. By supposition $\langle a \rangle = G$, so $|a| = nd$. By corollary 48 , $\langle a \rangle = \langle a ^{d} \rangle$ if and only if $(|a|, d)$ are coprime, but by supposition $|a|=nd$ and clearly $d$ is a common divisor of $|a|, d$, so $\langle a \rangle \neq \langle a^{d} \rangle$, so it follows that $\langle a^{d} \rangle$ is a proper nontrivial subgroup of $G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Normal Subgroups and Factor Groups

We build our way towards a reduction of group to smaller building blocks. To do that, we introduce the notion of a normal subgroup.

>[!definition] normal subgroup
>
>If $G$ is a group and $H \leqslant G$ is a subgroup of $G$ s.t. $\forall a \in G, aH=Ha$, then we say that $H$ is a *normal subgroup* of $H$, denoted $H \unlhd G$ (normality).


>[!proposition]
>
>Let $G$ be a group, then $\{e\}, Z(G)$ and $G$ are always normal subgroups of $G$.


**Proof.** Let $g \in G$.
1. $\{e\}=\langle e \rangle$ which is the trivial subgroup of $G$, and also $eg=ge$, so $g\langle e \rangle = \langle e \rangle g$, so $\{e\} \unlhd G$.
2. Consider $Z(G)$. By definition, $gZ(G)=\{gz | z \in Z(G) \}$, but since $z \in Z(G)$ then $gz=zg$, so $\{gz| z \in Z(G)\}=\{zg | z \in Z(G)\}=Z(G)g$, so $Z(G) \unlhd G$.
3. Consider $G$, since $g \in G$ then by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $gG=Gg=G$, so $G \unlhd G$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] subgroup with index $2$ is normal subgroup
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $H \leqslant G$ s.t. $[H:G]=2$, then $H \unlhd G$.

**Proof.** Suppose $G$ is a group with subgroup $H$, and that the order of $H$ in $G$ is $2$, then $G$ is exactly $H$ and $aH$ for some $a \notin H$. Since $a \notin H$, then $Ha \neq H$, hence $Ha=aH$, hence $H$ is normal by definition, hence $H \unlhd G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] simple group
>
>A group $G$ is *simple* if $\forall H \unlhd G$, either $H=\{e\}$ or $H=G$, i.e. it has no non-trivial proper normal subgroups.


>[!example] simple group
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ for some prime $p$ which is a cyclic group (either by identifying it has prime order, or by identifying that $1$ is a generator of the group) then by corollary 50  it follows that every cyclic subgroup of $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ (aside from the identity) is the same as $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$, and by theorem 52 (fundamental theorem of cyclic groups) we know that all subgroups of $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ are cyclic, so if $H \leqslant \mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ then $H=\{0\}$ or $H=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$, so $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ has no proper non-trivial subgroups, so it is also the case that it has no non-trivial proper normal subgroups, so $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{+}$ is simple.


We will later show that normal subgroups and simplicity of a group are preserved under an isomorphism.

Recall theorem 99 (properties of a coset). We have shown that $aH=Ha \iff H=aHa^{-1}$. We can now express this relation in terms of normality, and write $H \unlhd G \iff H=aHa^{-1}$. The direction in the backwards direction ($(H=aHa^{-1}) \implies (H \unlhd G)$) can be further relaxed:

>[!proposition]
>
>If $H \leqslant G$ and $\forall a \in G$,  $aHa^{-1} \subseteq H$, then $H \unlhd G$.


**Proof.** Left as an exercise (can be shown by the same argument as the one in the respective proposition of theorem 99 (properties of a coset)). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We collect a result on normal subgroups of $A_{n}$ for use later in our discussion of the insolvability of the quintic:

>[!theorem]
>
>If $N \unlhd A_{n}$ and $\exists \sigma \in N$ s.t. $\sigma$ is a $3$-cycle, then $N=A_{n}$ (for $3 \leq n$).


**Proof.** Suppose $N \unlhd A_{n}$ and $\sigma \in N$ is a $3$-cycle $(a \space b \space c)$, then by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $\forall \tau \in A_{n}$, $\tau \circ \sigma \circ \tau^{-1} \in N$. Also, recall that $A_{n}$ is generated by $3$-cycles on $S_{n}$ (corollary 85 ($A_{n}$ is generated by $3$-cycles)), so it is sufficient to show that if $\tau \in A_{n}$ is a $3$-cycle, then $\tau \in N$, from which it would follow that $N=A_{n}$.

w.l.o.g. let $\sigma=(1 \space 2 \space 3)$, and let $\tau=(a \space b \space c)$ be a general $3$-cycle in $A_{n}$. We will show $(a \space b \space c) \in N$ via gradual substitution of $1, 2, 3$ with $a, b, c$. 

Consider conjugation of $\sigma$ by $(1 \space 2 \space a) \in A_{n}$ (since it is a $3$-cycle it is in $A_{n}$), then $(1 \space 2 \space a)^{-1}(1 \space 2 \space 3)(1 \space 2 \space a) \in N$ since $N$ is normal in $A_{n}$. Calculating the composition explicitly, we have that it produces the $3$-cycle $(1 \space 3 \space a)$, so $(1 \space 3 \space a) \in N$. As a formal formula in FOL, we can write this as

$$
\tag{1}
(((1 \space 2 \space 3) \in N) \land ((1 \space 2 \space a) \in A_{n})) \implies ((1 \space 3 \space a) \in N)
$$
We label the above formula $(1)$. The formula above lets us substitute a letter in our $3$-cycle with an arbitrary letter and get a new $3$-cycle also in $N$.

We repeat the same process for $(1 \space 3 \space a) \in N$ and $(1 \space 3 \space b) \in A_{n}$, then by substitution $1 \mapsto 1, 2 \mapsto 3, 3 \mapsto a, a \mapsto b$ we get from $(1)$ above that $(1 \space a \space b)=(a \space b \space 1) \in N$. Since $N$ is a subgroup then $(a \space b \space 1)^{2} \in N$, and by proposition 71 (order of a cycle) and primality of $3$ we find that $(a \space b \space 1)^{2}=(a \space 1 \space b)$, so $(a \space 1 \space b) \in N$.

Finally, repeating the same process one final time with $(a \space 1 \space b) \in N, (a \space 1 \space c) \in A_{n}$, then again by $(1)$ above and substitution, we find $(a \space b \space c)\in N$, so $N$ consists of all $3$-cycles on $n$ letters, so by corollary 85 ($A_{n}$ is generated by $3$-cycles), $N=A_{n}$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The key to the insolvability of the quintic lies in the next theorem, the proof of which we skip as it is rather technical, although it definitely can be read and understood at this point:

>[!lemma]
>
>If $5 \leq n$, and $N \unlhd A_{n}$ and also $N \neq \{e\}$, then $N$ contains a $3$-cycle.


>[!theorem] $A_{5 \leq n}$ is simple
>
>$A_{n}$ is simple for $5 \leq n$.


**Proof.** Suppose $N \unlhd A_{n}$ and also $5 \leq n$. Suppose $N \neq \{e\}$. By lemma 113 , $\exists \sigma \in N$ s.t. $\sigma$ is a $3$-cycle. By theorem 112 , $N=A_{n}$, so $A_{n}$ has no proper normal subgroup, so $A_{n}$ is simple by definition 109 (simple group). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

By theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem), we know that $H$ divides $G$ into $\frac{|G|}{|H|}$ distinct cosets, denote them $G \diagup H=\{aH | a \in G\}$. This is a *quotient set* under the relation $aRb \iff aH=bH$, which trivially is an equivalence relation. If $H$ 
is a *normal* subgroup of $G$, we equip this quotient set (also called a *factor set*) with a binary operator to form a group, which we call the *quotient group* of $G$ under $H$ (also sometimes denoted $G \mod H$):

>[!definition] factor group
>
>Let $H \unlhd G$, then the structure $(G \diagup H, \cdot)$, where:
>1. $G \diagup H=\{aH | a \in G\}$, i.e. it is the quotient set of $G$ under $H$.
>2. $\cdot: (G \diagup H) \times (G \diagup H) \to G \diagup H$ is given by $aH \cdot bH = abH$. We call this operation *coset multiplication*.
>Is called the *factor group* of $G$ under $H$.


It remains to show that:
1. The binary operator is well-defined.
2. The structure satisfies the group axioms.

>[!proposition]
>
>$G \diagup H$ for $H \unlhd G$  is a group under coset multiplication.


**Proof.** First, we show the operation is well-defined. Suppose $H \unlhd G$ and consider $G \diagup H$, and consider two representatives of two elements of the quotient set, $a_{1}H=a_{2}H$ and $b_{1}H=b_{2}H$. It remains to show that $a_{1}H \cdot b_{1}H = a_{2}H \cdot b_{2}H$, i.e. that $a_{1}b_{1}H = a_{2}b_{2}H$. Since $H$ is a normal subgroup of $G$, then $b_{1}H=Hb_{1}$, so $a_{1}b_{1}H=a_{1}Hb_{1}$. Now, since $a_{1}H=a_{2}H$, then $a_{1}Hb_{1}=a_{2}Hb_{1}$, and since $b_{1}H=b_{2}H$ and $H$ is normal then $Hb_{1}=Hb_{2}$, so $a_{2}Hb_{1}=a_{2}Hb_{2}$, and finally $a_{2}Hb_{2}=a_{2}b_{2}H$ since $Hb_{2}=b_{2}H$, so we have $a_{1}b_{1}H=a_{2}b_{2}H$, so coset multiplication is well defined on normal subgroups. Also, since $\forall a, b \in G, ab \in G$ then $abH$ is a left coset of $H$, so it is an element of $G \diagup H$ by corollary 101 , so the domain of the mapping is the set, so it is indeed a binary operator on $G \diagup H$.

It remains to show that $(G \diagup H, \cdot)$ satisfies the group axioms. Note that we cannot use a subgroup test since $G \diagup H \not \subseteq H$.

1. (left identity) since $eH$ is a left coset of $H$ (where $e$ is the identity of $H$ so it is also the identity of $G$ since $H \leqslant G$), then by definition $\forall a \in G(ea=a)$, so by definition of coset multiplication $\forall aH \in G \diagup H(eH \cdot aH=aH)$, so $eH=H$ is the (left) identity of $G \diagup H$. By the same process we can demonstrate that it is also a right identity, but that is not necessary (see corollary 7 ).
2. (left inverse) let $a \in G$, so $a^{-1}H, aH \in G \diagup H$, and by definition $a^{-1}H \cdot aH = a^{-1}aH = eH=H$, which is the identity of $G \diagup H$, so every element has a left inverse.
3. (associativity) suppose $aH, bH, cH \in G \diagup H$, then $aH \cdot (bH \cdot cH) = aH \cdot (bcH)= a(bc)H$, and since $a, b, c \in G$ then by associativity of $G$, $a(bc)H=(ab)cH=(aH \cdot bH) \cdot cH$, so the operator is associative on $G \diagup H$.

We conclude that $(G \diagup H, \cdot)$ is a group. Note that normality of $H$ was necessary for the operator to be well-defined.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] natural homomorphism from group to factor group
>
>Let $H \unlhd G$, then $\phi: G \to G \diagup H,  a \mapsto aH$ is a group homomorphism. We call this $\phi$ the *natural homomorphism* of $G \to G \diagup H$. This homomorphism is also trivially surjective.


**Proof.** We prove this $\phi$ is a homomorphism. Let $a, b \in G$, then $\phi(ab)=(ab)H=aH \cdot bH = \phi(a) \phi(b)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] order of element in the factor group
>
>Let $N \unlhd G$ and $G \diagup N$ be a factor group, then $\forall aN \in G \diagup N$, the order of $aH$ is the smallest $k \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $a^{k} \in N$.

**Proof.** By definition of coset multiplication, $(aN)^{k}=a^{k}N$. The order of $aN$ is the smallest positive $k$ s.t. $(aN)^{k}=\text{id}_{G \diagup N}=eN=N$. Clearly if we take $(aN)^{|a|}$, where $|a|$ is the order of $a$ in $G$, then $(aN)^{|a|}=a^{|a|}N=eN=N$, so by the well-ordering principle the order of $aN$ exists and is well-defined since $|a|$ exists, so we have $a^{k}N=N$, and by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) this is true if and only if $a^{k} \in N$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] order of a factor group
>
>Let $N \unlhd G$ and $G \diagup N$ be a factor group, then $|G \diagup N| = [G:N]$, where $[G:N]$ is the index of $N$ in $G$.


**Proof.** By definition 102 (index of a subgroup), $[G:N]$ is the number of distinct left $N$-cosets of $G$. In definition 115 (factor group) we define $G \diagup N$ to be the group whose elements are the distinct left $N$-cosets of $G$, so by definition $|G \diagup N|=[G:N]$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Normal subgroup homomorphism & first isomorphism theorem

Here we demonstrate that normality of a subgroup and simplicity of a group are preserved under an isomorphism, and then present the first isomorphism theorem and show a few examples of the theorem.

>[!theorem] homomorphism preserves normality
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then:
>1. If $\phi$ is surjective and $H_{1} \unlhd G_{1}$, then $\phi(H_{1}) \unlhd G_{2}$ (the image of a normal subgroup is a normal subgroup of the codomain)
>2. If $H_{2} \unlhd G_{2}$, then $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \unlhd G_{1}$ (the pre-image of a normal subgroup is a normal subgroup of the domain)


**Proof.** Let $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$.

$1$. Suppose $\phi$ is surjective (note: this condition can be removed entirely since we know that $\phi: G_{1} \to \phi(G_{1})$ is surjective, and since $G_{1}$ is a group and $\phi$ is a homomorphism then the image of subgroups of $G_{1}$ is a subgroup of the domain so trivially $\phi(G_{1})$ is also a group by proposition 89 (Group homomorphism preserves subgroups)). Suppose $H_{1} \unlhd G_{1}$, so $H_{1} \leqslant G_{1}$, so by proposition 89 (Group homomorphism preserves subgroups) $\phi(H_{1}) \leqslant G_{2}$, so it remains to show that $\phi(H_{1})$ is normal. Let $y \in G_{2}$, since $\phi$ is surjective, $\exists x \in G_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=y$. Since $H_{1}$ is normal, then $xH_{1}=H_{1}x$, i.e. $\forall h \in H_{1} (xh=hx)$. Let $h' \in \phi(H_{1})$, then $\exists h \in H_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(h)=h'$, and since $h \in H_{1}$ then $xh=hx$, so $\phi(xh)=\phi(hx)$, now since $\phi$ is a homomorphism $\phi(xh)=\phi(x)\phi(h)=yh'$ and $\phi(hx)=\phi(h)\phi(x)=h'y$, and this is true $\forall y \in G_{2} \forall h' \in \phi(H_{1})$, so by definition $y\phi(H_{1})=\phi(H_{1})y$, so $\phi(H_{1}) \unlhd G_{2}$.

$2$. Suppose $H_{2} \unlhd G_{2}$, so $H_{2} \leqslant G_{2}$ and by proposition 89 (Group homomorphism preserves subgroups) $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \leqslant G_{1}$. It remains to show $\phi^{-1}(H_{2})$ is normal. Since $H_{2}$ is normal, then $\forall g_{2} \in G_{2}$, $g_{2}H_{2}=H_{2}g_{2}$, so by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $g_{2}H_{2}g_{2}^{-1}=H_{2}$.

Let $g_{1} \in G_{1}$, then $\phi(g_{1}) \in G_{2}$ since $\phi$ is a mapping from $G_{1}$ to $G_{2}$, so $g_{1}$ is a pre-image of some $g_{2} \in G_{2}$. Denote $H_{1}=\phi^{-1}(H_{2})$ and let $h_{1} \in H_{1}$, then it is the preimage of some $h_{2} \in H_{2}$, so we have $\phi(h_{1})=h_{2}$ and $\phi(g_{1})=g_{2}$. Consider $g_{1}h_{1}g_{1}^{-1} \in G_{1}$, since $\phi$ is a homomorphism then $\phi(g_{1}h_{1}g_{1}^{-1})=\phi(g_{1})\phi(h_{1})\phi(g_{1})^{-1}=g_{2}h_{2}g_{2}^{-1} \in g_{2}H_{2}g_{2}^{-1}$, which as we argued last paragraph is equivalent to $H_{2}$, so $g_{1}h_{1}g_{1}^{-1} \in \phi^{-1}(H_{2})=H_{1}$, and this holds $\forall g_{1} \in G_{1} \forall h_{1} \in H_{1}$, so by proposition 111 , $H_{1} \unlhd G_{1}$. 

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then $\text{Ker}(\phi) \unlhd G_{1}$.


**Proof.** By proposition 107  we know that $\{e_{2}\} \unlhd G_{2}$, and by definition $\phi^{-1}(\{e_{2}\})=\text{Ker}(\phi)$, so by theorem 120 (homomorphism preserves normality) $\text{Ker}(\phi) \unlhd G_{1}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then:
>1. The factor group $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$ is well-defined.
>2. If $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$, then $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong G_{1}$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. By corollary 121 , $\text{Ker}(\phi) \unlhd G_{1}$. By proposition 116 , if $H_{1} \unlhd G_{1}$ then the factor group $G_{1} \diagup H_{1}$ is well-defined, so $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$ is well-defined.

$2$. Suppose $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$ and consider the natural homomorphism $\psi: G_{1} \to G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi), a \mapsto a\text{Ker}(\phi)$ (see proposition 117 (natural homomorphism from group to factor group)). 

The identity of $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$ is $\text{Ker}(\phi)$. Suppose $\psi(a)=\text{Ker}(\phi)$ for some $a \in G_{1}$, so $a=\text{Ker}(\phi)$, but also by definition $a=a \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $a \text{Ker}(\phi)=\text{Ker}(\phi)$. By theorem 99 (properties of a coset), this is true if and only if $a \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, but we supposed $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e_{1}\}$, so $a=e_{1}$, so $\text{Ker}(\psi)=\{e_{1}\}$, so by theorem 87 , $\psi$ is injective.
