Also, $\psi$ is surjective, so $\psi$ is an isomorphism, so $G_{1} \cong G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$, and since isomorphism is an equivalence relation, it is symmetric, so $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong G_{1}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] isomorphism preserves simplicity
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is an isomorphism then $G_{1}$ is simple if and only if $G_{2}$ is simple.
>

**Proof.** Suppose $G_{1}$ is simple and $G_{2}$ is not, then $G_{2}$ has some normal subgroup $H_{2} \unlhd G_{2}$ s.t. $H_{2} \notin \{\{e_{2}\}, G_{2}\}$. By theorem 120 (homomorphism preserves normality), $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \unlhd G_{1}$. By theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism), $|H_{2}|=|\phi^{-1}(H_{2})|$, and since $|G_{1}|=|G_{2}|$ (since $\phi$ is bijective) then it must also be that $\phi^{-1}(H_{2}) \notin \{\{e_{1}\}, G_{1}\}$, but then $\phi^{-1}(H_{2})$ is a normal non-trivial subgroup of $G_{1}$, a contradiction. Following the same argument, it is shown that if $G_{2}$ is simple then $G_{1}$ must also be simple.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now prove a lemma towards the first isomorphism theorem (some writers call this lemma the first isomorphism theorem, however we reserve this name for a more general theorem)

>[!lemma]
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism, then $\psi: G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \to \phi(G_{1}), x \text{Ker}(\phi) \mapsto \phi(x)$ is an isomorphism, and $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(G_{1})$.


**Proof.** $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$ is a factor group as per corollary 121 . First, we show $\psi$ is well-defined. Suppose $a \text{Ker}(\phi)=b\text{Ker}(\phi)$ for $a, b \in G_{1}$, then by definition $\psi(a \text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(a)$ and $\psi(b \text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(b)$. For $\psi$ to be well-defined it must be single-valued, i.e. it must be that $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$, and indeed since $a\text{Ker}(\phi)=b\text{Ker}(\phi)$ then by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $ab^{-1} \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $\phi(ab^{-1})=e_{2}$, an since $\phi$ is a homomorphism then $\phi(ab^{-1})=\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1}=e_{2}$, so $\phi(b)^{-1}=\phi(a)^{-1}$, so $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$, so $\psi$ is single-valued and so it is well-defined.

Next, we show $\psi$ is a group homomorphism: Indeed, $\psi(a\text{Ker}(\phi)\cdot b \text{Ker}(\phi))=\psi(ab \text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)=\psi(a \text{Ker}(\phi)) \psi(b \text{Ker}(\phi))$, so $\psi$ is a group homomorphism.

Let $e_{2}$ be the identity on $\phi(G_{1})$, then $\psi(e_{1}\text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(e_{1})=e_{2}$, so $e_{1}\text{Ker}(\phi) \in \text{Ker}(\psi)$, so it is non-empty. Now let $x \in \text{Ker}(\psi)$, then $x = a \text{Ker}(\phi)$ for some $a \in G_{1}$, and $\psi(x)=\phi(a)$, and also $\psi(x)=e_{2}$ since $x \in \text{Ker}(\psi)$, so $\phi(a)=e_{2}$, so $a \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$ by definition, but $\forall a \in \text{Ker}(\phi)(a \text{Ker}(\phi)=\text{Ker}(\phi))$ by theorem 99 (properties of a coset), so in fact all choices for $a$ are congruent $\mod \text{Ker}(\phi)$, i.e. they all map to the same element, so $\text{Ker}{\psi}=\{a \text{Ker}(\phi) | a \in \text{Ker}(\phi)\}=\{\text{Ker}(\phi)\}$, which is the identity on $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so by theorem 87  $\psi$ is injective, and clearly $\psi$ is surjective (let $y \in \phi(G_{1})$, then $\exists x \in G_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(x)=y$, and also $x \text{Ker}(\phi) \in G \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $\psi(x \text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(x)=y$, so $\psi$ is surjective), so it is a bijection, so $\psi$ is an isomorphism, so $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(G_{1})$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] first isomorphism theorem
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a group homomorphism, then:
>1. $\text{Ker}(\phi) \unlhd G_{1}$
>2. $\phi(G_{1}) \leqslant G_{2}$
>3. $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(G_{1})$


**Proof.**  $(1)$ is proven in corollary 121 , $(2)$ is proven in proposition 89 (Group homomorphism preserves subgroups), and $(3)$ is proven in lemma 124 . <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a group homomorphism and $G_{1}$ is finite, then $|G_{1}|=|\text{Ker}(\phi)| \cdot | \text{Im}(\phi)|$.


**Proof.** By theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \text{Im}(\phi)$, by corollary 101  
since $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is a normal subgroup of $G_{1}$ and $G_{1}$ is finite,  we know $[G_{1}:\text{Ker}(\phi)]=\frac{|G_{1}|}{|\text{Ker}(\phi)|}$. By  proposition 119 (order of a factor group), $|G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)|=[G_{1}:\text{Ker}(\phi)]$, so $|G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)|=\frac{|G_{1}|}{|\text{Ker}(\phi)|}$. Since the two groups are isomorphic, then $|G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)| = |\text{Im}(\phi)|$, so $|G_{1}|=|\text{Ker}(\phi)| \cdot |\text{Im}(\phi)|$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

>[!proposition] order of element under homomorphism
>
>If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a group homomorphism, and $a \in G_{1}$, then the order of $\phi(a)$ in $G_{2}$ divides the order of $a$ in $G_{1}$, i.e. $|\phi(a)|_{G_{2}} \space \big | \space |a|_{G_{1}}$.


**Proof.** Consider $\phi$ restricted to $\langle a \rangle$, then $\phi(a^{k})=\phi(a)^{k} \in \langle \phi(a) \rangle$, so $\psi=\phi|_{\langle a \rangle}$ is a group homomorphism $\langle a \rangle \to \langle \phi(a) \rangle$. Suppose $\psi(a^{k})=e$, then $\psi(a^{k})=\psi(a)^{k}=\phi(a)^{k}=e$, so $|\phi(a)|$ must divide $k$ by previous results on cyclic groups. Since $\phi(e)=e$ (by proposition 19 (group homomorphism preserves identity and commutes with inverse)), and $a^{|a|}=e$, then $\phi(a^{|a|})=\phi(a)^{|a|}=e$, so $|\phi(a)| \space | \space |a|$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We proceed with some examples where we use the first isomorphism theorem.

>[!example]
>
>Recall $\text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ is a group, and consider $\det: \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R}) \to \mathbb{R}^{\times}$ (where $\mathbb{R}^{\times}$ is the group of real numbers with usual multiplication), then $e_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}}$ is the multiplicative identity of $\mathbb{R}$ which is $1$, so by definition $\text{Ker}(\det)=\{A \in \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R}) | \det (A) = 1\}$, which is just the definition of $\text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ (note: this also immediately proves that $\text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R})$ is a group under matrix multiplication once we show that $\det$ is a group homomorphism). Next, let $A, B \in \text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R})$, then by properties of the determinant $\det(AB)=\det(A) \det(B)$, so $\det$ is a group homomorphism, and it is also surjective (let $r \in \mathbb{R}$, then the uniform scaling matrix $rI$ has determinant $r$, so $\det$ is surjective) so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) we have that $\text{GL}(n, \mathbb{R}) \diagup \text{SL}(n, \mathbb{R}) \cong \mathbb{R}^{*}$.

We present the next example as a theorem:

>[!theorem] cyclic groups are isomorphic to modular addition groups of integers of same order
>
>If $G=\langle a \rangle$, then:
>1. If $|G|=\infty$, $G \cong \mathbb{Z}^{+}$ (which is just the group of integers under addition)
>2. If $|G|=n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}^{+}$, $G \cong \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$ (the group of integers under modular addition $\mod n$).


**Proof.**  

$1.$ Let $G=\langle a \rangle$ and suppose $|G|=\infty$, and consider the mapping $\phi: \mathbb{Z}^{+} \to G, z \mapsto a^{z}$. Observe that $\phi(z+n)=a^{z+n}=a^{z}a^{n}=\phi(z)\phi(n)$, so it is a group homomorphism from $\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ to $G$. Let $z \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $\phi(z)=e$ and also $\phi(z)=a^{z}$ (by definition of $\phi$), so $a^{z}=e$, which is true if and only if $z=0$ since $G$ is a non-finite cyclic group, so  $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{0\}$, which is also the identity of $\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ (this is to be expected since the identity of the domain is taken to the identity of the codomain by a group homomorphism), so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) $\mathbb{Z}^{+} \diagup \{0\} \cong G$. By corollary 122 , we have $\mathbb{Z}^{+} \cong \mathbb{Z}^{+} \diagup \{0\}$, so since isomorphism is an equivalence relation we have $G \cong \mathbb{Z}^{+}$.

$2$. Let $G=\langle a \rangle$ and suppose $|G|=n$, and similarly consider the mapping $\phi: \mathbb{Z}^{+} \to G$. By the same reasoning as before $\phi$ is a group homomorphism. Now let $z \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then as before $a^{z}=e$, which is true if and only if $n|z$ by corollary 25 , so $z \in \text{Ker}(\phi) \iff (z=nk \land k \in \mathbb{Z}^{+})$, so by definition of the right coset, $\text{Ker}(\phi)=n\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. By theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem), $\mathbb{Z}^{+} \diagup n\mathbb{Z}^{+} \cong G$, but $\mathbb{Z}^{+} \diagup n\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$, so $G \cong \mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example]
>
>Consider $\mathbb{R}^{+}$ (the group of real numbers under addition) and $\mathbb{C}^{\times}$ (the group of complex numbers under complex multiplication), and define $\phi: x \mapsto e^{ix}$, then $\phi(x+y)=e^{i(x+y)}=e^{ix+iy}=e^{ix}e^{iy}=\phi(x)\phi(y)$, so it is a group homomorphism. Furthermore, $\text{id}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})=1$, so $\forall x \in \text{Ker}(\phi) (\phi(x)=1)$, i.e. $e^{ix}=1$. Recall from Euler's formula that $e^{ix}=\cos x + i \sin x$, so $1=\cos x + i \sin x$ if and only if $\sin x = 0$ and $\cos x = 1$, which is true if and only if $x=2\pi k$ for $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=2\pi \mathbb{Z}$, so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) $R^{+} \diagup 2\pi \mathbb{Z} \cong \phi(\mathbb{R}^{+})$. Recall the geometric interpretation of $\cos x + i \sin x$; it is a point in the complex unit circle which we denote $S_{1}$, i.e. $\text{Im}(\phi) \subseteq S_{1}$. Let $s \in S_{1}$, then $s=\cos x + i \sin x$ for some $x$, so $x \in \mathbb{R}^{+}$, so $\phi(x)=s$, so $S_{1} \subseteq \text{Im}(\phi)$, so $\text{Im}(\phi)=S_{1}$, so in fact $\mathbb{R}^{+} \diagup 2 \pi \mathbb{Z} \cong S_{1}$. Geometrically we interpret it as follows: take the real line and slice it into intervals of the form $I_{k}=[2 \pi k, 2\pi (k+1)]$ for $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, then by wrapping $I_{k}$ we can construct a unit circle, and also addition of points in $I_{k}$ has the same affect as multiplying those points in $S_{1}$.

>[!theorem] $S_{n} \diagup A_{n} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$
>
>Let $4 \leq n$. The alternating group on $n$ letters $A_{n}$ is a normal subgroup of the symmetric group on $n$ letters $S_{n}$, and also $S_{n} \diagup A_{n} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$.


**Proof.** Consider the mapping $\phi: S_{n} \to \mathbb{Z}_{2}, \sigma \mapsto \text{sgn}(\sigma)$, where $\text{sgn}(\sigma)$ is the parity of the cycle $\sigma$ as defined in definition 79 (parity of a permutation), only that we let $\text{sgn}(\sigma)$ be $0$ when the permutation is even, and let it be $1$ otherwise. Since the parity of a permutation is well-defined, so is $\phi$. Clearly, $\phi$ is surjective for any $4 \leq n$ since $(12)(13)(14) \in S_{n}$ which has odd parity so $\phi((12)(13)(14))=1$, while $\text{id} \in S_{n}$ has even parity so $\phi(\text{id})=0$, hence $\phi$ is surjective, i.e. $\text{Im}(\phi)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}$.

Next, we show $\phi$ is a group homomorphism: let $\sigma, \tau \in S_{n}$, then there are $3$ cases:
1. $\sigma, \tau$ are both even, $\sigma \circ \tau$ is even, so $\phi(\sigma \circ \tau)=0=0+0=\phi(\sigma)+\phi(\tau)$
2. $\sigma, \tau$ are both odd, so $\sigma \circ \tau$ is even, so $\phi(\sigma \circ \tau)=0 \equiv_{2}2 = 1 + 1 = \phi(\sigma)+\phi(\tau)$
3. w.l.o.g $\sigma$ is even and $\tau$ is odd, so $\sigma \circ \tau$ is odd, so $\phi(\sigma \circ \tau)=1=0+1=\phi(\sigma)+\phi(\tau)$

So, $\phi$ is a surjective group homomorphism. Note that by definition of $\phi$, $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is the set of even permutations on $n$ letters, i.e. it is exactly $A_{n}$, hence $\text{Ker}(\phi)=A_{n}$, so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem):
1. $A_{n} \unlhd S_{n}$
2. $S_{n} \diagup A_{n} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $[S_{n}:A_{n}]=2$
>
>For $4 \leq n$, $[S_{n}:A_{n}]=2$.

**Proof.**  By corollary 126  and theorem 131 ($S_{n} \diagup A_{n} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$), $|S_{n}|=|A_{n}| \cdot |\mathbb{Z}_{2}|=2|A_{n}|$, so by definition 102 (index of a subgroup), $[S_{n}:A_{n}]=2$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Rank-Nullity Theorem

Recall the rank-nullity theorem from linear algebra:

>[!theorem] rank-nullity theorem
>
>If $T$ is a linear transformation between vector spaces and $\text{Domain}(T)$ finite dimensional, then
>$$
>\dim ({\text{Domain}(T)})=\dim (\text{Ker}(T)) + \dim (\text{Im}(T))
>$$
>


In this section we will briefly demonstrate that the first isomorphism theorem is a generalization of the rank-nullity theorem. First, we provide a quick refresher of relevant terminology from linear algebra (although usually in undergraduate linear algebra courses, these definitions appear before group theory has been properly introduced, so we offer a revised, more compact version of the same definitions, using definitions from group theory.)

>[!definition] vector space
>
>The structure $(V, +, \cdot, \mathbb{F})$ where:
>1. $\mathbb{F}$ is a field
>2. $V$ is a nonempty set
>3. $+: V \times V \to V$ is a binary operator on $V$ (vector addition)
>4. $\cdot: \mathbb{F} \times V \to V$ is an binary operator (scalar multiplication)
>   
>Is called a *vector space*, if the following properties are satisfied $\forall u, v \in V \forall a, b \in \mathbb{F}$:
>1. $(V, +)$ is an *abelian group*
>2. $a(bv)=(ab)v$ (scalar multiplication is compatible with field multiplication)
>3. If $1 \in \mathbb{F}$ is the multiplicative identity of $\mathbb{F}$, then $1v=v$.
>4. $a(u+v)=au+uv$ (sclar multiplication is distributive over vector addition)
>5. $(a+b)v=av+bv$ (scalar multiplication is distributive over field addition)
>

We note that $2, 3$ can be abbreviated to saying that $\mathbb{F}$ acts on $V$ via $\cdot$, but we have not yet presented group or field actions so we ignore that for now.

>[!definition] quotient vector space
>
>If $V$ is a vector space and $U \subseteq V$ is a vector subspace of $V$, then the *factor group* $V \diagup U$ is called the *quotient vector space* of $V$ under $U$, where vector addition and scalar multiplication are defined as follows:
>1. $[u] + [w]= [u+w]$
>2. $\alpha [u] = [\alpha u]$


Since a group generalizes vector spaces (vector spaces satisfy all group axioms and have more structure), then all our previous results apply, namely that vector addition in $V \diagup U$ is well-defined and that the natural homomorphism $V \to V \diagup U, v \mapsto v+U$ is a homomorphism from $V$ to its quotient space under $U$. More importantly, the first isomorphism theorem also holds. It it left to the reader to demonstrate that $\alpha[u] \mapsto [\alpha u]$ is also well defined.

We now show that the $V \diagup U$ is indeed a *vector space* under the same field $\mathbb{F}$ as $V$.

>[!proposition]
>
>Let $V$ be a vector space over a field $\mathbb{F}$ and $U$ be a vector subspace of $U$, then $V \diagup U$ is also a vector space over $\mathbb{F}$.

**Proof.** We already know from group theory that $V \diagup U$ is a group, and it is trivial to demonstrate that if $V$ is abelian and $U \unlhd V$ then $V \diagup U$ is also abelian. We proceed to demonstrate the $4$ other axioms are satisfied. Let $[u], [w] \in V \diagup U$ and $a, b \in \mathbb{F}$, then:

1. $a(b[u])=a([bu])=[abu]=(ab)[u]$
2. $1[u]=[1u]=[u]$
3. $a([u]+[w])=a([u+w])=[a(u+w)]=[au+aw]=[au]+[aw]=a[u]+a[w]$
4. $(a+b)[u]=[(a+b)u]=[au+bu]=[au]+[bu]=a[u]+b[u]$

Thus we conclude that $V \diagup U$ with the operations defined in definition 135 (quotient vector space) is a vector space.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Since $V \diagup U$ is a vector space, then we can discuss its *dimension*.

>[!proposition]
>
>If $V$ has finite dimension, then $\dim (V \diagup U)= \dim(V) - \dim(U)$


**Proof.** Let $b_{U}$ be a basis of $U$, then it is a set of $\dim (U)$ linearly independent vectors in $V$ and $\dim (U) \leq \dim(V)$ since $U$ is a subspace of $V$, so we can complete $b_{U}$ to a basis $b_{V}$ of $V$ by extending $b_{U}$ with $\text{dim}(V)-\dim(U)$ new linearly independent vectors, so now we have $|b_{V}|=|\dim(V)|$. Note that this extension is only possible since $V$ is *finite*.

Let $[v] \in V \diagup U$, then $[v]=x+U$ for some $x \in V$. Let $\phi: V \to V \diagup U$ be the natural homomorphism $x \mapsto x+U$, then by proposition 117 (natural homomorphism from group to factor group) it is trivially a surjection, so since $b_{V}$ spans $V$ then trivially $\phi(b_{V})$ spans $V \diagup U$.

By theorem 99 (properties of a coset) we know that $\forall x \in U$, $x+U=U$, and if $x \in U$ then it can be expressed as a linear combination of $b_{U}$. Let $x \in V$, then we can write $x$ as $x=x_{U}+x_{V}$, where $x_{U}$ is $x$ projected to $U$ and $x_{V}$ is $x$ projected to $V \setminus U$, so $x+U=x_{U}+x_{V}+U$ and since $V$ is abelian then $x_{U}+x_{V}+U=x_{V}+x_{U}+U=x_{V}+(x_{U}+U)$ and as we argued $x_{U} +  U = U$ since $x_{U} \in U$, so we have that every $[v] \in V \diagup U$ can be written as $x_{V}+U$ where $x_{V}$ is a linear combination of the basis vectors of $b_{V} \setminus b_{U}$, so $\dim (V \diagup U) \leq |b_{V} \setminus b_{U}|=\dim(V) - \dim(U)$. Since $b_{V} \setminus b_{U}$ is a set of linearly independent vectors, then it is trivial to show that it cannot be that $\dim(V \diagup U) \lt \dim(V) - \dim(U)$ (suppose it is, then it must be that at least two of the basis vectors which we denote $b_{1}, b_{2} \in b_{V} \setminus b_{U}$ map to the same coset, but then it must be that $b_{1}-b_{2} \in U$ by theorem 99 (properties of a coset), so $\{b_{1}, b_{2}\} \cup b_{U}$ is not a set of linearly independent vetors, a contradiction).

Thus we conclude $\dim (V \diagup U)= \dim(V)  - \dim(U)$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!lemma]
>
>If $T: V \to U$ is a bijective linear map and $V$ be finite dimensional, then $\dim (V) = \dim(U)$.


**Proof.** Let $v \in V$ and $B_{V}=\{b_{1}, \dots, b_{n}\}$ where $n=\dim V$, then $v=\sum_{i=1}^{n} \alpha_{i}b_{i}$ for some set of scalars. Let $u \in U$, since $T$ is bijective then $\exists v \in V$ s.t. $T(v)=u$. Substituting $v$ for a linear combination of basis vectors, we have $T(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}T(b_{i})=u$ from linearity of $T$, so $T(B_{V})$ spans $U$, so $\dim U \leq \dim V$. Similarly, by considering the inverse bijection $T^{-1}$ (it is left as an exercise to the reader to demonstrate that $T^{-1}$ is also linear) and a basis $B_{U}$ of $U$, we find that $T^{-1}(B_{U})$ spans $V$, so $\dim V \leq \dim U$. So we conclude $\dim V = \dim U$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We can now prove the rank-nullity theorem (theorem 133 (rank-nullity theorem)) using the first isomorphism theorem:

**Proof.** Denote $\text{Domain}(T)=V$, thus the theorem can be restated as $V=\dim (\text{Ker}(T)) + \dim (\text{Im}(T))$.

Since $T$ is a linear transformation then $\forall v, w \in V$, $T(u+w)=T(u)+T(w)$, so it is a group homomorphism, so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) $V \diagup \text{Ker}(T) \cong \text{Im}(T)$. 

It is to show that $\psi: v \text{Ker}(T) \mapsto T(v)$ is a linear map ($(\alpha v + u)\text{Ker}(T) \mapsto T(\alpha v + u)=\alpha T (v)+ T(v)$ since $T$ is linear, and also $\alpha v \text{Ker}(T) \mapsto T(\alpha v)=\alpha T(v)$, so $\psi ((\alpha v + u) \text{Ker}(T)) = \alpha \psi(v \text{Ker}(T)) + \psi(u \text{Ker}(T))$, so $\psi$ is linear), and $\psi$ is the mapping under which $V \diagup \text{Ker}(T) \cong \text{Im}(T)$ (as per the proof of theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem)), then it is by lemma 138 , $\dim (V \diagup \text{Ker}(T)) = \dim (\text{Im}(T))$.

By proposition 137 , $\dim (V \diagup \text{Ker}(T)) = \dim(V) - \dim (\text{Ker}(T))$, so $\dim(V) - \dim(\text{Ker}(T)) = \dim(\text{Im}(T))$. Rearranging the final expression, we get $\dim(V)=\dim(\text{Ker}(T)) + \dim(\text{Im}(T))$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

# Direct Product

Let $(G, \cdot), (H, \times)$ be groups and consider their cartesian product $G \times H$, whose elements are the tuples $(g, h)$ where $g \in G, h \in H$. It is natural to define a binary operator on $G \times H$ as follows: $\star: (G \times H)^{2} \to G \times H, (g_{1}, h_{1}) \star (g_{2}, h_{2}) \mapsto (g_{1} \cdot g_{2}, h_{1} \times h_{2})$, i.e. the operation on the product set is realized by applying the respective group operation on each component. We can verify that the resulting structure $(G \times H, \star)$ is indeed a group whose identity element is $(e_{G}, e_{H})$. We call this group the *external direct product* of $G$ and $H$. We present a more generalized definition below, and then proceed to show it is indeed a group:

>[!definition] external direct product
>
>Suppose $\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1}, \dots, G_{n}\}$ is a finite collection of groups, then the structure $(\prod_{i=1}^{n} G_{i}, \cdot)$, where:
>1. $\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}=G_{1} \times \dots \times G_{n}$, i.e. it is the cartesian product of all underlying sets
>2. $\cdot: (\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{i})^{2} \to \prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}$ is a binary operation on the product set, given by $(g_{1}, \dots, g_{n}) \cdot (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n})=(g_{1}h_{1}, \dots, g_{n}h_{n})$, where each $g_{i}h_{i}$ term is the result of applying the group operation of $G_{i}$ on $g_{i}, h_{i} \in G_{i}$.
>Is called the *external direct product* on $\mathcal{G}$. We denote it simply as $G_{1} \times \dots \times G_{n}$ or $\\times_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}$, via abuse of notation.


>[!proposition]
>
>1. The external direct product is a group
>2. The identity of the external direct product is the $n$-tuple whose $i$-th element is the identity of the $i$-th group in the product.
>3. The inverse of an element of the external direct product $a$ is the $n$-tuple $a^{-1}=(a_{1}^{-1}, \dots, a_{n}^{-1})$.


**Proof.** 

We show the structure defined in definition 139 (external direct product) satisfies the group axioms $\forall a, b, c \in \times_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}$:
1. (associativity) by definition $(a(bc))_{i}=a_{i}(b_{i}c_{i})$. By associativity of the group operation of $G_{i}$, $a_{i(b_{i})c_{i}}=(a_{i}b_{i})c_{i}$, which is exactly $((ab)c)_{i}$, so $a(bc)=(ab)c$.
2. (left identity) let $e=(e_{1}, \dots, e_{n})$ i.e. the $n$-tuple of identity elements of the underlying groups of the product, then by definition $(ea)_{i}=e_{i}a_{i}$, but since $e_{i}$ is the identity of $G_{i}$ then $e_{i}a_{i}=a_{i}$, so $ea=a$, so $e$ is the identity of the product.
3. (left inverse) let $a^{-1}=(a_{1}^{-1}, \dots, a_{n}^{-1})$, then by definition $(a^{-1}a)_{i}=a_{i}^{-1}a_{i}=e_{i}$, so $a^{-1}a=e$, so $a^{-1}$ is indeed the left inverse of $a$.

We conclude that the external direct product is a group and that sentences $(2)$ and $(3)$ of the proposition are true.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

If $H, K \leqslant G$, then we can consider the subset $HK = \{hk \in G | h \in H, k \in K \}$ under the group operation of $G$. Trivially we find that since $e \in H \cap K$ then $e \in HK$ (identity) and given $h_{1}k_{1}, h_{2}k_{2}, h_{3}k_{3} \in HK$, then $(h_{1}k_{1}h_{2}k_{2})h_{3}k_{3}=h_{1}k_{1}(h_{2}k_{2}h_{3}k_{3})$ (associativity) since $HK \subseteq G$ and the group operation is associative on $G$. However when we attempt to show that the resulting structure $(HK, \cdot)$ where $\cdot$ is the group operation of $G$ has the inverse property, we run into an issue: we want to find an inverse of $hk$ in $HK$. We know that $(hk)^{-1}=k^{-1}h^{-1}$, so we want $k^{-1}h^{-1} \in HK$ so we wish to find $h' \in H, k' \in K$ s.t. $h'k'=k^{-1}h^{-1}$, but this equation is not always solvable. However, if $H, K \unlhd G$, then we have $HK=KH$ and clearly since $k^{-1} \in K, h^{-1} \in H$, we find that $k^{-1}h^{-1} \in KH=HK$. This discussion motivates the following definition:

>[!definition] independent subgroups
>
>Let $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n} \leqslant G$ where $n \in \mathbb{N}, 2 \leq n$, then $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n}$ are *independent* if $\forall i \in \mathbb{N} \cap [1, n-1]$, $(\prod_{j=1}^{i}H_{j}) \cap H_{i+1} = \{e\}$ (we say that the intersection is *trivial*), where $\prod_{j=1}^{i}H_{j}=\{\prod_{j=1}^{i} h_{j} | h_{j} \in H_{j}\}$.


>[!definition] internal direct product
>
>Let $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n} \unlhd G$ be a finite set of independent normal subgroups , then the structure $(H_{1}\dots H_{n}, \cdot)$ where $H_{1}\dots H_{n}=\{h_{1}\dots h_{n} | h_{i} \in H_{i}\}$ and $\cdot$ is the group operation of $G$ is called the *internal direct product* of $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n}$ and is denoted $H_{1} \oplus \dots \oplus H_{n}$, or $\oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$. 

We have motivated why we are only interested in *normal* subgroups. The motivation behind the restriction that the subgroups by *independent* will become clearer later, when we will show that the internal direct product and the external direct product of the same subgroups are isomorphic.

>[!proposition]
>
>The internal direct product is a group.

**Proof.** The proof is identical to the proof presented for the case where $n=2$ in the discussion a few paragraphs above. Filling out the detail of the group for a general finite $n$ is left to the reader. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now prove a lemma towards the isomorphism theorem for external and internal direct product:

>[!lemma]
>
>Suppose $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n} \unlhd G$ and the intersection of any two $H_{i}, H_{j}, i \neq j$ is the identity (equivalently, suppose $\oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$ can be defined), then for all $a=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}$ and $b =\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}$, where $a_{i}, b_{i} \in H_{i}$ for all $i \in \mathbb{N} \cap [1, n]$:
>1.  If $a=b$, then for all such $i$, $a_{i}=b_{i}$.
>2.  $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\prod_{i=n}^{1}a_{i}$ (i.e. for $n=3$, we have $a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}=a_{3}a_{2}a_{1}$)


**Proof.** We prove both propositions by induction on $n$. For the direct product to be defined we must choose $n$ at least $2$, so our base case will be $n=2$.

$1$. Suppose $n=2$, then $a_{1}a_{2}=b_{1}b_{2}$ where $a_{1}, b_{1} \in H_{1}, a_{2}b_{2} \in H_{2}$. Right-multiplying by $a_{1}^{-1}$ and left-multiplying by $b_{2}^{-1}$, we get

$$
\begin{gathered}
a_{1}^{-1}(a_{1}a_{2})b_{2}^{-1}=a_{1}^{-1}(b_{1}b_{2})b_{2}^{-1} \\
(a_{1}^{-1}a_{1})a_{2}b_{2}^{-1}=a_{1}^{-1}b_{1}(b_{2}b_{2}^{-1}) \\
\underbrace{a_{2}b_{2}^{-1}}_{\in H_{2}}=\underbrace{a_{1}^{-1}b_{1}}_{\in H_{1}}
\end{gathered}
$$

So we have $a_{2}b_{2}^{-1} \in H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, and by supposition $H_{1} \cap H_{2} = \{e\}$, so $a_{2}b_{2}^{-1}=e$, so $a_{2}=b_{2}$. Similarly since we have $a_{1}^{-1}b_{1} \in H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, we have $a_{1}=a_{2}$, which completes the proof for the case where $n=2$.

Suppose the proposition holds for some $n$, and consider $n+1$, then we have 

$$
\begin{gathered}
\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}b_{i} \\
(\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})a_{n+1}=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i})b_{n+1}
\end{gathered}
$$

Note that $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}, \prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i} \in \oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$, so by letting $H=\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}, K=H_{n+1}$ we have $H, K \unlhd G$ and since all subgroups are independent, then by definition 141 (independent subgroups) $\oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \cap H_{n+1} = \{e\}$, so $H \cap K = \{e\}$, so denoting $h_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}, g_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i} \in H$ and $h_{2}=a_{n+1}, g_{2}=b_{n+1} \in K$, we have the $n=2$ case which we have already proven, so it follows that $h_{1}=h_{2}$ and $g_{1}=g_{2}$, i.e. $a_{n+1}=b_{n+1}$.

Consider $h_{1}=h_{2}$ and recall that this is just a change of notation for $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}$, so by the induction hypothesis $a_{i}=b_{i}$ for every $i \in \mathbb{N} \cap [1, n]$. Combining the two results together, we have that the proposition holds for $n+1$, so by induction the proposition is true.

Note that we didn't use normality here. This result applies to every set of independent subgroups.

$3$. For $n=2$, let $a_{1} \in H_{1}, a_{2} \in H_{2}$. Since $a_{1} \in H_{2} \subseteq G$, and $H_{1}$ is a normal subgroup, then by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $a_{2}H_{1}a_{2}^{-1}=H_{1}$, so $a_{2}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1} \in H_{1}$, so $a_{1}(a_{2}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1}) \in H_{1}$ (by closure of $H_{1}$ under the group operation). Similarly, since $H_{2} \unlhd G$ then $a_{1}H_{1}a_{1}^{-1}=H_{2}$, so $a_{1}a_{2}a_{1}^{-1} \in H_{2}$, so $(a_{1}a_{2}a_{1}^{-1})a_{2}^{-1} \in H_{2}$ (since $a_{2} \in H_{2} \therefore a_{2}^{-1} \in H_{2}$). Now by associativity, $a_{1}(a_{2}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1})=(a_{1}a_{2}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1})$, and as we shown this is an element of $H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, so by independency of the subgroups, $a_{1}a_{2}a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1}=e$, so $a_{1}a_{2}=a_{2}a_{1}$.

Suppose the proposition holds for some $n$ and consider $n+1$, again we observe that $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i} \in \oplus_{i=1}^{n} H_{i} \unlhd \oplus_{i=1}^{n+1}H_{i}$, so by denoting $h_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}, h_{2}=a_{n+1}$, this case is again reduced to the case where $n=2$ which we have already proven, so $h_{1}h_{2}=h_{2}h_{1}$, and by the induction hypotheiss $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\prod_{i=n}^{n}a_{i}$, so we have $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}=\prod_{i=n+1}^{1}a_{i}$, which concludes the proof by induction.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] direct products are isomorphic
>
>Let $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n} \unlhd G$ all indepdent (i.e. the internal direct product of all subgroups is defined), then their internal direct product is isomorphic to their external direct product:
>$$
>\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \cong \oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}
>$$


**Proof.** Denote $H_{\times}=\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}, \space H_{\oplus}=\oplus_{i=1}^{n} H_{i}$.

We define a natural homomorphism from the external inner product to the internal inner product as follows:

$$
\phi: H_{\times}\to H_{\oplus}, \space (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \mapsto \prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}
$$

Let $\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i} \in H_{\oplus}$, then by definition of $\phi$, $\phi(h_{1}, \dots, h_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}$, and $(h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \in H_{\times}$ as $h_{i} \in H_{i}$ for every $h_{i}$, so $\phi$ is surjective.

Let $a=(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}), b=(b_{1}, \dots, b_{n}) \in H_{\times}$.  If $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$, then $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}$, so by $(1)$ in lemma 144  $a_{i}=b_{i}$ for every $a_{i} \in H_{i}$, so $a=b$, so $\phi$ is injective. Since $\phi$ is also surjective, then it is a bijection. It remains to show it is a homomorphism.

To show $\phi$ is a homomorphism, we prove the following lemma by induction: if $H_{\oplus}$ is defined and $a=(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}), b = (b_{1}, \dots, b_{n}) \in H_{\times}$, then $(\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})(\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}b_{i})$.

For $n=2$ we have by $(2)$ in lemma 144  that $a_{1}a_{2}=a_{2}a_{1}$ and $b_{1}b_{2}=b_{2}b_{1}$, so $a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2} = a_{1}a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}$, and now we have $(a_{2}b_{2})b_{1} \in H_{2} \oplus H_{1}$ so again we have $(a_{2}b_{2})b_{1}=b_{1}(a_{2}b_{2})$, so $a_{1}a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}=a_{1}b_{1}a_{2}b_{2}$, so we conclude $a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}=a_{1}b_{1}a_{2}b_{2}$, so the proposition holds for $n=2$.

Suppose by induction that it holds for some $n$, and consider $n+1$, then we follow the usual trick of observing that by rewriting $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}$ as $\underbrace{(\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})}_{\in \oplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \unlhd G} \cdot \underbrace{a_{n+1}}_{\in H_{n+1} \unlhd G}$, then the case reduces to $n=2$, which we have already shown, so $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i} \prod_{i=1}^{n+1}b_{i}=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i} \prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i})(a_{n+1}b_{n+1})$, and by the induction hypothesis $\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}b_{i})$, so we have $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i} \prod_{i=1}^{n+1}b_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(a_{i}b_{i})$, which completes the proof by induction.

Consider $\phi(ab)=\phi(a_{1}b_{1}, \dots, a_{n}b_{n})$. By definition, $\phi(ab)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}b_{i})$. By the lemma we have just proven, $\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}b_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\prod_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=\phi(a)\phi(b)$, so $\phi$ is a homomorphism.

We find that $\phi$ is a bijective homomorphism, so it is an isomorphism, so $H_{\times} \cong H_{\oplus}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Consider the external direct product of $H_{1}, H_{2}, H_{3}$, given by $H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3} = \{(h_{1}, h_{2}, h_{3}) | h_{1} \in H_{1}, h_{2} \in H_{2}, h_{3} \in H_{3}\}$. If we think of the cartesian product as an $n$-ary operator, then trivially it is not associative since $H_{1} \times (H_{2} \times H_{3}) = \{(h_{1}, (h_{2}, h_{3})) | h_{i} \in H_{i}\}$ whereas $(H_{1} \times H_{2}) \times H_{3}=\{((h_{1}, h_{2}), h_{3})| h_{i} \in H_{i}\}$, and clearly the intersection of this two sets is the empty set. However when we consider the group structure of each group, we find that they are isomorphic to each other and to $H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3}$, so in the group theoretic sense we can think of the external direct product as associative. We show this partly in the following theorem:

>[!theorem] associativity of external direct product
>
>$$
>H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3} \cong (H_{1} \times H_{2}) \times H_{3} \cong H_{1} \times (H_{2} \times H_{3})
>$$


**Proof.**  First we show $H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3} \cong (H_{1} \times H_{2}) \times H_{3}$. Consider a mapping $\phi: (a, b, c) \mapsto ((a, b), c)$, it is easy to see it is a bijection, and also

$$
\begin{gathered}
\phi(a_{1}, b_{1}, c_{1}) \cdot \phi(a_{2}, b_{2}, c_{2})=\\
((a_{1}, b_{1}), c_{1}) \cdot ((a_{2}, b_{2}), c_{2}) =\\
((a_{1}a_{2}, b_{1}b_{2}), c_{1}c_{2})=\\
\phi((a_{1}, b_{1}, c_{1}) \cdot (a_{2}, b_{2}, c_{2}))
\end{gathered}
$$

So $\phi$ is a homomorphism, so it is an isomorphism, so $H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3} \cong (H_{1}, H_{2}) \times H_{3}$.

Similarly it can be shown that $H_{1} \times H_{2} \times H_{3} \cong H_{1} \times (H_{2}, \times H_{3})$ via $\phi: (a, b, c) \mapsto (a, (b, c))$, and since isomorphism is an equivalence relation then by transitivity we retrieve the original proposition.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] external direct product preserves isomorphism
>
>If $H \cong K$, then $H \times G \cong K \times G$, and also $G \times H \cong G \times K$.

**Proof.** Let $\phi: H \to K$ be an isomorphism, then it is easy to verify that $\psi: H \times G \to K \times G, (h, g) \mapsto (\phi(h), g)$ is an isomorphism. Similarly we can show that $G \times H \cong G \times K$ by considering $\tau: G \times H \to G \times K, (g, h) \mapsto (g, \phi(h))$ and showing $\tau$ is an isomorphism. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!theorem]
>
>If $(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}) \in \times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$, and the order of each $a_{i} \in H_{i}$ is finite, then $|(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})|=\text{lcm}(|a_{1}|, \dots, |a_{n}|)$.


**Proof.** Let $m=\text{lcm}(|a_{1}|, \dots, |a_{n}|)$, then $(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})^{m}=(a_{1}^m, \dots, a_{n}^{m})$. Since $|a_{i}| \space \big | \space m$ for every $a_{i}$, then $a{i}^{m}=e_{i}$, so $(a_{1}^{m}, \dots, a_{n}^{m})=(e_{1}, \dots, e_{n})$, which by proposition 140  is the identity of $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$, so $|(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})| \leq m$. Suppose $(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})^{k}=(e_{1}, \dots, e_{n})$ for some $k$, then $a_{i}^{k}=e_{i}$ for every $i$, so $|a_{i}| \space \big | \space k$, so $k$ is a common divisor of $(|a_{1}|, \dots, |a_{n}|)$, so $m \leq k$, so we conclude $|(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})|=m$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!theorem]
>
>If $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$ is defined and $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n}$ are finite cyclic groups, then $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$ is cyclic if and only if the orders of the cyclic subgroups are pairwise coprime.


**Proof.** If $H_{1}, \dots, H_{n}$ are cyclic, then for generators $h_{1} \in H_{1}, \dots, h_{n} \in H_{n}$, we have that every $a \in H_{i}$ can be written as $h_{i}^{k_{i}}$ for some $k_{i} \in \mathbb{N}$. Consider $\langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \rangle \leqslant \times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$, it is a cyclic subgroup of the external direct product. Note that $|\langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \rangle |= |(h_{1}, \dots, h_{n})|$ by proposition 41 (properties of a cyclic group), and $|(h_{1}, \dots, h_{n})|= \text{lcm}(|h_{1}|, \dots, |h_{n}|)$ by theorem 148 , and also $| \langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) = | \leq |\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \iff \langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \rangle = \times_{i=1}^{n} H_{i}$ since it is a subgroup of that group.

Finally by counting we note that for $a \in \times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$, there are $|H_{i}|=|h_{i}|$ distinct choices for each $a_{i}$, so in total $|\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}|h_{i}|$.

$\implies$We prove by induction on $n$.

For $n=2$, suppose $H_{1} \times H_{2}$ is cyclic, so $\langle (a_{1}, a_{2}) \rangle = H_{1} \times H_{2}$ for some $a_{1} \in H_{1}, a_{2} \in H_{2}$, so $|(a_{1}, a_{2})|=|H_{1} \times H_{2}|=|H_{1}| \cdot |H_{2}|$ .

Denote $m=|H_{1}| \cdot |H_{2}|$, and consider $d=\text{gcd}(|H_{1}|, |H_{2}|)$, then $\frac{|H_{i}|}{d} \in \mathbb{N}$ and so trivially $\frac{m}{d} \in \mathbb{N}$, so $(a_{1}, a_{2})^{\frac{m}{d}}$ is defined, so:

$$
(a_{1}, a_{2})^{\frac{m}{d}}=(a_{1}^{\frac{m}{d}}, a_{2}^{\frac{m}{d}})=((a_{1}^{|H_{1}|})^{\frac{|H_{2}|}{d}}, (a_{2}^{|H_{2}|})^{\frac{|H_{1}|}{d}})=(*)
$$
By theorem 46 , we know that $|a_{i}| \space \big | \space |H_{i}|$, so $a_{i}^{|H_{i}|}=e_{i}$, so

$$
(*)=(e_{1}^{\frac{|H_{2}|}{d}}, e_{2}^{\frac{|H_{1}|}{d}})=(e_{1}, e_{2}) = e_{H_{1} \times H_{2}}
$$
So it follows that $|(a_{1}, a_{2})| \leq \frac{m}{d}$, but also $|(a_{1}, a_{2})|=m$ by supposition, so $\frac{m}{d} \leq m$, so $d=1$, so $\text{gcd}(|H_{1}|, |H_{2}|)=1$, so $|H_{1}|, |H_{2}|$ are coprime.

Suppose by induction the proposition is true for some $n$ (i.e. that for this $n$, $\text{gcd}(|H_{i}|, |H_{j}|)=1$ for any $H_{i} \neq H_{j}$), and consider $n+1$. 

By definition, $\times_{i=1}^{n+1}H_{i} = \times_{i=1}^{n} H_{i} \times H_{n+1}$. Again we use our usual trick of reducing this case to the case where $n=2$: by theorem 146 (associativity of external direct product) we have $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \times H_{n+1} \cong (\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}) \times H_{n+1}$. By theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism), we know that $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i} \times H_{n+1}$ is cyclic if and only if $(\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}) \times H_{n+1}$ is cyclic, but $(\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}) \times H_{n+1}$ is simply the direct product of two groups so it reduces to the case where $n=2$ which we have already covered, so $\text{gcd}(|\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}|, |H_{n+1}|)=1$, but by the counting argument $|\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}|H_{i}|$, so $\text{gcd}(\prod_{i=1}^{n}|H_{i}|, |H_{n+1}|)=1$, so it must be that $\forall i \in \mathbb{N} \cap[1, n] (\text{gcd}(|H_{i}|, |H_{n+1}|)=1)$ (suppose not and denote the non-unit divisor $d$, then $d$ must also be the divisor of $\prod_{i=1}^{n}|H_{i}|$, so $\text{gcd}(\prod_{i=1}^{n}|H_{i}|, |H_{n+1}|) \gt 1$, a contradiction), so $|H_{n+1}|$ is coprime w.r.t. any the order of any other cyclic subgroup $H_{i}$. For any pair of the form $(|H_{i}|, |H_{j}|)$ where $i, j \neq n+1$, we know that it is a pair of coprimes by the induction hypothesis, thus we complete the proof by induction.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $|H_{1}|, \dots, |H_{n}|$ are all pairwise coprime, then $|h_{1}|, \dots, |h_{n}|$ are coprime, so $\text{lcm}(|h_{1}|, \dots, |h_{n}|)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}|h_{i}|$, so $|\langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \rangle| = \prod_{i=1}^{n}|h_{i}|$, so we have $|\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}|=|\langle (h_{1}, \dots, h_{n}) \rangle|$, so $\times_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}$ is a cyclic group with generator $(h_{1}, \dots, h_{n})$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] decomposition of modular addition group
>
>Let $k=\prod_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}$ (where each $m_{i} \in \mathbb{N}$), then $\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{+} \cong \times_{i=1}^{n} \mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}$ if and only if all elements of $m_{1}, \dots, m_{n}$ are pairwise relatively prime.


**Proof.** $\implies$ Suppose $\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{+} \cong \times_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}$, then since $\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{+}$ is cyclic (with generator $1$) then by theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism) $\times_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}$ is also cyclic, and all of $\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}$ are finite since all $m_{i} \in \mathbb{N}$, so by theorem 149  it follows that all $m_{1}, \dots, m_{n}$ are relatively prime.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $m_{i}, \dots, m_{n}$ are pairwise coprime, then by theorem 149  $\times_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}$ is cyclic, and by counting $|\times_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}|\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}=k$, so by theorem 129 (cyclic groups are isomorphic to modular addition groups of integers of same order) it follows that $\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{+} \cong \times_{i=1}^{n} \mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}^{+}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use this result, along with theorem 148 , to count the number of subgroups of a finite cyclic group with order $k$, via the process demonstrated in the following example:

>[!example] subgroups of order $3$ in cyclic group with order $138$
>
>First denote $138=3\cdot 46$, and $3, 46$ are relatively prime, so if $G$ is cyclic and $|G|=138$ then by corollary 150 (decomposition of modular addition group), $G \cong \mathbb{Z}_{3}^{+} \times \mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}$. Let $\phi$ be a group isomorphism between the isomorphic groups and let $H \leqslant G$, then since $G$ is cyclic by theorem 52 (fundamental theorem of cyclic groups) $H$ is cyclic, then $a \in H$ is a generator of $H$, i.e. $H=\langle a \rangle$, and by theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism) $\phi(\langle a \rangle)$ is the generator of a subgroup with the same order in $\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{+} \times \mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}$. Denote this generator $(q, p)$, where $q \in \mathbb{Z}_{3}^{+}, p \in \mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}$. By theorem 148  we know that $|(q, p)|=\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)$. Since $H \cong \langle (q, p) \rangle$, then $|H|=\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)$. We are looking for subgroups of $G$ with order $3$, so we can convert the problem to counting the total number of distinct elements $(q, p)$ s.t. $\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)=3$, which greatly simplifies the problem.
>
>Suppose then that $\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)=3$, and consider the following cases:
>1. $q=e$, then $|q|=1$, then $\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)=3 \iff |p|=3$, but since $p \in \mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}$ and $|\mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}|=46$ and by a previous result $|p|$ must divide $|\mathbb{Z}_{46}^{+}|$ and yet $(3, 46)$ are coprime, then there are no such elements.
>2. $p=e$, then $|p|=1$, then $\text{lcm}(|q|, |p|)=1 \iff |q|=3$, and since $q \in \mathbb{Z}_{3}^{+}$ which has primer order, by corollary 50  all non-identity elements if $\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{+}$ generate the group and so have order $3$, so this case contributes $2$ total generators.
>3. $p \neq e$ and $q \neq e$, in which case it must be that both $|q|, |p|$ are multiples of $3$ and also greater than $1$, but $3$ and $46$ are coprime so it is impossible that $1 \lt |p|$ and also $3 \space \big | \space |p|$, so there are no such cases.
>   
> We conclude that if $G$ is cyclic with order $138$, it has *exactly* two subgroups of order $3$.
>

It can further be shown that all groups under modular multiplication (i.e. $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\times}$) can be expressed as the external direct product of finite cyclic groups. We show this in [RSA Encryption](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/rsa_encryption.pdf).

## Second Isomorphism Theorem

In the previous section we discussed the internal and external direct products, and we defined the internal direct product such that, given independent normal subgroups of $G$, their internal direct product is also a subgroup of $G$. We mentioned that the requirement that the subgroups be independent is not needed for the product to be a subgroup.  Let us relex our definition further and examine the case where $H_{1}, H_{2} \leqslant G$ but neither are necessarily normal subgroups of $G$.

>[!definition] product of two subgroups
>
>If $H_{1}, H_{2} \leqslant G$, then their *product* is the structure $(H_{1}H_{2}, \cdot)$ where $H_{1}H_{2}=\{a \cdot b | a \in H_{1}, b \in H_{2}\}$ and $\cdot$ is the group operation of $G$.


This is similar to the definition of the internal direct product but without the condition that $H_{1}, H_{2}$ be normal subgroups nor independent.

>[!definition] normalizer of a subgroup
>
>If $H \leqslant G$, then the *normalizer* of $H$ in $G$, denoted $N_{G}(H)$, is given by $N_{G}(H)=\{g \in G| gHg^{-1}=H\}$.


The motivation behind the name is that the normalizer *normalizes* $H$ as a normal subgroup of itself, as we show in the following:

>[!theorem] properties of the normalizer
>
>1. $N_{G}(H) \leqslant G$
>2. $H \unlhd N_{G}(H)$
>3. If $H \unlhd G$, then $N_{G}(H)=G$
>4. $N_{G}(H)$ is the maximal (largest) subgroup of $G$ s.t. $H \unlhd N_{G}(H)$


**Proof.** 

$1$. First observe $e \in N_{G}(H)$ since $eHe^{-1}=H$, so $N_{G}(H)$ is a nonempty subset of $G$. We proceed by the one-step subgroup test: let $a, b \in N_{G}(H)$ and consider $ab^{-1}$. Consider that $(ab^{-1})H(ab^{-1})^{-1}=(ab^{-1})H(ba^{-1})=a(b^{-1}Hb)a^{-1}$, now since $b \in N_{G}(H)$ then trivially also $b^{-1}Hb=H$, so we have $a(b^{-1}Hb)a^{-1}=aHa^{-1}$, and since $a \in N_{G}(H)$ then $aHa^{-1}=H$, so $ab^{-1} \in N_{G}(H)$, so by the one-step subgroup test $N_{G}(H) \leqslant G$.

$2$. Let $h \in H$, then $hH=H$ by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) and also $Hh^{-1}=H$, so $hHh^{-1}=H$, so $h \in N_{G}(H)$, and also $H, N_{G}(H)$ are groups with the same operation, so $H \leqslant N_{G}(H)$.

To show $H$ is a normal subgroup of $N_{G}(H)$, let $n \in N_{G}(H)$. By proposition 111  it suffices to show $nHn^{-1} \subseteq H$ to show $H \unlhd N_{G}(H)$. Consider $nhn^{-1}$, then since $n \in N_{G}(H)$ by definition 153 (normalizer of a subgroup) it follows that $nhn^{-1} = h' \in H$, so we conclude $H \unlhd N_{G}(H)$.

$3$. Suppose $H \unlhd G$, then $\forall g \in G(gH=Hg)$, so $\forall g \in G (gHg^{-1}=H)$, so $N_{G}(H)=G$ by the membership test of $N_{G}(H)$.

$4$. Suppose by contradiction $N_{G}(H)$ is not maximal, then $\exists K \leqslant G$ s.t. $H \unlhd K$ and $|N_{G}(H)| \lt |K|$. From normality of $K$, $\forall k \in K$ it follows that $kHk^{-1} =H$, but then by definition 153 (normalizer of a subgroup) $k \in Z_{G}(H)$, so $K \subseteq N_{G}(H)$, but then $|K| \leq |N_{G}(H)|$, a contradiction, so $N_{G}(H)$ is maximal.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Via this properties of the normalizer, we can prove sufficient conditions for the product of two subgroups, as defined in definition 152 (product of two subgroups), to be a group:

>[!theorem]
>
>If:
>1. $G$ is a group
>2. $H_{1}, H_{2} \leqslant G$
>3. $H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$
>
>Then $H_{1}H_{2} \leqslant G$ (here $H_{1}H_{2}$ is the product as defined in definition 152 (product of two subgroups))


**Proof.** Suppose all conditions are satisfied. First, note that $e \in H_{1}, H_{2}$ so $ee=e \in H_{1}H_{2}$, and also $H_{1}H_{2} \subseteq G$. We proceed via the one-step subgroup test: let $a_{1}b_{1}, a_{2}b_{2} \in H_{1}H_{2}$ s.t. $a_{i} \in H_{1}, b_{i} \in H_{2}$, then

$$
(a_{1}b_{1})(a_{2}b_{2})^{-1}=(a_{1}b_{1})(b_{2}^{-1}a_{2}^{-1}) = a_{1}\underbrace{(b_{1}b_{2}^{-1})}_{\in H_{2}, \text{denote $b_{12}$}}a_{2}^{-1} = a_{1}b_{12}a_{2}^{-1}=(*)
$$

Since $H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$, then by definition $a_{1}b_{12}a_{1}^{-1}=b_{3}$ for some $b_{3} \in H_{2}$. Post-multiplying both sides by $a_{1}$, we get $a_{1}b_{12}=b_{3}a_{1}$. Substituting for $(*)$, we get

$$
(*)=b_{3} \cdot \underbrace{a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}}_{\in H_{1}, \text{ denote }a_{12}}
$$
By the same argument we have $a_{12}^{-1} b_{3} a_{12} = b_{4}$ for some $b_{4} \in H_{2}$, so $b_{3}a_{12}=a_{12}b_{4}$, which is clearly in $H_{1}H_{2}$ by definition, so $(a_{1}b_{1})(a_{2}b_{2})^{-1} \in H_{1}H_{2}$, so $H_{1}H_{2} \leqslant G$ by the one-step subgroup test.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We proceed with a couple of corollaries to the theorem:

>[!corollary]
>
>If all conditions of theorem 155  are satisfied, then:
>1. $H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}H_{2}$
>2. $H_{1} \cap H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}$


**Proof.** 

$1$. $H_{2} \leqslant H_{1}H_{2}$ is immediate from $H_{1}H_{2}, H_{2}$ being groups and from the definition of the product group, so it remains to show $(ab)H_{2}(ab)^{-1} \in H_{2}$ for every $a \in H_{1}, b \in H_{2}$. Let $h \in H_{2}$, then $(ab)h(ab)^{-1}=(ab)h(b^{-1}a^{-1})=a(bhb^{-1})a^{-1}$. Since $b, h \in H_{2}$ then from closure of $H_{2}$, $bhb^{-1} \in H_{2}$, now since $bhb^{-1} \in H_{2}$ and $b \in H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$, it follows that $a(bhb^{-1})a^{-1} \in H_{2}$, so we conclude that $H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}H_{2}$.

$2$. By proposition 34 (intersection of subgroups is a subgroup) we know that $H_{1} \cap H_{2} \leqslant H_{1}$, so it remains to show it is a normal subgroup. We need to show $a(H_{1} \cap H_{2})a^{-1} \subseteq H_{1} \cap H_{2}$ for every $a \in H_{1}$. Let $b \in H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, then $b \in H_{1}$, so $aba^{-1} \in H_{1}$ from closure. Also, since $a \in H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$, and $b \in H_{1} \cap H_{2} \therefore b \in H_{2}$, then $aba^{-1} \in H_{2}$. We conclude $aba^{-1} \in H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, so $H_{1} \cap H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}$. 

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

This leads us directly to the second isomorphism theorem:

>[!theorem] second isomorphism theorem
>
>If:
>1. $G$ is a group
>2. $H_{1}, H_{2} \leqslant G$
>3. $H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$
>   
>Then:
>1. $H_{1}H_{2} \leqslant G$
>2. $H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}H_{2}$
>3. $H_{1} \cap H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}$
>4. $H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2} \cong H_{1} \diagup (H_{1} \cap H_{2})$


**Proof.** The first proposition in the theorem is covered by theorem 155 , and propositions $2, 3$ are exactly corollary 156 . For $4$, consider a mapping $\phi: H_{1} \to H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2}$, given by $\phi: h_{1} \mapsto h_{1}H_{2}$. Note that since $H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}H_{2}$, the codomain is well-defined.

Let $a, b \in H_{1}$, then $\phi(ab)=abH_{2}=(aH_{2})(bH_{2})=\phi(a)\phi(b)$ so $\phi$ is a homomorphism (note that $abH_{2}=(aH_{2})(bH_{2})$ since this is how we defined the group operation of a factor group). 

We show $\phi$ is surjective. Let $(h_{1}h_{2})H_{2} \in H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2}$. Since $h_{2} \in H_{2}$, then $(h_{1}h_{2})H_{2}=h_{1}H_{2}$ where $h_{1} \in H_{1}$, so for $\phi(h_{1})$ we get $\phi(h_{1})=h_{1}H_{2}$, so $\phi$ is surjective, so $\text{Im}(\phi)=H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2}$. 

Consider $\text{Ker}(\phi)$. By definition $x \in \text{Ker}(\phi) \iff \phi(x)=H_{2}$ (which is the identity on the codomain), but also $\phi(x)=xH_{2}$, so we require $xH_{2}=H_{2}$. This is true if and only if $x \in H_{2}$, but $x \in H_{1}$ since $x$ is taken from the domain of $\phi$, so this is true if and only if $x \in H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=H_{1} \cap H_{2}$.

Thus by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) since $\phi$ is surjective and $\text{Ker}(\phi)=H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, we have $H_{1} \diagup (H_{1} \cap H_{2}) \cong H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2}$, which completes the proof.

Note that we did not use proposition $(3)$ in the theorem in the proof of $(4)$, so by corollary 121  and $(4)$, we immediately get $(3)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>



>[!corollary] product formula for normal subgroups
>
>If $H_{1}, H_{2} \unlhd G$ are finite, then $|H_{1}H_{2}|=\frac{|H_{1}| \space |H_{2}|}{|H_{1} \cap H_{2}|}$. (in fact, it suffices that $H_{1}, H_{2} \leqslant G$ and $H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$).


**Proof.** By the isomorphism from the last proposition in theorem 157 (second isomorphism theorem), and
theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Using this theorem, we can prove the following result:

>[!theorem]
>
>If $G$ is a finite abelian group with order divisible by a prime $p$, then $\exists a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=p$.


**Proof.** Suppose $G$ has order divisible by a prime $p$, so $|G|=np$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Since a group is nonempty then $n \neq 0$. We proceed by induction on $n$.

If $n=1$, then $|G|=p$, so by corollary 103  it follows that $G$ is cyclic, so $G=\langle a \rangle$ for some $a \in G$, so $|G|=|\langle a \rangle|=|a|=p$.

Suppose the proposition holds up to some $n$, and consider the case where $|G|=(n+1)p$. Since $G$ is composite, then by theorem 105 (every group of composite order has proper nontrivial cyclic subgroup) $G$ admits a proper subgroup $H \leqslant G$ s.t. $|H| \lt |G| = (n+1)p$. We choose a maximal such $H$ (i.e. $H$ is the proper subgroup of $G$ of greatest order, which must exist since $G$ is finite).

Consider $H$, there are two cases:
1. $p \space \big | \space |H|$, in which case by the induction hypothesis $\exists a \in H$ s.t. $|a|=p$, and also $a \in G$ since $H \leqslant G$, which completes the proof in this case.
2. $p \space \not \big | \space |H|$, in which case consider some $x \in G \setminus H$ (one must exist since $H$ is proper). This $x$ generates a cyclic group $\langle x \rangle \leqslant G$, and this cyclic group is also proper nontrivial since $H \neq \emptyset$ and $e \in H$ so $x \neq e$. Consider $h \in H$. Consider the internal direct product $\langle a \rangle \oplus H$. Since $G$ is abelian, then $H, \langle a \rangle \unlhd G$ so by corollary 158 (product formula for normal subgroups) it follows that $|H \langle a \rangle| =\frac{|H| \space |a|}{|H \cap \langle a \rangle|}$. Since by assumption $a \notin H$ then $H \cap \langle a \rangle = \{e\}$ so $|H\langle a \rangle|=|H| \space |a|$ (note - since $H, \langle a \rangle$ are independent we could have also used theorem 145 (direct products are isomorphic) to derive this relation). Consider two cases:
	1. $H \langle a \rangle$ is proper nontrivial. Since $|H| = \frac{|H \langle a \rangle|}{|a|}$ and $|H|$ is maximal, it must be that $|a|=1$ (otherwise $H \langle a \rangle$ would violate the maximality of $H$), then $|H|=|H \langle a \rangle|$, but then $a \in H$, a contradiction, so $H \langle a \rangle$ cannot be a proper subgroup.
	2. $H \langle a \rangle = G$, in which case $|G|= |H| \space |a|$, so $|H| \space |a| = (n+1)p$. By supposition, $p \space \not \big | \space |H|$, so by Euclid's lemma $p \big | \space |a|$, so by the induction hypothesis since $\langle a \rangle$ is a proper nontrivial subset of $G$ it follows that $\exists s \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $|a^{s}|=p$, which completes the proof.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We present an application to the second isomorphism theorem below. Consider the following definitions, most of which you should already be familiar with:
1. $\text{GL}_{n}(V)$ the *general linear group* of degree $n$ over the vector space $V$, which is the group of invertible $n\times n$ matrices whose entries are elements in $V$. We have demonstrated earlier that $\text{GL}_{n}(V)$ is a group.
2. $\text{SL}_{n}(V)$ the *special linear group* of degree $n$ over the vector space $V$, which is the group of $n \times n$ matrices with entries in $V$ that have determinant $1$. By theorem 31 (two-step subgroup test) it is trivial to see that $\text{SL}_{n}(V) \leqslant \text{GL}_{n}(V)$.
3. $Z_{n}(V)$ the group of *scalar transformations* over $V$, i.e. the set of all nonzero scalar $n \times n$ matrices of the form $Z=vI$ for some $v \in V$. It is trivial to see that $\text{Z}_{n}(V) \leqslant \text{GL}_{n}(V)$.
4. $\text{SZ}_{n}(V)$ the group of *special scalar transformations* over $V$, i.e. the set of all $n \times n$ scale matrices with determinant $1$.

Then, for $V=\mathbb{C}$:
1. $\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})=\{cI | c \in \mathbb{C} \setminus \{0\}\}$
2. $\text{SZ}_{n}(\mathbb{C})=\{\pm I\}$
3. $\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \cap \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})=\{\pm I\}=\text{SZ}_{n}$
4. The internal direct product $\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})Z_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ is exactly $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$
5. $\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \unlhd \text{GL}_{n}(C)$, since from linear algebra for every $cI \in \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}), A \in \text{GL}_{n}(V)$, we have $A^{-1}(cI)A=(A^{-1})c(IA)=cA^{-1}A=cI$, so $\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ is normal in $\text{GL}_{n}(V)$.
6. $\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \leqslant \text{N}_{G}(\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))$, since $\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ is normal in $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ so by theorem 154 (properties of the normalizer), the normalizer of $\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ in $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$ is the entire group, and when recalling the definitions we noted that $\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \leqslant \text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$. 

So, letting $H_{2}=\text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}), H_{1}=\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$, $G=\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$, all conditions of the second isomorphism theorem hold and we get:

$$
\begin{gathered}
(\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})) \diagup \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \cong \text{SL}_{n}(V) \diagup (\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \cap \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))\\
\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \diagup \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \cong \text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \diagup \text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
\end{gathered}
$$

In projective geometry, we *define* the *complex projective linear group* $\text{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$, as  $\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \diagup \text{Z}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$, and the *complex projective special linear group* $\text{PSL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$, as $\text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}) \diagup \text{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})$. The second isomorphism theorem provies the following results:
1. Both definitions are well-defined.
2. Both definitions are equivalent in the sense that the groups they define are isomorphic.
 
## Third Isomorphism Theorem

The third isomorphism theorem tells us that factoring a group further does not teach us anything new about its structure:

>[!theorem] third isomorphism theorem
>
>If $G$ is a group and $K \unlhd H \unlhd G$, then
>$$
>(G \diagup K) \diagup (H \diagup K) \cong G \diagup H
>$$


**Proof.**  Consider a mapping $\phi: G \diagup K \to G \diagup H$, given by $\phi(gK) \mapsto gH$. First, we show $\phi$ is single-valued: let $g_{1}K=g_{2}K$, then $\phi(g_{1}K)=g_{1}H, \phi(g_{2}K)=g_{2}H$. Since $g_{1}K=g_{2}K$ then $g_{1}g_{2}^{-1} \in K$, and since $K \unlhd H$ then $g_{1}g_{2}^{-1} \in H$, so $g_{2}H=g_{2}H$ (we use theorem 99 (properties of a coset)), so $\phi(g_{1}K)=\phi(g_{2}K)$ for any representatives $g_{1}K, g_{2}K$ of a congruence class of $G \diagup K$, o $\phi$ is single-valued, so it is a function.

Let $gH \in G \diagup H$, then $\phi(gK)=gH$ by definition of $\phi$, so $\phi$ is surjective. Furthermore, via the definition of the group operation of the factor group, $\phi(g_{1}K)\phi(g_{2}K)=(g_{1}H)(g_{2}H)=(g_{1}g_{2})H=\phi((g_{1}g_{2})K)=\phi((g_{1}K)(g_{2}K))$, so $\phi$ is a homomorphism.

Finally, let $xK \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $\phi(xK)=H$ and also by definition $\phi(xK)=xH$, so $xH=H$, which is true if and only if $x \in H$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=H \diagup K$ (which also immediately gives us that $H \diagup K \unlhd G \diagup K$ by corollary 121 ), so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) $(G \diagup H) \diagup (H \diagup K) \cong G \diagup H$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Isomorphism Theorems as Commutative Diagrams

The first three isomorphism theorems provide a good setting for the introduction of commutative diagrams. One of the objects studied in category theory, commutative diagrams are a way to represent the relation between objects under morphisms. A commutative diagram is drawn as a drawing of a directed graph, where the objects correspond to vertices and the morphisms correspond to edges.

A commutative diagram *commutes* in the sense that every two paths that share the same start and finish, start and end with the same result. We will not formally discuss commutative diagrams here, but only touch on them briefly as they provide a nice way of visualizing the isomorphism theorems for groups.

First, a warm up. Suppose $G, H, K$ are groups s.t. $\phi: G \to H$ is an isomorphism and $\psi: H \to K$ is a homomorphism, then $\tau: G \to K$ given by $\psi \circ \phi$ is also a homomorphism. This result can be expressed in the following commutative diagram:

<TikzSvg80fbe72ff3f6642b01b3109629b45e97 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>


We read the diagram as follows: a straight arrow ($\to$) represents a morphism. In our case we know what the morphisms are so we denote them via their symbol over the arrow. If the morphism is an isomorphism. we indicate this with a tilde (~) over the arrow representing the morphism. The diagram says that if we follow along edges from $G$ to $K$, we get the same result no matter which path we take - indeed, by following the edge $\phi$ to $H$ and then following the edge $\psi$, we arrive at $K$ (via a homomorphism), and we arrive at the same homomorphism via following the arrow from $G$ to $K$ which represents the composition $\phi \circ \psi$ - this is pretty much the definition of a composition of functions.

### First Isomorphism Theorem

We now turn our attention to a more exciting example, the first isomorphism theorem. Recall that the first isomorphism theorem states the following:

*If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a group homomorphism, then $\text{Ker}(\phi) \unlhd G_{1}, \phi(G_{1}) \leqslant G_{1}$, and $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(G_{1})$.*

To represent the theorem in a commutative diagram, we need to introduce a few new arrow types. In total, we use the following arrow:
1. $A \overset{\phi}{\to} B$ means that $A$ is homomorphic to $B$ via $\phi: A \to B$, and if $A \cong B$ we denote $A \overset{\sim}{\to}B$.
2. $A \twoheadrightarrow B$ means that there is a surjective homomorphism from $A$ to $B$. Here we use this to denote that $B$ is a factor group of $A$ over some normal subgroup.
3. $A \hookrightarrow B$ means that $\phi: A \to B$ is an injective homomorphism (monomorphism), we usually use this to indicate $A \leqslant B$ (since then $\text{id}_{A}$ is an injective homomorphism from $A$ to $B$)

We can then state the first homomorphism theorem as follows:

*If $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a group homomorphism. then the following graph commutes:*


<TikzSvgf0c4b91d16129851428d8266a076eb04 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>


We read the diagram as follows: if $\phi: G_{1} \to G_{2}$ is a homomorphism from $G_{1}$ to $\phi(G_{1})$ which is a subgroup of $G_{2}$, we can also go from $G_{1}$ to the quotient $G_{1} \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$, which will be isomorphic to $\phi(G_{1})$.

### Second Isomorphism Theorem

Recall theorem 157 (second isomorphism theorem). It can be restated by the following commutative diagram (although we should still mention all conditions of the theorem, since for example $H_{1} \leqslant N_{G}(H_{2})$ is not captured in this diagram), where we introduce a new two-lined arrow $A \implies B$ to denote normality (*$A$ is a normal subgroup of $B$*):

<TikzSvgce9b1e4b730ccc69c53b577221589a33 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

We read the diagram as follows:
1. $H_{1}H_{2} \leqslant G$
2. $H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}H_{2}$
3. $H_{1} \cap H_{2} \unlhd H_{1}$
4. $H_{1} \diagup (H_{1} \cap H_{2}) \cong H_{1}H_{2} \diagup H_{2}$ (this isomorphism is indicated by the $\cong$ symbol on the parallel edges $H_{1} \implies H_{1} \cap H_{2}$, $H_{2} \implies H_{1}H_{2}$)

Which is exactly what the second isomorphism theorem states.

Now that we have seen the second isomorphism theorem as a commutative diagram, it should come as no surprise that it is also called the *diamond theorem* or the *parallelogram theorem*.

### Third Isomorphism Theorem

Recall theorem 160 (third isomorphism theorem). We can restate it as follows:

*If all conditions of theorem 160 (third isomorphism theorem) are satisfied, the following diagram commutes:*



<TikzSvg419f88391700a5360eb9b400291a7675 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

Interpreting this diagram is left as an exercise to the reader.

## Group Action

Consider the dihedral group $D_{2n}$, which is the group of symmetries of a regular $n$-gon. We have seen in [Rigid Motions & Symmetry](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/rigid_motions.pdf) and earlier on in this discussion that $D_{2n}=\{\langle r, f \rangle \space \big| \space |r|=n, |f|=2, frf=r^{-1} \}$. Using this representation, we understand the dihedral group of order $2n$ by its group structure in the abstract sense, i.e. we forget the geometric motivation for $D_{2n}$ as the group of symmetries of a regular $n$-gon, and instead consider some abstract multiplicative group with elements $r, f$ and some rules (or relations) that govern the group (namely, the order of $r$, the order of $f$, and the relation $frf=r^{-1}$).

Recall that in our geometric motivation for $D_{2n}$, we thought of $r$ as a rotation by $\frac{2\pi}{n}$ radians, and as $f$ as a reflection through an axis of eflective symmetry. We have shown that these are in fact symmetries of the regular $n$-gon, and furthermore that their compositions are symmetries, and that we can realize all symmetries of the shape by the composition of only one reflection $f$ and one rotation $r$ (either CW or CCW).

More formally, what we did was show that for any $\sigma \in D_{2n}$, if $V_{n}$ is the set of vertices of the $n$-gon, then $\sigma(V_{n})$ *acts* on the vertices in a way that preserves the geometric structure of the $n$-gon, i.e. it produces a *symmetry*.

Note the distinction here: $D_{2n}$ is a *group* which we define in the abstract sense without any geometric meaning, and then we *define* how elements of the group *act* on a *set*, in this case *the vertices of a regular $n$-gon*, which formalizes the connection between the geometric motivation and the abstract algebraic structure.

What we have just demonstrated is a *group action* on the set $V_{n}$ by the group $D_{2n}$.

>[!definition] group action
>
>If $G$ is a group and $S$ is a set, then a mapping $\star: G \times S \to S$ is a *group action* if $\forall x \in S$:
>1. $\forall a, b \in G$, $a \star (b \star x) =  (ab) \star x$ (where $ab$ is group multiplication in $G$)
>2. $e \star x =x$, where $e$ is the identity of $G$.

>[!example] symmetric define a group action
>
>Let $S$ be a finite set and $G$ be a subgroup of $\text{Sym}(S)$ (i.e. the group of permutations, or the symmetric group, of $S$), then $\star: (\sigma, s) \mapsto \sigma(s)$ defines a group action:
>1. Clearly $\star$ is a mapping $G \times S \to S$.
>2. Take $\sigma_{a}, \sigma_{b} \in G$, then $(\sigma_{a} \sigma_{b}) \star (x) = (\sigma_{a} \circ \sigma_{b})(x)=\sigma_{a}(\sigma_{b}(x))=\sigma_{a} \star (\sigma_{b} \star x)$
>3. $\text{id}_{s} \star x = \text{id}_{s}(x)=x$

>[!example] group multiplication defines group action
>
>Let $H \leqslant G$, then $\star: H \times G \to G, h \star x \mapsto hx$ defines a group action:
>1. Let $h, g \in H$, then $hg \star x = hgx = h(gx) = h \star (gx) = h \star (g \star x)$.
>2. Let $e \in H$, then $e$ is the identity on $G$, so $e \star x = ex = x$.


The next theorem demonstrates that group action $G \times S \to S$ is really just another way of looking at a group homomorphism from a group $G$ to $\text{Sym}(S)$ of some set $S$.

>[!theorem] group actions are equivalent to group homomorphism to symmetric group
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $S$ be a set, then:
>1. If $\phi: G \to \text{Sym}(S)$ is a group homomorphism, then $a \star x \mapsto \phi(a)(x)$ is a group action
>2. If $\star: G \times S \to S$ is a group action, then $\star$ arises from a homomorphism $\phi: G \to \text{Sym}(S)$ as described in $(1)$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. Let $\phi: G \to \text{Sym}(S)$ be a group homomorphism, then we show $\star: G \times S \to S, a \star x \mapsto \phi(a)(x)$ is a group action:
1. First we verify the domain and co-domain are indeed correct. Since $\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant \text{Sym}(S)$ and $\text{Sym}(S)$ is the group of bijections $S \to S$, then $a \star x$ as given above is a mapping $G \times S \to S$.
2. Let $a, b \in G$, then $\phi(ab)=\phi(a) \phi(b)$ since $\phi$ is a group homomorphism. By definition, $a \star (b \star x)= a \star (\phi(b)(x)) = \phi(a)\phi(b)(x) = \phi(ab)(x) = (ab) \star x$.
3. Since $\phi$ is a group homomorphism then $\phi(e_{G})=\text{id}_{S}$, so $e_{G} \star x = \text{id}_{S}(x)=x$.

We conclude that $\phi$ realizes a group action.

$2$. Suppose $\star: G \times S \to S$ is a group action, and define $\phi: a \mapsto \star_{a}$ where $\star_{a}$ is the partial mapping of $\star$ where the first argument $(a \in G)$ is fixed. We wish to show that:
1. $\phi$ is a group homomorphism
2. $\phi$ is a mapping from $G$ to $\text{Sym}(S)$

Let $a, b \in G$ and let $x \in S$, then $\phi(ab) = \star_{ab}$, and since $\star$ is a group action then $\phi(ab)(x)=\star_{ab} x = (ab) \star x$, which by the properties of a group action is the same as $a \star (b \star x)$, which is the same as $\star_{x}(\star_{b}x)$, which by definition of $\phi$ is the same as $(\phi(a) \circ \phi(b))(x)$, so we have $\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)$, so $\phi$ is a group homomorphism.

We show $(2)$ by definition: $\phi(a) \in \text{Sym}(S)$ if and only if $\phi(a)$ is a bijective mapping from $S$ to itself. Let $a \in G$ and consider $\phi(a)$, then since $\phi(a)$ describes the action of $a$ on $S$, $\phi(a)$ is a mapping $S \to S$. Let $y \in S$.
1. (Surjective) Since $\star$ is a group action, then $e \star y = y$, but $e=aa^{-1}$ so $e \star y = (aa^{-1}) \star y$ which by the properties of group action is equivalent to $a \star (a^{-1} \star y)$, but since $a^{-1} \star y \in S$, then we have $\phi(a)(a^{-1} \star y)=\star_{a}(a^{-1} \star y)=y$, so $\phi(a)(x)=y$ has a solution $a^{-1} \star y$, so $\phi(a)$ is surjective.
2. (Injective) Suppose $\phi(a)(y)=\phi(a)(x)$, so $phi(a^{1}) \phi(a)(y) = \phi(a^{-1}) \phi(a)(x)$, so $\phi(a^{-1}a)(y)=\phi(a^{-1}a)(x)$, so $\text{id}_{S}(y)=\text{id}_{S}(x)$, so $y=x$, so $\phi(a)$ is injective.

We conclude $\phi(a) \in \text{Sym}(S)$, so $\phi: G \to \text{Sym}(S)$ and also $\phi$ is a group homomorphism, and by construction the group action $\star$ arises from $\phi$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] group action of dihedral group
>
>Consider the dihedral group $D_{2n}$ and set of $n$ vertices $V_{n}$, then the homomorphism $\phi: D_{2n} \to \text{Sym}(V_{n})$, which we showed in proposition 64 , realizes a *group action*. Conversely, any group action of the dihedral group on a set $S$ is realized via a homomorphism $\phi: D_{2n} \to \text{Sym}(S)$ as per the theorem.

The theorem we have shown trivially implies Cayley's theorem:

>[!theorem] Cayley's theorem
>
>If $G$ is a group then $G$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of the symmetric group $\text{Sym}(G)$.


**Proof.** Consider a group action $\star: G \times G \to G$, then by theorem 164 (group actions are equivalent to group homomorphism to symmetric group) the group action arises from a homomorphism $\phi: G \to \text{Sym}(G)$. Also, the only element for which $a \star g = g$ is $e$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{e\}$, so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem) and corollary 122  it follows that $G \cong \phi(G) \leqslant \text{Sym}(G)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Orbits and Stabilizers

>[!definition] orbit
>
>If $G$ acts on a set $S$, then the *orbit of $x$ under $G$*, denoted $\text{orb}_{G}(x)$, for some $x \in G$ is given by the *image* of $x$ under the action of $G$:
>$$
>\text{orb}_{G}(x) = \{s \in S | \exists g \in G(s = g \star x) \}
>$$
>
>We sometimes denote the orbit of $x$ under $G$ as $O(x)$.

>[!definition] stabilizer
>
>If $G$ acts on a set $S$, then the *stabilizer of $x$ under $G$*, denoted $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$, for some $x \in G$, is given by the elements of $G$ that fix $x$ under the group action:
>$$
>\text{stab}_{G}(x)=\{g \in G | \forall x \in S (g \star x = x)\}
>$$
>We sometimes denote the stabilizer of $x$ under $G$ as $S(x)$.

>[!example] orbit and stabilizer of group multiplication
>
>Let $G$ be a group and $H \leqslant G$. Recall example 163 (group multiplication defines group action). Let $x \in G$. The orbit of $g$ in $G$ is the set of all elements of $G$ s.t. $h \star x = g$ for some $h \in H$, but $h \star x = hx$, so the orbit is the set of all $g \in G$ s.t. $hx=g$, which by definition is the right $H$-coset of $x$, so $\text{orb}_{H}(x)=Hx$.
>
>To find the stabilizer of $x$, we wish to find all $h \in H$ s.t. $h \star x = hx = x$. By definition such $h$ is the identity, so $\text{stab}_{H}(x)=\{e\}$.

>[!example] orbit and stabilizer of scalar multiplication
>
>Consider a vector space $V$ over a field of scalars $\mathbb{F}$, then scalar multiplication defines a *group action* on $V$, $\star: \mathbb{F} \times V \to V, a \star v \mapsto av$:
>1. $a \star (b \star v) = a(bv)=(ab)v=(ab) \star v$
>2. $1 \star v = v$
>
>Let $v \in V$, then:
>1. $\text{stab}_{\mathbb{F}}(v)=\{a \in \mathbb{F} | av=v\}=\{1\}$.
>2. $\text{orb}_{\mathbb{F}}(v)=\{av | a \in \mathbb{F}\}$, i.e. it is the *line* in direction $v$.

>[!example] stabilizer of conjugation
>
>Let $G$ be a group and consider $\star: G \times G \to G, g \star x \mapsto gxg^{-1}$, then:
>1. $g \star (h \star x) = g \star (hxh^{-1}) = g(hxh^{-1})g^{-1} = (gx)h(gh)^{-1}=(gh) \star x$
>2. $e \star x = exe^{-1}=x$
>So $\star$ is the group action defined by conjugation.
>
>Consider $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$, it is the set of all $g \in G$ s.t. $g \star x=x$, but $g \star x = gxg^{-1}$, so $gxg^{-1}=x$. Post-multiplying both sides by $g$ we have $gx=xg$, so $\text{stab}_{G}(x)=\{g \in G | gx=xg\}$, which is exactly the centralizer of $x$ in $G$ as defined in definition 36 (centralizer of an element), so we have $\text{stab}_{G}(x)=C(G)$.


In all three examples, we have seen that the stabilizer of an element in a group is a subgroup of the group acting on the element. This is true in general.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $G$ acts of a set $S$, and $x \in G$, then $\text{stab}_{G}(x) \leqslant G$.


**Proof.** We prove by the two-step subgroup test. First, note $e \star x = x$ always, so $e \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so it is non-empty. For closure, suppose $a, b \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so $ab \star x = a \star (b \star x) = a \star x = x$, so $ab \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so it is closed under the group operation. For inverse, let $a \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, then $(a^{-1}a) \star x = a^{-1} \star (a \star x) = a^{-1} \star x$, but also $a^{-1}a=e$ and $e \star x = x$, so $a^{-1} \star x = x$, so $a^{-1} \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so $\text{stab}_{G}(x) \leqslant G$ by the two-step subgroup test. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now define an equivalence relation which will assist in proving the next theorem:

>[!lemma] orbit defines an equivalence relation
>
>If $G$ acts on $S$, then the realtion $(x \sim y) \iff (x \in \text{orb}_{G}(y))$ defined on $S$ is an equivalence relation.
>


**Proof.**  We show $\sim$ satisfies all axioms of an equivalence relation:
1. (reflexive) $x = e \star x$ so $x \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)$ so $x \sim x$.
2. (symmetric) suppose $x \sim y$ so $x \in \text{orb}_{G}(y)$ so $x = g \star y$ for some $g \in G$, and also $g^{-1} \star (g \star y) = (g^{-1}g) \star y = e \star y = y$, but $g \star y = x$, so $g^{-1} \star x = y$, so $y \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)$, so $y \sim x$.
3. (transitive) suppose $x \sim y$ and $y \sim z$, then $x \in \text{orb}_{G}(y)$ and $y \in \text{orb}_{G}(z)$, so for some $g_{1}, g_{2}$ we have $g_{1} \star y = x, g_{2} \star z = y$, so $x = g_{1} \star y = g_{1} \star (g_{2} \star z) =  (g_{1}g_{2}) \star z$, so $x \in \text{orb}_{G}(z)$, so $x \sim z$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] orbits partition the set
>
>If $G$ acts on a set $S$, then the orbits of the elements in $S$ under the group action of $G$ partition $S$ (i.e. the union of all orbits is $S$).


**Proof.** Recall the equivalence relation $\sim$ defined in lemma 173 (orbit defines an equivalence relation). Since $\sim$ is an equivalence relation,  then it partitions $S$, so the theorem follows immediately. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!corollary] size of set expressed via orbits
>
>If a finite $G$ acts on a finite set $S$, and $S \diagup G$ is the quotient of $S$ under the equivalence relation defined by the orbits of $S$ under the group action of $G$, then
>$$
>|S|=\sum_{[x] \in S \diagup G}|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|
>$$


The proof is immediate from theorem 174 (orbits partition the set) and counting.

>[!theorem] size of orbit of an element is the index of the stabilizer of the element
>
>If $G$ acts on a set $S$, and $S$ is finite, and $x \in S$, then:
>1. There is a one-to-one correspondence between $\text{orb}_{G}(x)$ and the left-cosets of $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$ in $G$.
>2. $|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|=[G:\text{stab}_{G}(x)]$, i.e. the size of $\text{orb}_{G}(x)$ is the index of the stabilizer of $x$ in $G$ (where the index is defined as in definition 102 (index of a subgroup)).


**Proof.** Let $G \diagup \text{stab}_{G}(x)$ be the set of left-cosets of $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$ in $H$ (by theorem 172  the stabilizer of $G$ is a subgroup of $G$, so by corollary 101  $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$ partitions $G$). To show there is a one-to-one correspondence, we show a bijective mapping between the two sets.

Consider $\phi: \text{orb}_{G}(x) \to G \diagup \text{stab}_{G}(x)$ defined as follows: for $y \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)$, there exists $g_{y} \in G$ s.t. $g_{y} \star x = y$ (by definition of the orbit of $x$ under $G$). We choose one such $g_{y}$ (if $G$ is infinite this requires the axiom of choice, if $G$ is finite then $H=\{g \in G | g \star x = y\} \subseteq G$ is also finite and also non-empty as we have just argued so by definition $\exists \psi: H \to [1, n]$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $\psi$ is bijective, so we can declare $g_{y}=\psi(1)$). Now, we define $\phi(y)=g_{y} \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, i.e. $\phi$ maps $y$ to the left coset of $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$ by $g_{y}$. 

The choice of $g_{y}$ is in fact irrelevant: given $g_{y_{1}}, g_{y_{2}}$ s.t. $g_{y_{1}} \star x = g_{y_{2}} \star x = y$, we have $g_{y_{1}}^{-1} g_{y_{2}} \star x = x$, so $g_{y_{1}}^{-1}g_{y_{2}} \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $g_{y_{1}}\text{stab}_{G}(x)=g_{y_{2}}\text{stab}_{G}(x)$.

Clearly, $\phi(y) \in G \diagup \text{stab}_{G}(x)$ since $g_{y} \text{stab}_{G}(x)$ is a left-coset of $\text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so $\phi$ is well-defined.

Now, suppose $\phi(z)=\phi(y)$, so $g_{y}\text{stab}_{G}(x)=g_{z}\text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $g_{y}^{-1}g_{z} \in \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so $g_{y}^{-1}g_{z} \star x = x$, so $g_{y}^{-1} \star (g_{z} \star x)=x$, acting on both sides by $g_{y}$ we get $g_{y} \star(g_{y}^{-1} \star (g_{z} \star x)) = g_{y} \star x$, simplifying we get $g_{z} \star x = g_{y} \star x$, but $g_{z} \star x = z$ and $g_{y} \star x = y$ by construction, so $z=y$, so $\phi$ is injective.

To show $\phi$ is surjective, let $g \space \text{stab}_{G}(x) \in G \diagup \text{stab}_{G}(x)$. Since $g \star x \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)$ by definition, then $g \star x \in \text{Domain}(\phi)$. As we argued above, it must be that $\phi(g \star x)=g \space \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, so $\phi$ is surjective.

We conclude $\phi$ is a bijection, which proves part $1$ of the theorem. For part $2$, since $\phi$ is a bijection then trivially $|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|=|G \diagup \text{stab}_{G}(x)|$, where the later is by definition $[G:\text{stab}_{G}(x)]$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
