>If $G$ is finite, then $\forall x \in G$ the number of unique conjugates of $x$ (i.e. distinct elements of the form $gxg^{-1} \in G$ for some $g \in G$) is the index of its centralizer $C(x)$.


**Proof.** By example 171 (stabilizer of conjugation), $\text{stab}_{G}(x)=C(x)$. By theorem 176 (size of orbit of an element is the index of the stabilizer of the element), $|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|=[G:\text{stab}_{G}(x)]=[G:C(x)]$. Since $\text{orb}_{G}(x)=\{gxg^{-1} | g \in G\}$ (since we consider the group action defined by conjugation), the theorem follows. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use the previous theorem to prove the orbit-stabilizer theorem:

>[!theorem] orbit-stabilizer theorem
>
>If a finite grup $G$ acts on a set $S$, then $\forall x \in S$:
>1. $|\text{orb}_{G}(x)| \space \big | \space |G|$.
>2. $|G|=|\text{orb}_{G}(x)| \space \cdot \space |\text{stab}_{G}(x)|$


**Proof.**  We prove $(1)$ and $(2)$ together since $(2)$ immediately implies $(1)$. By theorem 176 (size of orbit of an element is the index of the stabilizer of the element), $|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|=[G:\text{stab}_{G}(x)]$. By corollary 101 , $[G:\text{stab}_{G}(x)]=\frac{|G|}{|\text{stab}_{G}(x)|}$, so $|G|=|text{orb}_{G}(x)| \space \cdot \space |\text{stab}_{G}(x)|$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The orbit-stabilizer theorem, and group actions in general, have useful application in counting, as we will show in the next couple of examples, the first of which we state as a proposition:

>[!proposition] order of the dihedral group
>
>$$
>|D_{2n}|=2n
>$$
>

**Proof.**  Consider the dihedral group $D_{2n}$ acting on a set $V_{n}$ with $n$ elements as discussed in the introduction to [[#Group Action]]. In [Rigid Motions & Symmetry](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/rigid_motions.pdf), we argue that for every vertex of an $n$-gon, it can be rotated to any other vertex, i.e. the *orbit* of some $v \in V_{n}$ under $D_{2n}$ is in fact $V_{n}$, and it is preserved either by the *identity* or by a *reflection* where $v$ coincides with the line of reflexive symmetry, so the *stabilizer* of $v$ is $\text{id}_{V_{n}}, r$. By theorem 178 (orbit-stabilizer theorem), $|D_{2n}|=|\text{orb}_{D_{2n}}(v)| \cdot |\text{stab}_{D_{2n}}(v)|=n \cdot 2 = 2n$, so the dihedral group has order $2n$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!example] (informally) counting the rotational symmetries of a cube
>
>Consider the rotational symmetries of a cube. A cube has $6$ faces, and we can act on the cube via rotation to map any face to any other face, so the size of the orbit of any face is $6$. The face remains fixed by rotations whose axis is perpendicular to the face. There are $4$ such rotational symmetries (those that map the vertices of the face to themselves and not to vertices of other faces), so every face has stabilizer of order $4$, so by the orbit-stabilizer theorem the cube has $24$ symmetries.


We proceed with more applications of the orbit-stabilizer theorem.

>[!definition] conjugacy class
>
>If $G$ is a group and $x \in G$, then the *conjugacy class* of $x$ in $G$, denoted $x^{G}$ or $\text{Cl}(s)$, is given by the set $\{gxg^{-1} | g \in G\}$.

Observe that the conjugacy class of $x$ in $G$ is *exactly* the orbit of $x$ in $G$ under the action of conjugation, and by lemma 173 (orbit defines an equivalence relation) we can trivially define an equivalence relation based on conjugation which partitions the set acted on by the group into equivalence classes, so the use of *class* in the definition is properly motivated.

corollary 177  can be restated using conjugacy classes as follows:

>[!proposition]
>
>$$
>|x^{G}|=[G:C(x)]
>$$


This result gives rise to the conjugacy class equation, which we define in the following theorem:

>[!theorem] conjugacy class equation
>
>If $G$ is a group, then the *class equation* of $G$ is given by
>$$
>|G| = |Z(G)| + \sum_{i}[G: C(x_{i})]=|Z(G)|+\sum_{i}|x_{i}^{G}|
>$$
>Where $Z(G)$ is the center of $G$, and the sum is over representatives of the conjugacy classes of $x \in G \setminus Z(G)$ (i.e. the conjugacy classes of elements of $G$ not in its center).


**Proof.** Recall that theorem 174 (orbits partition the set) and the conjugacy class of $x$ is the orbit of $x$ under conjugation by proposition 182 , so the sum of the order of all distinct conjugacy classes is exactly $G$ (since $G$ acts on itself by conjugation), so by considering only the conjugacy classes of $G \setminus Z(G)$, we have that the sum of their orders is $|G| - |Z(G)|$. Adding $|Z(G)|$ to both sides produces the class equation of $G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!lemma]
>
>If $G$ is a group then $|x^{G}|=1 \iff x \in Z(G)$ ($Z(G)$ is the center of $G$).


**Proof.** $\impliedby$ Suppose $x \in Z(G)$, then $\forall g \in G(gx=xg)$, so $gxg^{-1}=g(xg^{-1})=g(g^{-1}x)=(gg^{-1})x=ex=x$, so $\forall g \in G(gxg^{-1}=x)$ in this case, so $x^{G}=\{x\}$, so $|x^{G}|=1$. 

$\implies$ Suppose $|x^{G}|=1$. First observe that for $e \in G$, we have $exe^{-1}=x$, so $x \in x^{G}$ always, so if $|x^{G}|=1$ then it must be that $\forall g \in G$, $gxg^{-1}=x$, which is equivalent to $\forall g \in G(gx=xg)$, which is by definition equivalent to $x \in Z(G)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use the lemma to prove Cauchy's theorem:

>[!theorem] Cauchy's theorem
>
>If $G$ is a finite group and $p$ is a prime s.t. $p\space | \space |G|$, then $\exists a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=p$.


**Proof.** Suppose $p \space | \space |G|$. We prove by induction on $|G|$. If $|G|=2$ (smallest prime), then $G \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}$ and $|1|=2$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}$. $2$ is the only prime that divides itself, so the theorem holds for $|G|=2$.

Suppose by induction the theorem holds for some $n$ and consider the case where $|G|=n+1$. Let $x \in G$, then by proposition 182  $|x^{G}|=[G:C(x)]=\frac{|G|}{|C(x)|}$.

If $Z(G)=G$, then $\forall g \forall x \in G (gx=gx)$ i.e. $G$ is abelian, then by theorem 159  it follows that since $p$ divides $G$ then $\exists a \in G$ s.t. $|a|=p$, which completes the proof.

If $Z(G) \neq G$, then $\exists x \in G \setminus Z(G)$. By lemma 184 , if $x \notin Z(G)$ then $1 \lt |x^{G}|$, so $|C(x)| \lt |G|$. If $p \space \big | \space |C(x)|$, in which case by the induction hypothesis $\exists a \in C(x) \leqslant G$ s.t. $|a|=p$. Consider the case where $C(x)$ is not divisible by $p$, then since $|x^{G}| \space |C(x)| = |G|$ and $|G|$ is divisible by $p$, then by Euclid's lemma it must be that $p \space \big | \space |x^{G}|$.

Recall by theorem 183 (conjugacy class equation) that $|G|=|Z(G)|+\sum_{i}|x_{i}^{G}|$ where $x_{i}$ is a representative element of a conjugacy class and also $x_{i} \in G \setminus Z(G)$. Since the argument from the last paragraphs holds for every such $x_{i}$, then it follows that $\sum_{i}|x_{i}^{G}|$ is divisible by $p$, so since $|Z(G)|=|G|-\sum_{i}|x_{i}^{G}|$ is the difference of two multiples of $p$ then $p$ also divides $Z(G)$. Since $Z(G)$ is abelian and also $p \space \big | \space |Z(G)|$, then again by theorem 159  it follows that $\exists a \in Z(G) \leqslant G$ s.t. $|a|=p$, which completes the proof.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

This theorem is a weaker version of a more general theorem, known as Sylow's first theorem. We state the first and third of Sylow's theorems without proof below. Proving the theorems relies on the Orbit-Stabilizer theorem, Cauchy's theorem, induction and the conjugacy class equation. Sylow's theorem are a powerful tool in the classification of subgroups of finite group, and provide a connection between the prime factors of the order of the group, and subgroups of the group. Thus, it reduces the group-theoretic problem of classifying the subgroups of some group, to a number-theoretic problem of finding prime factors of the order of the group and their multiplicity.

>[!definition] $p$-group
>
>If $G$ has order $p^{n}$ for some $p \in \mathbb{G}$, then $G$ is called a $p$-group.

>[!theorem] Sylow's first theorem
>
>If $G$ is a finite group and $p$ is a prime factor with multiplicity $n$ s.t. $p^{n} \mid |G|$, then $\exists H \leqslant G$ s.t. $|H|=p^{n}$. 


Sylow's first theorem guarantees the existence of a $p$-subgroup for every prime factor $p$ of $|G|$, which is why they are sometimes called Sylow $p$-subgroups. Sylow's third theorem teaches us about the structure of these $p$-groups:

>[!theorem] Sylow's third theorem
>
>If $G$ is a finite group, and $p, q \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $p$ is prime, $\text{gcd}(p, q)=1$, and $|G|=p^{n}q$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then:
>1. The index of the Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$ is $q$.
>2. Let $n_{p}$ be the number of Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$, then $n_{p} \mid q$.
>3. $n_{p} \equiv 1 \mod p$
>4. $n_{p}=[G:N_{G}(P)]$ where $P$ is any Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$, and $N_{G}(P)$ is the normalizer of $P$ in $G$.


The next theorem shows another result in the same spirit of the conjugacy class equation:

>[!theorem] Burnside's lemma
>
>If $G$ is a finite group acting on a finite set $X$, and $X^{g}$ is the set of elements in $X$ fixed by $g \in G$ under the group action (i.e. $X^{g}=\{x |g \star x = x\} \subseteq X$, not the be confused with the *stabilizer* of $x$ in $G$, which is a subset of $G$ s.t. its elements fix $x$ under the group action, nor with $x^{G}$ (note the lowercase $x$) which is the conjugacy class of some $x \in G$ under the group action of conjugation), then the number of (distinct) orbits, denoted $|X \diagup G|$, is given by
>$$
>|X \diagup G| = \frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|
>$$ 

**Proof.** First we note that by definition of $X^{g}, \text{stab}_{G}(x)$, and counting, we get:

$$
\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|=|\{(g, x ) \in G \times X | g \star x = x\}| = \sum_{x \in X}|\text{stab}_{G}(x)| 
$$

By theorem 178 (orbit-stabilizer theorem) $|\text{stab}_{G}(x)|=\frac{|G|}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}$. Via summation over all $x \in X$, we get:

$$
\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|=\sum_{x \in X}|\text{stab}_{G}(x)|=\sum_{x \in X} \frac{|G|}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}=|G|\sum_{x \in X}\frac{1}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}
$$
Where the first transition is justified by the relation we developed in the beginning of the proof.

Consider $\sum_{x \in X} \frac{1}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}$. Since the orbits of $X$ partition the set, we can express $X$ directly as the disjoint union of representatives of $X \diagup G$, and write

$$
\sum_{x \in X}\frac{1}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|} = \sum_{\text{orb}_{G}(x) \in X \diagup G} \sum_{x' \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)} \frac{1}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}=|X \diagup G|
$$
Putting it all together, we get

$$
\begin{gathered}
|G| \sum_{x \in X}\frac{1}{|\text{orb}_{G}(x)|}=\\
|G| \space |X \diagup G| = \\
\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}| \\
\therefore \\
|X \diagup G| =\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|
\end{gathered}
$$

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use Burnside's lemma in the next example, adapted from Wikipedia:

>[!example] colorings of a cube
>
>Consider a cube. We wish to count the rotationally distinct colorings of faces of the cube using $3$ colors, that is - we wish to count how many ways there are to paint the faces of a cube using $3$ colors such that one coloring $\chi$ cannot be mapped to another coloring $\chi'$ via rotation (this is what it means for a coloring to be *rotationally distinct*).
>
>Note that this is not the same question as counting the rotational symmetries of a cube, which we did in example 180 ((informally) counting the rotational symmetries of a cube) (and arrived at the number $24$), however only by acting on the coloring via a rotational symmetry of the cube can we map one colored face to another via rotation, so the question becomes *counting the distinct orbits of possible colorings under the rotational symmetry group* of the faces of the cube.
>
>Denote $G$ to be the group of rotational symmetries of the faces of the cube. We know $|G|=24$ by example 180 ((informally) counting the rotational symmetries of a cube). Given $n$ colors, there are $n^{6}$ possible different colorings of the faces of the cube. Let $X$ be the set of colorings of the cube, then $|X|=n^{6}$. The question is now finding $|X \diagup G|$, which by Burnside's lemma is exactly $\frac{1}{|G|} \sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|=\frac{1}{24}\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|$. 
>
>Consider $X^{g}$ for some $g \in G$ - it is the  set of colorings that are fixed by the group action of $g$, i.e. fixed under the rotation represented by $g$. Consider the action of the rotational symmetries of the cube on the coloring. By considering all rotations (we have not classified these yet, but you can convince yourself that the enumeration is true by considering the action of each described rotation on the cube, and recalling that we *did* show that in total there should be $24$ such rotations), we find that:
>1. The identity rotation fixes all colorings, so we have $|X^{e}|=n^{6}$
>2. All six $90 \degree$ rotations of each face each fix $n^{3}$ colorings, so in total they contribute $6n^{3}$ to the count
>3. All three $180 \degree$ rotations of each face fix $n^{4}$ colorings, so they contribute $3n^{4}$
>4. All eight $120 \degree$ vertex rotations fix $n^{2}$ colorings, so they contribute $8n^{2}$
>5. All six $180 \degree$ edge rotations fix $n^{3}$ colorings, so they contribute $6n^{3}$
>   
>Note that we have accounted for all $24$ rotations which we know represent all rotational symmetries of the cube, so in total, we have $\sum_{g \in G}|X^{g}|=n^{6}+6n^{3}+3n^{4}+8n^{2}+6n^{3}$. Substituting $3$ for $n$, we get that this sum is $1368$. Dividing by $|G|=24$, we get $|X \diagup G|=57$, so we conclude that there are $57$ rotationally distinct colorings of the faces of the cube using $3$ colors.

# Ring & Field Theory

Rings are an algebraic structure which, while having group structure, also have other properties. Fields, in turn, are rings with additional structure. Field and group theory were brought together by Evariste Galois in Galois theory, which reduces problems concerning fields to problems concerning groups, thus making them easier. A very famous application of Galois theory is proving that the quintic does not have a general solution by radicals. We will not discuss Galois theory here, but instead build a base of knowledge in ring & field theory, which can later be used to develop Galois theory.

## Basic Definitions

We begin with a survey of definitions. Once we have collected enough definitions to properly examine interesting examples, we show a few examples.

>[!definition] ring
>
>A structure $(R, +, \cdot)$ where:
>1. $R$ is a nonempty set
>2. $+$ is a binary operator on $R$ called *addition*
>3. $\cdot$ is a binary operator on $R$ called *multiplication*
>
>Is called a *ring*, if it satisfies the following properties $\forall a, b, c \in R$:
>1. $(R, +)$ is an abelian group
>2. Multiplication is associative, i.e. $a \cdot (b \cdot c)=(a \cdot b) \cdot c$
>3. Multiplication is distributive over addition, i.e. $a\cdot(b+c)=a \cdot b + a \cdot c$ (right distributive), and also $(a+b) \cdot c = a \cdot c + b \cdot c$ (left distributive)


Note that our definition of a ring is often referred to as a *pseudo-ring* or *non-unital ring*, or *rng*, and *ring* is reserved for what we call a *ring with unity*. I follow Gallian's definitions which call what is more commonly called a rng a *ring*, and a *ring* a *unital ring*.

>[!definition] commutative ring
>
>A ring is *commutative* if multiplication in the ring commutes, i.e. $\forall a, b \in R$, $a \cdot b = b \cdot a$.

>[!definition] unital ring
>
>If $\exists x \in R$ s.t. $\forall r \in R$, $xr=r=rx$, then we denote $x=1$ and say that $R$ has *unity* $1$, and say that $R$ is a *unital ring*. The unity is the *multiplicative identity of the ring*.

Alternatively, a *unital ring* is a ring s.t. $(R, \cdot)$ is a definition 1 (monoid) where the identity of that structure is denoted $1$. Some writers call a *unital ring* (or a *ring with unity*) a *ring with identity*. We try to reserve the term *identity* for the additive identity of the ring, and use the term *unity* for the multiplicative identity.

Now that we have the definition of a ring with unity, the name *rng* should perhaps be more obvious - it is a *ring* but without *i*(dentity). 

Unital rings can succinctly be defined (without referring to definition 191 (ring)) as a structure $(R, +, \cdot)$ s.t.:
1. $(R, +)$ is an abelian group
2. $(R, \cdot)$ is a monoid
3. Multiplication is (left & right) distributive over addition

The notion of *factor*, *divisor* and *multiple* generalize naturally to rings:

>[!definition] factor, divisor, multiple
>
>If $R$ is a ring and for some $a, b \in R$ exists $k \in R$ s.t. $a=kb$, we say that $b$ *divides* $a$, denoted $b | a$. We also say that $a$ is a *multiple* of $b$, and that $b$ is a *factor* of $a$.

>[!definition] unit of a ring
>
>If $R$ has unity $1$ and for $a \in R$ there exists $b \in R$ s.t. $ab=1$, we say that $a$ is a *unit* and write $b=a^{-1}$ and call $b$ the *multiplicative inverse* of $a$.

>[!theorem]
>
>1. If $1 \in R$ (i.e. if $R$ has unity $1$), then it is unique.
>2. If $a^{-1} \in R$, then it is unique.
>3. If $ab=1$ then both $a, b$ are units of $R$.

**Proof.**  For $(1)$, refer to proposition 2 (uniqueness of the identity). For $(2), (3)$, refer to proposition 6 (properties of the inverse)  <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] zero-divisors
>
>If $R$ is a ring and $ab=0$ for some $a, b \in R$ both nonzero (where $0$ is the additive identity of the ring), then we call $a, b$ *zero-divisors*.

Given $a \in R, z \in \mathbb{Z}$, we define an *action* of $\mathbb{Z}$ or $R$ along the same fashion of definition 13 (power notation) with definition 16 (additive notation). Note that now we can say something more interesting than we did in those definitions - we can identify this as a *group action*. Once we have defined this group action, we can define the ring characteristic:

>[!definition] ring characteristic
>
>If $R$ is a ring, then the *characteristic* of $R$, denoted $\text{char}(R)$, is the least $n \in \mathbb{Z}^{+}$ for which $\forall x \in R, nx=0$ (i.e. $n$ acting on any element of $R$ results in the additive identity of $R$). If no such $n$ exists, then we say $\text{char}(R)=0$.

>[!definition] integral domain
>
>A ring $R$ is an *integral domain* if $R$ is a commutative ring with unity (equivalently, a commutative monoid with respect to multiplication), that has no zero-divisors.

>[!definition] field
>
>A ring $R$ is a *field* if $R$ is a commutative ring where every nonzero (where $0$ is the additive identity of the field) element is a unit, i.e. it has an inverse w.r.t multiplication.


Equivalently, a field can be defined as a structure $(F, +, \cdot)$ where $F$ is a nonempty set, $+, \cdot$ are binary operators on $F$ called *addition* and *multiplication* respectively, s.t. $(F, +)$ is a commutative group, $(F, \cdot)$ is a commutative monoid,  multiplication is distributive over addition, and $\forall a \in F$, $a \neq 1 \implies a^{-1} \in F$.

>[!definition] group of units
>
>The *set of units* of a field $R$ is the set of all units in $R$, denoted $U(R)$. If $U(R)$ is nonempty then the *group of units* is the structure $(U(R), \cdot)$ where $\cdot$ is multiplication in the group.

>[!proposition]
>
>$U(R)$ is a group under ring multiplication (if $U(R)$ is nonempty).
>

**Proof.** $(U(R), \cdot)$ is a commutative monoid, so it remains to show that the inverse property is satisfied, and indeed if $a \in U(R)$ then $a$ has an inverse $a^{-1} \in R$, and $(a^{-1})^{-1}=a$ so $a^{-1}$ is also a unit so $a^{-1} \in U(R)$, thus $(U(R), \cdot)$ satisfies all group axioms, thus it is properly named. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now survey a few important examples.

>[!example] the integers
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}$ with the usual addition and multiplication operations. By the properties of the integers we already know that $\mathbb{Z}$ satisfies all ring axioms, so it is a ring. Furthermore, multiplication on integers commutes, so $\mathbb{Z}$ is a commutative ring. 
>
>$1 \in \mathbb{Z}$ is the *unity* of the ring (i.e. multiplicative identity), so $\mathbb{Z}$ is a commutative ring with unity, and also $ab=0$ implies either $a=0$ or $b=0$, so it has no zero-divisors, so it is an integral domain by definition. However, it is not a field - take $2 \in \mathbb{Z}$, we know the multiplicative inverse of $2$ is $\frac{1}{2}$, but $\frac{1}{2} \notin \mathbb{Z}$, so $2$ is not a unit of $\mathbb{Z}$, so $\mathbb{Z}$ is not a field. In fact, it is trivial to show that $U(\mathbb{Z})=\{-1, 1\}$.
>
>Finally, the characteristic of $\mathbb{Z}$ is $0$ since we know $nx=0$ for $x \neq 0$ implies $n=0$.

>[!example] integers under modular arithmetic
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$ i.e. the quotient set of the integers under a congruence defined by some modulus $n$, equipped with modular addition and multiplication. We know (from [Elementary Number Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/elementary_number_theory.pdf)) that this structure satisfies all ring axioms and also that multiplication is commutative so it is a commutative ring. Furthermore, we know that the only elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$ that have a multiplicative inverse also in $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$, i.e. *the units* of $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$, are numbers which are relatively coprime w.r.t. $n$, and they form the group we denoted mostly by $\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\times}$ before.
>
>Since it is always true that $1 \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}$ then these are commutative rings with unity, however for $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}$ s.t. $a\cdot b$ calculated in $\mathbb{Z}$ is a multiple of $n$, then $ab \equiv_{n} n \equiv_{n} 0$ by definition of modular congruence, for example with $n=9$ and $a, b =3$, we get $3 \not \equiv_{n} 0$ and yet $3 \cdot 3 = 9 \equiv_{9} 0$, so $3$ is a zero divisor in $\mathbb{Z}_{9}$, so it is not true in general that $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$ is an integral domain.
>
>Finally, since $\forall x \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}, nx \equiv_{n} 0$, then $\text{char}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}) \leq n$. By a standard division algorithm argument we can show that $\text{char}(\mathbb{Z}_{n})=n$.

>[!example] $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field
>
>We know from previous knowledge that the rationals, the reals and the complex numbers satisfy all *field* axioms under the usual addition and multiplication operations defined for these sets, so $\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{R}, \mathbb{C}$ are all examples of fields.

We turn our attention to a more exciting example - *polynomials over a commutative ring*. We will formally define this structure later, so the following example is not entirely accurate and appeals to previous math knowledge, but nonetheless it is worth considering and we will build upon it in later examples before we formally introduce the underlying structure discussed in this example.

First, a (slightly informal) definition:

>[!definition] univariate polynomial
>
>A polynomial in one indeterminate (one variable) is called a *univariate polynomial*.

We tend to use *univariate polynomial* and *polynomial* interchangeably.

>[!definition] polynomial over a commutative ring
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring then a *polynomial over $R$* is a polynomial with coefficients from $R$. The set of all such univariate polyonmials is denoted $R[x]$.

We will later see that if $R$ is a commutative ring then $R[x]$ is also a commutative ring, and that if $R$ is an integral domain then so is $R[x]$. For now, we present an example based on said previous knowledge:

>[!example] $\mathbb{Z}[n]$ is a ring
>
>Recall from previous knowledge that a univariate polynomial can be written as a finite sum $\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$ where $x$ is a variable and $a_{0}, \dots, a_{n}$ are coefficients. For some $p(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[n]$, we have $p(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$ for some $n$, s.t. all coefficients are in $\mathbb{Z}$. We know from previous knowledge that these polynomials satisfy all ring axioms, and furthermore they commute under multiplication ($(gf)(x)=(fg )(x))$) and $o(x)=1$ is the unity of $\mathbb{Z}[n]$ and if $(gf)(x)=0$ then either $g(x)=0$ or $f(x)=0$, so $\mathbb{Z}[n]$ forms an integral domain.

We define an *external direct product* on rings similarly to our definition on groups:

>[!definition] product ring
>
>If $R_{1}, \dots, R_{n}$ are rings, then $R_{1} \times \dots \times R_{n}$ (the Cartesian product of all elements of the rings) equipped with component-wise addition and multiplication (i.e. if $s, r \in R_{1} \times \dots \times R_{n}$, then $s+r=(s_{1}+r_{1}, \dots, s_{n}+r_{n})$ and $s \cdot r = (s_{1} \cdot r_{1}, \dots, s_{n} \cdot r_{n})$ where each component-wise operation is the respective operation of the ring the component belongs to) is called the *product ring* of $R_{1}, \dots, R_{n}$.

>[!proposition]
>
>The product ring is a ring.

**Proof.** Since the proof is simple and we have a lot of similar proofs in the past, we only sketch it and leave filling out the details to the reader. By proposition 140  we know that the product ring forms a commutative group with respect to addition (in truth, the proposition shows that it forms a group w.r.t. addition, but it is trivial to see that if each component-wise addition commutes, then addition on the product commutes). Closure of multiplication as defined above is also obvious, and associativity of multiplication follows from the associativity of component-wise multiplication operations. The distributive property is also shown in a similar fashion. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

This last definition provides one way of forming new rings from existing rings. Note that the polynomial ring is also another such method as we will see later. Finally, consider matrices over a ring.

>[!definition] matrix ring
>
>If $R$ is a ring, then the set of all $n \times n$ matrices with elements from $R$ (i.e. the set of all matrices s.t. $\forall i, j \in \mathbb{N} \cap [1, n] (A_{ij} \in R)$), denoted $R^{n \times n}$ or $M_{n}(R)$, equipped with usual matrix addition (which is carried component-wise) and multiplication ($(A \cdot B)_{ij} = \sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ik}B_{kj}$), i s called the *matrix ring* of $R$.

>[!proposition]
>
>The matrix ring forms a ring under matrix addition and multiplication, and if $R$ has unity $1$ then matrix ring of $R$ has unity $I$ where $I$ is an $n \times n$ matrix where $I_{ij}=\begin{cases} i & i=j \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}$, i.e. it is a diagonal matrix where all entries along the diagonal are $1$.


**Proof.** Let $R$ be a ring. First, we show $(M_{n}(R), +)$ (where $+$ is matrix addition) forms a commutative group. Let $A, B, C \in M_{n}(R)$, then:

1. (closure under addition) By definition, $(A+B)_{ij}=(A_{ij}+B_{ij})$ where the latter is addition in $R$, and since $R$ is a ring then elements of $R$ are closed under addition, so every $ij^{\text{th}}$ cell of $A+B$ is in $R$, so the $n \times n$ matrices whose elements are $(A+B)_{ij}$ is also a matrix where all elements belong to $R$, so it belongs to $M_{n}(R)$, hence $M_{n}(R)$ is closed under addition.
2. (associativity under addition) Since $R$ is a ring then addition in $R$ is associative, so $A_{ij}+(B_{ij}+C_{ij})=(A_{ij}+B_{ij})+C_{ij}$, the former is $(A+(B+C))_{ij}$ by definition and the latter is $((A+B)+C)_{ij}$, so $(A+B)+C=A+(B+C)$, so matrix addition is associative.
3. (additive identity) Consider $0 \in R$ the additive identity of $R$, and let $0_{M}$ be an $n \times n$ matrix s.t. all its elements are $0 \in R$, clearly $0_{M} \in M_{n}(R)$, and also $(A+0_{M})_{ij}=A_{ij}+0_{M, ij}=A_{ij}+0=A_{ij}$, so $A+0_{M}=0$, so $0_{M}$ is the additive identity of $(M_{n}(R), +)$ (we have only shown right identity but by an earlier theorem from group theory we know that it suffices to show the structure is closed under the operation, associative, and has identity and inverse in one side, to imply that the identity and inverse also exists for the other side and are in fact equal in both sides).
4. (additive inverse) Since $R$ is a ring then $-A_{ij} \in R$ as well. Consider a matrix $D$ given by $D_{ij}=-A_{ij}$, then $(A+D)_{ij}=A_{ij}+D_{ij}=A_{ij}-A_{ij}=0$, so $A+D=0_{M}$, so $D$ is the additive inverse of $A$.
5. (addition commutes) Since $R$ is a ring then addition is commutative on $R$, so by an identical argument to those presented above we find that $A+B=B+A$.

We conclude $(M_{n}(R), +)$ is a commutative group. We now consider $(M_{n}(R), +, \cdot)$ where $\cdot$ is matrix multiplication. Again let $A, B, C \in M_{n}(R)$, then:
1. (closure under multiplication) By definition $(A \cdot B)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ik}B_{kj}$. The RHS of the equation is a sum of product of elements of $R$. Since $R$ is a ring it is closed under these operations, so the RHS also belongs to $R$, so $AB \in M_{n}(R)$.
2. (associativity under multiplication) By definition,
$$
(A(BC))_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(A_{ik}(BC)_{kj})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(A_{ik} \sum_{k'=1}^{n}(B_{kk'}C_{k'j})) = (*)
$$

Since $R$ is a ring, multiplication in $R$ is distributive over addition in $R$, so

$$
(*)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\sum_{k'=1}^{n}(A_{ik}(B_{kk'}C_{k'j}))) \underset{\text{$R$ mult is associative}}{=} \sum_{k=1}^{n}(\sum_{k'=1}^{n}((A_{ik}B_{kk'})C_{k'j}))
$$

Exchanging the symbols for $k, k'$, the above expression becomes:

$$
\sum_{k'=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}((A_{ik'}B_{k'k})C_{kj}) = (*)
$$

On the other hand, again by definition:

$$
((AB)C)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(AB)_{ik}C_{kj})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}((\sum_{k'=1}^{n}A_{ik'}B_{k'k})C_{kj})
$$
Since ring addition commutes, we can exchange the order of summation, which gives us $(**)$, which is $(A(BC))_{ij}$, so $A(BC)=(AB)C$, so matrix multiplication is associative.


1. (distributive property) we only show right distributivity

$$
\begin{gathered}
(AB+AC)_{ij}=\\\sum_{k=1}^{n}(A_{ij}B_{jk}+A_{ij}C_{jk}=\\
\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ij}(B_{jk}+C_{jk})=\\
\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ij}(B+C)_{jk}=\\
(A(B+C))_{ij}
\end{gathered}
$$

So $A(B+C)=AB+AC$.

The matrix ring satisfies all ring axioms, so we conclude it is indeed a ring.

Next, suppose $R$ has unity $1$, and let $I$ be a diagonal $n \times n$ matrix where all entries along the diagonal are $1$, then $\forall A \in M_{n}(R)$:

$$
(IA)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}I_{ik}A_{kj}=\cancelto{0}{(\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}I_{ik}A_{kj})}+I_{ii}A_{ij}+\cancelto{0}{(\sum_{k=i+1}^{n}I_{ik}A_{kj})}=A_{ij}
$$

Similarly we have $(AI)_{ij}=A_{ij}$, so $IA=AI=A$, so $I$ is indeed the unity of $M_{n}(R)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It can be further shown that if $R$ is unital, then the group of units of $M_{n}(R)$ is the set $\{A \in M_{n}(R) | \det A \in U(R)\}$ where $U(R)$ is the group of units of $R$.

Note that this result can be applied recursively - since a matrix ring can be defined over an arbitrary ring, we can consider the following: consider $\mathbb{Z}$ the ring of integers, then $M_{n}(\mathbb{Z})$ the set of $n \times n$ matrices with elements from $\mathbb{Z}$ forms a ring under matrix addition and multiplication by proposition 212 , but then $M_{n}(M_{n}(\mathbb{Z}))$ the set of $n \times n$ matrices whose elements are themselves $n \times n$ matrices with elements from $\mathbb{Z}$ is also a ring by the same proposition. A similar argument can be applied to polynomial rings, i.e. the coefficients of the polynomials can themselves be polynomials and the structure would still form a ring.

Another immediate result is that every field is a vector space over itself.

>[!theorem] $F$ is a vector space over itself with dimension $1$
>
>If $F$ is a vector space, then $F$ is a vector space over $F$, and $\dim(F)=1$.


**Proof.** Recall that a vector space is a set of vectors $V$ and some scalar field $\mathbb{F}$ satisfying a set of axioms. By setting $V=\mathbb{F}$, we find that the axioms of a vector space coincide with those of a field:
1. $F$ is an abelian group under addition
2. Since we define $V=F$ and also $F$ to be the field of scalars, then obviously $\forall a, b \in F, u, v \in V=F$, we have $a(bv)=(ab)v$ ($F$ is a monoid w.r.t. multiplication), $1v=v$ ($F$ is unital), $a(u+v)=au+av$ and $(a+b)v=av+bv$ (distributive property of multiplication over addition in a ring).

So, $F$ is a vector space over itself. Furthermore, let $1 \in F$ and $r \in F$, then since $r1=r$ for every $r \in F$, it follows that $\{1\}$ is a basis of $F$ and $\dim(F)=1$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We define *subrings* in an analogous manner to *subgroups*:

>[!definition] subring
>
>If $R$ is a ring and $S \subseteq R$ is a nonempty set, then we say that $S$ is a *subring* of $R$ if it is a ring with respect to the ring operations of $R$.

>[!theorem] subring test
>
>If $S \subseteq R$ is nonempty, then $S$ is a ring w.r.t the ring operations on $R$, if:
>1. It is closed under subtraction, i.e. $\forall a, b \in S, a-b \in S$
>2. It is closed under multiplication, i.e. $\forall a, b \in S, ab \in S$.


**Proof.** Observe that $(1)$ is simply the one-step subgroup test for $(R, +)$, thus if $(1)$ is satisfied then $(S, +) \leqslant (R, +)$, and since $(R, +)$ is abelian then $(S, +)$ is also abelian. Suppose $(2)$ holds, then $S$ is closed under multiplication. Associativity of multiplication immediately follows from associativity of multiplication in $R$, and also right and left distributivity behave the same since the operators are the same, so $S$ is a subring of $R$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The next example is a cautionary tale:

>[!example] unities of subring and unity of ring aren't necessarily identical
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}_{6}$ whose elements are $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$, and consider the coset $2\mathbb{Z}_{6}=\{0, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10\}=\{0, 2, 4, 0, 2, 4\}=\{0, 2, 4\}$. We can verify that $2\mathbb{Z}_{6}$ forms a (commutative) ring under modular addition and multiplication $\mod 6$, however note that $1$ is the unity of $\mathbb{Z}_{6}$, whereas $4 \cdot 0 =0, 4 \cdot 2 = 8 \equiv_{6} 2, 4 \cdot 4 = 16 \equiv_{6} 4$, so $4$ is the unity of $2\mathbb{Z}_{6}$ even though it is a subring of $\mathbb{Z}_{6}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_{6}$ is also a ring with unity.


Of course, the additive identity is the same across all subrings since all subrings are in particular subgroups under addition.

## Elementary Results

The next theorem collect basic arithmetic properties of rings:

>[!theorem] ring arithmetic
>
>If $R$ is a ring then $\forall a, b, c \in R$:
>1. $a0=0a=0$ (here we talk about $0 \in R$, and not $0 \in \mathbb{Z}$)
>2. $a(-b)=(-a)b=-(ab)$
>3. $(-a)(-b)=ab$
>4. $a(b-c)=ab-ac$ and $(b-c)a=ba-ca$
>5. If $R$ has unity $1$, then $(-1)a=-a$ and $(-1)(-1)=1$
>6. (cancellation law) $R$ has no zero divisors if and only if $a \neq 0$ and $ab=ac$, then $b=c$


**Proof.** 

$1$. Observe that $0=0+0$ by the group structure of $(R, +)$, so $a0=a(0+0)=a0+a0$ by right-distrubitivity, so $a0=a0+a0$, so $a0=0$. Similarly $0a=(0+0)a=0a+0a$ hence $0a=0$ (here we used left-distributivity), so $0a=a0=0$.

$2$. Note $0=b-b$. By $1$ we know $a0=0$, so $a0=a(b-b)=ab+a(-b)=0$, so $a(-b)=-(ab)$ by uniqueness of the additive inverse. Similarly, $0b=(a-a)b=ab+(-a)b=0$, so $(-a)b=-(ab)$, so in conclusion $a(-b)=(-a)b=-(ab)$.

$3$. Denote $d=-b$, so by $2$ we have $(-a)(-b)=(-a)d=-(ad)$. Now, $a(-b)=-(ab)$ also by $2$, so we have $(-a)(-b)=-(-(ab))$, so $(-a)(-b)=ab$ since inverse is an involution.

$4$. Via right-distributivity $a(b-c)=a(b+(-c))=ab+a(-c)=ab-ac$ (the last transition is justified by $(2)$), similarly we find that $(b-c)a=ba+(-c)a=ba-ca$ via left-distributivity and $(2)$.

$5$. Suppose $R$ has unity $1$, then $1a=a$ and also $1-1=0$, so $(1-1)a=0a=0$ (by $(1)$). By $(4)$, $(1-1)a=1a+(-1)a=a+(-1)a=0$, so $(-1)a=-a$. Choosing $a=1$, we get $(-1)(-1)=1$.

$6$. $\implies$ Suppose $R$ has no zero divisors and suppose $a \neq 0$ and also $ab=ac$. Consider $a(b-c)$, by $(4)$ we have $a(b-c)=ab-ac$. By supposition $ab=ac$ so $-(ab)=ac$, so $ab-ac=0$, so $a(b-c)=0$, so since $R$ has no zero divisors then either $a=0$ or $b-c=0$, but by supposition $a \neq 0$ so $b-c=0$, so $b=c$. 

$\impliedby$ Suppose $ab=0$. If $a=0$ then we're done, otherwise let $c \in R$, then $\exists d \in R$ s.t. $d=b+c$, so $b=d-c$, so $ab=a(d-c)$, by $(4)$ we have $a(d-c)=ad-ac=0$, adding $ac$ to both sides we get $ad=ac$. Since $a \neq 0$, then it follows that $d=c$ by supposition, so $b=d-d=0$. We conclude that $R$ has no zero divisors.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring, then if $a$ is a zero-divisor and $ab \neq 0$ for some $b \in R$, then $ab$ is a zero-divisor.


**Proof.**  Suppose $a$ is a zero-divisor and $ab \neq 0$. Since $a$ is a zero-divisor, then $ac=0$ for some $c \neq 0$. Since $R$ is commutative, then $(ab)c=(ac)b=0b=0$ by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), but $ab \neq 0$ and also $c \neq 0$, so $ab$ is a zero-divisor. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] a finite integral domain is a field
>
>If $R$ is a finite integral domain, then $R$ is a field.

**Proof.** Since $R$ is assumed to be a finite integral domain, by definition we have that $R$ is a commutative ring with unity $1$ and no zero-divisors. It remains to show that the nonzero elements of $R$ are units. We follow an identical argument to the one outlined in proposition 21 (order of elements in finite group is finite).

Let $x \in R$. Consider the set $X=\{x^{n} \in R | n \in \mathbb{N}\}$ (since $R$ is a ring it is closed under multiplication and so $x^{n}$ as defined in definition 13 (power notation) is also in $R$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$). Since $R$ is finite and $X \subseteq R$ then $X$ is also finite, s $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \exists m \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $x^{m}=x^{n}$. Suppose w.l.o.g $m \gt n$, then $x^{m-n}=x^{m}x^{-n}=x^{n}x^{-n}=x^{n-n}=x^{0}=1$, so $x^{m-n-1}x=x^{m-n}=1$, so $x^{m-n-1} \in R$ is the multiplicative inverse of $x \in R$, in other words $x$ is a unit, so we conclude $R$ is a field.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $R$ is a ring with unity $1$ and no zero-divisors, the additive identity $0$ and the multiplicative identity $1$ are distinct elements.


**Proof.** Suppose by contradiction $1=0$, then also $1 \cdot 1 = 0$ (here $\cdot$ denotes group multiplication), but then $1$ is a zero-divisor, a contradiction, so $1 \neq 0$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>If $F$ is a field, then:
>1. $\forall a, b \in F$, $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$ (socks-shoes property is valid for fields)
>2. $F$ has no zero-divisors.


**Proof.** $(1)$ is proven using an identical argument to one for groups, presented in proposition 6 (properties of the inverse). For $(2)$, let $F$ be a field and suppose by contradiction $F$ has zero-divisors $a, b \in F, a, b \neq 0$, so $ab=0$. Since by supposition $a \neq 0$ then $a^{-1} \in F$ by field axioms, so $a^{-1}ab=a^{-1}0$. By theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), $a^{-1}0=0$, and by associativity of field multiplication $a^{-1}ab=(a^{-1}a)b=1b=b$, so $b=0$, a contradiction, so $F$ has no zero divisors. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] field are integral domains
>
>If $F$ is a field then $F$ is an integral domain.

**Proof.** By proposition 221 , if $F$ is a field then $F$ has no zero divisors, and also by field axioms $F$ is a commutative ring with unity $1$, so $F$ satisfies all integral domain axioms. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!lemma]
>
>If $R$ is a ring with unity $1$, then $\forall n \in \mathbb{Z} \forall x \in R$), $n \cdot x = (n \cdot 1)x$, where juxtaposition denotes ring multiplication, and $\cdot$ denotes the action of $\mathbb{Z}$ on $R$.


**Proof.**  Suppose $R$ is a ring with unity $1$. Let $n \in \mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and let $x \in R$, and consider $n \cdot x$, which we defined as $n \cdot x = \sum_{i=1}^{n}x$ (where $\sum$ is a summation over ring addition). Since $x=1x$ by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), it follows that $\sum_{i=1}^{n}x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1x$. By the distributive property, $\sum_{i=1}^{n}1x=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}1)x$. By definition of the action of $n$ on $R$, we have $\sum_{i=1}^{n}1=n \cdot 1$, so $(\sum_{i=1}^{n}1)x=(n \cdot 1)x$. In conclusion, $n \cdot x = (n \cdot 1)x$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] characteristic of a ring with unity
>
>If $R$ is a ring with unity $1$ then:
>1. If $|1|=\infty$ ($|1|$ is the order of $1$ in $(R, +)$) then $\text{char}(R)=0$
>2. If $|1|=n$ for some finite $n$, then $\text{char}(R)=n$


**Proof.** Let $x \in R$. By lemma 223  $n \cdot x = (n \cdot 1)x$.

$1$. We prove the contrapositive - if $\text{char}(R)=n$ then $|1|$ is finite. Suppose $\text{char}(R) \neq 0$, then $n \cdot x = 0$ for some positive integer $n$, but then $(n \cdot 1)x=0$ for the same $n$. By theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), $n \cdot 1 = 0$. By definition, $n \cdot 1 = \sum_{i=1}^{n}i$, so $|i| \leq n$. If follows that if $|1|=\infty$, $\text{char}(R)=0$ via contraposition.

$2$. Suppose $|1|=n$, then $n \cdot 1 = 0$, then $n \cdot x = 0x=0$, so $\text{char}(R) \leq n$. Suppose $\text{char}(R)= m \lt n$, then  for $m \cdot x = 0$, i.e. $m \cdot 1 = 0$, but then $|1| \leq m \lt n$, a contradiction, so $\text{char}(R)=n$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] unital subrings preserve characteristic
>
>If $R$ is a unital ring and $S$ is a unital subring of $R$ (i.e. a subring of $R$ with the same unity as $R$),  then $\text{char}(R)=\text{char}(S)$.


**Proof.** By the theorem, the order of the unity of the ring uniquely determines the characteristic of the ring. Since $S$ is a subring of $R$ then it is a subgroup of $R$ and so order of shared elements is preserved, and since they share the same unity we have $\text{char}(R)=\text{char}(S)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] characteristic of integral domain
>
>If $R$ is an integral domain, then $\text{char}(R)$ is either $0$ or a prime.


**Proof.** Let $R$ be an integral domain. Again by lemma 223 , $n \cdot x = (n \cdot 1)x$. By theorem 224 (characteristic of a ring with unity), if $|1|=\infty$ then $\text{char}(R)=0$. Suppose then that $|1|=n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then by the theorem $\text{char}(R)=n$, so it remains to show that if $R$ is an integral domain and $|1|$ is finite, then $|1|$ is prime.

Suppose $\text{char}(R)=1$, then $|1|=1$ then $1=0$, and by theorem 220  this is impossible, so $n \neq 1$.

Since $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and also $n \neq 1$ as we have argued above, then by the fundamental theorem of arithmetic we can write $n=kp$ for $p$ prime and $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $n \cdot 1 = (kp) \cdot 1=k \cdot (p \cdot 1)$ (the last transition is due to the properties of a group action). Again we refer to lemma 223  and get $k \cdot (p \cdot 1) = (k \cdot 1)(p \cdot 1)$ (where the RHS is multiplication in the ring), which is $0$ by choice of $n$. Since $R$ is an integral domain, it has no zero divisors, and so $(k \cdot 1)(p \cdot 1)=0$ implies that $k \cdot 1 = 0$ or $p \cdot 1 = 0$ (or both).

Since $n$ is minimal s.t. $n \cdot 1 = 0$, and also $n=kp$ and $(k \cdot 1)(p \cdot 1) = 0$, there are two cases:
1. $k \cdot 1 = 0$, but then since $n$ is minimal then it must be that $k=n$, but then $p=1$ while $1$ is not a prime, a contradiction.
2. $p \cdot 1 = 0$, then again $n=p$ and $k=1$.

Since only the second case is possible, we conclude $|1|=\text{char}(R)$ is prime.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field
>
>If $p$ is prime then $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$, equipped with modular addition and multiplication, is a field.


**Proof.** The elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ are identified with the numbers $0, \dots, p-1$. Note that $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ equipped with modular addition is always a group. Also by properties of modular arithmetic, $(\mathbb{Z}_{p}, +_{p}, \times_{p})$ is a *commutative ring* with unity $1$. Consider the group of units of this ring, denoted $U(p)$ (previously we also used the notation $\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}$). We know from number theory that the elements of this group are all positive natural numbers that are less than $p$ and are coprime w.r.t $p$. Since $p$ is prime, then this is exactly the set $1, \dots, p-1$, so $U(p) = \mathbb{Z}_{p} \setminus \{0\}$, which by definition means that $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

## Ideals

Ideals are to rings as normal subgroup are to groups, as we will see in this section.

>[!definition] ideal
>
>If $R$ is a ring then a subring $S$ is called an *ideal* of $R$ if $\forall r \in R \forall s \in S$, $sr \in S$ and $rs \in S$.


>[!example] $n\mathbb{Z}$ is an ideal of the integers
>
>For $n \in \mathbb{Z}$, the left coset $n \mathbb{Z}=\{nz | z \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ is a subring. Since $\mathbb{Z}$ is a commutative ring, then $n\mathbb{Z}$ is also commutative. so $nz=zn$.Let $nz \in n \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{Z}$, then $(nz)q=n(zq)$ and $zq \in \mathbb{Z}$ so by definition $(nz)q \in n\mathbb{Z}$, and also $q(nz)=n(qz)$ by commutativity of multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$, so $q(nz) \in n \mathbb{Z}$, so $n\mathbb{Z}$ is an ideal of $\mathbb{Z}$ by definition.


>[!proposition] ideals are normal subgroups
>
>If $R$ is a ring and $P$ is an ideal of $R$, then $P$ is a normal subgroup of $(R, +)$.

**Proof.** Suppose $P$ is an ideal, then $P$ is a subring so it is a subgroup, it remains to show it is normal i.e. that $\forall r \in R$, $r+P-r \subseteq P$. Let $p \in P$ and $r+p-r \in P$, since $(R, +)$ is abelian then $r+p-r=r-r+p=p$, so $r+p-r=p \in P$, so $r+P-r \subseteq P$, so $P \unlhd (R, +)$. Note that we have already seen this result - every subgroup of an abelian group is normal. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>  

>[!theorem]
>
>If $R$ is a ring, then:
>1. $\{0\}$ is an ideal of $R$
>2. $R$ is an ideal of $R$
>   
>We call such ideals *trivial ideals*. If $S$ is an ideal of $R$ that is non trivial, we say it is *proper*.


**Proof.** 

$1$. From theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) we know that $\forall r \in R, r0=0r=0$, so $r\{0\}=\{0\}r=\{0\}$, so $\{0\}$ is an ideal of $R$.

$2$. Since $R$ is closed under multiplication then $\forall x, y \in R$ we have $xy \in R$ and $yx \in R$ so $R$ is an (improper) ideal of $R$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] ideal test
>
>If $S \subseteq R$ and $S \neq \emptyset$, then $S$ is an *ideal* of $R$ if:
>1. $S$ is closed under subtraction
>2. If $s \in S$ and $r \in R$, then $sr \in S$ and $rs \in S$


**Proof.** Suppose $S \subseteq R$ and $S \neq 0$. If we pick $r \in R \cap S$ then by $(2)$ we have that $S$ is closed under multiplication so by theorem 215 (subring test) $S$ is a subring of $R$. The second part of $(2)$ is simply stating the condition for a subring to be an ideal as per definition 228 (ideal). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] principal ideal
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity and $a \in R$, then $\langle a \rangle=\{ar |r \in R\}$ is the *principal ideal* generated by $a$.


Note that a principal ideal of some $a \in \mathbb{Z}_{n}$ is simply the cyclic subgroup $\langle a \rangle=\{na\}$ in additive notation, equipped with modular multiplication.

>[!proposition]
>
>Principal ideal is an ideal. 

**Proof.** Consider $\langle a \rangle$ under the ring operations. Clearly $a0=0 \in \langle a \rangle$ so it is nonempty. We proceed by theorem 232 (ideal test). Let $ar_{1}, ar_{2} \in \langle a \rangle$, then $ar_{1}-ar_{2}=a(r_{1}-r_{2})$ by the distributive property. Since $R$ is a ring then $\exists r \in R$ s.t. $r=r_{1}-r_{2}$, so $ar=a(r_{1}-r_{2})$, so $ar \in \langle a \rangle$ by definition, so $\langle a \rangle$ is closed under subtraction. Next, let $ar_{1} \in \langle a \rangle$ and $r_{2} \in R$, then $(ar_{1})r_{2}=a(r_{1}r_{2})$ from associativity, and also $r_{1}r_{2} \in R$ from closure of $R$, so $a(r_{1}r_{2}) \in \langle a \rangle$ by definition. Consider $r_{2}(ar_{1})$, again from associativity $r_{2}(ar_{1})=(r_{2}a)r_{1}$. Since $R$ is a commutative ring (see definition 233 (principal ideal)) then $r_{2}a=ar_{2}$, so $(r_{2}a)r_{1}=(ar_{2})r_{1}=a(r_{2}r_{1})$. Again $r_{2}r_{1} \in R$ so $a(r_{2}r_{1})=r_{2}(ar_{1}) \in \langle a \rangle$. We conclude by theorem 232 (ideal test) that $\langle a \rangle$ is an ideal. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] principal ideal of a unit
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity and $a \in U(R)$ (i.e. $a$ is a unit of $R$), then $\langle a \rangle = R$.
>


**Proof.** $\langle a \rangle \subseteq R$ is immediate. To prove the other direction, let $r \in R$ and note that since $a \in U(R)$ then $a^{-1} \in R$, so $a^{-1}r \in R$, so $a(a^{-1}r) \in \langle a \rangle$ by definition, but this is simply $r$, so $R \subseteq \langle a \rangle$, so $\langle a \rangle = R$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] ideal is a superset of the principal ideals of its elements
>
>If $I$ is an ideal in a commutative ring $R$, and $d \in I$, then $\langle d \rangle \subseteq I$.


**Proof.** Suppose $I$ is an ideal in a commutative ring $R$ and $d \in I$ for some $d \in R$, then by definition $\forall s \in R$, $dr, rd \in I$. Since $R$ is commutative, $dr \in I \iff rd \in I$. By definition, $\langle d \rangle$ is the set of all elements of the form $dr$ for $r \in R$, so $\langle d \rangle \subseteq I$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] principal ideal domain
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain, and every ideal of $D$ is principal, then $D$ is called a *principal ideal domain*, or *PID* for short.


>[!theorem] fields are PIDs
>
>If $F$ is a field, then it is a PID.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field and $I$ is an ideal of $F$. If $I=\{0\}$, then $I=\langle 0 \rangle$, otherwise $a \in I$ s.t. $a \neq 0$, so $a^{-1} \in F$, so $a^{-1}a \in I$ by definition of an ideal, but $a^{-1}a=1$, so $1 \in I$. Let $f \in F$, then $f \cdot 1=f \in I$, so $I=F$, so a field has no nontrivial ideals, and also note that $\langle a \rangle = F$ for every non-zero $a$, and we have shown that the only ideals of $F$ are $\langle 0 \rangle, F$ and we have shown that $F$ is principle and every non-zero element in $F$ is a generator of $F$, so $F$ is a PID by definition. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

The notion of a principal ideal can be generalized to any finite number of elements.

>[!definition] ideal generated by multiple elements
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity and $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n} \in R$, then $\langle a_{1}, \dots, a_{n} \rangle = \{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{i} | r_{i} \in R\}$ is called the *ideal generated by $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}$*.

>[!proposition]
>
>The ideal generated by $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}$ is an ideal.


**Proof.** Denote $A=\langle a_{1}, \dots, a_{n} \rangle$. For an $n$-tuple $(r_{1}, \dots, r_{n})=(0, \dots, 0)$ we have $\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}0=0$ so $0 \in A$ so $A$ is nonempty. We proceed by theorem 232 (ideal test). Let $a, b \in A$, then $\exists r_{a}, r_{b} \in R^{n}$ s.t. $a=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{a, i}$ and $b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{b, i}$, then $a-b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(r_{a, i}-r_{b, i})$. Since each $r_{a, i}-r_{b, i} \in R$, then $r_{a-b}=(r_{a, 1}-r_{b, 1}, \dots, r_{a, n}-r_{b, n}) \in R^{n}$, so $a \cdot r_{a-b} \in A$ (here we take $a$ to be $(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})$ and $\cdot$ to be the dot product of the two $n$-tuples), so $A$ is closed under subtraction.

Let $a \in A$ where $a=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{a, i}$ for some $r_{a} \in R^{n}$, and let $r \in R$, then:

$$
ar=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}r_{a, i})r \underset{\text{left distributive, associativity}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(\underbrace{r_{a, i}r}_{\in R})
$$

So $ar \in A$ by definition. Similarly, from commutativity of the ring, $ar=ra$, so $ra \in A$ as well, so by theorem 232 (ideal test), $A$ is an ideal.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example]
>
>Consider the polynomial ring $\mathbb{R}[x]$, and consider the polynomial $x \in \mathbb{R}[x]$. The ideal generated by $x$ is, by definition, $\{xp(x) | p(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]\}$, clearly all polynomials $f(x)$ in $\langle x \rangle$ satisfy $f(0)=0$ (since $x=0 \therefore f(x)=xp(x)=0$). Consider a polynomial $h(x)$ s.t. $h(0)=0$, then either $h(x)=0$ or $h(x)=xh'(x)$ where $h'(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$, but since $0=0x$ then in both cases we have $h(x) \in \langle x \rangle$, so via mutual inclusion we can characterize the ideal generated by $\langle x \rangle$ in $\mathbb{R}[x]$ as the set of all polynomials $p(x)$ s.t. $p(0)=0$. This example relies on previous knowledge, but we will later discuss polynomials in depth and develop the appropriate machinery to derive this result without referring to external knowledge.

By now it should become quite apparent that ideals generated by elements are conceptually very similar to the span of vectors in linear algebra.

## Factor Rings

We define factor rings in a similar manner to how we defined factor groups.

>[!definition] factor ring
>
>If $R$ is a ring and $A$ is a subring of $R$, then if the following operators are well-defined on the quotient set $R \diagup A$ (which is always well-defined since $A \unlhd (R, +)$ since $(R, +)$ is an abelian group and from group theory we know that $A$ partitions $R$ so $R \diagup A$ is well-defined):
>1.$+: (R \diagup A)^{2} \to R \diagup A, (s+A)+(t+A) \mapsto (s+t)+A$
>1. $\cdot: (R \diagup A)^{2} \to R \diagup A, (s+A)\cdot (t+A) \mapsto st + A$
>
>Then we say that $(R \diagup A, +, \cdot)$ is the *factor ring* of $R$ over $A$.


>[!theorem] factor ring is a ring
>
>The factor ring is a ring if and only if $A$ is an ideal. Furthermore, the additive identity $0$ of $R \diagup A$ is $A$, and if $R$ has unity $1$ then $1+A$ is the unity (multiplicative identity) of the factor ring.


**Proof.** $\impliedby$ Suppose $A$ is an ideal. Since $A \unlhd (R, +)$ as argument in the definition of a factor ring, then addition as defined on the structure is well-defined, so it remains to show multiplication is also well-defined. Choose two representatives from each equivalence class $s_{1}+A=s_{2}+A, t_{1}+A=t_{2}+A$, it remains to show $s_{1}t_{1}+A=s_{2}t_{2}+A$.

Recall that $r+A$ for some $r \in R$ is a *left coset* of $A$ (in additive notation), so by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) (converted to additive notation), $s_{1}+A=s_{2}+A \iff s_{1}-s_{2} \in A$, and similarly $t_{1}+A=t_{2}+A \iff t_{1}-t_{2} \in A$, and also $s_{1}t_{1}+A=s_{2}t_{2}A \iff s_{1}t_{1}-s_{2}t_{2} \in A$. We will show $s_{1}t_{1}-s_{2}t_{2} \in A$.

Since $s_{1}-s_{2} \in A$, then $\exists a_{1} \in A$ s.t. $s_{1}-s_{2}=a_{1}$, i.e. $s_{1}=s_{2}+a_{1}$, similarly $\exists a_{2} \in A$ s.t. $t_{1}=t_{2}+a_{1}$. Taking the product $s_{1}t_{1}$, we get $s_{1}t_{1}=(s_{2}+a_{1})(t_{2}+a_{2})$, which by ring axioms (specifically the distributive property and commutativity of addition) expands to $s_{1}t_{1}=s_{2}t_{2}+a_{1}t_{2}+(s_{2}+a_{1})a_{2}$. Solving for $s_{1}t_{1}-s_{2}t_{2}$, we get $s_{1}t_{1}-s_{2}t_{2}=a_{1}t_{2}+(s_{2}+a_{1})a_{2}$. Since $t_{2} \in R$ then since $A$ is an ideal, $a_{1}t_{2} \in A$, and similarly since $s_{2}, a_{1} \in R$ then by closure under addition $s_{2}+a_{1} \in R$ and since $A$ is an ideal $(s_{2}+a_{1})a_{2} \in A$, finally since $A$ is a subring then it is closed under addition so $a_{1}t_{2}+(s_{2}+a_{1})a_{2} \in A$, and we conclude $s_{1}t_{1}-s_{2}t_{2} \in A$, so $s_{1}t_{1}+A=s_{2}t_{2}+A$, so multiplication is well-defined.

It remains to show that $R \diagup A$ with these operations satisfies all ring axioms. Note that $R \diagup A$ being an abelian group under addition follows from $A$ being a normal subgroup of $R \diagup A$ under addition, so we only need to show:
1. multiplication is associative - let $a, b, c \in R$, then $a(bc)=(ab)c$ from associativity of multiplication in the ring $R$, then by definition $(a+A)(bc+A)=a(bc)+A=(ab)c+A=(ab+A)(c+A)$
2. distributive property - let $a, b, c \in R$, then $a(b+c)=ab+ac$, and by definition $(a+A)((b+A)+(c+A))=(a+A)(b+c+A)=a(b+c)+A=ab+ac+A=(ab+A)(ac+A)$, which proves right distributivity. Left distributivity can be shown via an identical argument.

Thus we conclude $R \diagup A$ is a *ring* under the operations defined. Furthermore, the additive identity of $R \diagup A$ is $A$ by known results from group theory. If $R$ has unity $1$, then let $r \in R$, then by definition and by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) $(1+A)(r+A)=1r+A=1+A=r1+A=(r+A)(1+A)$, so $1+A$ is the unity of $R \diagup A$.

$\implies$ We prove by contraposition - if $A$ is not an ideal, then $R \diagup A$ is not a ring. It suffices to show that multiplication as defined in definition 242 (factor ring) is not well defined. 

Suppose $A$ is not an ideal but is a subring, then for some $r \in R$ and $a \in A$, either $ra \notin A$ or $ar \notin A$. Consider that by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $\forall a \in A(a+A=0+A)$. Consider $a\in A, r \in R$ s.t. not both $ar \in A \land ra \in A$, then if multiplication on the quotient set is well defined then since $a+A=0+A$ we expect $(a+A)(r+A)=(0+A)(r+A)$, but $(a+A)(r+A)=ar+A$ while $(0+A)(r+A)=0r+A=A$ (by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic)). If $ar+A=A$, then $ar \in A$. If not, then the operation is not well-defined for the $a, r$ we chose so $R \diagup A$ has no hope of being a ring, and we are done. Suppose then $ar \in A$, then by supposition $ra \notin A$, so $ra+A \neq A$, but then $(r+A)(a+A) \neq (r+A)(0+A)$, so multiplication is again not well defined, so $R \diagup A$ can't be a ring under these operations, which completes the proof.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] identifying the complex numbers in factor ring of polynomials
>
>
>Consider the polynomial ring $\mathbb{R}[x]$. Consider the factor ring $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$, which per theorem 243 (factor ring is a ring) forms a ring. Denote this factor ring $A=\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. We begin with a warm up to help the reader get acquainted to ring arithmetic in the factor ring: 
>
>Suppose $f(x), g(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ are in the same equivalence class, i.e. $f(x)+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = g(x) + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$, then by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) it follows that $f(x)-g(x) \in \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. By definition 233 (principal ideal), this means $f(x)-g(x)=(x^{2}+1)h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$, or equivalently $g(x)=f(x)-(x^{2}+1)h(x)$. Consider the case where $h(x)=1$ and $f(x)=x^{2}+2x+2$, then $g(x)=x^{2}+2x+2-x^{2}-1=2x+1$, so $2x+1 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = x^{2}+2x+2 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$.
>
>Now we consider the equivalence class represented by $x^{2}+1$. By theorem 99 (properties of a coset), we find that in this factor ring, $(x^{2}+1)+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. Subtracting $-1 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ from both sides, we get by ring arithmetic in the factor ring that $x^{2} + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = -1 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. Using $a \equiv b \mod x^{2}+1$ to denote $a+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle=b + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$, we find that $x^{2}\equiv -1 \mod x^{2}+1$.
>
>Using $a \equiv b \mod x^{2}+1$ to denote $a+ \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = b + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$, we can rewrite the above as $x^{2} \equiv -1 \mod x^{2}+1$,
>
>Using this last relation, we can write every $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i} \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ as follows $\mod x^{2}+1$:
>1. If $i$ is even, then $x^{i}=-1$
>2. If $i$ is odd, then $x^{i}=x$
>
>Then by commutativity of addition in the ring $\mathbb{R}[x]$, we can write $f(x) \equiv_{x^{2}+1} a_{0}+a_{1}x-a_{2}+a_{3}x-a_{4}x + \dots$, i.e. for some $b_{0}, b_{1} \in \mathbb{R}$ we have $f(x) \equiv b_{0}+b_{1}x \mod x^{2}+1$ for every polynomial in $\mathbb{R}[x]$, so $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = \{b_{0}+b_{1}x + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle | b_{0}, b_{1} \in \mathbb{R}\}$.
>
>Consider multiplication in this factor ring, choose two representatives $a+bx, c+dx$, then $(a+bx)(c+dx) \equiv_{x^{2}+1}ac+adx+bcx+bdx^{2}$ (we used the fact that $\mathbb{R}[x]$ is a commutative ring to get $bx \cdot c = bcx$), and by the aforementioned result hat $x^{2} \equiv -1 \mod x^{2}+1$, this expression becomes $(ac-bd)+(ad+bc)x$, i.e. in terms of coefficients, multiplication can be expressed as $(a, b)(c, d)=(ac-bd, ad+bc)$. Recall the definition of multiplication of complex numbers: $(a+bi)(c+di)=(ac-bd)+(ad+bc)i$. These are in fact the same. In conclusion, we identified a structure similar to the field $\mathbb{C}$ in $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ (of course, this exploration does not yet show that they are in fact the isomorphic, but it is trivial to show that as well once we have presented the first isomorphism theorem for rings, which is analogous to that of groups).
>

 
>[!definition] prime ideal
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring, then an ideal $P$ of $R$ is *prime* if it is a *proper nontrival ideal* of $R$, s.t. $\forall a, b \in R(ab \in P \implies (a \in P \lor b \in P))$.


This definition is similar to the statement of Euclid's lemma, which states that if some prime $p \in \mathbb{N}$ divides the product of two integers, then it must also divide one of the integers. The following examples explores the connection between the two:

>[!example] $p\mathbb{Z}$ is prime ideal of integer ring for $p$ prime
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}$. Let $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ be prime, and consider the left coset $p\mathbb{Z}$. By example 229 ($n\mathbb{Z}$ is an ideal of the integers) we know $p\mathbb{Z}$ is an ideal. Let $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ and suppose $ab \in p\mathbb{Z}$, then $ab=pz$ for some $z \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $p|ab$. By Euclid's lemma, it follows that either $p|a$ or $p|b$. In the former case we have $a \in p\mathbb{Z}$ and in the later we have $b \in p\mathbb{Z}$, so it follows that $p\mathbb{Z}$ is a prime ideal of $\mathbb{Z}$.

>[!definition] maximal ideal
>
>An ideal $P$ of a commutative ring $R$ is *maximal* if $P$ is *proper* and also there does not exist another proper ideal $A$ s.t. $P \subseteq A$.

>[!example] $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a maximal ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$
>
>Suppose by contradicition it is not maximal, then $\exists A$ ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$ s.t. $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a proper subset of $A$ and also $A \neq \mathbb{R}[x]$. Take $f(x) \in A \setminus \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$,  then by polynomial division we can write $f(x)=q(x)(x^{2}+1)+r(x)$ for polynomials $q(x), r(x)$ over the reals such that $r(x)=ax+b$ for some $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$, and also $r(x) \neq 0$ (otherwise $f(x) \in \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ which by supposition is not the case).
>
>Since $r(x)=f(x)-q(x)(x^{2}+1)$, and $f(x) \in A$ and also $q(x)(x^{2}+1) \in \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ by definition and by supposition $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \subset A$, then $r(x) \in A$ since $A$ being an ideal means it is closed under subtraction (since it is a subring of $\mathbb{R}[x])$. Take $ax-b \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ (with the same $a, b$), then since $A$ is an ideal and $r(x)=ax+b \in A$, then $(ax-b)(ax+b)=a^{2}x^{2}-b^{2} \in A$, and since $x^{2}+1 \in \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ and $a^{2} \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ then since $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is an ideal we have $(x^{2}+1)a^{2}=x^{2}a^{2}+a^{2} \in \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ so it is also in $A$. Again from closure under subtraction of $A$ we get $(a^{2}x^{2}+a^{2})-(a^{2}x^{2}-b^{2})=a^{2}+b^{2} \in A$.
>
>Since $r(x)=ax+b \neq 0$ then at least one of $a, b \neq 0$ so $a^{2}+b^{2} \neq 0$. Since $\mathbb{R}$ is a field and $a^{2}+b^{2} \neq 0$, then $\exists (a^{2}+b^{2})^{-1} \in \mathbb{R}$ s.t. $(a^{2}+b^{2})^{-1}(a^{2}+b^{2})=1$, and since $a^{2}+b^{2} \in A$ and $A$ is an ideal then this product is also in $A$, so $1 \in A$.
>
>Let $p(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$, then $1p(x) \in A$, but $1p(x)=p(x)$, so $\mathbb{R}[x] \subseteq A$ and also $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}[x]$ by supposition, so $A=\mathbb{R}[x]$, but we supposed $A \neq \mathbb{R}[x]$, a contradiction, so $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a maximal ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$.


>[!definition] comaximal ideals
>
>If $R$ is a ring with ideals $I, P$ s.t. $I, P$ are maximal and $I + P = R$, then we say $I, P$ are *comaximal*.

The notion of comaximal ideals somewhat corresponds to the idea of coprimes, as we demonstrate in the next example:

>[!example] coprime numbers span the integers
>
>Let $a, b$ be coprime, i.e. $\text{gcd}(a, b)=1$, and consider $\langle a, b \rangle$ the ideal generated by $a, b$. By definition, $\forall x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, $xa+yb \in \langle a, b \rangle$. By Bezout's identity, $\exists x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $xa+yb=\text{gcd}(a, b)=1$, so $1 \in \langle a, b \rangle$. Let $z \in \mathbb{Z}$, by definition of an ideal $\forall s \in \langle a, b \rangle$, $sz \in \langle a, b \rangle$, so for $s=1$ we find $z \in \langle a, b \rangle$, so $\langle a, b \rangle = \mathbb{Z}$.
>
>It can be shown that in $\mathbb{Z}$, prime ideals are also maximal, and any pair of primes are coprime, so every pair of prime ideals in $\mathbb{Z}$ is also comaximal by the above.

As another example, consider the lattice diagram of the ring $\mathbb{Z}_{36}$:


<TikzSvgde6060dc0baf5b07c02dba1e894cffec style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

Identify that $\mathbb{Z}_{36} \diagup \langle 2 \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$, and $\mathbb{Z}_{36} \diagup \langle 3 \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}_{3}$, which are both fields by proposition 227 ($\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field) since $2, 3$ are both prime. Also, by the lattice diagram it is clear that $\langle 2 \rangle, \langle 3 \rangle$ are maximal ideals of $\mathbb{Z}_{36}$, and they are in fact comaximal. This is not a coincidence, as we will soon see.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity $1$, and $P$ is an ideal of $R$, then $R \diagup P$ is an integral domain if and only if $P$ is prime.


**Proof.** $\implies$Suppose $R \diagup P$ is an integral domain and let $a, b \in R$ s.t. $ab \in P$ (there has to be at least one such pair, for example the case where $a=b=0$). Since $ab \in P$ then $ab+P=P$, but by theorem 243 (factor ring is a ring) we know that the $0$ of $R \diagup P$ is $P$ and by definition of factor ring multiplication $(a+P)(b+P)=ab+P$, so we have $ab \equiv_{P} 0$. Since $R \diagup P$ is an integral domain, then either $a \equiv_{p} 0$ or $b \equiv_{p} 0$, so either $a+P=P$ or $b+P=P$. By theorem 99 (properties of a coset) this means that either $a \in P$ or $b \in P$, so by definition $P$ is prime.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $P$ is prime and let $ab \in P$, then $ab+P=P$, again by definition $ab+P=(a+P)(b+P)$, so by the same argument either $a+P=P$ or $b+P=P$, so by definition $R \diagup P$ is an integral domain.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity $1$, and $P$ is an ideal of $R$, then $R \diagup P$ is a *field* if and only if $P$ is maximal.


**Proof.** $\implies$ Suppose $R \diagup P$ is a field and suppose by contradiction $P$ is not maximal, i.e. there exists some $P \subset A \subset R$ s.t. $A$ is an ideal of $R$. Pick $a \in A \setminus P$, then $a+P \neq P$, however since $R \diagup P$ is a field then $\exists b \in R$ s.t. $(a+P)(b+P)=ab+P=1+P$. By theorem 99 (properties of a coset), it follows that $1-ab \in P$. Since $P \subset A$ then $1-ab \in A$ as well. Finally, since $a \in A$ and $b \in R$, and $A$ is an ideal, then $ab \in A$, so $ab+(1-ab) \in A$ due to closure of $A$ as a subring of $R$, but $ab+(1-ab)=1$, so $1 \in A$. Let $r \in R$, then $r=1 \cdot r$ by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), but then by definition since $A$ is an ideal $r \in A$, so $R=A$, but we supposed $A \subset R$, a contradiction, so $P$ is maximal.

$\impliedby$ Let $a \in R$ and consider the coset $aR+P=\{ar+p | r\in R, p \in P\}$. We will show $aR+P$ is an ideal by theorem 232 (ideal test):

1. (closure under subtraction) $(ar_{1}+p_{1})-(ar_{2}+p_{2})=a(r_{1}-r_{2})+(p_{1}-p_{2})$ by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) and the commutative property of ring addition. Since $R, P$ are rings then $r_{1}-r_{2} \in R, p_{1}-p_{2} \in P$, so $aR+P$ is closed under subtraction.
2. (closure under multiplication by $r \in R$) let $r \in R$ and let $ar_{0}+p_{0} \in aR+P$, then $r(ar_{0}+p_{0})=rar_{0}+rp_{0}$ by the distributive property, and since $R$ is a commutative ring then $r(ar_{0}+p_{0})=(ar_{0}+p_{0})r$ so it suffices to show $r(ar_{0}+p_{0}) \in aR+P$. Since $P$ is an ideal and $r \in R, p_{0} \in P$, then $rp_{0} \in P$. Consider $rar_{0}$, since $R$ is commutative then $rar_{0}=arr_{0}$, and $rr_{0} \in R$ from closure under ring multiplication, so for $r'=rr_{0}$ and $p'=rp_{0}$ we have $r(ar_{0}+p_{0})=ar'+p' \in aR+P$.

We conclude $aR+P$ is an ideal. Now, since $0 \in P, 1 \in R$ (since $R$ is unital by supposition) then $a1+0=a \in aR+P$, and also by fixing $r=0$ we get $ar+p=a0+p=p$, so $P \subseteq aR+P$.

Consider two cases:
1. $a \notin P$, then since $P \subseteq aR+P$ and also $a \in aR+P$, and $P$ is maximal, it follows that $aR+P=P$. Since $1 \in R$ and $aR+P=1$ then $ar+p=1$ for some $r \in R, p \in P$. Since $p \in P$ then by coset properties $(ar+p)+ P = ar +P$, and since $ar+p=1$ then $(ar+p)+P=1+P$, so $ar+P=1+P$, which is the unity of $R \diagup P$. By definition of factor ring multiplication, $(a+P)(r+P)=ar+P=1+P$, so $r+P$ is the multiplicative inverse of $a+P$.
2. $a \in P$, then $a+P=0+P$, so $a+P$ is the zero element of $R \diagup P$.

By the above argument it follows that every non zero element of $R \diagup P$ is a unit. It remains to show that $R \diagup P$ is a commutative ring: let $a+P, b+P \in R \diagup P$ for some $a, b \in R$, then $ab=ba$ since $R$ is commutative by supposition, then $ab+P=ba+P$, then $(a+P)(b+P)=(b+P)(a+P)$, then $R \diagup P$ is a commutative ring.

We find that $R \diagup P$ is a commutative ring where every nonzero element is a unit, hence by definition 200 (field), it is a field.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a field
>
>By example 248 ($\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a maximal ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$) and by theorem 252 , $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a field.

## Ring Homomorphism

A ring homomorphism is a mapping that preserves ring structure. In this section, we give precise definition for a ring homomorphism, consider some examples, and prove that all properties we expect to be invariant under a ring homomorphism are in fact invariant.

>[!definition] ring homomorphism
>
>If $R, S$ are rings then a function $\phi: R \to S$ is a *ring homomorphism* if it satisfies the following properties $\forall a, b \in R$:
>1. $\phi(a+b)=\phi(a)+\phi(b)$ (i.e. it is a *group homomorphism* with respect to the group structure of $R, S$ under the respective addition operations)
>2. $\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)$ ($\phi$ preserves multiplication)
>3. If $R, S$ are unital, then $\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$. ($\phi$ preserve unity)

If we remove $(3)$ from the above definition, we have what is commonly known as a *rng homomorphism*. 

>[!proposition] rng homomorphism and unity
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a rng homomorphism and $R, S$ are unital, then:
>1. If $R$ is an integral domain, either $\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$ or $\phi(1_{R})=0_{S}$.
>2. If $\phi$ is surjective, then $\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$.

**Proof.**  

1. If $\phi$ is a rng homomorphism then we have $\phi(1_{R})=\phi(1_{R} 1_{R})=\phi(1_{R})\phi(1_{R})$, then $\phi(1_{R})(1_{S}-\phi(1_{R}))=0_{S}$ (here we used theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) to justify the transition). If $R$ is an integral domain, then $\phi(1_{R})(1_{S}-\phi(1_{R}))=0_{S}$ implies then either $\phi(1_{R})=0_{S}$ or $1_{S}-\phi(1_{R})=0_{S} \therefore \phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$.
2. If $\phi$ is surjective then $\forall s \in S \exists r \in R$ s.t. $\phi(r)=s$. By theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) $1_{R}r=r$, so $\phi(1_{R}r)=\phi(r)=s$, but also $\phi(1_{R}r)=\phi(1_{R})\phi(r)$ since $\phi$ preserves multiplication, so $\phi(1_{R})\phi(r)=\phi(r)$, and symmetrically $\phi(r 1_{R})=\phi(r)\phi(1_{R})=\phi(r)$, so by definition $\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] ring isomorphism
>
>A ring isomorphism is a bijective ring homomorphism. If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring isomorphism. we write $R \cong S$.

As mentioned earlier, all properties we expect a ring homomorphism to preserve are preserved:

>[!theorem] properties of ring homomorphism
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring homomorphism, then:
>1. $\forall r \in R \forall n \in \mathbb{Z}$, $\phi(n \cdot r)=n \phi(r)$ and $\phi(r^{n})=\phi(r)^{n}$, where $n \cdot r$ is the additive action defined by $\mathbb{Z}$ on $R$ and $r^{n}$ is the multiplicative action defined by $\mathbb{Z}$ or $R$.
>2. $\text{Im}(\phi)$ is a subring of $S$
>3. $\phi$ preserves ideals, i.e. if $P$ is an ideal of $R$ then $\phi(P)$ is an ideal of $\text{Im}(\phi)$.
>4. The preimage of an ideal is an ideal of the domain, i.e. if $B$ is an ideal of $S$, then $\phi^{-1}(B)$ is an ideal of $R$.
>5. If $R$ is commutative, then $\text{Im}(\phi)$ is commutative.
>6. $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is an ideal of $R$.
>7. $\phi$ is a ring isomorphism if and only if $\phi$ is surjective and also $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{0_{R}\}$.
>8. If $\phi$ is a ring isomorphism then $\phi^{-1}$ is also a ring isomorphism.


**Proof.** Most proofs are analogous to the proof of the analogous proposition stated for groups, where ideals are replaced by normal subgroups.

$1$. $\phi(n \cdot r)=n\phi(r)$ by proposition 88 (group homomorphism commutes with group operation) (in additive notation). By inspecting the proof we find that we have not used the inverse property of the group operation, so the same proof also holds for ring multiplication, so $\phi(r^{n})=\phi(r)^{n}$.

$2$. A ring homomorphism is also a group homomorphism so $\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant (S, +_{S})$. It remains to show it is closed under multiplication, then by theorem 215 (subring test) we will have $\text{Im}(\phi)$ is a subring of $S$. Let $y_{1}, y_{2} \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, then $y_{1}y_{2}=\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})$ for some $x_{1}, x_{2} \in R$. Since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, then by definition $\phi(x_{1}x_{2})=\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})=y_{1}y_{2}$, so $y_{1}y_{2} \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, so it is a subring of $R$.

$3$. From group theory we know $\phi$ preserves normal subgroups. Suppose $P$ is an ideal of $R$, then it is a normal subgroup of $R$ under addition, then $\phi(P)$ is a normal subgroup of $\text{Im}(\phi)$ (which is a subring of $S$ by $(2)$) under addition. We proceed by theorem 232 (ideal test), it remains to show that $\forall  x \in \phi(P) \forall y \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, $xy, yx \in \phi(P)$. Since $x \in \phi(P)$, $\exists a \in P$ s.t. $\phi(a)=x$, similarly $\exists b \in R$ s.t. $\phi(b)=y$, then $\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)=xy$ and also $\phi(ba)=\phi(b)\phi(a)=yx$. Since $P$ is an ideal, then both $ab, ba \in P$, so it follows that $xy, yx \in \phi(P)$, so it is an ideal of $\text{Im}(\phi)$.

$4$. Again from group theory we know $\phi^{-1}(B)$ is a subgroup of $(R, +_{R})$. We proceed by theorem 232 (ideal test). Let $a \in \phi^{-1}(B), b \in S$, then $\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)$ where $\phi(a) \in B$ and $\phi(b) \in S$, since $B$ is an ideal of $S$ then $\phi(a)\phi(b) \in B$, so $ab \in \phi^{-1}(B)$. Similarly, we find $ba \in \phi^{-1}(B)$. We conclude $\phi^{-1}(B)$ is an ideal of $R$.

$5$. Suppose $R$ is commutative and let $x, y \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, then $\exists a, b \in R$ s.t. $\phi(a)=x, \phi(b)=y$. Since $R$ is commutative, $ab=ba$, so $\phi(ab)=\phi(ba)$, so $\phi(a)\phi(b)=\phi(b)\phi(a)$, so $xy=yx$, so $\text{Im}(\phi)$ is commutative.

$6$. We already know $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is a normal subgroup of $R$. To show it is an ideal, let $a \in \text{Ker}(\phi), b \in R$, then $\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)=0_{S} \phi(b)=0_{S}$, so $ab \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$. Similarly, $\phi(ba)=\phi(b)0_{S}=0_{S}$ so $ba \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is an ideal of $R$.

$7$. Since a ring isomorphism is also a group isomorphism and this proposition is exactly the same as a known result from group theory, no new argument is needed.

$8$. We know from group theory that if $\phi$ is a group isomorphism then $\phi^{-1}$ is a group isomorphism, so it remains to show $\phi^{-1}$ preserves multiplication and unity if the rings are unital. Let $x, y \in S$, then $\exists a, b \in S$ s.t. $\phi(a)=x, \phi(b)=y$, and also $\phi(ab)=xy$ since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, so $\phi^{-1}(xy)=ab=\phi^{-1}(x)\phi^{-1}(y)$, so $\phi^{-1}$ preserves multiplication. Suppose $R, S$ are unital, then $\phi^{-1}(\phi(1_{R}))=\phi^{-1}(1_{S})=1_{R}$. We conclude $\phi^{-1}$ is a ring isomorphism.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] ring homomorphism and multiplicative inverse associate
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring homomorphism and $r \in R$ is a unit, then $\phi(r^{-1})=\phi(r)^{-1}$.

**Proof.** Suppose $r \in R$ is a unit, then $\phi(r^{-1}r)=\phi(r^{-1})\phi(r)$ and also $\phi(r^{-1}r)=\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$ by definition of ring homomorphism, so $\phi(r^{-1})\phi(r)=1_{S}$, so $\phi(r), \phi(r^{-1})$ belong to $U(S)$ the group of units of $S$, and $\phi(r^{-1})=\phi(r)^{-1}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!corollary] ring homomorphism is a group homomorphism between group of unit
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring homomorphism, and $U(R), U(S)$ are nonempty, then $\phi|_{U(R)}$ is a group homomorphism $U(R) \to U(S)$.

**Proof.** Suppose $r \in U(R)$ then $r^{-1} \in U(R)$, so $\phi(r^{-1})=\phi(r)^{-1}$, so $\phi(r) \in U(S)$, so the domain and range of $\phi$ are correct. Since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism it also satisfies all group homomorphism properties, and since $U(R), U(S)$ are groups, then $\phi|_{U(R)}$ is a group homomorphism $U(R) \to U(S)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] ring isomorphism preserves characteristic
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring isomorphism, then $\text{char}(R)=\text{char}(S)$.

**Proof.** Suppose $\text{char}(R)=0$ and $\text{char}(S) \neq 0$, then $\exists n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $\forall s \in S, n \cdot s = 0_{S}$. Let $a \in R$ s.t. $\phi(a)=s \in \phi(R)$, then $n \cdot s = s + \dots + s = \phi(a) + \dots + \phi(a) = n \cdot \phi(a)$, and also $n \cdot \phi(a)=0_{S}=\phi(0_{R})$ by supposition. By theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism), $n \cdot \phi(a) = \phi(n \cdot a)= \phi(0_{R})$. Since $\phi$ is an isomorphism, it is injective, so $n \cdot a = 0_{R}$ for every $a \in R$, but by supposition $\text{char}(R)=0$, a contradiction, so it must be that $\text{char}(R)=0$ implies $\text{char}(S)=0$.

Suppose now that $\text{char}(R)=n$ and let $s \in S$. Since $\phi$ is surjective, $\exists r \in R$ s.t. $\phi(r)=s$, so $n \cdot \phi(r) = n \cdot s$, and also $n \cdot \phi(r) = \phi(n \cdot r)=\phi(0_{R})=0_{S}$, so $n \cdot s = 0_{S}$, so $\text{char}(S) \leq n$. Suppose $m=\text{char}(S) \lt n$,  then by the same argument applied to the ring isomorphism $\phi^{-1}$ we find that $\text{char}(R) \leq m \lt n$, but by supposition $\text{char}(R)=n$, a contradiction, so we conclude $\text{char}(R)=\text{char}(S)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>If there exists a ring homomorphism from $R$ to $S$, and $R, S$ are unital with finite characteristic, then $\text{char}(S) \space  | \space \text{char}(R)$.

**Proof.** By theorem 224 (characteristic of a ring with unity), $|1_{R}|=\text{char}(R)$ and $|1_{S}|=\text{char}(S)$ since the characteristics are finite by supposition. By proposition 127 (order of element under homomorphism), we have $\text{char}(S)=|1_{S}| \space | \space |1_{R}|=\text{char}(R)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


The first isomorphism theorem also holds for rings using the appropriate definitions:
