>[!theorem] first isomorphism theorem for rings
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring homomorphism, then:
>1. $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is an ideal of $R$
>2. $\phi(R)$ is an ideal of $S$
>3. $R \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(R)$ (via $\psi: r+\text{Ker}(\phi) \to \phi(r)$)


**Proof.** First, $(1)$ and $(2)$ were already proven in theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism). For $(3)$, we know that $\psi$ is well defined and a group isomorphism by the proof for theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem), so it remains to show $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism, then by theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism) (specifically, proposition $(7)$) it will follow that $\psi$ is a ring isomorphism.

1. (multiplication is preserved) let $r_{1}+\text{Ker}(\phi), r_{2}+\text{Ker}(\phi) \in R \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $(r_{1}+\text{Ker}(\phi))(r_{2}+\text{Ker}(\phi))=r_{1}r_{2}+\text{Ker}(\phi)$. By definition, $\psi(r_{1}r_{2}+\text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(r_{1}r_{2})$. Since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, $\phi(r_{1}r_{2})=\phi(r_{1})\phi(r_{2})$. By definition, $\phi(r_{1})\phi(r_{2})=\psi(r_{1}+\text{Ker}(\phi))\psi(r_{2}+\text{Ker}(\phi))$, so $\psi$ preserves multiplication.
2. (unity is preserved) Suppose $R, S$ are unital, then $\psi(1_{R}+\text{Ker}(\phi))=\phi(1_{R})=1_{S}$ by definition.

We conclude $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism, and it is also bijective, so $R \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong \phi(R)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We proceed with a short survey of examples of ring homomorphisms:

>[!example] conjugation map
>
>Let $\phi: \mathbb{C} \to \mathbb{C}, c \mapsto c^{*}$ where $c^{*}$ is the complex conjugate of $c$, i.e. if $c=a+bi$, then $c^{*}=a-bi$. The reader can verify via the definition of complex number multiplication and addition that $\phi$ indeed preserves multiplication and addition, and also since $1_{\mathbb{C}}=1+0i=1$, we have $\phi(1+0i)=1-0i=1+0i$ so $\phi$ preserves unity, so $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism. Furthermore, $(\phi \circ \phi)(a+bi)=\phi(a-bi)=a+bi$, so $\phi$ is its own inverse (i.e. complex conjugation is an involution), so it is a bijective homomorphism. so it is a ring isomorphism, so $\phi \in \text{Aut}(\mathbb{C})$ and also $|\phi|=2$ (since $\phi^{2}=\text{id}$ as we have shown).
>
>Furthermore, note that $\phi(c)=c \iff \text{Im}(c)=0$, so the real numbers can be characterized as the set of elements in $\mathbb{C}$ for which $\phi$ acts as the identity.

>[!example] evaluation map
>
>Consider the evaluation map of a polynomial $\phi_{a}: R[x] \to R$ given by $\phi_{a}(f(x))=f(a)$ for some $a \in R$, i.e. $\phi_{a}$ *evaluates* a polynomial over $R$ given some value $a$ (i.e. $x$ in the polynomial expression is replaced with $a$, and then the expression is evaluated in $R$). Since $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\alpha_{i}x^{i}$ where $\alpha_{i} \in R$, then $f(a)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\alpha_{i}a^{i} \in R$ due to closure of a ring under addition and multiplication, so the codomain is well defined.
>
>$R[x]$ has unity if and only if $R$ has unity, and $1_{R[x]}=1_{R}$. By definition, $\phi_{a}(1_{R[x]})=1_{R}$, so $\phi_{a}$ preserves unity. Furthermore, $\phi_{a}((f+g)(x))=(f+g)(a)=f(a)+g(a)=\phi_{a}(f(x))+\phi_{a}(g(x))$, so $\phi_{a}$ preserves multiplication (here we rely on previous knowledge of polynomials), similarly we find that $\phi_{a}$ preserves multiplication, so the evaluation map is a ring homomorphism.


In group theory, once we have presented the idea of a group isomorphism, we spent a great amount of time understanding the structure of groups in terms of well-studied subgroups which we identify (via an isomorphism, or a direct product, or both) in the groups. We begin to give rings the same treatment.

>[!theorem] group action of integers on a unital ring defines a ring homomorphism
>
>If $R$ is a unital ring with unity $1$, then $\phi: \mathbb{Z} \to R, n \mapsto n \cdot 1$ is a ring homomorphism.


**Proof.** First, we show $\phi$ preserves unity: observe $\phi(1)=1 \cdot 1$ which by definition is $1$ (of $R$). Next, we show $\phi$ preserves multiplication:

For $n=0$, trivially $\forall m \in \mathbb{Z} \phi(nm)=\phi(0)=0_{R}=0_{R}\phi(m)=\phi(n)\phi(m)$, so $\phi$ preserves multiplication. Consider $mn \in \mathbb{Z}$.

For $n$ positive, we defined $n \cdot 1$ as $\sum_{i=1}^{n}1$ (where summation is via the addition of $R$), so for positive $m$, $mn \cdot 1 = \sum_{i=1}^{mn} 1$. By the associative property of addition and distributive property of multiplication over addition in $R$, we have $\sum_{i=1}^{mn}1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1$. By theorem 217 (ring arithmetic) $r=1r$, so $\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}1\sum_{i=1}^{n}1=(m \cdot 1)(n \cdot 1)$, so $\phi(mn)=\phi(m)\phi(n)$ if $0 \lt m, n$ we have that $\phi$ preserves multiplication.

For $n$ negative, we defined $n \cdot 1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}-1$ which by ring arithmetic is equal to $-\sum_{i=1}^{|n|}1$. Let $m$ be negative, then $mn$ is positive by properties of multiplication of integers (and also by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic)), then by the same argument as before $\sum_{i=1}^{mn}1=\sum_{i=1}^{|m|}1 \sum_{i=1}^{|n|}1$. By ring arithmetic, we have $ab=(-a)(-b)$, so $\sum_{i=1}^{|m|}\sum_{i=1}^{|n|}1=(-\sum_{i=1}^{|m|}1)(-\sum_{i=1}^{|n|}1) = \phi(m)\phi(n)$, so if $m, n \lt 0$ we have that $\phi$ preserves multiplication.

Since $\mathbb{Z}$ is commutative, then $\phi(nm)=\phi(mn)$, so it suffices to show that for $n$ negative and $m$ positive, $\phi$ preserves multiplication. Suppose that is the case, then $mn \lt 0$ so by definition $\phi(mn)=\sum_{i=1}^{|mn|}-1$. By the same argument we used before, $\sum_{i=1}^{|mn|}-1=\sum_{i=1}^{|m|} \sum_{i=1}^{|n|}-1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}1 \sum_{i=1}^{|n|}-1=\phi(m)\phi(n)$.

We conclude $\phi$ preserves multiplication.

Next, we show $\phi$ preserves addition. Consider $m+n \in \mathbb{Z}$. If $n$ is positive and $m$ is positive, then $\phi(m+n)=(m+n) \cdot 1 = \sum_{i=1}^{m+n}1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}1=\phi(m)+\phi(n)$ by associativity of addition in $R$. A similar argument holds for the case where $m, n$ are both negative. If $n$ is zero then then $\phi(m+n)=\phi(m+0)=\phi(m)=\phi(m)+0=\phi(m)+\phi(0)=\phi(m+n)$. 

If $m$ is positive and $n$ is negative (since addition in $\mathbb{Z}$ commutes it suffices to check only this case and not also the case where $m$ is negative and $n$ is positive), then there are two cases:
1. $|m| \geq |n|$, then $\phi(m+n)=\sum_{i=1}^{m+n}1=\sum_{i=1}^{m-|n|}1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}1+\sum_{i=1}^{|n|}-1=\phi(m)+\phi(n)$.
2. $|m| \lt |n|$, then $\phi(m+n)=\sum_{i=1}^{m+n}-1=\sum_{i=1}^{|n|-m}-1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}-1+\sum_{i=1}^{|n|}-1=\phi(m)+\phi(n)$ 

We conclude that $\phi$ satisfies all the properties of a ring homomorphism.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $R$ is a ring with unity, then:
>1. If $\text{char}(R)=n$, then $R$ contains a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$.
>2. If $\text{char}(R)=0$, then $R$ contains a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. Suppose $\text{char}(R)=n$, then by theorem 224 (characteristic of a ring with unity) it follows $|1|=n$. Consider the homomorphism described in theorem 265 (group action of integers on a unital ring defines a ring homomorphism): $\phi:\mathbb{Z} \to R,  z \mapsto z \cdot 1$ defines a ring homomorphism. Let $z \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $\phi(z)=0$, and also $\phi(z)=z \cdot 1$. By the division algorithm, $z=qn+r$ for some $q, r \in \mathbb{Z}, r \in [0, n)$, so $\phi(z)=\phi(qn+r)$. By the theorem, $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, so $\phi(qn+r)=\phi(q)\phi(n)+\phi(r)=(q \cdot 1)(n \cdot 1)+r \cdot 1$. By supposition $n \cdot 1 = 0$, so $\phi(z)=(q \cdot 1)0+r \cdot 1 =r \cdot 1$. If $r$ is a positive integer, and also by the division algorithm $r$ less than $n$, then it cannot be that $r \cdot 1 = 0$ since otherwise $|1| \leq r \lt n$, a contradiction, so it follows $r=0$, so $z=qn$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=n\mathbb{Z}$.

By theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings), $\mathbb{Z}{\diagup \text{Ker}(\phi)} = \mathbb{Z} \diagup n \mathbb{Z} = \mathbb{Z}_{n} \cong \phi(\mathbb{Z})$. By theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism), $\phi(\mathbb{Z})$ is a subring of $R$, so a subring of $R$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$, which completes the proof.

$2$. Suppose $\text{char}(R)=0$, then $|1|=\infty$ by theorem 224 (characteristic of a ring with unity), then $\phi(z)=0$ has no solution except for the trivial solution $z=0$, then $\text{Ker}(\phi)=0$, then $\mathbb{Z} \diagup \{0\} \cong \phi(\mathbb{Z})$ which is a subring of $R$. By an analogous theorem to corollary 122  stated for rings, we have $\mathbb{Z} \diagup \{0\} \cong \mathbb{Z}$, since isomorphism is an equivalence relation it follows $\mathbb{Z}$ is isomorphic to a subring of $R$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>The mappnig $\phi: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}_{n}, z \mapsto [z]_{n}$ is a ring homomorphism.

**Proof.** Observe that in $\mathbb{Z}$, $z=z \cdot 1$, so $[z]_{n}=[z \cdot 1]_{n}= z \cdot [1]_{n}$, so it is simply a special case of the isomorphism from theorem 265 (group action of integers on a unital ring defines a ring homomorphism). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] Steinitz, 1910
>
>If $\mathbb{F}$ is a field, then:
>1. If $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})=p$, then $\mathbb{F}$ contains a subfield isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$. (here, $p$ is prime)
>2. Otherwise (by corollary 226 (characteristic of integral domain), the only other case is $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})=0$), $\mathbb{F}$ contains a subfield isomorphic to $\mathbb{Q}$, the rationals.


$1$. By corollary 266 , $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})=p$ implies that $\mathbb{F}$ contains a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$. By proposition 227 ($\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field), it follows that the subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ must also be a field, so it is a subfield.

$2$. Suppose $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})=0$, then by corollary 266  $\mathbb{F}$ has a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$ via an isomorphism $\phi: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{F}$. Note that $\mathbb{Q}=\{ab^{-1} | a, b \in \mathbb{Z}, b \neq 0\}$. Since $\forall a, b \in \mathbb{Z}, \phi(a), \phi(b) \in \mathbb{F}$, we find that if $\phi(b) \neq 0$, then $\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1} \in \mathbb{F}$, i.e. $Q=\{\phi(a)\phi(b)^{-1} | a, b \in \mathbb{Z}, b \neq 0\}$ is a subset of $\mathbb{F}$. Notice that $Q$ is an isomorphic copy of $\mathbb{Q}$ (for a more formal argument, one can recall (as we will soon discuss at length) that $\mathbb{Q}$ is constructed from $\mathbb{Z}$ via equivalence relations and addition and multiplication which arise from the definitions of addition and multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$, and following the same construction we can create a field from any integral domain. If two integral domains are isomorphic, then we will find that their isomorphism naturally extends to an isomorphism between these structures. Here we simply saw a special case of this, rather hand-wavely).

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] prime subfield
>
>If $\mathbb{F}$ is a field and $\mathbb{P}$ is a subfield of $\mathbb{F}$ that is also contained in all other subfields of $\mathbb{F}$, we call $\mathbb{P}$ the *prime* subfield of $\mathbb{F}$.

>[!proposition] prime subfield is unique
>
>The prime subfield of a field is unique.

**Proof.** Suppose not, then $\mathbb{P}, \mathbb{Q}$ are both prime subfields of $\mathbb{F}$, so by definition $\mathbb{P}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$ are contained in each other, so $\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] corollary to Steinitz
>
>If $\mathbb{F}$ is a field, then:
>1. If $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})=p$ for some prime $p$, then the prime subfield of $\mathbb{F}$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$.
>2. Otherwise, the prime subfield of $\mathbb{F}$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$.

**Proof.** Suppose $\mathbb{S}$ is a subfield of $\mathbb{F}$, then by corollary 225 (unital subrings preserve characteristic) $\text{char}(\mathbb{S})=\text{char}(\mathbb{F})$, so by corollary 268 (Steinitz, 1910) if $\text{char}(\mathbb{F})$ is prime then $\mathbb{S}$ has a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{n}$, otherwise it has a subring isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$. Since the field characteristic is preserved under subfields, we can always find such subfields, so the one matching the field characteristic must be the prime subfield of $\mathbb{F}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Field of Fractions

In [Introduction to Set Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/intro_set_theory.pdf), we presented a formal constructional of the rationals $\mathbb{Q}$ from the integers $\mathbb{Z}$. In our construction, we defined the rationals via the quotient of an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^{+}$ (here we use $\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ to denote the positive subset of $\mathbb{Z}$):

>[!definition] set theoretic rational number
>
>The rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ are given by $\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^{+} \diagup \sim$, where $\sim$ is the following equivalence relation: $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff ad=bc$, where $ad, bc$ are multiplications in $\mathbb{Z}$.


The motivation behind this definition is that what we really want is to satisfy the known property of fractions, that $\frac{a}{b}=\frac{c}{d} \iff b, d \neq 0 \land ad=bc$. Equivalently, we could have simply specified that $\mathbb{Q}$ is the quotient of $\mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$.

In our construction, we have shown that $\sim$ is indeed an equivalence relation, so $\mathbb{Q}$ as a quotient group is well defined. We later defined *addition* and *multiplication* in $\mathbb{Q}$ as follows:

>[!definition] rational number arithmetic
>
>We equip $\mathbb{Q}$ with the following operations:
>1. $+: (a, b)+(c, d)=(ad+bc, bd)$ where $+$ in the RHS is addition in $\mathbb{Z}$ and juxaposition is multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$.
>2. $\times: (a, b) \times (c, d)=(ac, bd)$ where juxtaposition in the RHS is multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$.

We have shown that this operations are well defined, and further seen that $1_{\mathbb{Q}}=(1, 1)$ and $0_{\mathbb{Q}}=(0, 1)$. We have shown that $(\mathbb{Q}, +, \times)$ is a *field*, and shown that the mapping $\phi: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q}, z \mapsto (z, 1)$ is the natural injective ring homomorphism between the two structures (since $\phi(ab)=(ab, 1)=(a, 1)(b, 1)=\phi(a)\phi(b)$, $\phi(a+b)=(a+b, 1)=(a, 1)+(b, 1)=\phi(a)+\phi(b)$ and finally $\phi(1)=(1, 1)$ so $\phi$ satisfies all properties of a ring homomorphism, injectivity follows from observing that $(a, 1)=(b, 1)$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ if and only if $a1=1b$, therefore if and only if $a=b$, so $\phi$ is injective).

The reader can verify that, in that construction and in the verification of all results relevant to showing that addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$ are well defined and that $\mathbb{Q}$ equipped with those operations is a field, we only used the properties of $\mathbb{Z}$ as an integral domain (i.e. a commutative ring with unity and no zero divisors). Using only these properties, we were able to construct a new structure $(\mathbb{Q}, +, \times)$ that is a *field* and also found a natural *ring homomorphism* from $\mathbb{Z}$ to this field. The field behaves the same way we expect fractions to behave, so we called its elements *fractions*.

All of this is to say, that the exact same process, with the exact same proofs, can be carried for every integral domain $D$. If the reader is not convinced, the reader should refer to the aforementioned construction and follow the same arguments for a general integral domain $D$ instead of $\mathbb{Z}$, and consider $\mathbb{Q}$ as a quotient set of $D \times (D \setminus \{0_{D}\})$ under the equivalence relation defined the same way as in definition 272 (set theoretic rational number). Substituting the domain and range of $\phi$ with the appropriate once for this new construction, we find a ring homomorphism from $D$ to the field constructed from $D$, which, since it acts in a similar manner to fractions, we can the *field of fractions* of the integral domain $D$.

>[!definition] field of fractions
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain, then the field $F$ constructed as above is called the *field of fractions* of $D$. In this field, we denote the ordered pairs belonging to the equivalence classes used in the construction of the field by $(a, b) = \frac{a}{b}$, then we have that *addition* in the field is given by $\frac{a}{b}+\frac{c}{d}=\frac{ad+bc}{bd}$ and *multiplication* is given by $\frac{a}{b}\times \frac{c}{d}=\frac{ac}{bd}$.


>[!theorem] every integral domain is isomorphic to a subring of its field of fractions
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain and $F$ is its field of fractions, then $F$ has a subring isomorphic to $D$.

**Proof.** As argued above, $\phi: D \to F, a \mapsto \frac{a}{1}$ is an injective ring homomorphism. By theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism), $\phi(D)$ is a subring of $F$. Since $\phi$ is injective, then $\phi: D \to \phi(D)$ is a bijection, so it is an isomorphism, so $D$ is isomorphic to a subring of its field of fractions $F$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

>[!theorem] fraction fields of isomorphic integral domains are isomorphic
>
>If $D_{1}, D_{2}$ are integral domains and $F_{1}, F_{2}$ are their respective fields of fraction, and $D_{1} \cong D_{2}$, then $F_{1} \cong F_{2}$.


**Proof.** Suppose $D_{1}, D_{2}$ are integral domain isomorphic via $\phi: D_{1} \to D_{2}$. $\phi$ naturally gives rise to another mapping $\psi: F_{1} \to F_{2}$ given by $\psi: (a, b) \mapsto (\phi(a), \phi(b))$. We will show that $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism.

First, we will show $\psi$ is well-defined. Suppose $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$, then by definition (definition 272 (set theoretic rational number)) $ab'=ba'$, so $\phi(ab')=\phi(ba')$, and since $\phi$ is an isomorphism $\phi(ab')=\phi(a)\phi(b')$ and $\phi(ba')=\phi(b)\phi(a')$, which by definition implies $\psi(a, b) =(\phi(a), \phi(b)) \sim (\phi(a'), \phi(b')) = \psi(a', b')$, so $\psi$ is well-defined.

Next, we show it is a homomorphism.

Let $(a, b), (c, d) \in F_{1}$, then $(a, b)+(c, d)=(ad+bc, bd)$. Observe that

$$
\begin{gathered}
\psi(ad+bc, bd)=\\
(\phi(ad+bc), \phi(bd))=\\
(\phi(a)\phi(d)+\phi(b)\phi(c), \phi(b)\phi(d))=\\
(\phi(a), \phi(b))+(\phi(c), \phi(d))
\end{gathered}
$$

Where the second to last transition is justified since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, and the last transition is justified by the definition of addition in a field of fractions. For multiplication, by definition $(a, b)(c, d)=(ac, bd)$, so

$$
\psi(ac, bd)=(\phi(ac), \phi(bd))=(\phi(a)\phi(c), \phi(b), \phi(d))=\phi(a, b)\phi(c, d)
$$

So, $\psi$ preserves addition. Finally, $\psi(1_{D_{1}}, 1_{D_{1}})=(\phi(1_{D_{1}}), \phi(1_{D_{1}}))=(1_{D_{2}}, 1_{D_{2}})$ again since $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism.

So, we conclude, $\psi$ is a field homomorphism. Let $(a, b) \in \text{Ker}(\psi)$, then $\psi(a, b)=0$, so $(\phi(a), \phi(b))=(0, r)$ for some $r \in D_{2}$, so $\phi(a)=0$, so $a=0_{D_{1}}$ since $\phi$ is an isomorphism so in particular it is injective, so $\psi$ is injective. Next, suppose $(c, d) \in D_{2}$, then since $\phi$ is an isomorphism it is surjective and $\exists a, b \in D_{1}$ s.t. $\phi(a)=c, \phi(b)=d$, so $\psi(a, b)=(c, d)$ by definition, so $\psi$ is surjective.

We conclude that $\psi: F_{1} \to F_{2}$ is a bijective field homomorphism so it is a field isomorphism, so $F_{1} \cong F_{2}$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that the above theorem immediately gives a more satisfying proof for corollary 268 (Steinitz, 1910).

## Polynomial Ring

### Motivation

In analysis, we think of polynomials as function (potentially multivariable) whose rule is given explicitly (i.e., given a vector of variables $X$ and a polynomial $f$, an explicit rule is given for calculating $f(X)$). Furthermore, for $f$ to be a polynomial, the function rule has to be a mathematical expression consisting of indeterminates (which in analysis is synonymous with variables) and coefficients (which are taken from a field but more generally, from some commutative ring $R$), involving only addition (and subtraction), multiplication, and exponentiation to positive integer powers. Finally, we want $f$ to have *finitely many nonzero terms*. The following definition somewhat formalizes this notion, but it is still informal:

>[!definition] polynomial function (informal)
>
>A polynomial function is a function which can be described explicitly in the form of a mathematical expression consisting of indeterminates (variables) and coefficients, involving only basic arithmetical operations, that is - addition (and subtraction), multiplication, and exponentiation to positive integer powers,  consisting of finitely many nonzero terms.

As a concrete example, consider $f(x)=x^{2}+5x-x^{5}$. The expression consists only of coefficients from $\mathbb{Z}$ (the nonzero coefficients are $1, 5, -1$ and they are the coefficients of $x^{2}, x, x^{5}$ respectively), and consists of finitely many terms, so it is a polynomial.

On the other hand, consider a convergent power series, i.e. an expression of the from $f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}x^{n}$. Suppose all coefficients $a_{n}$ are elements of a ring. Is $f(x)$ a polynomial function over the ring? No, because although it is given explicitly by an expression involving only addition, multiplication, and exponentiation of coefficients and a variable $x$, it involves infinitely many terms, and it can be that $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \exists m \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $m \gt n$ and also $a_{m} \neq 0$, i.e. it potentially involves infinitely many nonzero terms. so by definition a power series, even if it is convergent, is not necessarily a polynomial. On the other hand, if from some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, all $a_{m}=0$ for every $m \gt n$, then it is a polynomial function.

As an example of the other extreme, consider $f(x)=0$. It is a polynomial, since it is a power series that consists of finitely many nonzero terms ($0$ such terms, in fact). We call it the *zero polynomial*.

Consider $f(x)=1+\sin(x)$, is $f(x)$ a polynomial function over the reals? No, because although the Taylor expansion of $\sin(x)$ is convergent everywhere in $\mathbb{R}$, i.e. $\sin(x)$ can be expressed in terms of a power series, it is:
1. Pedantically, $1+\sin(x)$ is not a power series unless we specifically allow substitution of $\sin(x)$ with its power series expansion in $\mathbb{R}$, and more importantly
2. The power series of $\sin(x)$ consists of infinitely many nonzero terms, so any expression involving $\sin(x)$ cannot express a polynomial function, as argued above.

Of course, polynomial functions need not always be considered in $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{C}$. Consider the following motivating example: consider $f(x)=x^{3}-x$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$, whose elements are $\{0, 1, 2\}$. Note that the nonzero coefficients of the polynomial are $-1$ and $1$, and $-1 \equiv 2 \mod 3$, so $f(x)$ is a polynomial function over $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$ (and also we can write $f(x)=x^{3}+2x$). Formally speaking, a function is a mapping such that every element in the domain maps to a single element in the codomain, so we can also describe $f(x)$ in terms of where it sends the elements of $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$. We find $f(0)=0^{3}-0=0, f(1)=1^{3}-1=0, f(2)=2^{3}-2=8-2=6 \equiv_{3} 0$, so we find that $f(x)=0$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$, so we have that the polynomial $f(x)=x^{3}-x$ and the zero polynomial, when considered as functions, are equivalent in $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$, so the two polynomial *functions* are the same, yet the two polynomial *expressions* are different, in the sense that different powers of $x$ have different coefficients. This example motivates defining a *polynomial expression* more carefully.

Furthermore, consider the polynomial expression $2x^{3}+1$. The same expression can describe a real-valued polynomial, where $x$ is a variable representing some real number, or a matrix polynomial where $x$ is some matrix. Indeed, we can replace the indeterminates of a polynomial by any element on which we define *scalar multiplication* and *exponentiation by positive integers*. This means that in our formalism, we should not think of $x$ as a variable that stands for some element in the commutative ring from which we take the coefficients, but instead think of $x$ as some *constant* the we *substitute* when we *evaluate* the polynomial at some value (this is precisely what we have shown in example 264 (evaluation map) although at that point we haven't introduced the proper formalism for polynomial rings).

What follows is a formal construction of polynomial rings in the abstract algebra sense.

### Construction

Our end-goal here is to provide a framework that, given a ring $R$ and a polynomial expression $\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$ where $a_{i} \in R$, identifies the polynomial with some finite sequence $(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})$, and equip these sequences with addition and multiplication s.t. these operations behave as we would expect addition and multiplication of polynomial *functions* to behave, i.e. we wish to build a *model* for polynomials within abstract algebra.

For the sake of discussion, we let $R$ be a commutative ring, and we take our coefficients from $R$. In our construction, we will build the *polynomial ring* of $R$.

We struct with a model of a power series which as we have seen in our introductory discussion, generalizes the notion of a polynomial expression.

>[!definition] formal power series over a ring
>
>A *formal power series* of a commutative ring $R$ is a sequence $\phi: \mathbb{N} \to R, n \mapsto a_{n}$, where each $a_{i}$ is called a *coefficient* of the power series, and $a_{i}$ is called the $i^{\text{th}}$ coefficient of the power series.


We call these *formal power series* since we do not require that the power series *converge*. At first it may seem strange to think of a power series, which we are conditioned to think of as $\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}x^{i}$, in terms of a sequence. We will make that connection later when we formally define what we mean by $x$, but for now consider that intuitively the expression is determined by its coefficient, so we don't lose any information when we represent of a power series in terms of its coefficients.

>[!definition] polynomial over a ring
>
>A *polynomial over a ring* $R$ is a *formal power series* s.t. $\exists n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $\forall m \in \mathbb{N} (m \gt n \implies a_{m}=0)$ where $0$ is the additive identity of $R$. Equivalently, it is a formal power series such that there are finitely many nonzero coefficients.


>[!example]
>
>Given $R=\mathbb{Z}_{3}$, the sequence $(1, 0, 2, 1, 0, \dots)$ is a polynomial, yet $(1, 0, 2, 1, 1, \dots)$ is not (where $\dots$ means that we repeat the previous element ad infinitum).

We introduce the familiar of a *degree*, and *leading* and *free* coefficients.

>[!definition] zero polynomial
>
>The polynomial $(0, \dots)$ (i.e. the polynomial whose formal power series is $\phi: n \mapsto 0$) is called the *zero polynomial*.


>[!definition] degree of a polynomial
>
>The *degree* of a polynomial $f$ is the smallest $n \in \mathbb{N}$ after which, all coefficients are zero. If $f$ is the zero polynomial, the degree of the polynomial is undefined. We denote the degree of a polynomial $f$ by $\text{deg}(f)$.

>[!example]
>
>If $f=(c, \dots)$ for some nonzero $c \in R$, then we say that $f$ is a *constant polynomial*, and by definition $\text{deg}(f)=0$. If $f=(0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 3, 1, 0, \dots)$, then by definition $\text{deg}(f)=6$.

>[!definition] leading and free coefficients
>
>If $f$ is a nonzero polynomial, then its *free coefficient* is $a_{0}$ (i.e. $\phi(0)$ as defined in definition 278 (formal power series over a ring)), and its *leading coefficient* is $a_{\text{deg}(f)}$.

>[!definition] monic polynomial
>
>If $f$ is a nonzero polynomial over a unital ring $R$ and its leading coefficient is the unity of $R$, then $f$ is *monic*.

Since polynomials have finitely many nonzero coefficients, a simpler notation for $f$ would be $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{\text{deg}(f)})$ as opposed to $(a_{0}, \dots, a_{\text{deg}(f)}, 0, \dots)$. In this notation, we can think of $f$ as some finite sequence of coefficients.

Consider two polynomials over a ring $R$. We equip it with addition and multiplication as defined below:

>[!definition] polynomial addition and multiplication
>
>If $f, g$ are polynomials over $R$, and the coefficients of $f$ are denoted $a_{i}$ and those of $g$ are denoted $b_{i}$, then:
>1. $f+g=(a_{0}+b_{0}, \dots)$ (i.e. we add the sequences component wise)
>2. $f\cdot g=(s_{0}, \dots)$ where $s_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}$ (readers may identify that this is the *discrete convolution*)


The reader should note that these definitions agree with polynomial multiplication as studied in high-school math.

>[!proposition]
>
>The above operations are well-defined.


**Proof.** We need to show that $+, \cdot$ as defined in definition 286 (polynomial addition and multiplication) are indeed binary operators on the polynomials over $R$.

For addition, observe that since we perform addition component-wise, it is the case that $\text{deg}(f+g) \leq \text{max}\{\text{deg}(f), \text{deg}(g)\}$, so $f+g$ is clearly a polynomial over $R$ (since it has finitely many nonzero terms).

For multiplication, we can see that $\text{deg}(f \cdot g) \leq \text{deg}(f) +\text{deg}(g)$, so again $f \cdot g$ is a polynomial over $R$, which would complete the proof. Denote $\text{deg}(f)=m, \text{deg}(g)=n$, and denote $f=(f_{0}, \dots, f_{m}), g=(g_{0}, \dots, g_{n})$, then $\forall i \gt m+n$, $(fg)_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{k}f_{j}g_{i-k}$, if $m \lt k$ then $f_{k}=0$, so this sum is the same as $\sum_{k=0}^{m}f_{k}g_{i-k}$. Now, both $m+n \lt i$ and $k \lt m$, so $-m \lt -k$, so $n=(m+n)-m \lt i - k$, so $i-k \gt n$, so by definition of the degree of $g$, $g_{i-k}=0$, so $\forall i \gt n+m ((f\cdot g)_{i}=0)$, so $\text{deg}(f \cdot g) \leq \text{deg}(f)+\text{deg}(g)$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Since $R$ is a ring, then addition on $R$ commutes, so instead of writing $\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}$ we can rearrange the term any way we'd like, which motivates the following notation: instead of writing $s_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}$, we write $s_{i}=\sum_{s+t=i}a_{s}b_{t}$, where $\sum_{s+t=i}$ is shorthand for *a summation over all unique $s, t$ s.t. $s+t=0$ and $s, t$ are non-negative integers*. We show the two are equivalent in the next proposition:

>[!proposition]
>
>As defined above,
>$$
>\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}=\sum_{s+t=i}a_{s}b_{t}
>$$


**Proof.** Consider $s+t=i$, equivalently we can write $t=i-s$, and by the definition from the paragraph above it is implied that $s, t \geq 0$, so we have
$$
\sum_{s+t=i}a_{s}b_{t}=\sum_{s+(i-s)=i}a_{s}b_{i-s}=a_{0}b_{i}+a_{1}b_{i-1}+a_{2}b_{i-2}+\dots+a_{i}b_{0}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}
$$

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Aside from being more compact, the above notation plays nicely with multiplication, as we will see later on.

>[!example]
>
>Consider the polynomials $f=(1, -7, 3)$ and $g=(5, 9, -1)$ over $\mathbb{Z}$. We calculate $f+g$ and $f \cdot g$ from definition:
>1. $f+g=(6, 2, 2)$
>2. We calculate $f \cdot g$ by definition. By proposition 287 , we can stop once we have calculated the coefficient at index $\text{deg}(f)+\text{deg}(g)=2+2=4$.
>	1. $(f \cdot g)_{0}=f_{0}g_{0}=1 \cdot 5 = 5$.
>	2. $(f \cdot g)_{1}=f_{0}g_{1}+f_{1}g_{0}=1 \cdot 9 + (-7) \cdot 5 = 9  - 35 = -26$
>	3. $(f \cdot g)_{2}=f_{0}g_{2}+f_{1}g_{1}+f_{2}g_{0}=1 \cdot (-1) + (-7) \cdot 9 + 3 \cdot 5 = -1 - 63 + 15 = -49$
>	4. $(f \cdot g)_{3}=f_{0}g_{3}+f_{1}g_{2}+f_{2}g_{1}+f_{0}g_{3}=0+7+27+0=34$
>	5. $(f \cdot g)_{4}=f_{0}g_{4}+f_{1}g_{3}+f_{2}g_{2}+f_{3}g_{1}+f_{4}g_{0}=3 \cdot -1 = -3$
>   We conclude $f \cdot g = (5, -26, -49, 34, -3)$.
>
>We will eventually want to show that $f$ is a faithful representative of the polynomial expression whose coefficients are the elements of $f$, i.e. that $f$ represents $1-7x+3x^{2}$, and similarly $g$ represents $5+9x-x^{2}$. Indeed, by carrying out the calculations as thought in school, we find $(1-7x+3x^{2})+(5+9x-x^{2})=6+2x+2x^{2}$, and similarly $(1-7x+3x^{2})(5+9x-x^{2})=5-26x-49x^{2}+34x^{3}-3x^{4}$.

Consider all polynomials over a ring $R$. Equipped with the operations defined above, it is the *polynomial ring* of $R$.

>[!definition] polynomial ring
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring and $R[x]$ is the set of all polynomials over $R$ as defined in definition 279 (polynomial over a ring), then $(R[x], +, \cdot)$ (as defined in definition 286 (polynomial addition and multiplication)) is called the *polynomial ring* of $R$.


>[!theorem] polynomial ring is a commutative ring
>
>$R[x]$ is a commutative ring over polynomial addition and multiplication.


**Proof.** Let $R$ be a commutative ring. First, we show $(R[x], +)$ is an abelian group.

Let $f, g, h \in R[x]$ and let $n=\text{max} \{ \text{deg}(f), \text{deg}(g), \text{deg}(h) \}$, and denote $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})$, $g=(b_{0}, \dots, b_{n})$ and $h=(c_{0}, \dots, c_{n})$, then:
1. (associative) $(f+(g+h))_{i}=a_{i}+(b_{i}+c_{i})\underset{R \text{ is a ring}}{=}(a_{i}+b_{i})+c_{i}=((f+g)+h)_{i}$
2. (identity) $(f+0)_{i}=a_{i}+0=a_{i}=f$, where $0$ is definition 281 (zero polynomial).
3. (inverse) let $f'=(-a_{0}, \dots, -a_{n})$, then $(f+f')_{i}=a_{i}-a_{i}=0$, so $f+f'=0$, so $f'=-f$.
4. (commutative) $(f+g)_{i}=a_{i}+b_{i}\underset{R\text{ is a ring}}{=}b_{i}+a_{i}=(g+f)_{i}$

We conclude $(R[x], +)$ is an abelian group.

Next, we show $R[x]$ associates and commutes under multiplication.
1. (associative) by proposition 288 , 
 
$$
\begin{gathered}
(f \cdot (g \cdot h))_{i}=\\
\sum_{s+t=i}(f_{s}(g \cdot h)_{t})=\\
\sum_{s+t=i}f_{s}\sum_{x+y=t}g_{x}h_{y} \underset{\text{distributivity in }R}{=}\\
\sum_{s+t=i}\sum_{x+y=t}f_{s}g_{x}h_{y}\underset{\text{associativity in }R \text{, counting}}{=}\\
&\sum_{s+x+y=i}f_{s}g_{x}h_{y}
\end{gathered}
$$

Similarly we show the same for $((f \cdot g) \cdot h)_{i}$, and conclude associativity.

1. (commutative) $(f \cdot g)_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{k}b_{i-k}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}b_{i-k}a_{k}$ from commutativity of $R$. Note that the latter expands to $b_{i}a_{0}+b_{i-1}a_{1}+b_{i-2}a_{2}+\dots+b_{0}a_{i}$. Since $R$ is a ring then addition in $R$ also commutes, so this expression is the same as $b_{0}a_{i}+b_{1}a_{i}+\dots+b_{i}a_{0}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}b_{k}a_{i-k}$, which by definition is the $i^{\text{th}}$ coefficient of $g \cdot f$, so multiplication commutes.

Finally, we show multiplication is distributive w.r.t addition:
1. (left distributive) $(fg+fh)_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{i}b_{k-i}+\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{i}c_{k-i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}a_{i}(b_{k-i}+c_{k-1})=(f(g+h))_{i}$
2. (right distributive) Since $fg+fh=f(g+h)$ from left distributivity and multiplication in $R[x]$ commutes by the above, $f(g+h)=(g+h)f=fg+fh=gf+hf$, so we have right distributivity.

We conclude $R[x]$ is a commutative ring. It is worth noting that the same proof, with the omission of commutativity, shows that if $R$ is a (not neccessarily commutative) ring then $R[x]$, defined as in definition 290 (polynomial ring), is a ring.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

From now on, we also assume that $R$ is also *unital* with unity $1$.

>[!proposition] unity of $R[x]$
>
>$R$ is unital if and only if $R[x]$ is unital, furthermore $1_{R[x]}=(1_{R}, 0_{R}, \dots)$, i.e. it is a constant polynomial whose only coefficient is $1_{R}$.


**Proof.** $\implies$ Suppose $R$ is unital and consider $p=(1, 0, \dots) \in R[x]$. Let $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n}) \in R[x]$. By definition $(p \cdot f)_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}p_{i}a_{k-i}$, where $p_{i}=1$ if $i=0$, otherwise $p_{i}=0$, then $\sum_{k=0}^{i}p_{i}a_{k-i}=p_{0}a_{k-0}+\cancelto{0}{\sum_{k=1}^{i}p_{i}a_{k-i}}=a_{k}$, so $p \cdot f = f$. Since $R[x]$ is commutative, $f \cdot p = f$ (we could have also shown this by calculating $f \cdot p$ again by definition), so $p$ is the unity of $R[x]$. 

$\impliedby$ Supopose $R[x]$ is unital with unity $(1, 0, \dots)$, and let $c \in R$. Consider $(c, 0, \dots) \in R[x]$, since $(1, 0, \dots)$ is the unity of $R[x]$ then $(1, 0, \dots) \cdot(c, 0, \dots) = (c, 0, \dots) \cdot (1, 0, \dots) = (c, 0, \dots)$, so we only need to calculate the first coefficient of the product polynomial. By definition, $((1, 0, \dots)(c, 0, \dots))_{0}=1 \cdot c$, so $1 \cdot c = 1$ and also $c \cdot 1 = 1$, so $1$ is the unity of $R$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Recall our original motivation - we sought a formal model which we can identify with polynomial expressions. Consider some polynomial $f \in R[x]$. Intuitively, we understand that $f=(1, 5, -3, 0, 4)$ corresponds to the univariate polynomial expression $1+5x-3x^{2}+4x^{4}$. 

One way to formalize this connection, is to *embed* $R$ into $R[x]$ as follows: $\phi: R \to R[x], r \mapsto (r, 0)$ (one can show that this is a ring homomorphism trivially). Next, we introduce an *indeterminate* $x$ into $R[x]$, with the following operations:
1. *multiplication with itself (exponentiation)*: we define $x^{0}=1_{R}$ and define $xx^{n}=x^{n}x=x^{n+1}$ for every integer $n$.
2. *multiplication with elements of $R$*: we define $r \cdot x^{n}$ as an $n+1$-tuple (representing a polynomial of degree $n+1$) s.t. $(r \cdot x^{n})_{i}=\begin{cases} r & i=n+1 \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}$ for $r \neq 0$, and for $0$ we define $r \cdot x^{n}=(0)$ the zero polynomial. Furthermore, we want $r \cdot x^{n}=x^{n} \cdot r$, in other words we want the operation to be commutative.

Once we have defined these operations, if we are willing to identify $r \in R$ with its corresponding image $\phi(r)$, then the following expression

$$
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}
$$

Is well-defined in $R[x]$ via the definitions provided above and definition 286 (polynomial addition and multiplication), and clearly

$$
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}=(a_{0}, 0)+(0, a_{1}) + (0, 0, a_{2}) + \dots (0, \dots, a_{n}) = (a_{0}, a_{1}, a_{2}, \dots, a_{n})
$$

So the sequence of coefficients faithfully represents the polynomial expression.

If $R$ is a ring with unity, we can model such $x$ via a concrete element of $R[x]$, as shown below:

>[!definition] $x$ in $R[x]$ over a unital $R$
>
>We define $x=(0, 1, 0, \dots)$ where $1$ is the unity in $R$. We define *exponentiation* of $x$ the same way we define exponentiation in any ring or group - as a shorthand for consecutive multiplications of the same element with itself.


>[!proposition] $x$ models an indeterminate
>
>$x$ as defined above models an indeterminate, namely:
>
>1. $x^{2}=(0, 0, 1, 0, \dots)$
>2. $\forall n \in \mathbb{N}$, $x^{n}=e_{n}$ where $e_{n}$ is an $n+1$-tuple s.t. $e_{n, i}=0$ if $i \neq n$ and $e_{n, n}=1$.
>3. $(c, 0) \cdot x^{n} = \vec{v}$ where $\vec{v}_{i}=c$ if $i=n+1$, otherwise $\vec{v}_{i}=0$. 


**Proof.** 

$1$. We know $\text{deg}(x^{2}) \leq \text{deg}(x)+\text{deg}(x)=2$, so it suffices to calculate $(x^{2})_{0}, (x^{2})_{1}, (x^{2})_{2}$. By definition, $(x^{2})_{0}=x_{0}x_{0}=0$, and also $(x^{2})_{1}=x_{0}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{0}=0+0=0$,  finally $(x^{2})_{2}=x_{0}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{1}+x_{2}x_{0}=0+1\cdot 1 + 0 = 1$, so $x^{2}=(0, 0, 1)$.

$2$. We prove by induction. $x^{0}=1_{R[x]}=(1, \dots)$ by definition, for $x$ the proposition holds also by definition of $x$, and we have shown the proposition holds for $x^{2}$ in $(1)$. Suppose by induction the proposition holds up to some $n$, and consider $x^{n+1}$. By definition, $x^{n+1}=x^{n}x$. Consider $(x^{n}x)_{i}=\sum_{s+t=i}(x^{n})_{s}x_{t}$. We know that $x_{t} \neq 0 \iff t=1$, so $(x^{n}x)_{0}=0$ since it involves no terms with $t=1$, and $\forall i \gt 0$, $(x^{n}x)_{i}=\sum_{s+1=i}(x^{n})_{s}$. By the induction hypothesis, $(x^{n})_{s} \neq 0 \iff s = n$, so $(x^{n}x)_{i} \neq 0 \iff n+1=i$, in which case we have $(x^{n}x)_{i}=1$, so $x^{n+1}=e_{n+1}$, which completes the proof by induction.

$3$. Denote $(c, 0)=\vec{c}$. By definition, $(\vec{c} \cdot x^{n})_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}\vec{c}_{k}(x^{n})_{i-k}$. Since $\vec{c}_{k}$ is nonzero only for $k=0$, in which case it is equal to $c$, we have that the sum is simply $c(x^{n})_{i}$. From $(2)$, we know $(x^{n})_{i}$ is nonzero (and equal to $1$) only for $i=n+1$, so we conclude $(\vec{c} \cdot x^{n})_{i}$ is $0$ if $i \neq n+1$, otherwise it is $c$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Once these results have been established, the correspondence between a polynomial expression and a polynomial sequence quickly follows.

>[!theorem] polynomial sequence and polynomial expression correspondence
>
>Let $R$ be a unital ring of coefficients and $f \in R[x]$ s.t. $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})$, then $f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$, where $x^{i}$ defined as in definition 293 ($x$ in $R[x]$ over a unital $R$).

**Proof.** Follows trivially from proposition 294 ($x$ models an indeterminate) as argued informally a few paragraphs earlier. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now have a working formal model for polynomial expressions. Now that we got all the formalism out of the way, the reader should gain a newfound appreciation for example 264 (evaluation map) - the evaluation map connects a polynomial *expression* to a polynomial *function* with variables from some domain.

We further find that if $R$ is an integral domain, then so is $R[x]$:

>[!corollary] polynomial ring of integral domain is an integral domain
>
>If $R$ is an integral domain, then $R[x]$ is an integral domain.


**Proof.** Suppose $R$ is an integral domain, then $R$ is a commutative ring with unity and no zero-divisors. From theorem 291 (polynomial ring is a commutative ring) and proposition 292 (unity of $R[x]$) we know that $R[x]$ is a commutative ring with unity, so it remains to show that $\forall f, g \in R[x]$, $f \cdot g = 0$ implies $f=0$ or $g=0$, where $0$ is the zero polynomial. If $f=0$ then we are done, otherwise we proceed by induction on $\text{deg}(f)$ to prove that $f \neq 0$ implies $g=0$.

Since $f \cdot g=0$ then $\forall i \in \mathbb{N}, (f\cdot g)_{i}=0$. If $\text{deg}(f)=0$, then $f=(c, 0, \dots)$ for some $c \in R$, so $(f \cdot g)_{i}=cg_{i}$, so $cg_{i}=0$ for every $i$. If $c=0$, then $f=0$, which we supposed is not the case, so $c \neq 0$, but then since $R$ is an integral domain then it must be that $g_{i}=0$. This holds for every $g_{i}$, so $g=0$.

Suppose the proposition holds for all $\text{deg}(f) \leq n \in \mathbb{N}$, and consider the case where $\text{deg}(f)=n+1$, then $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n}, a_{n+1})$ where $a_{i} \in R, a_{n+1} \neq 0$. By definition of addition in $R[x]$, we have $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})+(0, \dots, a_{n+1})$, so $fg=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})g+(0, \dots, a_{n+1})g=0=(0, \dots, 0)$. Let $g=(b_{1}, \dots, b_{m})$ where $m=\text{deg}(g)$. Observe that, for $i \leq n+1$ in particular, we have by definition $(f \cdot g)_{i}=a_{n+1}g_{i-(n+1)}$, which must be $0$ since $(0)_{i}=0$ (since all coefficients of the zero polynomial are zero). By the same argument as the previous case, it follows that either $a_{n+1}=0$, or $g_{i-(n+1)}=0$. So, there are two cases: either $a_{n+1}=0$, or $g=0$ i.e. $g$ is the zero polynomial. If $g=0$, then we are done, otherwise $g \neq 0$, so $a_{n+1}=0$ and we have $fg=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})g+(0, \dots, a_{n+1})g=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})g=0$, and now by the induction hypothesis since $g=0$, $(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})=(0, \dots, 0)$, and since $a_{n+1}=0$ as well then $f=0$, but we supposed $f \neq 0$, a contradiction, so $g=0$.

We conclude by induction that if $f \cdot g = 0$ and $f \neq 0$, then $g=0$, so $R[x]$ is an integral domain.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain
>
>If $D$ is an integer domain, then $\forall f, g \in D[x], f, g \neq 0$, $\text{deg}(fg)=\text{deg}(f)+\text{deg}(g)$.


**Proof.** Suppose $D$ is an integral domain. By proposition 287 , $\text{deg}(fg) \leq \text{deg}(f)+\text{deg}(g)$. Denote $\text{deg}(f)=n, \text{deg}(g)=m$, and consider $i=n+m$, then by definition $(fg)_{n+m}=\sum_{k=0}^{n+m}f_{k}g_{n+m-k}$. w.l.o.g suppose $n \lt m$ (an integral domain is a commutative ring so $fg=gf$), then

$$
\begin{gathered}
\sum_{k=0}^{n+m}f_{k}g_{n+m-k}\underset{n \lt k \implies f_{k=0}}{=}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{n}f_{k}g_{n+m-k}=\\
f_{0}g_{n+m}+f_{1}g_{n+m-1}+\dots+f_{n}g_{m} \underset{m \lt i \implies g_{i}=0}{=} \\
f_{n}g_{m}
\end{gathered}
$$

Suppose $f_{n}g_{m}=0$, then since $D$ is an integral domain either $f_{n}=0$ or $g_{m}=0$, but we know this is not the case since $\text{deg}(f)=n$ means that $f_{n} \neq 0$, and similarly $g_{m} \neq 0$, so $f_{n}g_{m} \neq 0$, so $\text{deg}(fg)=\text{deg}(f)+\text{deg}(g)$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It is worthwhile to note here that if $D$ is an integral domain then the units of $D[x]$ arise naturally from $U(D)$ the units of $D$.

>[!corollary] group of units of polynomial over integral domain
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain, then $U(D[x])=\phi(U(D))$ where $\phi$ is the natural homomorphism $\phi: D \to D[x], c \mapsto (c, 0)$.


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x) \in U(D[x])$, then $\exists g(x) \in U(D[x])$ s.t. $f(x)g(x)=1$ and also both a nonzero polynomials so their degree is well-defined. By proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain), $\text{deg}(f(x)g(x))=\text{deg}(f(x))+\text{deg}(g(x))$. Since the degree of a polynomial is non-negative and their sum must be $\text{deg}(1)=0$, then $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(g(x))=0$, so $f(x)=(c, 0)$ for some $c \in D$, and $g(x)=(d, 0)$ for some $d \in D$. By definition of polynomial multiplication, $f(x)g(x)=(cd, 0)$, which by supposition is equal to the unity of $D[x]$, i.e. $cd=1$, i.e. $c, d \in U(D)$. Conversely, if $c \in U(D)$ then $c^{-1} \in D$ and so $\phi(c)\phi(c^{-1})=1$, so $\phi(c) \in U(D[x])$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] coefficient ring homomorphism induces polynomial ring homomorphism
>
>If $\phi: R \to S$ is a ring homomorphism, then $\psi: R[x] \to S[x], (r_{0}, \dots, r_{n}) \mapsto (\phi(r_{0}), \dots, \phi(r_{n}))$ is a homomorphism between the polynomial rings of the two rings. We call such $\psi$ the natural polynomial homomorphism induced by the coefficient rings homomorphism.


**Proof.** Verify that $\psi$ satisfies all ring homomorphism properties. Left as an exercise. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Zeros and Division

>[!theorem] polynomial division algorithm
>
>If $F$ is a field, and $f(x), g(x) \in F[x]$, then $\exists! q(x), r(x) \in R[x]$ s.t. $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$, and $f(x)=q(x)g(x)+r(x)$, where $q(x)$ is called the *quotient* of the division of $f(x)$ by $g(x)$, and $r(x)$ is called the *remainder* of the division.


**Proof.** 

**Existence**: We prove by induction on $\text{deg}(f(x))$. If $\text{deg}(f(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$, then choosing $q(x)=0$ and $r(x)=f(x)$ we have $f(x)=0g(x)+f(x)=f(x)$. Consider the case where $\text{deg}(f(x)) \geq \text{deg}(g(x))$, and suppose the proposition holds up to some $n=\text{deg}(f(x))$. Denote $f=(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})$ and $\text{deg}(g(x))=m$ and $g=(b_{0}, \dots, b_{m})$. By definition of the degree of a polynomial, $a_{n}, b_{m} \neq 0$. Since $F$ is a field, $b_{m}$ has a multiplicative inverse in $F$ and so $a_{n}b_{m}^{-1} \in F$, so $a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m} \in F[x]$ (since by supposition $m \leq n$, so $n-m \in \mathbb{N}$). Now, consider $a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m}g(x)$, by definition of polynomial multiplication we have for $i=n$:

$$
\begin{gathered}
(a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m}g(x))_{n}=\\
\sum_{k=0}^{n}(a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m})_{k}b_{n-k}=\\
a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}b_{n-(n-m)}=\\
a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}b_{m}=\\
&a_{n}
\end{gathered}
$$

Consider $f'(x)=f(x)-a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m}g(x)$. Also by definition, $f'(x)_{n}=a_{n}-a_{n}=0$, and by proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain) $\text{deg}(a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m}g(x))=(n-m)+\text{deg}(g(x))=n-m+m=n$, so it follows that $\text{deg}(f'(x)) \leq n-1$ since $f'(x)_{i}=0$ for every $n \leq i$, so by the induction hypothesis $\exists q(x), r(x)$ s.t. $f'(x)=q(x)g(x)+r(x)$, so

$$
\begin{gathered}
f(x)-a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m}g(x)=f'(x)=q(x)g(x)+r(x) \\
f(x)=(q(x)+a_{n}b_{m}^{-1}x^{n-m})g(x)+r(x)
\end{gathered}
$$
Note that $r(x)$ is the same remainder term as before so it satisfies $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$, and the expression is of the form $f(x)=q'(x)g(x)+r(x)$. This completes the existence part of the proof via induction.

**Uniqueness**: We prove uniqueness similarly to the proof of Euclidean division algorithm for integers. Let $q(x), p(x), r(x), s(x) \in R[x]$ s.t. $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$ and also $\text{deg}(s(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$, and $f(x)=q(x)g(x)+r(x)$ and also $f(x)=p(x)g(x)+s(x)$, then

$$
\begin{gathered}
q(x)g(x)+r(x)=p(x)g(x)+s(x)\\
(q(x)-p(x))g(x)=s(x)-r(x)
\end{gathered}
$$

If $q(x)=p(x)$, then $q(x)-p(x)=0$, so the above becomes $0=s(x)-r(x)$, therefore $s(x)=r(x)$, and we are done. Otherwise, $q(x) \neq p(x)$, then by proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain) we have $\text{deg}(q(x)-p(x))g(x) = \text{deg}(q(x)-p(x))+\text{deg}(g(x)) \geq \text{deg}(g(x))$, so $\text{deg}(g(x)) \leq \text{deg}(s(x)-r(x))$, but as we have argued in proposition 287  $\text{deg}(s(x)-r(x))$ is at most $\text{max}\{s(x), r(x)\}$ which as we have argued above must be less than $\text{deg}(g(x))$, a contradiction, so it must be that $q(x)=p(x)$, and then also $s(x)=r(x)$, so the quotient and remainder of the division are unique.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Next, we use the fact that polynomial evaluation is a ring homomorphism to prove a useful corollary to the theorem:

>[!corollary]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $a \in F, f(x) \in F[x]$, then $f(a)$ (i.e. $f$ evaluated at $x=a$ as per example 264 (evaluation map), then written as a polynomial via the natural homomorphism) is the remainder of the division of $f(x)$ by $x-a$.


**Proof.** By the division algorithm, $\exists! q(x), r(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $f(x)=q(x)(x-a)+r(x)$. Now, $f(a)=\phi_{a}(f(x))=\phi_{a}(q(x)(x-a)+r(x))$. Since the evaluation map is a ring homomorphism, $\phi_{a}(q(x)(x-a)+r(x))=q(a)(a-a)+r(a)=q(a)0+r(a)=r(a)$, so $f(a)=r(a)$.

Now, since $\text{deg}(x-a)=1$, then either $r(x)=0$, or $\text{deg}(r(x))=0$ (since it must be less than $\text{deg}(x-a)$ by definition). By the embedding of $F$ in $F[x]$, it follows that $r(x)$ is given by some constant $r \in R$, equivalently $\forall a, b \in R (r(a)=r(b))$. As we found above, $r(a)=f(a)$, so $r(x)=f(a)$, so $f(a)$ is indeed the remainder of the division. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

>[!definition] zero of a polynomial
>
>If $f(x) \in R[x]$ and for some $a \in R$, $f(a)=0$, we say that $a$ is a *zero* of the polynomial $f(x)$.


>[!proposition]
>
>The following are equivalent:
>1. $a$ is a zero of $f(x)$
>2. $x-a$ is a factor of $f(x)$, where by *factor* we mean a polynomial that divides $f(x)$ s.t. the remainder term is zero.


**Proof.** $1 \implies 2$: By corollary 301  we have that $f(x)=q(x)(x-a)+f(a)$. By definition 302 (zero of a polynomial), $f(a)=0$, so $f(x)=q(x)(x-a)$.

$2 \implies 1$: Suppose $f(x)=q(x)(x-a)$, via evaluation $f(a)=q(a)(a-a)=0$, so $a$ is a zero of $f(x)$ by definition.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
This equivalence lets us define the *multiplicity of a zero*:

>[!definition] multiplicity of a zero
>
>If $f(x) \in R[x]$ and $a$ is a zero of $f(x)$, then the *multiplicity of $a$* is the least $n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. the polynomial $(x-a)^{n+1}$ is not a factor of $f(x)$.

When we say that a polynomial has $n$ zeros, we mean that we are also counting multiplicities. For example, $f(x)=(x-2)^{2}$ in $\mathbb{R}[x]$ has *two* zeros, where $2$ is counted twice since it has multiplicity $2$.

>[!proposition] a polynomial of degree $n$ has at most $n$ zeros
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F(x)$, then $f(x)$ has at most $\text{deg}(f(x))$ zeros, counting multiplicity.


**Proof.** We prove by contradiction. Let $\text{deg}(f(x))=n$. Suppose $f(x)$ has more than $n$ zeros, then each $i^{\text{th}}$ zero generates some factor (not necessarily distinct) $x-a_{i}$, so $\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x-a_{i}) | f(x)$. By proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain), $\text{deg}(\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x-a_{i}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1=n$.

Now, suppose $f(x)$ has more than $n$ zeros, so there must be at least another factor $x-a_{n+1}$, so $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(x-a_{n+1})|f(x)$, but then the product of these polynomials has degree $n+1$ which is greater than the degree of $f(x)$, but $f(x)=q(x)\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(x-a_{n+1})$ for some nonzero $q(x)$, so $\text{deg}(f(x))$ must be at least $n+1$, a contradiction, so $f(x)$ has at most $n$ zeros.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Recall that a PID (definition 237 (principal ideal domain)) is an integral domain where all ideals are principal. The next theorem draws a connection between $F$ being a field and its polynomial ring $F[x]$ being a PID.

>[!theorem] polynomial ring over a field is a PID
>
>If $F$ is a field, then $F[x]$ is a PID.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field, then $F$ is an integral domain, so by corollary 296 (polynomial ring of integral domain is an integral domain) we know that $F[x]$ is an integral domain. It remains to show that all ideals of $F[x]$ are principal.

Let $I \in F$ be an ideal of $F$. If $I=\{0\}$, then $I=\langle 0 \rangle$ by theorem 231 . Otherwise, suppose $I \neq \{0\}$, and consider the set $I \setminus \{0\}$ of all nonzero polynomials in $I$. Since by definition the degree of a nonzero polynomial is a natural number, by the well-ordering principle $I \setminus \{0\}$ has a polynomial of least degree (not necessarily unique), denote this polynomial $g(x)$. Let $f(x) \in I$ and suppose $f(x) \neq 0$, then by theorem 300 (polynomial division algorithm) $f(x)=q(x)g(x)+r(x)$ for unique $q(x), r(x) \in F[x]$, where $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(g(x))$ or $r(x)=0$.

By supposition $f(x) \in I$ and also $g(x) \in I$. Since $g(x) \in I$ and $I$ is an ideal, $q(x)g(x) \in I$. Since $q(x)g(x), f(x) \in I$ then from closure under subtraction, $f(x)-q(x)g(x) \in I$, but $f(x)-q(x)g(x)=r(x)$. If $r(x)$ is nonzero then we have found a polynomial in $r(x)$ with degree less than $g(x)$, violating the minimality of the degree of $g(x)$, so it must be that $r(x)=0$, so $f(x)=q(x)g(x)$, so $f(x) \in \langle g(x) \rangle$ by definition. Since this is true for every $f(x) \in I$, and since trivially $\langle g(x) \rangle \subseteq I$, then $\langle g(x) \rangle = I$, so $F[x]$ is a PID.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $I$ is a nonzero ideal of $F[x]$, and $g(x) \in I$, then $I=\langle g(x) \rangle$ if and only if $g(x)$ is a nonzero polynomial of least degree in $I$.


**Proof.** The above proof is a constructive proof wherein we showed exactly this. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] polynomial field generator
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$,  then $\langle p(x) \rangle = F[x]$ if and only if $\text{deg}(p(x))=0$.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$.

$\implies$ Suppose $\langle p(x) \rangle = F[x]$, then $\forall f(x) \in F[x] \exists h(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $f(x)=p(x)h(x)$. Suppose $f(x)$ is constant nonzero, then $\text{deg}(f(x))=0$ and also $p(x), h(x) \neq 0$ (if $p(x)=0$ then $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is the trivial zero ideal so it cannot be $F[x]$, if $h(x)=0$ then $p(x)h(x)=0$ but we suppose $f(x) \neq 0$). By the degree equation, $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(p(x))+\text{deg}(h(x))$, where both operands on the RHS are non-negative and the LHS is $0$ by supposition, so it must be that $\text{deg}(p(x))=0$ and $\text{deg}(h(x))=0$, so in particular $\text{deg}(p(x))=0$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $\text{deg}(p(x))=0$ (which also implies $p(x) \neq 0$), then $p(x)=c$ for some nonzero constant $c \in F$. Let $f(x) \in F[x]$, since $F$ is a field and $c \neq 0$ then $c^{-1} \in F$, so $h(x)=c^{-1} \in F[x]$, and from closure $h(x)f(x) \in F[x]$. Consider $p(x)(h(x)f(x)) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$, by commutativity of multiplication in $F[x]$ we have $p(x)(h(x)f(x))=(p(x)h(x))f(x)=(cc^{-1})f(x)=f(x)$, so $f(x) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$, so $\langle p(x) \rangle = F[x]$. 
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now get back to our example of $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. We saw, in example 244 (identifying the complex numbers in factor ring of polynomials), that the structure of this quotient ring seems suspiciously similar to that of $\mathbb{C}$. We later saw that the principle ideal generated by $x^{2}+1$ is maximal inexample 248 ($\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a maximal ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$), which by theorem 252  makes $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ maximal. Now we will show formally that this structure is indeed isomorphic to $\mathbb{C}$.

>[!example] $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \cong \mathbb{C}$
>
>Consider $\phi: \mathbb{R}[x] \to \mathbb{C}, f(x) \mapsto f(i)$, then:
>1. $\phi(1)=1$
>2. $\phi(f(x)) \cdot \phi(g(x)) = f(i)g(i) = (f \circ g)i = \phi((f \circ g)(x))$
>3. $\phi(f(x))+\phi(g(x))=f(i)+g(i)=(f+g)(i)=(\phi)((f+g)(x))$
>   
>So $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, and also $\phi(f(x))=0 \iff f(i)=0$. Take such nonzero $f(x) \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$. If $\text{deg}(f(x))=0$, then $f(x)=c$ for some nonzero $c \in \mathbb{R}$, and clearly $c \neq 0$, so there is no such $f(x)$ with degree $0$. If $\text{deg}(f(x))=1$, then $f(x)=mx+b$ for some $m, b \in \mathbb{R}$, then $f(i)=mi+b$, so $f(i)=0 \therefore b = -mi$, but $-mi$ is purely imaginary whereas $b$ is real, so this is also impossible, so $\text{deg}(f(x))$ is at least $2$.
>
>Consider $f(x)=x^{2}+1$, since $i^{2}=-1$ (this is the defining property of $i$ in $\mathbb{C}$) then $f(i)=i^{2}+1=-1+1=0$, so $f(i)=0$, so $x^{2}+1 \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$. By theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism), $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is an ideal of $\mathbb{R}[x]$, so by
> corollary 307  $\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = \text{Ker}(\phi)$.
> 
By theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings), $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \cong \text{Im}(\phi)$. Let $a+bi \in \mathbb{C}$, then given $f(x)=a+bx$, we have $\phi(f(x))=f(i)=a+bi$, so $\phi$ is surjective, so its image is $\mathbb{C}$, so we have that $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \cong \mathbb{C}$.


## Polynomial Factorization

From high school algebra we have some basic informal understanding of polynomial factorization. For example, we know that $f(x)=x^{2}-4$ can be written as $f(x)=(x-2)(x+2)$, or that $h(x)=5x^{3}+15$ can be written as $h(x)=5(x^{3}+5)$. In both cases, we took a polynomial and expressed it as the product of two other polynomials, which we call *factors*. However, context is important - consider $g(x)=x^{2}-1$. We know that in $\mathbb{C}$, $g(x)=(x-i)(x+i)$, however if we consider the polynomial in $\mathbb{R}[x]$ then we have no such coefficient $i \in \mathbb{R}$, so $g(x)$ in $\mathbb{R}[x]$ *cannot* be factorized further. In this section, we discuss how we factor polynomials (mostly using the term *reduction* instead of *factorization*).

>[!definition] irreducible polynomial
>
>If:
>1. $D$ is an integral domain
>2. $f(x) \in D[x]$ is nonzero polynomial and not a unit in $D[x]$, and 
>3. $\forall g(x), h(x) \in D[x], g(x), h(x) \neq 1$, if $f(x)=g(x)h(x)$ then one of $g(x), h(x)$ is a unit in $D[x]$, 
> 
>then we say that $f(x)$ is *irreducible* over $D$. Otherwise, we say $f(x)$ is *reducible* over $D$.


The idea we attempt to capture in the above definition is that, a polynomial is *reducible* if it has a non-trivial factorization. Of course, *non-trivial* is relatively ill-defined, hence the definition. 

>[!proposition] zero implies reducible
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain and $f(x) \in D[x]$ a polynomial of degree at least $2$ has a zero in $D$, then $f(x)$ is reducible in $D$.


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x) \in D[x]$ has a zero $a \in D$, then for $g(x)=x-a$, $\exists h(x)$ s.t. $g(x)h(x)=f(x)$ per proposition 303  . By corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain), $g(x)$ cannot be a unit of $D[x]$ since it has nonzero degree. By proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain), $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(g(x))+\text{deg}(h(x))=1+\text{deg}(h(x))$. By supposition, $\text{deg}(f(x))$ is at least $2$, so it follows that $\text{deg}(h(x))$ is at least $1$, so again by corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain), $h(x) \notin U(D[x])$, so we have found some $g(x), h(x) \in D[x]$ s.t. $g(x), h(x) \notin U(D[x])$ and also $f(x)=g(x)h(x)$, so by definition 310 (irreducible polynomial) we say that $f(x)$ is *reducible* in $D$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that the converse is not always true, as illustrated in the next example:

>[!example]
>
>Consider $2x^{2}+2 \in \mathbb{Z}$, clearly $2 \notin U(\mathbb{Z})$ so $2 \notin U(\mathbb{Z}[x])$ by corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain), and also $x^{2}+1$ has degree $2$ so it too is not a unit of $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, and also $2x^{2}+2=2(x^{2}+1)$, so $2x^{2}+2$ is reducible in $\mathbb{Z}$, however $x^{2}+1$ has no zero in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ (since we know that the roots of polynomial are in fact $\pm i$), so the converse of proposition 311 (zero implies reducible) is not true.
>
>It is worth noting that $2x^{2}+2$, while reducible in $\mathbb{Z}$, is *not* reducible in $\mathbb{Q}$, since $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field and so $2 \in U(\mathbb{Q})$ so $2 \in U(\mathbb{Q}[x])$, so $2(x^{2}+1)$ no longer demonstrated that $2x^{2}+2$ is reducible since $2$ is a unit in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$. In this case, we have not disproven the converse of the previous proposition.

The second part of the example hints at the possibility that, at least in some cases, the converse of proposition 311 (zero implies reducible) does hold for fields (the main difference between $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$ is that $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field whereas $\mathbb{Z}$ is an integral domain). The next theorem proves that this is indeed the case for polynomials of degrees $2$ and $3$:

>[!theorem]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$ is a polynomial of degree $2$ or $3$, then $f(x)$ is reducible in $F$ if and only if it has a zero in $F$.


**Proof.** Let $F$ be a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$ be a polynomial of degree $2$ or $3$.

$\implies$ Suppose $f(x)$ is reducible in $F$, so $\exists g(x), h(x) \in F[x]$ non-units and nonzero so they have degree at least $1$ (by corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain)) s.t. $f(x)=g(x)h(x)$, so $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(g(x))+\text{deg}(h(x))$.

There are two cases: $\text{deg}(f(x))=2$, in which case the degrees of both $g(x)$ and $h(x)$ are $1$, or $\text{deg}(f(x))=3$, in which case the degree of one of the polynomials factors is $1$ while the other is $2$, so in both cases one of the polynomials has degree $1$. Suppose w.l.o.g that this is $g(x)$. Since $g(x)$ has degree $1$ it is of the form $ax+b$ for some $a, b \in F$. Since $F$ is a field, then $-ba^{-1} \in F$, and clearly $g(-ba^{-1})=0$, so $g(x)$ has a zero in $F$, and since $g(x)$ is a factor of $f(x)$ then $-ba^{-1}$ is also a zero of $f(x)$, so $f(x)$ has a zero in $F$. 

$\impliedby$Demonstrated in proposition 311 (zero implies reducible).

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that the theorem fails for degrees greater than $3$. As an example, consider a polynomial of degree $4$, $x^{4}+4x^{2}+4=(x^{2}+2)^{2}$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ Clearly it is reducible in $\mathbb{Q}$ since it has $x^{2}+2 \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ as a factor, however $(x^{2}+2)^{2}$ has no zeros in $\mathbb{Q}$.

### Content of a Polynomial and Primitive Polynomials

In this section, we initially limit our discussion to $\mathbb{Z}[x]$.

To motivate our next definition, consider $f(x)=2x^{2}+6x+12$. We can factor out $2$ and get $f(x)=2(x^{2}+3x+6)$. Suppose we wish to write $f(x)=nh(x)$ for some $n \in \mathbb{Z}$ (here we finally stop the ceremonious mentioning that formally, this $n$ is actually an element of $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ induced via the natural homomorphism $\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}[x]$, and refer to $n$ simply as an element of $\mathbb{Z}$), $h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$. If $h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ then all its coefficients must also be integers, so any such $n$ must be a common divisor of all coefficients of $f(x)$ (because $f_{i}=(nh(x))_{i}=nh_{i}$, so $n|f_{i}$ must ). From number theory, we know that such maximal $n$ exists, and it is the greatest common divisor of all coefficients of $f(x)$. Since $\text{gcd}(2, 6, 12)=2$, then the factor we produced is maximal. In the context of polyonmials with integers coefficients, we call the $\text{gcd}$ of these coefficients the *content* of the polynomial, and we call polynomials for which $\text{gcd}(f_{0}, \dots, f_{n})=1$ *primitive polynomials*.

>[!definition] content of a polynomial
>
>If $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ and $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$, then the greatest common divisor of its coefficients $\text{gcd}(a_{0}, \dots, a_{n})$ is called the *content* of $f(x)$. If the content of $f(x)$ is $1$, then we say that $f(x)$ is *primitive*.

Clearly, every polynomial where one of the nonzero coefficients is $1$ is primitive, so in particular monic polynomials (in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$) are primitive. The content captures the natural factorization of $f(x)$ in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ (since the gcd of all coefficients can trivially be factored out), which then produces a primitive polynomial, as shown in the next proposition:

>[!proposition] primitive representative
>
>If $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is non-zero and $c$ is its content and $c \neq 1$, then $f(x)=ch(x)$ where $h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is primitive, and $h(x)$ is unique. We call this $h(x)$ the *primitive representative* of $f(x)$.


**Proof.** By definition $c$ is a divisor of all coefficients of $f(x)$ so clearly the constant polynomial $g(x)=c$ divides $f(x)$, so by theorem 300 (polynomial division algorithm), there exists a unique $h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ s.t. $f(x)=ch(x)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] Gauss' lemma (primitivity)
>
>If $f(x), g(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ are primitive, then their product $f(x)g(x)$ is primitive.


**Proof.** Suppose by contradiction $f(x), g(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ are primitive while $f(x)g(x)$ is not. Since $f(x)g(x)$ is non-primitive, then its content is not $1$ and hence divisible by some prime $p$, so $f(x)g(x)=p \cdot h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$. 

Via the natural homomorphism $\phi: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}_{p}, z \mapsto [z]_{p}$, we induce a ring homomorphism $\psi: \mathbb{Z}[x] \to \mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ (see proposition 299 (coefficient ring homomorphism induces polynomial ring homomorphism)). Since $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism, we have:

$$
\psi(p \cdot h(x))=\psi(p) \psi(h(x)) \underset{p \equiv 0 \mod p}{=}0
$$

Also, since $f(x)g(x)=p \cdot h(x)$:

$$
0 = \psi(p \cdot h (x)) = \psi(f(x)g(x)) = \psi(f(x)) \cdot \psi(g(x))
$$

Since $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is an integral domain, then $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ is an integral domain by corollary 296 (polynomial ring of integral domain is an integral domain), so the above implies that either $\psi(f(x))=0$ or $\psi(g(x))=0$, but this is true if and only if $p$ is a factor of the content of either $f(x), g(x)$, but by supposition both contents are $1$, so this is impossible, a contradiction, so $f(x)g(x)$ must be primitive. 
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] Gauss' lemma (reducibility)
>
>If a non-constant polynomial $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is reducible in $\mathbb{Q}$, then it is reducible in $\mathbb{Z}$.


**Proof.** Let $f(x)$ considered in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$, and suppose it is reducible, so $\exists \overline{g(x)}, \overline{h(x)} \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ both non-units s.t. $f(x)=\overline{g(x)}\space \overline{h(x)}$. Let $m, n$ be the $\text{lcm}$ of the denominators of the coefficients of $\overline{g(x)}, \overline{h(x)}$ respectively, and denote $g(x)=m\overline{g(x)}, h(x)=n\overline{h(x)}$, then $g(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x], h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$. Let $c_{g}, c_{h}$ be the contents of $g(x), h(x)$, then by proposition 315 (primitive representative), $\exists! \widetilde{g(x)}, \widetilde{h(x)} \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ primitive polyonmials s.t. $g(x)=c_{g}\widetilde{g(x)}, h(x)=c_{h}\widetilde{h(x)}$.

Since $\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{Z}$ are integral domains, then $\mathbb{Q}[x], \mathbb{Z}[x]$ are integral domains by corollary 296 (polynomial ring of integral domain is an integral domain), so multiplication commutes, so $g(x)h(x)=c_{g}c_{h}\widetilde{g(x)}\space\widetilde{h(x)}$, and $f(x)=\overline{g(x)} \space \overline{h(x)}=\frac{1}{mn}c_{g}c_{h} \widetilde{g(x)} \space \widetilde{h(x)}$. Now, by theorem 316 (Gauss' lemma (primitivity)), since $\widetilde{g(x)}, \widetilde{h(x)}$ are primitive then their product $\widetilde{f(x)}$ is primitive, so the content of $f(x)$ is given by $\frac{c_{g}c_{h}}{mn}$, i.e. $\frac{c_{g}c_{h}}{mn} \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $f(x)$ is reducible over $\mathbb{Z}$ with factors $\frac{c_{g}c_{h}}{mn}\widetilde{g(x)}, \widetilde{h(x)}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ is prime and $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a non-constant polynomial, and $\overline{f(x)}$ is the corresponding polynomial of $f(x)$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ produced via proposition 299 (coefficient ring homomorphism induces polynomial ring homomorphism), then if $\overline{f(x)}$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$, and $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(\overline{f(x)})$, then $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$. 


**Proof.** Suppose by contradiction $\overline{f(x)}$ satisfying the conditions of the theorem is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ but reducible in $\mathbb{Q}$, then by theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)) we can find $h(x), g(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ s.t. $f(x)=h(x)g(x)$ where $h(x), g(x)$ are not units in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ (where $h(x), g(x)$ were attained by multiplying non-units in $\mathbb{Q}$, see the proof of that theorem for the construction of these polynomials). 

Consider the corresponding polynomials in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$, $\overline{h(x)}, \overline{g(x)}$, then since the correspondence is a ring homomorphism $\overline{f(x)}=\overline{h(x)} \cdot \overline{g(x)}$, Since by supposition $\overline{f(x)}$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$, then it must be that either $\overline{h(x)}$ or $\overline{g(x)}$ is a unit in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$. 

Suppose w.l.o.g that $\overline{h(x)}$ is a unit. By corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain), we have that $\overline{h(x)} \in U(p)$, so $\text{deg}(\overline{h(x)})=0$. Since $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x], \mathbb{Z}[x]$ are both integral domains, $\text{deg}(\overline{f(x)})=\text{deg}(\overline{g(x)})$, and also $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(h(x))+\text{deg}(g(x))$. By supposition, $\text{deg}(f(x))=\text{deg}(\overline{f(x)})=0+\text{deg}(\overline{g(x)})=\text{deg}(\overline{g(x)})$. Also, since the leading coefficient of $g(x)$ might be a multiple of $p$, then $\text{deg}(\overline{f(x)})=\text{deg}(\overline{g(x)}) \leq \text{deg}(g(x))$.

Suppose $\text{deg}(h(x))=0$, and recall that by construction $h(x)=ah'(x)$ where $h'(x) \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$, so it follows that $\text{deg}(h'(x))=0$ (since $\text{deg}(h(x))=\text{deg}(a)+\text{deg}(h'(x))=0+\text{deg}(h'(x))$), but then $h'(x)$ is a unit in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$, a contradiction, so $\text{deg}(h(x))$ is greater than $0$, so $\text{deg}(g(x)) \lt \text{deg}(h(x))+\text{deg}(g(x))=\text{deg}(f(x))$.

Putting everything together, we get $\text{deg}(\overline{f(x)}) \lt \text{deg}(f(x))$, a contradiction, so it must be that $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] showing a polynomial is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$
>
>Using the theorem, we show $f(x)=27x^{3}+5x^{2}+6x-1$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Q}$. Consider the polynomial in $\mod 2$. After reducing the coefficients $\mod 2$, we find $\overline{f(x)}=x^{3}+x^{2}+1$. It is a polynomial of degree $3$, so by theorem 313  it is reducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ if and only if it has a zero in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$. Plugging $x=0, x=1$, we find that $\overline{f(0)}=0+0+1=1, \overline{f(1)}=1+1+1=1$, so $\overline{f}(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$, so by theorem 318  it is irreducible in $\mathbb{Q}$.

Using the theorem we can also demonstrate that polynomial in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ are irreducible in $\mathbb{Q}$, by replacing the polynomial with rational coefficients with a polynomial with integer coefficients by multiplying the polynomial with the $\text{lcm}$ of all denominators of coefficients, as demonstrated in theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)). See the example below:

>[!example] showing a polynomial in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$
>
>Consider $f(x)=(3/7)x^{4}-(2/7)x^{2}+(9/35)x+3/5$ in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$. The least common divisor of the denominators of the coefficients is $35$, so $35f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$, denote it $h(x)$, then $h(x)=35f(x)=15x^{4}+10x^{2}-9x+21$. Consider the polynomial in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$, then it reduces to $\overline{h(x)}=x^{4}+x+1$. We will show that all possible factorizations of $\overline{h(x)}$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$ involve units, thus $\overline{h(x)}$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ by definition.
>
>Consider $0, 1 \in \mathbb{Z}_{2}$, then $\overline{h(0)}=1, \overline{h(1)}=1+1+1=1$, so $\overline{h(x)}$ has no zeros in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$, so it has no linear factors. If $g(x)$ is a factor of degree $2$ of $\overline{h(x)}$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$, then there are $4$ cases: $g(x)=x^{2}$, or $g(x)=x^{2}+1$, or $g(x)=x^{2}+x$, or $g(x)=x^{2}+x+1$ (convince yourself that there are no other polynomials of degree $2$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$). If $g(x)$ is a factor of $\overline{h(x)}$, then $\exists q(x) \in \mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$ s.t. $g(x)q(x)=\overline{h(x)}$, and by the degree equation since $\text{deg}(\overline{h(x)})=4, \text{deg}(g(x))=2$, it must be that $\text{deg}(q(x))=2$.
>
>Note that, $x^{2}, x^{2}+1, x^{2}+x$ both have zeros in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$, so they cannot be factors of $\overline{h(x)}$. It follows that if $\overline{h(x)}$ has a factor of degree $2$ in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$, then it must be $x^{2}+x+1$, so it must be that $\overline{h(x)}=(x^{2}+x+1)^{2}=x^{4}+2x^{3}+3x^{2}+2x+1=x^{4}+x^{2}+1$, but clearly this is not $x^{4}+x+1=\overline{h(x)}$, so $\overline{h(x)}$ has no factor of degree $2$.
>
>Next, if the degree of the factor is $0$, then by corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain) it is a unit of $\mathbb{Z}_{2}[x]$. Similarly, if the degree is $4$, then that of the other factor is $0$, so again we have a unit. If the degree is $3$, then that of the other factor is $1$, so it is linear, but $\overline{h(x)}$ has no linear factors as we have demonstrated. Finally, as argued above, $\overline{h(x)}$ has no factors of degree $2$. We conclude that $\overline{h(x)}$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ by definition, so by theorem 318 , $h(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.
>
>Suppose $f(x)$ is reducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, then $f(x)=f_{1}(x)f_{2}(x)$ both not units, but then $35f_{1}(x)$ is also clearly not a unit in $\mathbb{Q}$ since it does not have degree $0$ since $\text{deg}(f_{1}(x)) \gt 0$, but then $35f_{1}(x) \cdot f_{2}(x)$ is a factorization of $h(x)$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ s.t. none of the factors is a unit, a contradiction to $h(x)$ being irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, so it follows that $f(x)$ must also be irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.

We state Eisenstein's criterion without proof here. We will prove it later, as part of [[#Unique Factorization Domain]]. Eisenstein's criterion provides a very powerful tool for determining when a polynomial with integer coefficients is reducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.

>[!theorem] Eisenstein's criterion
>
>Let $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i} \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ where $n=\text{deg}(f(x))$ and $2 \leq n$. If $\exists p \in \mathbb{N}$ prime such that:
>1. $p \not \mid a_{n}$
>2. $p^{2} \not \mid a_{0}$
>3. $p \mid a_{j}$ for $j \in [0, n-1]$
>Then $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.


### Unique Factorization

In this section, we show that if $F$ is an field, then $F[x]$ is a unique factorization domain. Informally, a unique factorization domain is an integral domain where an analogous statement to the fundamental theorem of arithmetic holds. We define this term formally later when discussing divisibility and factorization in general integral domains. For now, we focus on showing that such statement does hold for $F[x]$.

First, we show that irreducible polynomials are building blocks of fields, and later we argue that an analogous statement to Euclid's lemma for integers holds for polynomials, where irreducible polynomials assume the role of primes.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$, then $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is a maximal ideal in $F[x]$ if and only if $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field, then it is also an integral domain, and by corollary 296 (polynomial ring of integral domain is an integral domain), $F[x]$ is an integral group. Let $p(x) \in F[x]$.

$\implies$Suppose $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is maximal, then $p(x)$ is nonzero and nonconstant (since the ideal is proper, see corollary 308 (polynomial field generator)), and suppose via contradiction that $p(x)$ is reducible over $F$, i.e. $\exists g(x), h(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $g(x), h(x)$ are not units of $F[x]$, and also $p(x)=g(x)h(x)$. Since we suppose $g(x), h(x)$ are not units, then by corollary 298 (group of units of polynomial over integral domain) it must be that they are non-constant, non-zero polynomials, i.e. they have degree greater than $0$.

Consider $\langle g(x) \rangle$. By definition 233 (principal ideal), $g(h)h(x)=p(x) \in \langle g(x) \rangle$, so from closure of the ideal it follows that $\langle p(x) \rangle \subseteq \langle g(x) \rangle$. By supposition, $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is maximal, so it must be that either:
1. $\langle p(x) \rangle = \langle g(x) \rangle$, in which case by corollary 307  it follows that $\text{deg}(p(x))=\text{deg}(g(x))$, so by proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain) it follows that $\text{deg}(h(x))=0$, a contradiction to $h(x)$ not being a unit, so this case is impossible.
2. $\langle g(x) \rangle = F[x]$, in which case $\text{deg}(g(x))=0$ by corollary 308 (polynomial field generator), but then $g(x)$ is a unit, so this case is impossible.

Both cases led to a contradiction, so it must be that $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$, and suppose $I \in F[x]$ is an ideal of $F[x]$ such that $\langle p(x) \rangle \subseteq I$. By corollary 307 , it must be that $I=\langle g(x) \rangle$ for some $g(x) \in F[x]$. Since by supposition $\langle p(x) \rangle \subseteq I = \langle g(x) \rangle$, then $p(x)=g(x)h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in F[x]$. Since by supposition $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$, then either of $g(x), h(x)$ is a unit. If $g(x)$ is a unit, then $\langle g(x) \rangle = F[x]$ by corollary 308 (polynomial field generator). If $h(x)$ is a unit, then by the degree equation $\text{deg}(p(x))=\text{deg}(g(x))$, so by corollary 307  $p(x)$ is also a generator of $\langle g(x) \rangle$, so $\langle p(x) \rangle = \langle g(x) \rangle$. We conclude $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is maximal.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
