>[!corollary]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible over $F$, then $F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle$ is a field.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible over $F$. By theorem 322 , it follows that $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is a maximal ideal in $F[x]$. By theorem 252 , $F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle$ is a field. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] Euclid's lemma for polynomials
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible over $F$, and $\exists a(x), b(x) \in F[x]$ such that $p(x) | a(x)b(x)$ (i.e. $p(x)$ divides the product $a(x)b(x)$). In other words if, $p(x)|a(x)b(x)$, then either $p(x)|a(x)$ or $p(x)|b(x)$.


**Proof.** It should be noted that it is also relatively straightforward to prove this in a manner analogous to the classical proof of Euclid's lemma for integers, but for the sake of discussion we prove the theorem using the machinery we developed here.

By corollary 323 , $F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle$ is a field, so in particular it is an integral domain, so by theorem 251 , $\langle p(x) \rangle$ is a prime ideal. Since by supposition $p(x) | a(x)b(x)$, then $a(x)b(x)=p(x)h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in F[x]$, in other words $a(x)b(x) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$. By definition 245 (prime ideal), it follows that either $a(x) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$, or $b(x) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$, which implies either $p(x)|a(x)$ or $p(x)|b(x)$, which completes the proof.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It should be noted that, as in $\mathbb{Z}$, this is an if and only if relation:

>[!proposition] converse Euclid's lemma for polynomials
>
>If $p(x) \in F[x]$ and $\forall a(x), b(x) \in F[x]$, we find that $p(x) | a(x)b(x)$ implies either $p(x)|a(x)$ or $p(x)|b(x)$, then $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$.


**Proof.** As with $\mathbb{Z}$ (see ([Elementary Number Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/elementary_number_theory.pdf)), we prove via contraposition: suppose $p(x)$ is reducible over $F$, then we show there exists $a(x), b(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $p(x)| a(x)b(x)$ and yet $p(x) \not \mid a(x)$ and also $p(x) \not \mid b(x)$.

By supposition that $p(x)$ is reducible, $\exists a(x), b(x) \in F[x]$ non-unit s.t. $p(x)=a(x)b(x)$, so $p(x)|a(x)b(x)$ with $q(x)=1$ and $r(x)=0$. By the degree equation, $\text{deg}(p(x))=\text{deg}(a(x))+\text{deg}(b(x))$, and by supposition both are non-unit so both have degree greater than $0$, so $\text{deg}(a(x)), \text{deg}(b(x)) \lt \text{deg}(p(x))$. Suppose $p(x)|a(x)$, then $a(x) \in \langle p(x) \rangle$ but by corollary 307  $p(x)$ must be a polynomial of least degree in its principal ideal, so $a(x) \notin \langle p(x) \rangle$, so $p(x) \not \mid a(x)$, and likewise for $b(x)$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] Extended Euclid's lemma for polynomials
>
>If $p(x) \in F[x]$ and $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$ and $p(x) \mid \prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)$ where $F_{i}=\{f_{1}(x), \dots, f_{n}(x)\} \subseteq F[x]$, then $\exists f_{i}(x) \in F_{i}$ s.t. $p(x) \mid f_{i}(x)$.


**Proof.** We prove by induction on $n$. If $n=1$ then it must be that $f_{1}(x)=p(x)$, so the proposition holds. If $n=2$, the n by corollary 324 (Euclid's lemma for polynomials) the proposition holds. Suppose by induction it holds for some $n$, and consider $n+1$. Since $F[x]$ is a ring, multiplication in $F[x]$ associates, and $\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}f_{i}(x)=(\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x))f_{n+1}(x)$, the RHS is the product of two polynomials, so again by Euclid's lemma either $p(x)$ divides $f_{n+1}(x)$, in which case we're done, or it divides the product of the other $n$ polynomials, in which case by the induction hypothesis it divides at least one of those polynomials, which complets the proof by induction. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

These last results lead directly into the analogous statement of the fundamental theorem of arithmetic for polynomial rings over a field (recall that classic proofs of the fundamental theorem of arithmetic make use of Euclid's lemma for all the heavy liftings). We will later define an integral domain that satisfies a property analogous to that theorem as a unique factorization domain (UFD). 

At this point we will state, without proof, that $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ satisfies this property (i.e. every polynomial in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ admits a unique factorization into irreducible polynomials, where uniqueness is up to an ordering and multiplication by units). Later we will formally derive this result as a corollary of a more general theorem regarding PIDs, but the reader may use Euclid's lemma as stated above for polynomials over a field and follow a proof of the fundamental theorem of arithmetic and see that the same proof applies to $\mathbb{Q}[x]$. We make use of this fact and of theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)) to show that all polynomials in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ admit a unique factorization into irreducibles over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$.

>[!theorem] fundamental theorem of arithmetic for polynomials
>
>If $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a non-zero polynomial, then $f(x)$ can be decomposed into the product of irreducible polynomials over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, and this decomposition is *unique* up to an ordering and multiplication of the irreducibles by units.


**Proof.** As stated above, we make use of the fact that if we consider $f(x) \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$, then $f(x)$ can be factored into a unique (up to ordering and units) product of irreducibles over $\mathbb{Q}[x]$. If $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, then it must also be irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ (otherwise it would be reducible with the same factors), so irreducibles over $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ with integer coefficients are also irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$. By theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)), if $f(x)$ is reducible over $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ then the reduction can be carried out in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, so factorization in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ ensure factorization in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$. For uniqueness, suppose the factorization is not unique, then it also corresponds to a non-unique factorization in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$, but we stated that in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ the factorization is unique, a contradiction, so the factorization must also be unique. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Divisibility in Integral Domains

In this section, we generalize the discussion started in [[#Polynomial Factorization]] to general integral domains. First, a few definitions, which should seem familiar:

>[!definition] divisor
>
>If $R$ is a unital ring, and $a, b \in R$ and $\exists c \in R$ s.t. $b=ca$, then we say that $a$ *divides* $b$, or that $a$ is a *factor* of $b$, and denote this relation $a|b$.

>[!definition] associates, irreducible, primes
>
>If $R$ is a unital ring, then:
>1. $a, b \in R$ are *associates* if $\exists u \in U(R)$ (the group of units of $R$) s.t. $b=au$.
>2. $a$ is *irreducible* in $R$ if $\forall c, d \in R$ s.t. $a=cd$, either $a \in U(R)$ or $b \in U(R)$.
>3. $a$ is *prime* if $a \neq 0$ and $\forall bc \in R$, if $a|bc$ then $a|b$ or $a|c$

In $\mathbb{Z}$, we usually define primes as numbers $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. whenever $p=cd$, then one must be $1$, i.e. a unit of $\mathbb{Z}$, and the other $p$, which coincides with our definition of *irreducible*. Our general definition for *prime*, stated for an element of $\mathbb{Z}$, is simply Euclid's lemma. In elementary number theory, we see that the converse of Euclid's lemma is also true, i.e. in $\mathbb{Z}$ we find that *prime* and *irreducible* are equivalent.

Similarly, if $F$ is a field and then as we have demonstrated in corollary 324 (Euclid's lemma for polynomials) and in proposition 325 (converse Euclid's lemma for polynomials), in $F[x]$ we find that prime and irreducible are equivalent as well.

However, this is not always the case, as we will soon demonstrate. For now, we study an important example, which will help us work our way towards the definition of a unique factorization domain (UFD).

>[!definition] square-free integer
>
>If $n \in \mathbb{Z}$ is neither $1$ nor $0$, and is not divisible by any perfect square in $\mathbb{Z}$, we say that $n$ is *square-free*. 

Equivalently, we say that $n$ is *square-free* if $n \neq 1, 0$ and also $n$ has no prime factor with even multiplicity in its unique factorization as per the fundamental theorem of arithmetic. I leave it to the reader to become convinced that these definitions are equivalent.

>[!example]
>
>Note that $25=5^{2}$, so it is not square-free. Also, note that $4^{2}=16|32$, so $32$ is not square-free. However, $7$ is prime, so trivially it is square-free. Also, $15=3 \cdot 5$, so it has no prime of even multiplicity in its factorization, so $15$ is also square-free.

>[!definition] quadratic integer ring
>
>If $D \in \mathbb{Z}$ is *square-free*, then the *quadratic integer ring* of $D$, denoted $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, is given by the set of all quadratic integers of the form $a+b\sqrt{D}$ where $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, i.e. $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]=\{a+b\sqrt{D} | a, b \in \mathbb{Z}\}$. We equip this set with natural extensions of addition and multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ to form a ring:
>1. *addition*: $(a+b\sqrt{D})+(c+d\sqrt{D})=(a+c)+(b+d)\sqrt{D}$
>2. *multiplication*: $(a+b\sqrt{D}) \times (c + d)\sqrt{D}) = (ac+bdD)+(ad+bc)\sqrt{D}$


The reader can verify that this is indeed a ring under the given operations, and that it is in fact an *integral domain*: trivially it has unity $1$ and multiplication is commutative since multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ is commutative. In the next proposition, we show it also has no zero divisors, so it is an integral domain:

>[!proposition] quadratic integer ring has no zero divisors
>
>If $D \in \mathbb{Z}$ is square-free, then $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ has no zero-divisors.


**Proof.** We prove by contradiction. Let $a+b\sqrt{D} \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ be a zero divisor, i.e. $a+b\sqrt{D}$ is non-zero. Consider $(a+b\sqrt{D})(a-b\sqrt{D})$, which by definition simplifies to $a^{2}-b^{2}D$. If $a^{2}-b^{2}D=0$, then $a^{2}=b^{2}D$. If $b=0$, then $a^{2}=0$ so $a=0$, so $a+b\sqrt{D}=0_{\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]}$, which contradicts our supposition, so we can multiply both sides by $b^{-1} \in \mathbb{Q}$, take the square root and get $\sqrt{D}=\frac{a}{b} \in \mathbb{Q}$, but from elementary number theory we know that if $D$ is not a perfect square then $\sqrt{D} \notin \mathbb{Q}$, and by supposition $D$ is square-free so clearly it is not a perfect square, so $a^{2}-b^{2}D \neq 0$, so $(a+b\sqrt{D})(a-b\sqrt{D}) \neq 0$. 

By proposition 218 , since $a+b\sqrt{D}$ is a zero divisor by supposition, it follows that $a^{2}-b^{2}D$ is also a zero-divisor, so for some $c+d\sqrt{D} \neq 0$ we find $(a^{2}-b^{2}D)(c+d\sqrt{D})=0$, which by definition simplifies to $(a^{2}-b^{2}D)c+(a^{2}-b^{2}D)d\sqrt{D}=0$. By supposition, $a^{2}-b^{2}D \neq 0$, so we can multiply both sides by $(a^{2}-b^{2}D)^{-1} \in \mathbb{Q}$ and get $c+d\sqrt{D}=0$, but this is a contradiction to our supposition that $c+d\sqrt{D} \neq 0$.

We conclude that $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ has no zero-divisors.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Next, we introduce the concept of a *norm* (not to be confused with norms from metric spaces, although they do share some similarities). Using the concept of a norm, we will show that in the quadratic integer ring $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-3}]$, irreducible and prime are not equivalent.

>[!definition] norm
>
>If $R$ is an integral domain, and $N: R \to \mathbb{N}$ is a function that preserves zero (i.e. $N(0_{R})=0$), then we say that $N$ is a *norm* on $R$. If $a \neq 0 \implies 0 \lt N(a)$, we say that $N$ is a *positive norm*.

In our study of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, we equip it with a norm inspired by the modulus of a complex number. Recall that the modulus of a complex number $a+bi$ is denoted $|a+bi|$ and defined as $\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$, and equivalently via definition of multiplication of complex numbers, we find $(a+bi)(a-bi)=a^{2}-b^{2}(-1)=a^{2}+b^{2}=|a+bi|^{2}$. Note that in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, we also find that $(a+b\sqrt{D})(a-b\sqrt{D})=a^{2}-b^{2}D$ (we have shown this in the previous proposition). (The sharp-eyed would recognize that $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{1}]$ is simply a subring of $\mathbb{C}$)

>[!definition] norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$
>
>Let $D$ be square-free, then we define the norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ to be given by $N: a+b\sqrt{D} \mapsto |a^{2}-b^{2}D|$.


Form the definition it is apparent that $N$ is indeed a mapping from $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}] \to \mathbb{N}$ since $a, b, D$ are all integers so $a^{2}-b^{2}D \in \mathbb{Z}$ from closure under ring operations, and finally the absolute value maps from $\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{N}$.

It is also apparent that $N(0+0\sqrt{D})=|0-0D|=0$, and also that $a \neq 0 \implies 0 \lt N(a)$ since the absolute value is always non-negative (which takes care of $0 \leq N(a)$) and $a^{2}-b^{2}D=0$ if and only if $a^{2}=b^{2}D$ which as we argued above leads to a contradiction to $D$ being square free if $a, b$ are not both $0$, which shows that $N(a)=0 \iff a=0$, thus $N$ as defined above is a *positive norm*. We explore more useful properties of $N(a)$ in the next theorem:

>[!theorem] properties of the norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$
>
>$\forall x, y \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$,
>1. $N(x)=0 \iff x= 0$
>2. $|xx^{*}|=N(x)$, where if $x=a+b\sqrt{D}$, then we define its conjugate $x^{\star}$ to be $a-b\sqrt{D}$, in a similar nature to the complex conjugate.
>3. $N(xy)=N(x)N(y)$, i.e. the norm is *multiplicative*
>4. $x$ is a unit if and only if $N(x)=1$
>5. If $N(x)$ is prime, then $x$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$


**Proof.** We have already proven $1$ in the paragraph above, so we proceed from $2$.

$2$. By definition $xx^{*}=a^{2}-b^{2}D$ (see calculation in proposition 333 (quadratic integer ring has no zero divisors)). Also by definition, $N(x)=|a^{2}-b^{2}D|$, so $|xx^{*}|=N(x)$.

$3$. Denote $x=a+b\sqrt{D}, y=c+\sqrt{D}$. By definition, $xy=(ac+bdD)+(ad+bc)\sqrt{D}$. So, $N(xy)=|(ac+bdD)^{2}-(ad+bc)^{2}D|$. Consider the following:

$$
\begin{gathered}
N(x)N(y)=\\
&|a^{2}-b^{2}D| \cdot | c^{2}-d^{2}D| = \\
&| (a^{2}-b^{2}D)(c^{2}-d^{2}D)| = \\
&a^{2}c^{2}-a^{2}d^{2}D - b^{2}Dc^{2}+b^{2}Dd^{2}D = \\
&a^{2}c^{2}+b^{2}d^{2}D^{2} - (a^{2}d^{2} + b^{2}c^{2})D =\\
&a^{2}c^{2}+2acbdD + b^{2}d^{2}D^{2}-(a^{2}d^{2}+b^{2}c^{2}+2abcd)D=\\
&(ac+bdD)^{2}-(ad+bc)^{2}D=\\
&&N(xy)
\end{gathered}
$$

$4$. $\implies$Suppose $x$ is a unit, then $x=a+b\sqrt{D}$ with $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $\exists x^{-1} \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ s.t $xx^{-1}=1$ the unity of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$. By $(3)$, $N(xx^{-1})=N(x)N(x^{-1})$, and also $xx^{-1}=1$ so $N(xx^{-1})=N(1)=|1|=1$, so $N(x)N(x^{-1})=1$. Since $N(x), N(x^{-1}) \in \mathbb{N}$,  then the only solution to the above is $N(x)=N(x^{-1})=1$, so $N(x)=1$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $N(x)=1$, then since $N(x)=|xx^{\star}|$ it follows that $1=|xx^{*}|$, i.e. $xx^{*}=\pm 1$, then $x^{-1}=\mp x^{*}$.

$5$. Suppose $N(x)$ is prime, and suppose via contradiction $x$ is reducible in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, i.e. $x=ab$ for some non-units $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$. By $(3)$, $N(x)=N(ab)=N(a)N(b)$, so $N(a)|N(x), N(b)|N(x)$. Since by supposition $N(x)$ is prime, it follows that one of $N(a), N(b)$ must be $p$ and the other $1$, w.l.o.g suppose $N(a)=1$, then by $(4)$, $a$ is a unit in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, a contradiction, so $x$ is irreducible.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
Of the above, it is worth noting that if $N$ is a multiplicative positive norm of a ring then $(1)$, $(3)$ of the above are satisfied, which implies $(4)$, $(5)$ (examine the respective parts of the proof to verify this), so we can also state the following:

>[!theorem] properties of multiplicative positive-definite norm
>
>If $R$ is a rind and $N: R \to \mathbb{N}$ is a multiplicative positive-definite norm (i.e. it is a positive norm and $\forall x, y \in R(N(xy)=N(x)N(y))$ and $N(x)=0 \iff x = 0$), then:
>1. $x \in R$ is a unit if and only if $N(x)=1$
>2. If $N(x)$ is prime, then $x$ is irreducible in $R$.


For now, we return to our discussion of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$.

Using these results we can demonstrate that while $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-3}]$ is an integral domain as argued above, we do not find that prime and irreducible are the same in this domain.

>[!example] $1+\sqrt{-3}$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-3}]$, but not prime
>
>First we will demonstrate $1+\sqrt{-3}$ is not prime. Observe that $(1+\sqrt{-3})(1-\sqrt{-3})=1^{2}-(-3)=4=2^{2}$, so $(1+\sqrt{-3}) | 2^{2}$. If $1+\sqrt{-3}$ is prime, then it must also divide $2$ by definition, so we should find some $a+b\sqrt{-3}$ with $a, b$ integers s.t. $(1+\sqrt{-3})(a+b\sqrt{-3})=2$, expanding the LHS we get $(a-3b)+(b+a)\sqrt{-3}=2$, so it must be that $a-3b=2$ and also $b+a=0$, so $2=a-3b=b+a-4b=-4b$, so $b=-\frac{1}{2}$, but this $b$ is not an integer, so the above has no solution for $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $1+\sqrt{-3} \not \mid 2$, so it is not prime.
>
>Next, we demonstrate it is irreducible. We do this by theorem 336 (properties of the norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$). We calculate $N(1+\sqrt{-3})=|1^{2}-(-3)|=|1+3|=4$. Suppose $1+\sqrt{-3}=xy$ for some $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-3}]$, then if $x, y$ are both not units (i.e. $1+\sqrt{-3}$ is reducible) then since $N(xy)=N(x)N(y)=4$, it must be that $N(x)=N(y)=2$. Denote $x=a+b\sqrt{-3}$ and solve $|a^{2}-b^{2}(-3)|=a^{2}+3b^{2}=2$. We find that if $b \neq 0$, then $2 \lt a^{2}+3b^{2}$ since $b \in \mathbb{Z}$ so $3 \leq 3b^{2}$ and also $0 \leq a^{2}$, so it must be that $b=0$. So, $a^{2}=2$, but then $a=\sqrt{2}$, which we know to be irrational, so obviously $a \notin \mathbb{Z}$, a contradiction, so $1+\sqrt{-3}$ is irreducible.
>
>We see that in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-3}]$, irreducible does not imply prime.

>[!example] $7 \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}]$ is irreducible
>
>Next, we show it is irreducible. Suppose not, then $7=xy$ for some non-units $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}]$, then by theorem 336 (properties of the norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$) we find $49=7^{2}=N(7)=N(x)N(y)$. Since $N(x), N(y) \in \mathbb{N}$, and both are not unit by supposition so their norms are not $1$ (see property $(4)$ in the mentioned theorem), then $N(x)=N(y)=7$. Let $x=a+b\sqrt{5}$, then we find $N(a+b\sqrt{5})=7$, so $|a^{2}-5b^{2}|=7$, so $a^{2}-5b^{2}=\pm 7$. We can exchange this for a modular equation $a^{2}-5b^{2} \equiv 0 \mod 7$.
>
>Since each of $a, b$ has $7$ possible values $\mod 7$, there are $49$ total solution candidates to consider. Via explicitly considering each solution, we find that the only solution is the trivial solution $a \equiv_{7} b \equiv_{7} 0$. It follows that $7|a$ and also $7|b$, so $49|a^{2}, 49|b^{2}$ and we have $49|a^{2}-5b^{2}$, but $a^{2}-5b^{2}=\pm 7$ which is clearly not divisible by $49$, so there is no such $x \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}]$, hence $7$ is irreducible.
>

With more study of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, we can show that $7$ is also prime in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}]$. This can be demonstrated via showing that $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}] \diagup \langle 7 \rangle$ is an integral domain, which we can show via an isomorphism with a factor group of the polynomial ring $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ which we can trade $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}] \diagup \langle 7 \rangle$ for. This raises the question - we have seen the irreducible does not imply prime in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$, but we have seen an example where some prime is also irreducible, so perhaps prime $\implies$ irreducible does hold. The next theorem affirms this suspicion:

>[!theorem] every prime is irreducible in an integral domain
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain and $p \in D$ is prime, then $p$ is also irreducible.


**Proof.** Suppose $D$ is an integral domain and $p \in D$ is prime, and suppose $p=ab$ for some $a, b \in D$, then $p|ab$, and since $p$ is prime then either $p|a$ or $p|b$. Suppose w.l.o.g $p|a$, then $a=pc$, so $p=ab=(pc)b=p(cb)$, so $cb=1$, so $b$ is a unit. This is true for every factoring of $p$ into a product of two elements, so $p$ is irreducible. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $R$ is a unital commutative ring, then $\forall a, b \in R$:
>1. If $a, b$ are associates, then $\langle a \rangle = \langle b \rangle$
>2. If $R$ is an integral domain, and $\langle a \rangle = \langle b \rangle$, then $a, b$ are associates


**Proof.** 

$1$. Suppose $a, b$ are associates, then $b=au$ for some $u \in U(R)$. We will prove $\langle a \rangle = \langle b \rangle$ by mutual inclusion. Let $ar \in \langle a \rangle$, then since $u \in U(R)$ we can find $u^{-1} \in R$ s.t. $uu^{-1}=1$, so $r=1r=uu^{-1}r$, so $ar=(au)(u^{-1}r)=b(u^{-1}r) \in \langle b \rangle$, so $\langle a \rangle \subseteq \langle b \rangle$. Conversely, consider that since $u \in R$ then trivially $b=au  \in \langle a \rangle$ by definition. Since $\langle a \rangle$ is an ideal, the it must contain all elements of the form $br$ for $r \in R$ by definition, and by definition this set of elements is exactly $\langle b \rangle$, so $\langle b \rangle \subseteq \langle a \rangle$, so $\langle a \rangle = \langle b \rangle$.

$2$. Suppose $R$ is an integral domain and $\langle a \rangle = \langle b \rangle$, so $a \in \langle b \rangle, b \in \langle a \rangle$, so $\exists s, t \in R$ s.t. $a=bs, b=at$, so $a=(at)s=a(ts)$. If $a=0$ then $\langle a \rangle = \{0\}$ and so $b=0$, so trivially $a, b$ associate. Otherwise, since $R$ is an integral domain then it has no zero-divisors, so by the cancellation law for ring with no zero-divisors, $1=ts$, i.e. $t, s \in U(R)$, and recall that $a=bs, b=at$, so $a, b$ are associates by definition.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] in a PID, prime and irreducible are equivalent
>
>If $D$ is a PID, then $a \in D$ is prime if and only if $a$ is irreducible.


**Proof.** $\implies$A PID is an integral domain, so this direction is covered by theorem 340 (every prime is irreducible in an integral domain).

$\impliedby$Suppose $a$ is irreducible, i.e. $\forall x, y$ s.t. $a=xy$, either $x$ or $y$ is a unit. Let $bc \in D$ s.t. $a|bc$, i.e. $bc=ak$ for some $k \in D$. Consider $I=\{\alpha a + \beta b | \alpha, \beta \in D\}$, clearly it is a subset of $D$. We will show it is an ideal of $D$ via theorem 232 (ideal test):
1. (nonempty) For $\alpha=1$ and $\beta=0$ we find $a \in I$ so clearly $I$ is nonempty.
2. (closed under subtraction) Let $\alpha_{1}a+\beta_{1}b, \alpha_{2}a+\beta_{2}b \in I$, note that $(\alpha_{1}a+\beta_{1}b)-(\alpha_{2}a+\beta_{2}b)=(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})a+(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})b \in I$ (for $\alpha=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}, \beta=\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}$).
3. (closure under multiplication with ring elements) let $r \in D$ and $\alpha a + \beta b \in I$, then by theorem 217 (ring arithmetic), $r(\alpha a + \beta b)=(r\alpha)a+(r\beta)b$, which is an element of $I$. Since $D$ is a PID then it is commutative, so $(\alpha a + \beta b)r=r(\alpha a + \beta b) \in I$.

We conclude that $I$ is an ideal. Since $D$ is a PID then by definition $I$ is generated by some $d \in D$. As we have argued above, $b \in I$ so $a=td$ for some $t \in D$ since $I=\langle d \rangle$. By supposition, $a$ is irreducible, so either $d$ is a unit, or $t$ is a unit. 

If $t$ is a unit, then by definition $a, d$ are associates, so by theorem 341  $\langle a \rangle = \langle d \rangle$, so $a$ divides all elements of $I$. In particular, with $\alpha=0, \beta=1$ we find $b \in I$, so $a|b$, in agreement with the hypothesized primality of $a$.

Otherwise, $d$ is a unit, then $d^{-1} \in D$, so $dd^{-1} \in \langle d \rangle$, so $1 \in \langle d \rangle$, so $1=\alpha a + \beta b$ for some $\alpha, \beta \in D$. Multiplying both sides by $c$, we find $c=\alpha a c + \beta bc$. Clearly $a|\alpha a c$, and by supposition $a | bc$ so $a|\beta bc$, so by properties of the divisor $a|(\alpha a c + \beta b c)$, so $a|c$.

We have that $a$ irreducible in a PID and $a|bc$ implies $a|b$ or $a|c$, so $a$ is prime.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

This theorem lets us conflate *prime* and *irreducible* in a PID. Note that, as we have seen in our discussion of polynomial rings, if the coefficients are taken from a field $F$, then in $F[x]$, prime and irreducible are equivalent. Recall that by theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID), if $F$ is a field then $F[x]$ is a PID. The above theorem immediately implies this result.

>[!corollary] in a PID, maximal ideal and prime ideal are interchangeable
>
>Let $D$ be a PID and $I$ an ideal of $D$, then $I$ is prime if and only if $I$ is maximal.


**Proof.** Note that theorem 251  and theorem 252  immediately give prime $\impliedby$ maximal since every field is also an integral domain, so it follows that if $I$ is prime then $I \subseteq M$ for some maximal ideal $M$. 

$\implies$ Suppose $I$ is prime. Since $D$ is a PID then $I=\langle p \rangle, M=\langle m \rangle$ for some $p, m \in D$. Since $p \in \langle m \rangle$ then $\exists n \in D$ s.t $p=nm$, but $p$ is prime so by theorem 342 (in a PID, prime and irreducible are equivalent) it is also irreducible so either $m$ or $n$ is a unit.

If $m$ is unit, then $\langle m \rangle=D$ by proposition 235 (principal ideal of a unit), so $\langle m \rangle$ is not maximal, so this is impossible. Otherwise, $n$ is a unit so $p, m$ are associates, so by theorem 341   $\langle p \rangle = \langle m \rangle$, so $I=M$, so $I$ is also maximal.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
Recall Bezout's identity from number theory, which states that given $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, $\exists x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $xa+yb=\text{gcd}(a, b)$. The identity can be generalized to PIDs. First, we pick a workable definition for the $\text{gcd}$ in a commutative ring:

>[!definition] greatest common divisor in a commutative ring
>
>Let $R$ be a commutative ring and let $a, b \in R$, then $d \in R$ is the *greatest common divisor* of $a, b$, denoted $\text{gcd}(a, b)$, if $\forall c \in R$, $c \mid a \land c \mid b \implies c \mid d$, i.e. it is divisible by any other common divisor of $a, b$ (where a common divisor is some $c \in R$ s.t. $cx=a, cy=b$ for some $x, y \in R$).

>[!theorem] Bezout's identity for PIDs
>
>If $D$ is a PID and $a, b, d \in D$ s.t. $\text{gcd}(a, b)=d$, then $\exists x, y \in D$ s.t. $xa+yb=d$.
>


**Proof.** Consider $\langle a, b \rangle$ the ideal generated by $a, b$. By proposition 240  it is an ideal of $D$. We will show $\langle a, b \rangle = \langle d \rangle$. 

Since $d$ is a common divisor for $a, b$ then $a, b \in \langle d \rangle$, so $\langle a, b \rangle \subseteq \langle d \rangle$. Conversely, since $D$ is a PID, then $\exists r \in D$ s.t. $\langle a, b \rangle = \langle r \rangle$, but since $a, b \in r$ then by definition $r \mid a, r \mid b$, so $r$ is a common divisor of $a, b$, so $r \mid d$, so $d \in \langle r \rangle$, so $\langle d \rangle \langle r \rangle = \subseteq \langle a, b \rangle$. By mutual inclusion, we conclude $\langle a, b \rangle = \langle d \rangle$.

Next, since $\langle a, b \rangle = \langle d \rangle$ then $d \in \langle a, b \rangle$ so $\exists x, y \in D$ s.t. $xa+yb=d$ by definition of the ideal generated by $a, b$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Unique Factorization Domain

Recall that the fundamental theorem of arithmetic shows that in $\mathbb{Z}$, every integer admits a unique factorization into a product of primes up to a reordering and associates (i.e. if $a=b$ and $a=u_{2}bu_{1}$ where $u_{2}, u_{1}$ are units and $b$ is irreducible, we say that both factorizations are the same up to a reordering and associates, since $b$ clearly associates with itself, in $\mathbb{Z}$ this means that we consider $2 \cdot 5$ and $2 \cdot (-1) \cdot 5 \cdot (-1) \cdot 1$ to be essentially the same factorization). In theorem 327 (fundamental theorem of arithmetic for polynomials), we have shown that an equivalent proposition is true for $\mathbb{Z}[x]$.

Both of these results usually follow from a similar sequence of results:
1. First, we equip our integral domain with a *division algorithm*.
2. Next, we identify that our *irreducibles*, which are those elements that, under the division algorithm, are divisible only by the unit of the ring and themselves, also satisfy the *prime* property.
3. Using these two results, we begin to factorize elements into *primes*, which are also *irreducible*. We find that we are able to write every element in terms of a product of primes.
4. Next, we find that, in the cases we have examined, this factorization is *unique* (up to a reordering and associates).

In this section, we consider this process in the abstract sense and define structures that let us more generally discuss it.

First, we need to have a general notion of an integral domain with a division algorithm. We call such domains *Euclidean domains* (ED), as defined below:

>[!definition] Euclidean domain
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain, and $N$ is a norm s.t. $\forall a, b \in D, b \neq 0$, there exists $q, r \in D$ such that:
>1. $a=qb+r$
>2. Either $r=0$, or $N(r) \lt N(b)$
>
>then we say that $D$ equipped with this norm is a *Euclidean domain*, and call $q$ the *quotient* and $r$ the *remainder* of the *division* of $a$ by $b$.


Consider a few examples:

>[!example] $\mathbb{Z}$ is a Euclidean domain
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}$ with the norm $N: z \mapsto |z|$. Note that $N(xy)=|xy|=|x| \cdot |y|=N(x)N(y)$, and also $N(x)=0 \iff x=0$ otherwise $0 \lt N(x)$, so $N$ is a multiplicative positive norm on $\mathbb{Z}$. Consequently, by theorem 337 (properties of multiplicative positive-definite norm), we have that the units of $\mathbb{Z}$ are the elements that satisfy $|z|=1$, i.e. $\pm 1$.
>
>By the division algorithm in $\mathbb{Z}$, we know that $\forall a, b \in \mathbb{Z}, b \neq 0$ then $\exists ! q, r \in \mathbb{Z}$ s.t. $a=qb+r$, and also $r \in [0, b)$. Since $r \in [0, b)$ then $0 \leq N(r) \lt b$. It should be noted here that the choice of $r$ to be non-negative is arbitrary - for every $q, r$ pair with $r$ non-negative we can demonstrate that $q'=q+1, r'=r-b$ satisfies $a=q'b+r'$ ($q'b+r'=(q+1)b+r-b=qb+b+r-b=qb+r$), and also $0 \leq N(r') \lt b$. 
>
>Since $\mathbb{Z}$ with the natural norm admits a division algorithm as described in definition 346 (Euclidean domain), it is a Euclidean domain.

>[!example] $F[x]$ is a Euclidean domain
>
>If $F$ is a field then $F[x]$ equipped with the norm $N: 0 \mapsto 0, p(x) \mapsto \text{deg}(p(x))$ is a Euclidean domain: first, we observe that if $f(x), g(x)$ are both non-zero then $N(f(x)g(x))=\text{deg}(f(x)g(x))=\text{deg}(f(x))+\text{deg}(g(x))$, so $N$ is not multiplicative, and also $N(x)=0$ does not necessarily give $x=0$ (any constant polynomial also has norm $0$ as defined here).
>
>In theorem 300 (polynomial division algorithm) we have demonstrated that $\forall a(x), b(x) \in F[x], b(x) \neq 0$, we can find $q(x), r(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(b(x))$ or $r(x)=0$, and $a(x)=q(x)b(x)+r(x)$, so using our norm we find that $N(r(x)) \lt N(b(x))$, or $r(x)=0$, so $F[x]$ equipped with this norm is a Euclidean domain by definition.

>[!theorem] every Euclidean domain is a PID
>
>If $D$ is a Euclidean domain, then it is a principal ideal domain.


**Proof.** We follow the same structure as the proof for theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID). Let $D$ be Euclidean with norm $N$ and let $I$ be some ideal of $D$. If $I=\{0\}$, then $I=\langle 0 \rangle$ so it is principal. Otherwise, $I \neq \{0\}$. In this case, consider $N(I)$ the image of $I$ under the norm equipped to $D$. Since $N(I) \in \mathbb{N}$, then by the WOP $\exists m \in I$ s.t. $\forall n \in I (N(m) \leq N(n))$, i.e. $m$ has minimal norm.

Let $a \in I$. Since $D$ is Euclidean with norm $N$, $\exists q, r \in D$ s.t. $a=qm+r$ and $r=0$ or $N(r) \lt N(m)$. Since $I$ is an ideal and $m \in I$, then $qm \in I$, and since $a \in I$ then $a-qm \in I$, so $r=a-qm \in I$. Since $m$ has minimal norm in $I$ then $N(r) \lt N(m)$ is impossible, then $r=0$, so $a \in \langle m \rangle$, so $I=\langle m \rangle$, so $D$ is a PID.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that this theorem is a generalization of theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID), so we could have proven that theorem directly as an instantation of theorem 349 (every Euclidean domain is a PID) for the Euclidean domain $F[x]$.

Eventually, we are interested in domains where the factorization of elements is unique. We call these *unique factorization domains* (UFDs), as defined below:

>[!definition] unique factorization domain (UFD)
>
>If $D$ is an integral domain, and $\forall a \in D a \neq 0$, we can factor $a$ into a product of irreducibles, and that product is unique up to a reordering and associates, then we say that $D$ is a *unique factorization domain*.

We will soon prove that every PID is a UFD, and later show that every Euclidean domain is a PID, so every Euclidean domain is a UFD, thus generalizing the fundamental theorem of arithmetic for integers. The general layout of the argument we will use to show that every PID is a UFD is very similar to a standard proof of the fundamental theorem of arithmetic (while adjusting our terminology from integers to integral domains), so it would be beneficial to recall such proof in broad strokes:

1. We proved existence in $\mathbb{N}$ via strong induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$. The key to the proof is that if $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is prime, then it is already given in factorized from since $n$ is irreducible, otherwise $n$ is reducible so it reduces into the product of two lesser integers for which the induction hypothesis applies, which shows existence. Existence in $\mathbb{Z}$ follows from the fact that, if $z \in \mathbb{Z}$ then either $z \in \mathbb{N}$ or $-z \in \mathbb{N}$, for which we have shown existence via induction.
2. We proved uniqueness by supposing that there exists two distinct factorizations of the same element, and considered one prime $p$ that appears only in one of the two factorizations. It then followed that one of the primes $q$ in the factorization where $p$ does not appear must be divisible by $p$, but $p$ is prime so it is not a unit, and that would imply that $q$ is reducible, but $q$ is prime so this is impossible, so the factorization must be unique.

Another way of looking at existence is the following: suppose $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then we show that $\forall n \in \mathbb{N}$, either $n$ is prime or has a prime factor, in which case $n=mp$ for some prime $p$ and other integer $m$ that is also a non-trivial factor of $n$ so it is lesser than $n$,. Eventually this process must terminate since in each step we divide the number by at least $2$ (the smallest prime), so eventually we must hit a prime. Collecting all primes along the way, we arrive at a prime power factorization for $n$. This way of thinking is more aligned with the proof we will follow in this section.

>[!definition] ascending chain condition
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity, and for every sequence $I_{1}, I_{2}, \dots$ of increasing (ascending) ideals in $R$, i.e. $I_{1} \subseteq I_{2} \subseteq \dots$, the sequence must terminate (or *stabilize*), i.e. $\exists N \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $\forall n \gt N, I_{n}=I_{N}$, then we say that $R$ satisfies the *ascending chain condition* (ACC).
>
>Equivalently, some writers require the ideals be *strictly increasing*, i.e. $I_{1} \subset I_{2} \subset \dots$, in which case the requirement is that the sequence be *finite* in length.

It is trivial to demonstrate the definitions are equivalent: removing all duplicate ideals in the first definition gives exactly the second definition. 

>[!lemma] union of ascending ideals is an ideal
>
>If $I_{1}, I_{2}, \dots$ is an ascending chain of ideals in a ring $R$, i.e. $I_{1} \subseteq I_{2} \subseteq \dots$, then their union $I=\cup_{j \in \mathbb{N}}I_{j}$ is an ideal.


**Proof.** We prove by theorem 232 (ideal test). Clearly $I$ is non-empty.
1. (closure under subtraction) let $a, b \in I$, then $a \in I_{n}, b \in I_{m}$ for some $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$. Suppose w.l.o.g $n \leq m$, then since the chain is ascending, $a \in I_{n}$ implies $a \in I_{m}$, so we have $a, b \in I_{m}$, and since $I_{m}$ is an ideal then $a-b \in I_{m}$., so $I$ is closed under subtraction.
2. (closure under multiplication with ring elements) suppose $r \in R, s \in I$, then $s \in I_{n}$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and since $I_{n}$ is an ideal $rs \in I_{n}, sr \in I_{n}$, but by definition $I_{n} \subseteq I$, so $rs, sr \in I$.

So $I$ is an ideal.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Rings that satisfy the ACC have a special name:

>[!definition] Noetherian ring
>
>If $R$ is a commutative ring with unity, then $R$ is *Noetherian* if it satisfies the ascending chain condition.

>[!theorem] PIDs are Noetherian
>
>If $D$ is a PID, then $D$ is Noetherian.


**Proof.** We will demonstrate that $D$ satisfies the ACC. Let $I_{1}, I_{2}, \dots$ be a strictly ascending sequence of ideals. By lemma 352 (union of ascending ideals is an ideal) their union $I$ is also an ideal. Since $D$ is a PID, then $I=\langle d \rangle$ for some $d \in D$, so $d \in I$, but also $d \in I_{N}$ for some $N \in \mathbb{N}$ by definition of $I$ as a union. By theorem 236 (ideal is a superset of the principal ideals of its elements), it follows that $\langle d \rangle \subseteq I_{N}$, but then $I \subseteq I_{N}$, but by definition $I_{1}, \dots$ is strictly ascending, so it must terminate with $I_{N}$, so $D$ satisfies the ACC, so it is Noetherian. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

>[!lemma]
>
>In a PID, every element is either irreducible, or has an irreducible factor.


**Proof.** Let $D$ be a PID and let $a_{1} \in D$. If $a$ is irreducible, then we are done. Otherwise, $a_{1}=a_{2}b_{2}$ for non-units $a_{2}, b_{2} \in D$. If $a_{2}$ is irreducible, then we are done. Otherwise, $a_{2}=a_{3}b_{3}$ for non-units $a_{3}, b_{3} \in D$. We continue this sequence and consider that for each $\langle a_{n} \rangle$, we have $a_{n}=a_{n+1}b_{n+1}$, so by definition $a_{n} \in \langle a_{n+1} \rangle$, so $\langle a_{n} \rangle \subset \langle a_{n+1} \rangle$, so we have an ascending chain of ideals $\langle a_{1} \rangle \subset \langle a_{2} \rangle \subset \dots$, so by theorem 354 (PIDs are Noetherian) the chain must terminate, so eventually we must find some $a_{n}$ term for $n \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $a_{n}$ is irreducible, and back-substituting all other expressions we find $a_{1}=a_{n} \prod_{i=2}^{n}b_{i}=a_{n}k$, so $a_{1}$ has an irreducible factor. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

We can now prove that every PID is a UFD:

>[!theorem] PIDs are UFDs
>
>If $D$ is a PID, then $D$ is a UFD.


**Proof.** Let $a \in D$ and suppose $D$ is a PID.

**Existence**: by lemma 355  we have that $a=p_{1}k_{1}$ for some $p_{1}$ irreducible and $k_{1} \in D$. If $k_{1}$ is not a unit, we follow the same procedure for $k_{1}$ and so by the same lemma we have $k_{1}=p_{2}k_{2}$ for some $p_{2}$ irreducible and $k_{2} \in D$. If $k_{2}$ is not a unit, we again employ lemma 355  to write $k_{2}=p_{3}k_{3}$ for some $p_{3}, k_{3} \in D$ with $p_{3}$ irreducible. Notice that $k_{1} \in \langle k_{2} \rangle$ and $k_{2} \in \langle k_{3} \rangle$ and so on, so by an identical argument to the one in lemma 355  we have an ascending chain of ideals $\langle k_{1} \rangle \subset \langle k_{2} \rangle \subset \dots$ which must terminate by theorem 354 (PIDs are Noetherian), so eventually we have $a=\prod_{p_{i} \in P}p_{i}$ for some $P=\{p_{1}, \dots, p_{n}\}$ irreducibles in $D$.

**Uniqueness**: Suppose the above factorization is not unique, so we can find $P=\{p_{1}, \dots, p_{n}\}$ and $Q=\{q_{1}, \dots, q_{n}\}$ both sets of irreducibles in $D$ s.t. $\prod_{p \in P}p = \prod_{q \in Q}q = a$, and also $P \neq Q$. Suppose w.l.o.g. $\exists p_{i} \notin Q$, then by an analogous theorem to proposition 326 (Extended Euclid's lemma for polynomials) we find that since $p_{i} \mid a=\prod_{q \in Q}q$ then $\exists q_{j} \in Q$ s.t. $p_{i} \mid q_{j}$, but since $q_{j}$ is prime and also not $1$ then it must be that $p_{i}=q_{j}$, so $p_{i} \in Q$, so $P=Q$, so the factorization is unique.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $F[x]$ with $F$ field is a UFD
>
>IF $F$ is a field then its polynomial ring $F[x]$ is a UFD.


**Proof.** By theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID), $F[x]$ is a PID, so by theorem 356 (PIDs are UFDs) it is a UFD. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ is a UFD
>
>$\mathbb{Q}[x]$ is a UFD.

**Proof.** $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field, hence by corollary 357 ($F[x]$ with $F$ field is a UFD) we find that $\mathbb{Q}[x]$ is a UFD. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

In theorem 327 (fundamental theorem of arithmetic for polynomials), we used the above fact to show that $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a UFD. Recall that, since $\mathbb{Z}$ is not a field, it is not immediately a corollary of corollary 357 ($F[x]$ with $F$ field is a UFD) that $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a UFD, so we needed to involve theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)) as well to complete that proof.

>[!corollary] EDs are UFDs
>
>If $D$ is a Euclidean domain, then it is a UFD.

**Proof.** By theorem 349 (every Euclidean domain is a PID), $D$ is a PID. By theorem 356 (PIDs are UFDs), it is a UFD. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] fields are UFDs
>
>If $F$ is a field then $F$ is a UFD.

**Proof.** By theorem 238 (fields are PIDs), and by theorem 356 (PIDs are UFDs). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It is important to internalize that an integral domain is not guaranteed to be a UFD - only a Euclidean domain is, and finding examples where an ED fails to be a UFD does not require anything exotic. This led to many false proofs of Fermat's last theorem throughout the decades. The next eample demonstrates one such case:

>[!example] $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$ is an integral domain but a UFD
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$. We know that that quadratic integer rings are integral domains, and we also have a positive multiplicative norm as defined in definition 335 (norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$).
>
>By theorem 336 (properties of the norm of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$), it quickly follows that the units of $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$ are $\pm 1$ (since those are the only solution to $N(a+b\sqrt{-5})=1$). Note that $46=2 \cdot 23$, but also $46=1+45=1+9\cdot 5 = 1 - 3^{2} \sqrt{-5}^{2}=(1+3\sqrt{-5})(1-3\sqrt{-5})$.
>
>Suppose $2=xy$ for some $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$, then $N(xy)=N(x)N(y)=N(2)=4$. If both of $x, y$ are not units then the only solutions are $N(x)=N(y)=2$, but then we have $2=N(x)=N(a+b\sqrt{-5})=|a^{2}+5b^{2}|=a^{2}+5b^{2}$, and since $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ this equation has no solutions, so this is impossible, so $2$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$. A similar argument shows that $23$ is also irreducible in this ring, so $2 \cdot 23$ is a proper factorization of $46$ to irreducible elements in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$.
>
>Suppose $1+3\sqrt{-5}=xy$, and note that $N(1+3\sqrt{-5})=1+5\cdot 9 =46$, so $N(x)N(y)=46$, and the only solution where $N(x), N(y) \neq 1$ is $\{N(x), N(y)\}=\{2, 23\}$, but we have already shown that $N(x)=2$ has no solution for $x \in \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$, so $1+3\sqrt{-5}$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$. Similarly, $N(1-3\sqrt{-5})=1+5\cdot 9 = N(1+3\sqrt{-5})$, so it too is irreducible, so $(1+3\sqrt{-5})(1-3\sqrt{-5})$ is also a proper factorization to irreducibles in $\mathbb{Z}[-5]$.
>
>We have found that $46$ admits two distinct factorizations in $\mathbb{Z}[-5]$, so it is not a UFD, so it is also not Euclidean nor a PID.

Using this last result, we can prove theorem 321 (Eisenstein's criterion) (credited to Richard Singer in the source I follow)

**Proof.** Recall the conditions of the theorem: if $f(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a polynomial with degree $n$ at least $2$, and $p \in \mathbb{N}$ is a prime, and:
1. $p \not \mid a_{n}$
2. $p^{2} \not \mid a_{0}$
3. $\forall i \in [0, n-1] (p \mod a_{i})$

Then $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$. We prove the theorem by contradiction: suppose all conditions hold, yet $f(x)$ is reducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, so it is reducible over $\mathbb{Z}$ by theorem 317 (Gauss' lemma (reducibility)), so we have $f(x)=g(x)h(x)$ for some $g(x), h(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ that are not units, and have degree at least $1$, so both have degree less than $n$ (see construction in theorem 318  for more detail).

Let $\psi: \mathbb{Z}[x] \to \mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ be the natural homomorphism, and denote $f'(x)=\psi(f(x)), g'(x)=\psi(g(x)), h'(x)=\psi(h(x))$ be the reduced polynomials in $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$. Also, note that $\text{deg}(\psi(a(x))) \leq \text{deg}(a(x))$ for every non-zero polynomial $a(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$, or $\psi(a(x))=0$.

By supposition. all non-leading coefficients of $f(x)$ are divisible by $p$, so $f'(x)=a_{n}x^{n}$. Since $\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field by proposition 227 ($\mathbb{Z}_{p}$ is a field), then by corollary 357 ($F[x]$ with $F$ field is a UFD) $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$ is a UFD. Note that $x$ is irreducible on $\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]$, so the factorization of $f'(x)$ necessarily has $x$ with multiplicity $n$ as a factor, and likewise for the factorizations of $g'(x), h'(x)$ since $f'(x)=\psi(f(x))=\psi(g(x)h(x))=\psi(g(x))\psi(h(x))=g'(x)h'(x)$ (since $\psi$ is a homomorphism), so $g'(x)=bx^{r}, h'(x)=cx^{n-r}$ for some $b, c \in \mathbb{Z}_{p}, r \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $r \in [0, n]$ and $bc=a_{n}$.

If $r=0$ or $r=n$, then one of $g'(x), h'(x)$ is a polynomial of degree $n$, but both polynomials must have degree less than $n$ since the degree of a polynomial does not increase under $\psi$, so both cases are impossible, i.e. $g'(x)=bx^{r}, h'(x)=cx^{n-r}$ both with degree at least $1$, so both have $x$ has a factor, so both have $0$ as a zero, i.e. $g(0) \equiv 0 \mod p, h(0) \equiv 0 \mod p$, so $p^{2} \mid g(0)h(0)$, but $g(0)h(0)=f(0)=a_{0}$, so $p^{2} \mid a_{0}$, a contradiction, so it must be that $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We have reached a milestone where it is worth considering the hierarchy of the algebraic structures we have seen so far. The following graph should be read as follows: an arrow indicates that the structure at the tail of the arrow always satisfies the properties of the structure at the head of the arrow, but this is not an IFF relation (for example, $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ is a UFD although it is not a PID).


<TikzSvg5589f828d65b8dd3b341f8a81c4ca29f style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

## Extension Fields

Consider $\mathbb{Z}$ (an integral domain) and $\mathbb{Q}$ (a field). From previous knowledge, we know that $\mathbb{Q}$ contains a copy of $\mathbb{Z}$ as a subring, although when formalizing this relation via construction of $\mathbb{Q}$ as per definition 274 (field of fractions), we find that $\mathbb{Q}$ does not actually contain $\mathbb{Z}$ as a subring, but rather contains an isomorphic copy of $\mathbb{Z}$ as a subring, however it is easier to think of $\mathbb{Z}$ as a proper subring of $\mathbb{Q}$ without explicitly mentioning the homomorphism $\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q}, z \mapsto \frac{z}{1}$. It is useful to think of $\mathbb{Q}$ as an *extension* of the integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ into a field.

The same goes for $\mathbb{Q}$ versus $\mathbb{R}$ (both fields). While in formal constructions of $\mathbb{R}$ from $\mathbb{Q}$ via e.g. Dedekind cuts (which we have talked about before in [Introduction to Set Theory](https://yuvatia.io/pdfs/intro_set_theory.pdf)) we find that $\mathbb{Q}$ is not a proper subfield of $\mathbb{R}$, it is useful to think of $\mathbb{R}$ as an *extension* of the field $\mathbb{Q}$.

This brief discussion motivates the following definition.

>[!definition] extension field
>
>If $F$ is a field and $E$ is a field that has a subfield isomorphic to $E$, then we call $E$ an *extension field* of $F$, and $F$ the *base field* of $E$.

Via abuse of language, we tend to think of the above as *$E$ is an extension field of $F$ is $E$ restricted to $F$ is equal to $F$*.

>[!example] extension fields
>
>$\mathbb{C}$ is an extension of $\mathbb{R}$, which itself is an extension of $\mathbb{Q}$. Note that $\mathbb{C}$ is also an extension of $\mathbb{Q}$, and $\mathbb{R}$ is also an extension of itself - extension fields define a natural equivalence relation.
>
>We also see that $\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{D}]$, defined in a similar manner to definition 332 (quadratic integer ring) only with rational coefficients rather than simply ring coefficients, is an extension of $\mathbb{Q}$, and that $\mathbb{R}$ is itself an extension of $\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{D}]$.

>[!lemma]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$ is an irreducible polynomial over $F$ and $f(x) \neq 1$, then $F \diagup \langle f(x) \rangle$ is an extension field of $F$.


**Proof.** Note that since $F$ is a field, $F[x]$ is a PID by theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID). By supposition, $f(x)$ is irreducible, and since $F[x]$ is a PID then by theorem 342 (in a PID, prime and irreducible are equivalent) it is also prime, and by corollary 343 (in a PID, maximal ideal and prime ideal are interchangeable) $\langle f(x) \rangle$ is maximal, so by theorem 252  $E=F[x] \diagup \langle f(x) \rangle$ is a field. It remains to show that $F$ is embedded in $E$, i.e. find an injective field homomorphism $F \to E$, and indeed we have a natural injective ring homomorphism $\phi: F \to E, r \mapsto r+\langle f(x) \rangle$ (more rigorously, it should be noted that $r$ on the RHS is actually the constant polynomial $r$ in $F[x]$ and not $r \in F$, but at this point the reader should be able to fill such gaps themselves).

It is trivial to verify $\phi$ is indeed a field homomorphism (a field homomorphism is simply a ring homomorphism between fields). To verify it is injective, suppose not, then $r+\langle f(x) \rangle = s + \langle f(x) \rangle$ for some $r, s \in F, r \neq s$, so by theorem 99 (properties of a coset) $r-s \in \langle f(x) \rangle$, but $0 \leq \text{deg}(r-s) \leq \max \{\text{deg}(r), \text{deg}(s)\} = 0$ since $r, s$ are constant polynomials, and $f(x)$ is irreducible and not the unit so $1 \leq \text{deg}(f(x))$, so we have a polynomial of degree less than $f(x)$ in the principal ideal generated by $f(x)$, but then $r-s=f(x)h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in F[x]$ and by proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain) $0=\text{deg}(r-s)=\text{deg}(f(x))+\text{deg}(h(x))$ which is at least $\text{deg}(f(x))$ which is greater than $0$ by supposition, a contradiction, so it must be that $r=s$ (in which case the degree of $r-s$ is, of course, undefined), so $\phi$ is injective.

We conclude that $\phi$ is an injective field homorphism $F \to E$, so $E$ is an extension field of $F$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] fundamental theorem of field theory (Kronecker, 1887)
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$ is a non-zero non-constant polynomial, then there exists an extension field $E$ of $F$ in which $f(x)$ has a zero.


**Proof.** Since $F$ is a field then $F[x]$ is a UFD so we can write $f(x)=g(x)h(x)$ where $g(x)$ is irreducible, so by lemma 364 , $E=F[x] \diagup \langle g(x) \rangle$ is an extension field of $F$. Recall that, in coset notation, $(r+\langle A \rangle)^{n}=r^{n}+\langle A \rangle$ and also $(r+\langle A \rangle)(s+\langle A \rangle)=(r+s)+\langle A \rangle$ by definition of coset addition. 

Let $\alpha = x + \langle g(x) \rangle$, and write $g(x)$ explicitly as $g(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$, then via the evaluation map of $g(x)$ in $E=F[x] \diagup \langle g(x) \rangle$, we find:

$$
\begin{gathered}
g(\alpha)=\\
&g(x+\langle g(x) \rangle)=\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(x+\langle g(x) \rangle)^{i}=\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(x^{i}+\langle g(x) \rangle)=\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}+\langle g(x) \rangle = \\
&g(x) + \langle g(x) \rangle \underset{g(x) \in \langle g(x) \rangle}{=}\\
&&0
\end{gathered}
$$

So $\alpha$ is a zero of $g(x)$ in $E$, and since $g(x)$ is a factor of $f(x)$, then $f(0)=g(0)h(0)=0h(0)=0$, so it is a zero of $f(x)$ in $E$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The proof of the above provides a recipe for construction of such extension fields, as we will see in the next few examples:

>[!example] Constructing $\mathbb{C}$ from $\mathbb{R}$
>
>Consider $f(x)=x^{2}+1 \in \mathbb{R}$. It is a polynomial of degree $2$ with no zeros in $\mathbb{R}$, so it is irreducible over $\mathbb{R}$. Consider $E=\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$. By the above theorem, $\alpha=x+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is a zero of $f(x)$ in $E$, and indeed $f(\alpha)=(x+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle)^{2}+(1 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle)=(x^{2}+1)+\langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = 0_{E}$.
>
>By proposition 311 (zero implies reducible), we have $f(x)=(x-\alpha)h(x)$, and since $\deg(f(x))=2$ then $\text{deg}(h(x))=1$ so the only choice for $h(x)$ is $x+\alpha$, so we have $f(x)=(x-\alpha)(x+\alpha)$ (it is not immediately obvious, but can be shown using the fact that $x^{2} + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle = -1 + \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$), so we have $\pm \alpha$ are zeros of $x^{2}+1$ in $E$ (where we agreed to identify $1$ with $1+\langle x^{2}+1\rangle$ implicitly) . Note that these zeros satisfy $(\pm \alpha)^{2}=-1$,  i.e. they agree with our definition for $i$ as $\sqrt{-1}$, and indeed in example 309 ($\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \cong \mathbb{C}$) we saw that this factor field is exactly the field of complex numbers.
>
>The above construction provides a more natural way of forming $\mathbb{C}$ from $\mathbb{R}$.

>[!example]
>
>Consider $f(x)=x^{4}-9x^{2}+18=(x^{2}-6)(x^{2}-3)$ in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$. We know that it has no zeros in $\mathbb{Q}$ since $6, 3$ are both not perfect squares. theorem 365 (fundamental theorem of field theory (Kronecker, 1887)) provides us with instructions on how to extend $\mathbb{R}$ into fields where $f(x)$ has zeros by considering one of the irreducible factors and constructing a new field in which $f(x)$ has a zero originating from that factor.
>
>We can consider $E_{1}=\mathbb{Q}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}-6 \rangle$ and in that field we find $\alpha=x+\langle x^{2}-6\rangle$ s.t. $f(\pm \alpha)=0$, and likewise in $E_{2}=\mathbb{Q}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2} - 3 \rangle$ we find that for $\beta=x+\langle x^{2}-3 \rangle$, we have $f(\pm \beta)=0$. 
>
>In $E_{1}[x]$, we can factor $f(x)$ to $(x-\alpha)(x+\alpha)(x^{2}-3)$ and in $E_{2}[x]$, we can factor $f(x)$ to $(x^{2}-6)(x-\beta)(x+\beta)$ (recall that $E_{1}, E_{2}$ are extensions of $\mathbb{Q}$, but the polynomials themselves reside in the polynomial ring of those extension fields, i.e. $E_{1}[x], E_{2}[x]$). By combining both extension fields, we can find a factorization of $f(x)$ in terms of $4$ linear factors:
>
>First, instead of using $x$ as the indeterminate, we use $t$ to avoid confusion, and consider $E_{1}[t]=(\mathbb{Q}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}-6 \rangle)[t]$. As mentioned earlier, in this polynomial ring we find $f(t)=(t-\alpha)(t+\alpha)(t^{2}-3)$. We observe that $t^{2}-3$ is irreducible over $E_{1}$, so again by considering $E_{12}=E_{1}[t] \diagup \langle t^{2}-3 \rangle$ we find that $\beta=t+\langle t^{3}-3 \rangle$ is a zero of $f(t)$ in $E_{12}$, and similarly $-\beta$ is also a zero since $\text{deg}(t^{2}-3)=2$, so in conclusion we find that in the $E_{12}=(\mathbb{Q}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}-6 \rangle)[t] \diagup \langle t^{2}-3 \rangle$, $z^{4}-9z^{2}+18 \in E_{12}[z]$ can be factored into $(z-\alpha)(z+\alpha)(z-\beta)(z+\beta)$, where $\pm \alpha, \pm \beta$ are zeros of $f(z)$.


>[!definition] algebraic and transcendental
>
>If $E$ is an extension field of a field $F$, then $a \in E$ is called:
>1. *algebraic over $F$* if $\exists f(x) \in F[x], f(x) \neq 0$ for which $f(a)=0$ (where $f$ is evaluated in $\mathbb{C}$ via the evaluation map)
>2. *transcendental over $F$* otherwise.

>[!example] algebraic and transcendental
>
>Consider $i \in \mathbb{C}$. Since $\mathbb{C}$ is an extension field of $\mathbb{Q}$, and $f(x)=x^{2}-1$ is a polynomial in $\mathbb{Q}[x]$, and $f(i)=0$ (evaluated in $\mathbb{C}$), so $i$ is *algebraic* over $\mathbb{Q}$.
>
>Let $r \in \mathbb{R}$, then trivially for $f(x)=x-r$ we fined $f(r)=0$ evaluated in $\mathbb{R}$, so every element in $\mathbb{R}$ is algebraic over $\mathbb{R}$. This is true in general, and not a special property of $\mathbb{R}$. As an concrete example, we find $\pi$ and $e$ are algebraic over $\mathbb{R}$ since $x-\pi, x-e \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ have zeros in $\pi, e$ respectively.
>
>On the other hand, by the [Lindemann–Weierstrass theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindemann%E2%80%93Weierstrass_theorem) we can show that $e, \pi$ are *transcendental over $\mathbb{Q}$*. Showing a number is transcendental is much more involved then showing it is algebraic.
>
>$\sqrt{2}$ is algebraic over $\mathbb{Q}$, since it is a zero of $x^{2}-2 \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$.

If $a \in \mathbb{C}$ is a number constructed via a sequence of additions, subtraction, multiplication, and positive roots, it can be shown that $a$ is always algebraic over $\mathbb{Q}$. The proof for this is rather straightfoward via induction: essentially, every single operation can be "unravelled" via a matching inverse operation when constructing a polynomial: for example, if we took a positive root of $a$, we raise the matching polynomial expression to the exponent of that root, or if we added, we can subtract, etc. The next example demonstrates this process.

>[!example] proving $2+5\sqrt{3-2\sqrt[3]{5}}$ is algebraic over $\mathbb{Q}$
>
>Write $a=2+5\sqrt{3-2\sqrt[3]{5}}$. We proceed as follows:
>$$
>\begin{gathered}
>a=2+5\sqrt{3-2\sqrt{5}} \\
>a-2 = 5\sqrt{3-2\sqrt[3]{5}}\\
>\frac{1}{5}a-\frac{2}{5}=\sqrt{3-2\sqrt[3]{5}}\\
>(\frac{1}{5}a-\frac{2}{5})^{2}=3-2\sqrt[3]{5}\\
>(\frac{1}{5}a-\frac{2}{5})^{2}-3=-2 \sqrt[3]{5}\\
>((\frac{1}{5}a-\frac{2}{5})^{2}-3)^{3}=-8 \cdot 5 \\
>((\frac{1}{5}a-\frac{2}{5})^{2}-3)^{3}+40=0
>\end{gathered}
>$$
>Consider $f(x)=((\frac{1}{5}x-\frac{2}{5})^{2}-3)^{3}+40 \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ (it is clear that this is a polynomial since it consists of multiplication, addition and subtraction of polynomials, and polynomials over a field are a ring (in fact, an PID) so they are closed under these operations). By the above calculation, it is obvious that $f(a)=0$, so $a=2+4\sqrt{3-2\sqrt[3]5}$ is indeed algebraic over $\mathbb{Q}$.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $F$ is a field with extension $E$, and $a \in E$, then $a$ is transcendental over $F$ if and only if the evaluation map $\phi_{a}: F[x] \to E$ (see example 264 (evaluation map)) is injective.

**Proof.** By definition $a$ is transcendental over $F$ if and only if $\forall f(x) \in F[x], f(x) \neq 0 \implies f(a) \neq 0$, where $f(a)$ is simply $\phi_{a}(f(x))$. By definition, $\text{ker}(\phi_{a})=\{f(x) \in F[x] | \phi_{a}(f(x))=0\}$, so $a$ is transcendental over $F$ if and only if $\text{ker}(\phi_{a})=\{0\}$, and by theorem 87  this is true if and only if $\phi_{a}$ is injective (recall that $\phi_{a}$ is a ring homomorphism). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

It is worth stating that by theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings), this theorem also provides that $F[x] \cong \phi_{a}(F[x])$.

In number theory when a number is called *algebraic* or *transcendental*, it is considered in the context of the field $\mathbb{Q}$:

>[!definition] algebraic and transcendental number
>
>$a \in \mathbb{C}$ is called:
>1. A *transcendental number* if it is *transcendental over $\mathbb{Q}$*.
>2. An *algebraic number* otherwise.

Hence, we say $\pi$ and $e$ are *transcendental numbers*.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $E$ is an extension of a field $F$, and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, then:
>1. $\exists p(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$
>2. If $f(x) \in F[x], f(x) \neq 0$ s.t. $f(\alpha)=0$, then $p(x) \mid f(x)$, and
>3. $\text{deg}(p(x)) \leq \text{deg}(f(x))$, i.e. this $p(x)$ is the unique polynomial of least degree s.t. $p(\alpha)=0$ up to a multiplication by a constant.


**Proof.** Suppose $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, then the set $P=\{p(x) \in F[x] | p(\alpha)=0, p(x) \neq 0\}$ is nonempty, so by the WOP on the degrees of the elements of $P$, there exists a polynomial of least degree in $P$, denote it $p(x)$. This proves $(3)$.

If $p(x)$ is reducible over $F$, then $p(x)=h(x)g(x)$ for some non-unit $h(x), g(x) \in F[x]$, and also $p(\alpha)=h(\alpha)g(\alpha)$ hence $h(\alpha)g(\alpha)=0$. Since $F$ is a field, then either $h(\alpha)=0$ or $g(\alpha)=0$ since $F$ has no zero-divisors, so $\alpha$ is a zero of one of the factors of $p(x)$, but since by assumption both are non-units then $\text{deg}(h(x)), \text{deg}(g(x)) \lt \text{deg}(p(x))$ and yet w.l.o.g. $h(\alpha)=0$, violating the minimality of $\text{deg}(p(x))$, so $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$, which proves $(1)$.

To prove $(2)$, we will show that $P$ is an ideal: clearly it is non-empty since we assume $\alpha$ to be algebraic over $F$, so we proceed by theorem 232 (ideal test):
1. (closure under subtraction) let $a(x), b(x) \in P$, then $(a(x)-b(x))(\alpha)=a(\alpha)-b(\alpha)=0$ (here we used the fact that the evaluation map is a ring homomorphism)
2. (closure under multiplication with elements of $F[x]$) let $a(x) \in P, f(x) \in F$, then $a(x)f(x)=f(x)a(x)$ since $F[x]$ over a commutative ring is commutative, so it suffices to show $(a(x)f(x))(\alpha)=0$, and indeed under the evaluation map we find $(a(x)f(x))(\alpha)=a(\alpha)f(\alpha)=0f(\alpha)=0$, so $a(x)f(x) \in P$.

We conclude $P$ is an ideal. Since $F$ is a field then $F[x]$ is a PID by theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID), so $P=\langle t(x) \rangle$ for some $t(x) \in P$ s.t. $t(x)$ is a polynomial of least degree in $P$, so $P=\langle p(x) \rangle$, so $\forall f(x) \in P, f(x)=g(x)p(x)$ by definition of the principal ideal generated by an element, and by construction $P$ is the set of all polynomials in $F[x]$ that have $\alpha$ as a zero over $F$, so every such polynomial is divisible by $p(x)$, which proves $(2)$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that every polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $f(\alpha)=0$ and $f(x) \neq 0$ can also be written as a monic polynomial by dividing each coefficient by the leading coefficient, while maintaining the zero and the degree of the polynomial (since this is essentially multiplying by a constant polynomial). For example, $f(x)=2x^{2}+2$ has $i$ as a zero, and dividing all coefficients by $2$ (the leading coefficient of $f(x)$) gives $g(x)=x^{2}+1$, which is monic, has same degree, and also has $i$ as a zero. This fact, along with the theorem, justify the next definition:

>[!definition] irreducible polynomial for $\alpha$ over $F$
>
>If $E$ is an extension field of $F$ and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, then the unique monic polyonmial of least degree $p(x)$ which has $\alpha$ as a zero, is called the *irreducible polynomial for $\alpha$ over $F$*, or the *minimial polynomial* of $\alpha$ over $F$, and is denoted $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$. The degree of $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ is called the *degree of $\alpha$ over $F$*, denoted $\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$.

The previous theorem justifies the uniqueness of this polynomial and the use of the term *irreducible.

>[!example]
>
>In $\mathbb{Q}$, we find that $x^{5}-2$ is the polynomial of least degree that has $\sqrt[5]{2}$ as a zero, so we have $\text{irr}(\sqrt[5]{2}, \mathbb{Q})=x^{5}-2$ and also $\text{deg}(\sqrt[5]{2}, \mathbb{Q})=5$.

### Simple Extensions

>[!definition] field adjunction
>
>If $E$ is a field extension of $F$ and $S \subseteq E$ where $S=\{\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}\}$ a finite subset of elements of $E$, then the *smallest subfield* of $E$ that contains the set $F \cup S$ is called the field $F$ with $S$ adjoined (read "$F$ adjoin $S$") and denoted $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$.


It is crystal clear that $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ is the intersection of all subfields of $E$ that contain $F \cup S$.

>[!definition] simple extension
>
>If in definition 376 (field adjunction) we adjoin a set $S$ with order $1$ where $S=\{\alpha\}$ for some $\alpha \in E$, then $F(\alpha)$ as defined in that definition is called a *simple extension* of $F$, and we read it as "$F$ adjoin $\alpha$".


At this point we have seen two similar notations: we used square brackets when discussing definition 290 (polynomial ring) and definition 332 (quadratic integer ring), and parenthesis when discussing definition 377 (simple extension). In the case of a polynomial ring, we can think of $R[x]$ as $R$ adjoined with the indeterminate $x$, and in the case of the quadratic integers $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{D}]$ we similarly think of it as $\mathbb{Z}$ adjoined with $\sqrt{D}$. Notice that this is closely ties to our definition of $F(\alpha)$. As we will see in the next theorem, the definitions agree if $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$. 

Note that the notation is somewhat different, since each notation caries different meaning: the square brackets carry the information that the base structure is a ring, while the parenthesis are usually used to denote that the base structure is a field, i.e. $T[x]$ usually hints that we think of $T$ as a *ring*, while $T(x)$ hints that we think of $T$ is a *field*. We will follow this rule throughout our discussion. In short:
1. When we write $T[x]$, we refer to an extension of a *ring*.
2. When we write $T(x)$, we refer to an extension of a *field*.

>[!theorem] $F(\alpha)$ is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[\alpha]$
>
>Let $F$ be a field and $E$ be an extension over $F$ and $\alpha \in E$, then $F(\alpha)$ is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[\alpha]$ (i.e. isomorphic to the field of rational functions in $\alpha$).


**Proof.** In this proof we abuse the subset notation to denote "included via isomorphism", i.e. $A \subseteq B$ means "$B$ contains an isomorphic copy of $A$".

By definition $F(\alpha)$ is the smallest subfield of $E$ such that $F \cup \{\alpha\} \subseteq F(\alpha)$. Clearly $F[\alpha]$ contains an isomorphic copy of $F \cup \{\alpha\}$ in the form of its constant polynomials and the linear polynomial $f(\alpha)=\alpha$. Let $Q=\text{Frac}(F[\alpha])$ be the field of fractions of $F[\alpha]$ as defined in definition 274 (field of fractions), i.e. it is the field of rational functions in $\alpha$. We know that $Q$ is a field, and also $F[\alpha]$ is embedded in $Q$ via the natural homomorphism from an integral domain to its field of fractions,
so $F \cup \{\alpha\} \subseteq F[\alpha] \subseteq Q$. Via minimality of $F(\alpha)$, it follows that $F(\alpha) \subseteq Q$.

Since $F(\alpha)$ is a field and thus a ring containing $F$ and $\alpha$ then it must contain all expressions of the form $\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\alpha^{i}$ for $a_{i} \in F$, so $F[\alpha] \subseteq F(\alpha)$. Since $F(\alpha)$ is a field, then it must contain all quotients of elements in its subring $F[\alpha]$, which is exactly $Q$, so $Q \subseteq F(\alpha)$.

We have $F(\alpha) \subseteq Q$ and $Q \subseteq F(\alpha)$, so via mutual inclusion $F(\alpha)=Q$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] $F(\alpha) \cong F[\alpha]$ if $\alpha$ algebraic over $F$
>
>If $E$ is a field extension of $F$ and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, then $F(\alpha)$ as defined in definition 377 (simple extension) isomorphic to $F[\alpha]$ which is the polynomial ring over $F$ with $\alpha$ as the indeterminant.


**Proof.** Consider $\phi: F[x] \to F[\alpha]$ given by the substitution homomorphism (i.e. we substitute $x$ for $\alpha$ as the indeterminate of the univariate polynomial). Clearly, $\text{Im}(\phi)=F[\alpha]$, so by theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings) $F[x] \diagup \text{Ker}(\phi) \cong F[\alpha]$. Note that $\text{Ker}(\phi)$ is exactly the set of polynomials $f(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $f(\alpha)=0$.

There are two cases:
1. $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$, then $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$, so $F[\alpha] \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$. In theorem 380 ($F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$) ) (the next theorem), we show that the latter is isomorphic to $F(\alpha)$, so via the transitive property, $F[\alpha] \cong F(\alpha)$.
2. $\alpha$ is transcendental over $F$, then $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\{0\}$, so $F[\alpha] \cong F[x]$. Taking the field of fractions $\text{Frac}(F[\alpha])$, we find that it is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[x]$ by theorem 276 (fraction fields of isomorphic integral domains are isomorphic), which is just the field of rational functions in $x$.  By theorem 378 ($F(\alpha)$ is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[\alpha]$) ), we have that $F(\alpha)$ is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[x]$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] $F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$
>
>If $E$ is a field extension of $F$ and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, then $F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$.


**Proof.** Consider $\phi: F[x] \to F(\alpha), f(x) \mapsto f(\alpha)$, i.e. $\phi$ is the evaluation map over $F(\alpha)$ for $x=\alpha$. We know that $\phi$ is a ring homomorphism, so consider its kernel: let $f(x) \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $f(\alpha)=0$, so $f(\alpha)$ is either $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$, or it has $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ as a factor, so in all cases we find $f(x) \in \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$, and obviously all polynomials which have $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ as a factor belong to $\text{Ker}(\phi)$, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$, so by theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings), $F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle \cong \text{Im}(\phi)$. It is clear that $\phi(x)=\alpha$ and that $\forall c \in C (\phi(c)=c)$, so since $F \cup \{ \alpha \} \in \text{Im}(\phi)$ then by definition $F(\alpha) \subseteq \text{Im}(\phi)$.

Let $y \in \text{Im}(\phi)$, then $\phi(f(x))=y$ for some $f(x)$, so $y=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\alpha$ for some $\{a_{i}\} \in F$ by definition of the evaluation homomorphism, so clearly $y \in F(\alpha)$, so $\text{Im}(\phi) \subseteq F(\alpha)$, so $F(\alpha) = \text{Im}(\phi)$. We conclude $F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F) \rangle$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible over $F$  and $\alpha, \beta$ are zeros of $f(x)$ in some extensions $E_{a}, E_{b}$ respectively, then $F(\alpha) \cong F(\beta)$.


**Proof.** By theorem 380 ($F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$) ) since $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)=\text{irr}(\beta, F)=p(x)$, then $F(\alpha) \cong F(\beta)$ by the transitive property of isomorphism as an equivalence relation. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The next corollary lets us think of $F(\alpha)$ in terms of a finite dimensional vector space.

>[!corollary] $\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$
>
>If $E$ is a field extension of $F$ and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$ and $1 \leq \text{deg}(\alpha, F)$, then $F(\alpha)$ is a vector space (where $F$ is the field of scalars) with dimension $\dim(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$.


**Proof.** We leave it to the reader to show that $F(\alpha)$ satisfies all vector space axioms. We will show that $\text{deg}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$. Let $\beta \in F(\alpha)$, then by theorem 380 ($F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$) ), $\beta$ corresponds to some unique coset $f(x)+\langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$ (in the sense that $\phi_{\alpha}(f(x)+\langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F))=\beta$).

Since $F$ is a field, then  by theorem 300 (polynomial division algorithm) we can find unique $q(x), r(x) \in F[x]$, $\text{deg}(r(x)) \lt \text{deg}(\alpha, F)$ s.t. $f(x)=q(x)\text{irr}(\alpha, F)+r(x)$, so by the properties of a coset, we find $f(x)+\langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle=r(x) + \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$.

Denote $\text{deg}(\alpha, F)=n$ and $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)=p(x)$ and recall that $\alpha$ is identified with $x+\langle p(x) \rangle$, i.e. $\phi_{\alpha}(x+\langle p(x) \rangle)=\alpha$. First, since $\text{deg}(r(x)) \leq n$, then we can write $r(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}x^{i}$ for some $a_{i} \in F$. Next, since $\phi_{\alpha}(r(x)+\langle p(x) \rangle)=\beta$ by supposition, and $\phi_{\alpha}$ is the evaluation homomorphism, then

$$
\begin{gathered}
\beta = \\
&\phi_{\alpha}(r(x)+\langle p(x) \rangle) =\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n-1} \phi_{\alpha}(a_{i}x^{i} + \langle p(x) \rangle) =\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi_{\alpha}(a_{i})\phi_{\alpha}(x+\langle p(x) \rangle)^{i} = \\
&\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi_{\alpha}(a_{i})\alpha^{i} = \\
&& \sum_{i=0}^{n-1}b_{i}\alpha^{i}
\end{gathered}
$$

So $\beta$ is given by a linear combination of $e=\{1, \alpha, \alpha^{2}, \dots, \alpha^{n-1}\}$, so $\text{span}(e)=F(\alpha)$, so $\text{dim}(F(\alpha)) \leq n$.

Next, we show $e$ consists of a set of linearly independent vectors. Let $A$ be a diagonal matrix where $A_{ii}=e_{i}$, and let $t \in F^{n}$, and consider $At$. Suppose $At=0$ for some $t \neq 0$, then it follows that $At$ corresponds to some polynomial $t(x)$ of degree $n-1$ s.t. $t(\alpha)=0$ (because $At$ is exactly $\phi_{\alpha}(t(x))$), but by supposition the least degree of a polynomial that has $\alpha$ as a zero is $n$, a contradiction, so the only solution to $At=0$ is $t=0$, so by definition the columns of $A$ are linearly independent, i.e. $e$ is a set of linearly independent vectors in $F(\alpha)$.  

We have a set of $n$ linearly independent vectors that span the vector field, so $e$ is a basis of $F(\alpha)$, so $\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=|e|=n$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!example] $\mathbb{C}$ as a vector space
>
>By theorem 380 ($F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$) ) we see that $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{R}(i)$, and by a previous result we have seen that it is also isomorphic to $\mathbb{C}$, so $\mathbb{C}$ and $\mathbb{R}(i)$ are in a sense identical. Note that $\text{deg}(i, \mathbb{R})=2$ (since $\text{irr}(i, \mathbb{R})=x^{2}+1$),  so by corollary 382 ($\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$) it follows that $\mathbb{C}$ is a $2$ dimensional vector space. The corollary also provides us with the basis $\{1, i\}$ for $\mathbb{C}$.

>[!definition] point-wise fixing mapping
>
>If $C \subseteq A, C \subseteq B$ and $\phi: A \to B$ is a mapping s.t. $\phi_{C}: C \to C$ is $\text{id}_{C}$, then we say that $\phi$ *fixes $F$ point-wise*.

>[!corollary]
>
>If all conditions of corollary 381  are met, then there exists an isomorphism $\phi: F(\alpha) \to F(\beta)$ s.t. $\alpha \mapsto \beta$ and $\phi$ fixes $F$ pointwise.


**Proof.** Suppose $\alpha, \beta$ are both zeros of an irreducible $f(x) \in F[x]$ over some extension fields. By corollary 382 ($\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$),  and since $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)=\text{irr}(\beta, F)=f(x)$, then it follows $\dim(F(\beta))=\dim(F(\alpha))$, and furthermore that corollary also gives an explicit base for $F(\alpha), F(\beta)$: let $n=\text{dim}(F(\alpha))$, then $\{1, \dots, \alpha^{n-1}\}$ is a basis of $F(\alpha)$, and $\{1, \dots, \beta^{n-1}\}$ is a basis of $F(\beta)$.

Consider the mapping $\phi: F(\alpha) \to F(\beta)$ expressed in the following terms: for every $v \in F(\alpha)$, write $v=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\alpha^{i}$, then $\phi(v)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\beta^{i}$. Note that this mapping is well-defined, since from linear algebra we know that every vector has a unique coordinates $\begin{pmatrix} a_{0} \\ \dots \\ a_{n-1} \end{pmatrix}$ (suppose otherwise, then $v=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\alpha^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}b_{i}\alpha^{i}$, so $\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(a_{i}-b_{i})\alpha^{i}=0$,  and since $\{1, \dots, \alpha^{n-1}\}$ is a set of $n$ linearly independent vectors then $a_{i}=b_{i}$ for every $i$ by definition otherwise it violates linear independence of the basis).

Clearly, $\phi(\alpha)=\beta$ and $\forall f \in F$, we have $f=1 \cdot f$ so $\phi(f)=f$, so $\phi$ acts of $F(\alpha)$ as desired. It is easy to verify that $\phi$ also preserves addition, multiplication and unity, so it is a ring homomorphism, and clearly $\phi(a)=0 \iff a =0$ so it is injective, and finally it is trivially surjective since for every $y =\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\beta^{i}$ in $F(\beta)$, we have $v=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\alpha^{i} \in F(\alpha)$ s.t. $\phi(v)=y$. So, we conclude $\phi$ is an isomorphism that satisfies the specified properties.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
We late provide a different from for a more general claim to the one above, in our 
discussion of splitting fields.

>[!theorem]
>
>If $E$ is an extension field of $F$ and $S \subseteq E$, $S=\{a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}\}$, then:
>1. $F(a_{1}, a_{2})=F(a_{1})F(a_{2})=F(a_{2})(a_{1})$
>2. $F(a_{1}, \dots, a_{n})=F(a_{1})(\dots)(a_{n})$


**Proof.** 

$1$. By definition, $F(a_{1}, a_{2})$ is the smallest subfield of $E$ containing $a_{1}, a_{2}$ and $F$, so clearly $F(a_{1}, a_{2}) \subseteq F(a_{1})(a_{2})$. Also by definition of a simple extension, $F(a_{1}) \subseteq F(a_{1}, a_{2})$, so we have $F(a_{1}) \subseteq F(a_{1}, a_{2}) \subseteq F(a_{1}, a_{2})$, but by definition $F(a_{1})(a_{2})$ is the smallest subset containing both $F(a_{1})$ and $a_{2}$, and clearly $F(a_{1}, a_{2})$ contains $F(a_{1})$ and also $a_{2}$, so $F(a_{1})(a_{2}) \subseteq F(a_{1}, a_{2})$, so we conclude $F(a_{1})(a_{2})=F(a_{1}, a_{2})$. An identical argument for $F(a_{2})(a_{1})$ shows $F(a_{2})(a_{1})=F(a_{1}, a_{2})$, which completes the proof of $(1)$.

$2$. Trivially from $(1)$ via induction, left as an exercise.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The above theorem lets us forget about the order in which we adjoined $S$ to $F$. For example, to add $\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3}$ to $\mathbb{Q}$ and form $\mathbb{Q}$ adjoined with $\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3}$, we can:
1. Extend $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$, then extend $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})(\sqrt{3})$.
2. Extend $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3})$, then extend $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3})$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3})(\sqrt{2})$.
3. Extend $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3})$.

By the theorem, all three constructions are the same.

## Splitting Fields

>[!definition] splitting field
>
>If $E$ is an extension field of $F$ and $f(x) \in F[x]$ has degree at least $1$, then we say that $f(x)$ *splits* in $E$ if $\exists a \in F$ and $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n} \in E$ s.t. $f(x)=a\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x-\alpha_{i})$. If $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$, then we say that $E$ is a *splitting field* for $f(x)$ over $F$.

Equivalently, we say that $E$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$ over $F$ is $E$ is an extension of $F$ and $f(x)$ can be factored into linear factors over $E$.

>[!example] $\mathbb{R}(i)$ is a splitting field for $x^{2}+1$ over $\mathbb{R}$
>
>Consider our main example again of $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle$.
