We have seen that $\mathbb{R}[x] \diagup \langle x^{2}+1 \rangle \cong \mathbb{R}(i)$, and that in $\mathbb{R}(i)[x]$, we can write $x^{2}+1$ as $(x+i)(x-i)$, i.e. $x^{2}+1$ can be factored into linear factors over $\mathbb{R}(i)$, so by definition $\mathbb{R}(i)$ is a splitting field of $x^{2}+1$ over $\mathbb{R}$.

>[!example] $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{6})$ is a splitting field of $x^{4}-9x^{2}+18$ over $\mathbb{Q}$
>
>Consider example 367   in which we considered $f(x)=x^{4}-9x^{2}+18=(x^{2}-6)(x^{2}-3)$ over $\mathbb{Q}$. We have seen that in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3})(\sqrt{6})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{6})$ (by theorem 386 ), $f(x)$ is splittable, so $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{6})$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$ over $\mathbb{Q}$.
>
>Note that the base field is obviously important here - since $\pm \sqrt{3}, \pm \sqrt{6} \in \mathbb{R}$, then $\mathbb{R}$ also splits $f(x)$ over itself (but it is *not* a splitting field of $f(x)$ since it contains other elements such as $\sqrt{5}$ which is not in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{6})$, however in $\mathbb{Q}$ we need to consider a proper extension to allow for $f(x)$ to be splittable.

>[!theorem] splitting field existence theorem
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$ has degree at least one, then there exits a splitting field $E$ for $f(x)$ over $F$.
>


**Proof.** We prove by induction on $\text{deg}(f(x))$. If $\text{deg}(f(x))=1$, then we're done as $f(x)$ is already splittable over $F$ since $f(x)=ax+b$ where $a, b \in F$ and $-b^{-1}a \in F$ is the only zero of $f(x)$ (by proposition 305 (a polynomial of degree $n$ has at most $n$ zeros)). Suppose the proposition holds for all degrees less than some $1 \lt n$. By theorem 365 (fundamental theorem of field theory (Kronecker, 1887)), there exists an extension field $E$ of $F$ in which $\exists \alpha_{n} \in E$ s.t. $f(\alpha_{n})=0$, so we can write $f(x)=(x-\alpha_{n})g(x)$ in $E[x]$. By proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain), $\deg(g(x))=\deg(f(x))-1=n-1$, so by the induction hypothesis there exists a splitting field $E_{n-1}=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n-1})$. Consider $E_{n-1}$ adjoined with $\alpha_{n}$, then we have a new field $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n-1})(\alpha_{n})=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$, where $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ are $n$ zeros of $f(x)$, so $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$ over $F$.<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Essentially, the previous theorem constructs the splitting field of a polynomial by recursively applying theorem 365 (fundamental theorem of field theory (Kronecker, 1887)) until no non-linear irreducible factors are left for $f(x)$.

Splitting fields play a pivotal role in Galois theory.

>[!theorem] zeros of an irreducible over a splitting field
>
>If $f(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible over $F$ and $E$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$ over $F$, then all zeros of $f(x)$ in $E$ have same multiplicity.

**Proof.** Suppose $\alpha, \beta$ are zeros of $f(x)$, then $f(x)=(x-\alpha)^{n}(x-\beta)^{m}g(x)$ by definition for some $n, m \in \mathbb{N}^{+}, g(x) \in E[x]$. By corollary 385  there exists an isomorphism $\phi: F(\alpha) \to F(\beta)$ that exchanges the roles of $\alpha, \beta$, so it must be that $\alpha$ has multiplicity at least $m$. Likewise, since $\phi^{-1}: F(\beta) \to F(\alpha)$ is an isomorphism satisfying the same property, we have that the multiplicity of $\beta$ is at least that of $\alpha$, so we have $n \leq m$ and $m \leq n$, so $m=n$, so all zeros of $f(x)$ have same multiplicity.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Suppose $E, K$ are both splitting fields for $f(x) \in F[x]$ over $F$. The question arises, is $E \cong K$? The answer is yes, as we will now show.

>[!lemma]
>
>Let $F, F'$ be fields and $E, K$ be extension fields of $F, F'$ respectively and $\alpha \in E, \beta \in K$, then if:
>1. $F \cong F'$ via $\phi: F \to F'$
>2. $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$ with minimal polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$
>3. $\beta$ is a zero of the image of $\overline{\phi}(f(x)) \in F'[x]$, where $\overline{\phi}$ is the natural extension of $\phi$ to a mapping between the polynomial rings, given by $\overline{\phi}: F[x] \to F'[x], \sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i} \mapsto \sum_{i=0}^{n}\overline{\phi}(a_{i})x^{i}$.
>
>Then $F(\alpha) \cong F'(\beta)$, and furthermore $\exists! \tilde{\phi}: F(\alpha) \to F'(\beta)$ s.t. $\tilde{\phi}_{|F}=\phi$ (i.e. $\tilde{\phi}$ agrees with $\phi$ on $F$) and $\tilde{\phi}(\alpha)=\beta$.
>


**Proof.** By theorem 380 ($F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle \text{irr}(\alpha, F)\rangle$) ), we have that $F(\alpha) \cong F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle$ via some isomorphism $\sigma: F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle \to F(\alpha)$, and $F'(\beta) \cong F'[x] \diagup \langle \overline{\phi}(p(x))\rangle$ via an isomorphism $\tau: F'[x] \diagup \langle\overline{\phi}(p(x)) \rangle \to F'(\beta)$. By theorem 257 (properties of ring homomorphism) that $\langle (\overline{\phi}(p(x))) \rangle = \overline{\phi}(\langle p(x) \rangle)$. Denote $\overline p(x) \rangle = I$, and consider a mapping $\psi: F[x]  \to F'[x] \diagup \overline{\phi}(I)$ given by $\psi: f(x) \mapsto \overline{\phi}(f(x))+\overline{\phi}(I)$. It is trivial to see that $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism from the properties of $\overline{\phi}$ as a homomorphism and the definition of addition and multiplication in factor rings. Also, $\psi(f(x))=0 \iff f(x) \in \overline{\phi}(I)$ by theorem 99 (properties of a coset), so $\text{Ker}(\psi)=I$, so by theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings) $F[x] \diagup \langle p(x) \rangle  \cong F'[x] \diagup \langle \overline{\phi}(p(x)) \rangle$. So by the transitive property, $F(\alpha) \cong F'(\beta)$. 

We can find the implicit isomorphism that arises from this construction: let $\overline{\psi}: F[x] \diagup \langle (p(x)) \rangle \to F'[x] \diagup \langle \overline{\phi}(p(x)) \rangle$ be the isomorphism that arises naturally from $\psi$, then $\overline{\psi}: f(x) + \langle p(x) \rangle \mapsto \overline{\phi}(f(x)) + \langle \overline{\phi}(p(x)) \rangle$ (see proof for theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings) for the construction of this isomorphism), then since the composition of isomorphisms is an isomorphism, we have that $\omega = \tau \circ \overline{\psi} \circ \sigma^{-1}: F(\alpha) \to F'(\beta)$ is an isomorphism. Let $f \in F$, then $\omega(f)=\tau(\overline{\psi}(\sigma^{-1}(f)))=\tau(\overline{\psi}(f))=\tau(\phi(f))=\phi(f)$, so $\omega$ agrees with $\phi$ on $F$, and also $\omega(\alpha)=\tau(\overline{\psi}(\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)))=\tau(\overline{\psi}(x))=\tau(x)=\beta$, so $\omega(\alpha)=\beta$, as desired. (in the theorem, we denoted this $\omega$ by $\tilde{\phi}$).

Next, we show uniqueness. Since every element of $F(\alpha)$ is given by $x=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\alpha^{i}$ for $a_{i} \in F$, then by properties of a homomorphism any isomorphism that agrees with $\phi$ on $F$ and sends $\alpha$ to $\beta$ must map $x$ to $\sum_{i=0}^{n}\phi(a_{i})\beta^{i}$, so such mapping is unique, so $\omega$ is unique.

The following commutative diagram illustrates the relations between these structures:

<TikzSvge4ac778030c9818194a4117faaf16201 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] splitting fields of polynomials under isomorphism are isomorphic
>
>Let $F, F'$ be isomorphic via $\phi: F \to F'$ and $p(x) \in F[x]$ be a non-constant polynomial and $E, K$ be splitting fields for $p(x)$ over $F$, $\overline{\phi}(p(x))$ (defined naturally via $\phi$) over $F'$, then $E \cong K$.


**Proof.** Let $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ be the zeros of $p(x)$ in the splitting field $E$ and let $\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n}$ be the zeros of $q(x)=\overline{\phi}(p(x))$ in the splitting field $K$, so by definition $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ and $K=F'(\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n})$. 

We prove by induction on $n$: for $n=1$, by lemma 392  $F(\alpha_{1}) \cong F'(\beta_{1})$. Suppose the theorem holds for some $n$, and consider $n+1$, then by theorem 386 , $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n+1}) = F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})(\alpha_{n+1})$ and also $F'(\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n+1})=F'(\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n})(\beta_{n+1})$. By the induction hypothesis, $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}) \cong F'(\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n})$, so again we have fields of the form $P(\alpha')$ and $P'(\beta')$ where $P \cong P'$ so again by lemma 392 , $P(\alpha') \cong P'(\beta')$, so by induction $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}) \cong F'(\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{n})$, so $E \cong K$.

This construction is expressed below:

<TikzSvge9507385b5590a0d05250d90f0bfa0a3 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] uniqueness of splitting field
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ is non-constant, and $E$ is a splitting field for $p(x)$ over $F$, then it is unique up to an isomorphism, which fixes $F$.


**Proof.** By theorem 393 (splitting fields of polynomials under isomorphism are isomorphic), substituting $F'$ for $F$, we find that every two splitting fields for $p(x)$ over $F$ are isomorphic, so the splitting field is unique up to an isomorphism that also fixes $F$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Let $p(x)$ be irreducible over $F$, then in this section we have established that there is a unique (up to isomorphism) splitting field $E$ over $F$ for $p(x)$. We have also seen that clearly, a polynomial may split in a field which is not its splitting field. Consider a simple example: let $x^{2}-2 \in \mathbb{Q}$, then $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}, -\sqrt{2})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ is a splitting field for $x^{2}-2$ over $\mathbb{Q}$, however $x^{2}-2$ also splits in $\mathbb{R}$, so we expect that $\mathbb{R}$ also contains a subfield $K$ s.t. $K$ is a splitting field for $x^{2}-2$ over $\mathbb{Q}$, and also that $K$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ (by corollary 394 (uniqueness of splitting field)). More generally, we expect that if $p(x) \in F[x]$ splits over an extension $E$ over $F$, and $K$ is a splitting field for $p(x)$, then $K$ is isomorphic to a subfield of $E$. The next theorem shows that this is true:

>[!theorem]
>
>If $F$ is a field and $p(x) \in F[x]$ splits in an extension $E$ over $F$, and $K$ is a splitting field for $p(x)$ over $F$, then $K$ is isomorphic to a subfield of $E$.


**Proof.** Suppose $p(x)$ splits in $E$ and also that $F \subseteq E$ (through an isomorphism, since we suppose $E$ is an extension of $F$), and let $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ be the zeros of $p(x)$ in $E$ (not necessarily distinct), then $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ is a subfield of $E$ and also by definition it is a splitting field for $p(x)$, so by corollary 394 (uniqueness of splitting field), $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}) \cong K$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Now, suppose $E$ is an extension over some field where every polynomial that has a zero in $E$ also splits in $E$. We can such field a *normal extension field*. We make use of this definition later, when we discuss Galois theory.

>[!definition] normal extension
>
>If $E$ is an extension of a field $F$, and $\forall p(x) \in F[x]$, either $p(x)$ is irreducible over $E$ or $p(x)$ splits over $E$ (equivalently, if $p(x)$ has a zero in $E$ then it splits over $E$), we say that $E$ is a *normal extension* of $F$.


>[!example] $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ is not a normal extension of $\mathbb{Q}$
>
>Note that $\text{irr}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \mathbb{Q})=x^{3}-2$ (by Eisenstein's criteria it is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$). By De Moivre's formula, we find that the other zeros of $x^{3}-2$ are complex, yet $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{\sqrt{2}}) \subset \mathbb{R} \subset \mathbb{C}$, so $x^{3}-2$ doesn't split over $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ even though clearly this polynomial is reducible over that field, so this field is not a normal extension.


In the next section, we study the multiplicity of the zeros of (mostly irreducible) polynomials.

## Multiple Zeros of a Polynomial

In [[#Zeros and Division]] we discussed the zeros of a polynomial and derived some elementary results. In proposition 305 (a polynomial of degree $n$ has at most $n$ zeros) we say that $\text{deg}(f(x))=n$ implies that $f(x)$ has at most $n$ zeros counting multiplicity. Here we discuss necessary and sufficient conditions for a zero of a polynomial to have multiplicity greater than $1$, and for a polynomial to have such zero. We call such zero a *multiple zero*.

>[!definition] multiple and simple zero
>
>If $\alpha$ is a zero of a polynomial $f(x)$ and $\alpha$ has multiplicity more than $1$, then we say $\alpha$ is a *multiple zero* of $f(x)$. If a zero of $f(x)$ has multiplicity $1$, we say it is a *simple zero* of $f(x)$.

>[!definition] separable polynomial
>
>A polynomial $f(x)$ is *separable* if all its zeros are simple, i.e. if it has number of distinct zeros equal to ints degree.

>[!example]
>
>In $(x-3)^{2}$, $3$ has multiplicity $2$ since $(x-3)^{2}$ splits to $(x-3)(x-3)$ over e.g. $\mathbb{Z}$, so the zeros of $(x-3)^{2}$ are $\{3, 3\}$ i.e. $3$ has multiplicity $2$, so it is a multiple zero of $(x-3)^{2}$. Also, $(x-2)(x+5)(x-7)$ is separable, since it has degree $3$ and $3$ distinct roots.

Consider $f(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$. If $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is a zero of $f(x)$ with multiplicity greater than $1$, then $f(x)=(x-\alpha)^{n}g(x)$ where $n \in \mathbb{N}, 1 \lt n$. From calculus, we know by the product rule that $f'(x)$ (here we use $f'(x)$ to denote the *derivative* of $f(x)$, unlike in other sections where we used it freely to denote *something that related to $f(x)$ but isn't $f(x)$*) is given by $(n-1)(x-\alpha)^{n-1}g(x)+(x-\alpha)^{n}g'(x)$. Clearly, we find that if $1 \lt n$ then $f'(\alpha)=0$, so we find that if $\alpha$ is a multiple zero of $f(x)$ then $f(\alpha)=f'(\alpha)=0$. Building upon this result it is easy to show by Bezout's identity for PIDs that $f(x), f'(x)$ have a common factor. We would like to state this argument for a general polynomial ring over a field, but as the notion of a derivative from calculus makes use of limits which we cannot define on an arbitrary field, so instead we present a definition for the derivative of a polyonmial which does not make of use limits, and agrees with the definition from calculus in case a limit can be defined on the polynomials at a point.

>[!definition] formal derivative
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i} \in F[x]$, then the *formal derivative* of $f(x)$, denoted $f'(x)$, is given by $f'(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i \cdot a_{i}x^{i-1}$.

>[!example] formal derivative of $x^{3}-5x^{2}+9$ over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$
>
>By definition, the formal derivative of $x^{3}-5x^{2}+9$ is given by $(2)\cdot (-5x^{2-1})+3 \cdot x^{3-1}=-10x+3x^{2}$ over $\mathbb{Z}[x]$. Observe that this agrees with the derivative of $x^{3}-5x^{2}+9$ over $\mathbb{R}$ from calculus.

>[!theorem] properties of the formal derivative
>
>If $f(x), g(x) \in F[x], \alpha \in F$, then:
>1. $(f(x)+g(x))'=f'(x)+g'(x)$ (linearity)
>2. $(\alpha f(x))'=\alpha f'(x)$ (homogeneity)
>3. $(f(x)g(x))'=f'(x)g(x)+g'(x)f(x)$ (product rule)


We will not prove this theorem here, and it should not come as a surprise to anyone familiar with elementary calculus. $(1)$ and $(2)$ follow immediately form the definition, and $(3)$ follows from $(1)$ and induction on $\text{deg}(f(x))$.

Now we can complete the argument we started before presenting this definition and state it for any polynomial ring over a field:

>[!theorem] criterion for multiple zeros
>
>Let $F$ be a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$, then $f(x)$ has a multiple zero over some extension $E$ over $F$ if and only if $f(x), f'(x)$ have a common non-constant factor in $F[x]$.


**Proof.** $\implies$Suppose $\alpha \in E$ is a multiple zero of $f(x)$, then $f(x)=(x-\alpha)^{n}g(x)$ for some $g(x) \in F[x]$, $1 \lt n$. By the product rule from theorem 403 (properties of the formal derivative), we have $f'(x)=n(x-\alpha)^{n-1}g(x)+(x-\alpha)^{n}g'(x)$. Clearly, $f'(\alpha)=0$. If $f(x), f'(x)$ have no common non-constant factor in $F[x]$, then $\text{gcd}(f(x), f'(x))=1$ and so $a(x)f(x)+b(x)f'(x)=1$ for some $a(x), b(x) \in F[x]$ by theorem 306 (polynomial ring over a field is a PID) and theorem 345 (Bezout's identity for PIDs). Evaluating $a(x)f(x)+b(x)f'(x)$ at $x=\alpha$, we have $0=1$, which is impossible, so $f(x), f'(x)$ must have a common non-constant factor in $F[x]$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $\exists h(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $1 \leq \text{deg}(h(x))$ and also $h(x) \mid f(x), h(x) \mid f'(x)$, then $\exists g(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $f(x)=h(x)g(x)$. Let $\alpha$ be a zero of $h(x)$ over some extension field $E$, then $h(x)=(x-\alpha)t(x)$ for some $t(x) \in F[x]$, so $f(x)=(x-\alpha)t(x)g(x)$. Denote $t(x)g(x)=a(x)$, then by the product rule $f'(x)=t(x)+(x-\alpha)t(x)$. Observe that $f'(\alpha)=t(\alpha)$, but by supposition $h(x) \mid f'(x)$ so as $h(\alpha)=0$ then $f'(\alpha)=0$, so $t(\alpha)=0$, so $x-\alpha \mid t(x)$, so it follows that $h(x)=(x-\alpha)^{2}b(x)$ for some $b(x) \in F[x]$, so $h(x)$ has $\alpha$ as a multiple zero, so $f(x)$ has $\alpha$ as a multiple zero.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!lemma]
>
>If $f(x) \in F[x]$ is a non-constant irreducible over a field $F$, and $f(x)$ has a multiple zero, then $f'(x)=0$ constantly.


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x) \in F[x]$ is a non-constant irreducible and has a multiple zero, then by theorem 404 (criterion for multiple zeros), it follows that $f(x), f'(x)$ have some $g(x) \in F[x]$ as a common factor s.t. $1 \leq \deg(g(x))$, but since $f(x)$ is irreducible over $F$ then it must be that $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ associate, so $f(x)=\alpha g(x)$ for some $\alpha \in F$, so homogeneity of the derivative $f'(x)=\alpha g'(x)$, but also we know that $f'(x)=g(x)h(x)$ for some $h(x) \in F[x]$ by supposition. If $f'(x)$ is non-zero, then $\deg (f'(x))=\deg(g'(x)) = \deg(g(x))+\deg(h(x))$ by the degree equation, but by definition of the derivative it is obvious that $\deg(g'(x)) \lt \deg(g(x))$ for every non-constant $g(x)$, a yet here we have $\deg (g(x)) \leq \deg(g'(x))$, a contradiction, so it follows that $h(x)=0$, so $f'(x)=0$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!theorem] multiple zeros of an irreducible
>
>Let $f(x) \in F[x]$ be irreducible over $F$, then:
>1. If $\text{char}(F)=0$, then $f(x)$ has no multiple zeros (i.e. it is separable)
>2. Otherwise, $f(x)$ has a multiple zero only if $f(x)=g(x^{\text{char}(F)})$ for some $g(x) \in F[x]$. 


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x) \in F[x]$ and suppose $f(x)$ has a multiple zero in some extension field $E$ over $F$, then by lemma 405  it follows that $f'(x)=0$. Let $n=\text{deg}(f(x))$, then $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}$ for some $a_{i} \in $, and by definition $f'(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i \cdot a_{i} x^{i-1}$. It follows that $f'(x)=0$ if and only if $\forall i \in [1, n]$, $i \cdot a_{i} = 0$. If $\text{char}(F)=0$, then this is impossible by definition of the characteristic. Otherwise, denote $\text{char}(F)=p$, then $i \cdot a_{i}=0$ implies that $i$ divides $p$, since this must hold for every non-zero coefficient $a_{i}$ then it must be that $f(x)=g(x^{p})$ for some $g(x) \in F[x]$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The above theorem motivates studying the structure of fields that share this property. Such fields are called *perfect fields*.

>[!definition] perfect field
>
>A field $F$ is *perfect* if $\text{char}(F)=0$ or if $\text{char}(F)=p \neq 0$ and $F^{p}=\{a^{p} | a \in F\}=F$. 


The next theorem shows that all finite fields are perfect.

>[!theorem] finite fields are perfect
>
>Every finite field is perfect.


**Proof.** Let $F$ be a field and suppose $F$ is finite, then $F$ has characteristic $p$ for some prime $p \in \mathbb{N}$ (the characteristic cannot be $0$ because then it is impossible for $F$ to be finite). Let $\phi_{p}: F \to F, a \mapsto a^{p}$. We will show that $\phi_{p}$ is an automorphism on $F$, i.e. $\phi_{p}$ is an isomorphism from $F$ to itself.

First, we show $\phi_{p}$ is a ring homomorphism: let $a, b \in F$, then $\phi_{p}(a+b)=(a+b)^{p}$. We expand $(a+b)^{p}$ in $F$ by the binomial theorem:

$$
(a+b)^{p}=\frac{p!}{0!p!}a^{p}b^{0}+\frac{p!}{1!(p-1)!}a^{p-1}b^{1}+\dots + \frac{p!}{(p-1)!1!}a^{1}b^{p-1}+\frac{p!}{p!0!}a^{0}b^{p}
$$
Since $p$ is prime (by corollary 226 (characteristic of integral domain) since $\text{char}(F) \neq 0$ then it is prime), then $p \mid p!$ and also $p \not \mid k!$ for any $k \in [0!, (p-1)!]$, it follows that all binomial coefficients of the form $\binom{p}{k}$ where $k \neq 0, p$ are divisible by $p$, so all terms with these coefficients vanish since $\text{char}(F)=p$, so the only terms that remain are the first and the last (since in both cases we have $\binom{p}{0}=\binom{p}{p}=1$, which is not divisible by $p$), and so

$$
\phi_{p}(a+b)=(a+b)^{p}=a^{p}+b^{p}=\phi_{p}(a)+\phi_{p}(b)
$$

This result is sometimes jokingly referred to as [Freshman's dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshman%27s_dream).

Next, we show $\phi_{p}$ preserves multiplication:

$$
\phi_{p}(ab)=(ab)^{p}=a^{p}b^{p}=\phi_{p}(a)\phi_{p}
$$

Finally, we find that $\phi_{p}$ preserves unity, since trivially $\phi_{p}(1)=1^{p}=1$. So, $\phi_{p}$ is a ring (field) homomorphism from $F$ to itself.

Next, we show $\phi_{p}$ is injective by showing that its kernel is $0$. Suppose $\phi_{p}(a)=0$, then $a^{p}=0$, and by definition $aa^{p-1}=0$ (since $1 \lt p$ since $p$ is prime). Since $F$ is a field, then it has no zero-divisors, so either $a=0$ or $a^{p-1}=0$. By induction on $p$ it is clear that it must be that $a=0$ (since for every $1 \lt p$ it follows that if $a \neq 0$ then $a^{p-1}=0$, and eventually we reach $p=2$ for each $a=a^{p-1}=a^{1}$), so $\text{ker}(\phi_{p})=\{0\}$, so $\phi_{p}$ is injective.

Note that the results above hold for any field with characteristic $p \neq 0$.

Finally, we will show $\phi_{p}$ is surjective. Since $\phi_{p}$ is injective and $F$ is finite, then $|F|=|\text{Im}(\phi_{p})|$, and since $\text{Im}(\phi_{p}) \subseteq F$, then obviously $\text{Im}(\phi_{p})=F$ (note that this is *not* the case if $F$ is not finite - consider for example the mapping $f: (0, 2) \to (0, 1), x \mapsto \frac{x}{2}$, clearly it is injective yet the sets $(0, 2)$ and $(0, 1)$ are not the same, even though $(0, 1) \subseteq (0, 2)$).

Since $\phi_{p}$ is injective, surjective, and a field homomorphism from $F$ to itself, then it is an *automorphism* from $F$ to itself, and clearly $\text{Im}(\phi_{p})=F^{p}$ as defined in definition 407 (perfect field), so $F$ is a perfect field. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The mapping we defined in the proof of theorem 408 (finite fields are perfect) is called *the Frobenius endomorphism*.

>[!definition] Frobenius endomorphism
>
>The *Frobenius endomorphism* is a mapping $\phi_{p}: R \to R, a \mapsto a^{p}$, where $R$ is a commutative ring with prime characteristic $p$.

By the proof of theorem 408 (finite fields are perfect), the Frobenius endomorphism is indeed a commutative ring endomorphism (an *endomorphism* is a homomorphism from a structure to itself).

>[!theorem] equivalent definitions of a perfect field
>
>The following are equivalent:
>1. $F$ is a perfect field.
>2. $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$, or the Frobeinus endomorphism is an automorphism on $F$.


**Proof.** $2 \implies 1$ is covered by theorem 406 (multiple zeros of an irreducible).

$1 \implies 2$: If $F$ has characteristic $0$ then it is perfect by definition. Otherwise, suppose $\text{char}(F)=p$ for some prime $p$ and that $\phi_{p}: a \mapsto a^{p}$ is an automorphism on $F$, so $F=F^{p}$ since an automorphism is a surjection, so $F$ is perfect.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] irreducible polynomials over a perfect field are separable
>
>If $f(x)$ is an irreducible polynomial over a perfect field $F$, then $f(x)$ is separable.


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x)$ is irreducible over a perfect field $F$. If $\text{char}(F)=0$, then already it follows $f(x)$ has no multiple zeros by theorem 406 (multiple zeros of an irreducible). Otherwise, $\text{char}(F)=p$ for some prime $p \in \mathbb{N}$. Suppose $f(x)$ has a multiple zero, then by the theorem $f(x)=g(x^{p})$ for some $g(x) \in F[x]$. Also, since $F$ is perfect, then $\forall a \in F \exists b \in F$ s.t. $a=b^{p}$, so all coefficients $a_{i}$ of $g(x)$ can be written as $a_{i}=b_{i}^{p}$ for some $b_{i} \in F$, so

$$
g(x^{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(x^{p})^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}^{p}(x^{p})^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(b_{i}x^{i})^{p}=(*)
$$

Since $F$ has prime characteristic, then the Frobenius endomorphism is indeed an endomorphism on $F$ by our proof of theorem 406 (multiple zeros of an irreducible), and so immediately we find that $(*)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(b_{i}x^{i})^{p}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\phi_{p}(b_{i}x^{i})=\phi_{p}(\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}x^{i})$, where the last transition is due to addition being preserved under a homomorphism. Denote $\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}x^{i}=h(x)$, then $f(x)=g(x^{p})=\phi_{p}(h(x))=(h(x))^{p}$, but then $h(x)$ is a factor of $f(x)$, contradicting $f(x)$ being irreducible.

We conclude that it must be that $f(x)$ has no multiple zeros, so it is separable
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The last theorem motivates the following definition:

>[!definition] separable extension
>
>If $E$ is an extension of a field $F$, and $\alpha \in E$, its minimal polynomial $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ exists (i.e. $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$) and is separable, then $E$ is a *separable extension field* of $F$.


## Algebraic Extensions

>[!definition] algebraic extension
>
>An extension $E$ of a field $F$ is called *algebraic* if $\forall \alpha \in E$, $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$. Otherwise, the extension is called a *transcendental extension*.


The above simplifies definition 412 (separable extension): we say that $E$ is a separable extension over $F$ if $E$ is an algebraic extension and every minimal polynomial for $\alpha \in E$ is separable (the difference is that the requirement that such minimal polynomial exists is substituted with requiring that $E$ be algebraic over $F$).

>[!theorem] algebraic extension of a perfect field is separable
>
>If $E$ is an algebraic extension of a field $F$, and $F$ is a perfect field, then $E$ is a separable extension of $F$.


**Proof.** Let $\alpha \in E$, then since $E$ is algebraic, $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ exists. By theorem 411 (irreducible polynomials over a perfect field are separable), it is separable, hence by definition $E$ is separable. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] finite extension
>
>If an extension $E$ of a field $F$ forms a vector space of finite dimension $n$ over $F$, then we say that $E$ is  a *finite extension of degree $n$* and write $[E:F]=n$.


Note that we used a similar notation to denote the index of a subgroup as a part of our discussions on Lagrange's theorem and orbits and stabilizers. The context should make our intention using the notation clear.

>[!proposition]
>
>If $E$ is an extension of $F$, then $[E:F]=1 \iff E = F$.


**Proof.** $\implies$Suppose $[E:F]=1$, then $\dim(E)=1$ (where $F$ is the field of scalars). Obviously, $1 \in E$, so $1$ is a basis of $F$, but in theorem 213 ($F$ is a vector space over itself with dimension $1$) we see that $1$ is also a basis of $F$ over itself, so $E=F$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $E=F$, then by theorem 213 ($F$ is a vector space over itself with dimension $1$) clearly $\dim(E)=\dim(F)=1$, so $[E:F]=1$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] finite extensions are algebraic
>
>If $E$ is a finite extension over $F$, then $E$ is algebraic over $F$.


**Proof.** Suppose $E$ is a finite extension, then $[E:F]=n$ for some finite $n$, and let $\alpha \in E$. Consider the set $\{1, \alpha, \dots, \alpha^{n}\}$. IT is a set of $n+1$ vectors in $E$, and since $\text{deg}(E)=n$ it is linearly dependent, so we can find some $c_{0}, \dots, c_{n} \in F$ s.t. $\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}\alpha^{i}=0$. Consider the polynomial $f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}x^{i} \in F[x]$. Clearly, $f(\alpha)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}\alpha^{i}=0$, so $f(x)$ has $\alpha$ as a zero, so $\alpha$ is algebraic over $F$, so $E$ is algebraic over $F$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Note that an algebraic extension is not always separable, but since most fields we are interested in are either finite (for example, fields that we identify with the congruence classes and modular arithmetic of a prime modulus $p$) or have characteristic $0$ (for example, $\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{R}, \mathbb{C}$) then most natural examples we'll see are of separable extensions as per theorem 414 (algebraic extension of a perfect field is separable). The theorem fails for infinite fields with nonzero characteristic, as we will show in the next example:

>[!example] algebraic extension that is inseparable
>
>Consider $\mathbb{F}_{2}=\{0, 1\}$ under modular arithmetic $\mod 2$, and let $t$ be transcendental over $\mathbb{F}_{2}$, then we can consider $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$ the group of rational functions in the indeterminate $t$ (see discussion in theorem 378 ($F(\alpha)$ is isomorphic to the field of fractions of $F[\alpha]$)). This field is infinite - suppose it is finite, and let $n=|\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)|$, then $A=\{0, t, t^{2}, \dots, t^{n}\}$ has $n+1$ distinct elements and yet $A \subseteq \mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$, so it is infinite. However, $\text{char}(\mathbb{F}_{2}(t))$ is still $2$ since $2 \cdot 1 \equiv_{2} 0$ (alternatively, since corollary 225 (unital subrings preserve characteristic) and $\mathbb{F}_{2}$ is a unital subring of $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$ and has characteristic $2$, then $\text{char}(\mathbb{F}_{2}(t))=2$). It follows that $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$ is not perfect, so theorem 414 (algebraic extension of a perfect field is separable) does not apply.
>
>We now consider $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)[x]$, i.e. the polynomial ring of $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$ in the indeterminate $x$, so $x^{2}-t \in \mathbb{F}_{2}(t)[x]$. Also, recall that in the proof for theorem 408 (finite fields are perfect) we have shown that every field of finite characteristic (not necessarily a finite field) exhibits a property which we called the *Freshman's dream* when calculating $(a+b)^{p}$ where $p$ is the field characteristic: $(a+b)^{p}=a^{p}+b^{p}$. Since $\text{char}(\mathbb{F}_{2}(t))=2$, then $(x-\alpha)^{2}=x^{2}-\alpha^{2}$, so by equation coefficients we find $t=\alpha^{2}$, i.e. $\alpha=\pm \sqrt{t}$ (here we use $\sqrt{t}$ as notation for *an element that, multiplied with itself, yields $t$*, and we find that in the splitting field, $\sqrt{t}=-\sqrt{t}=\alpha$).
>
>Suppose $\sqrt{t} \in \mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$, i.e. $\sqrt{t}$ is a rational function in $t$, so $\sqrt{t}=\frac{p(t)}{h(t)}$ for some $p(t), h(t) \in \mathbb{F}_{2}[t]$, so $t \cdot h(t) \cdot h(t) = p(t) \cdot p(t)$. By the degree equation, $\text{deg}(t\cdot h(t)\cdot h(t))=1+2\text{deg}(h(t))$, so it is odd, however $\text{deg}(p(t) \cdot p(t))=2\text{deg}(p(t))$, so it is even. Since parity partitions $\mathbb{Z}$, a number cannot be both even and odd, so we have a contradiction, so $\sqrt{t}$ is not a rational function, so $\sqrt{t} \notin \mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$.
>
>It follows that $x^{2}-t$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$, but we have seen that $\text{irr}(\alpha, \mathbb{F}_{2}(t))=x^{2}-t$ and clearly $\alpha=\sqrt{t}=-\sqrt{t}$ is a multiple zero of $x^{2}-t$, so $\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)$ is inseparable since $x^{2}-t \in \mathbb{F}_{2}(t)[x]$ is inseparable.

Consider the cubic $x^{3}-2 \in \mathbb{Q}$. We know that it is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$ by theorem 321 (Eisenstein's criterion) with $p=2$, and also from number theory we know $\sqrt[3]{2} \notin \mathbb{Q}$ so $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ is a simple extension over $\mathbb{Q}$ with degree $3$ (since $\text{deg}(x^{3}-2)=3$). However, as we have discussed in example 397 ($\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ is not a normal extension of $\mathbb{Q}$), $x^{3}-2$ does not split in this field although it has a zero in it, so there exists an extension $E$ over $\mathbb{Q}$ in which $x^{3}-2$ splits. The question about the relation of $[E:\mathbb{Q}], [E:\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})]$ and $[\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}):\mathbb{Q}]$ naturally arises. We discuss it over the next few theorems:

>[!theorem] $[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$
>
>If $E$ is a finite extension over $F$, and $K$ is finite extension over $E$, then $[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$.


**Proof.** Suppose $F$ is a field, $E$ is a finite extension over $F$, and $K$ is a finite extension over $E$, and denote $[K:E]=n, [E:F]=m$. Consider $E$ as a vector space over $F$, then by definition $[E:F]=\dim E$. Let $e_{E} \subseteq F$ be a basis of $E$, then $|e_{E}|=\dim E = [E:F]=m$, and $\forall \alpha \in E$ we can write $\alpha$ as a linear combination of vectors from $e_{E}$. Likewise, $K$ is a vector space over $E$, and $e_{K} \subseteq K$ is a basis of $K$ s.t. $|e_{K}|=\dim K = [K:E]$.

Let $\beta \in K$, then $\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}e_{K, i}$, and also each $e_{K, i} \in E$ so $b_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{i, j}e_{E, j}$ for some $a_{i, j} \in F$, i.e.

$$
\beta = \sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}e_{K, i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n} \Big (\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{i, j}e_{E, j} \Big )e_{K, i} = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{i, j}(e_{E, j}e_{K, i})
$$
Let $e_{K'}=\{e_{E, j}e_{K, i} | i \in [1, n], j \in [1, m]\}$. Clearly $|e_{K'}|=nm=[K:E][E:F]$, and clearly by the above $\text{span}\{e_{K'}\}=K$ over $F$. We will show $e_{K'}$ is a set of linearly independent vectors in $K$ over the field $F$. Suppose not, then for some $c_{i}, \dots, c_{nm}$ not all zero, we have $0=\sum_{i=1}^{nm}c_{i}e_{K', i}$, which would imply $\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}e_{K, i}=0$ for some $d_{i} \in E$, but by supposition $e_{K}$ is a basis so it must be that all $d_{i}$ terms are $0$, but these $d_{i}$ terms are simply a linear combination of $e_{E}$ with coefficients from $F$ and since $e_{E}$ is a linearly independent set then it must be that all coefficients of these terms are $0$, so in conclusion $\sum_{i=1}^{nm}c_{i}e_{K', i}=0$ has no non-zero solutions, so $e_{K'}$ is a linearly independent set over $F$, so $e_{K'}$ is a basis of $K$ of $F$, so $[K:F]=nm=[K:E][E:F]$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The above theorem can naturally be extended by induction to any ascending chain of extensions, so in particular we derive the following corollary:

>[!corollary] degree of splitting field
>
>If $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$, and $n=\text{deg}(p(x))$, and $E$ is a splitting field of $p(x)$ over $F$, then $[E:F] \leq n!$.

**Proof.** By definition of the splitting field of a polynomial and since $\text{deg}(p(x))=n$, then $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ where $\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ are zeros of $p(x)$ over $E$. By corollary 394 (uniqueness of splitting field) it suffices to consider one such splitting field $E$ to prove the theorem. First, note that since $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$, then $\alpha_{1} \notin F$, so by corollary 382 ($\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$) and by definition 415 (finite extension) we have $[F(\alpha_{1}):F]=\text{deg}(p(x))=n$. Now, in $F(\alpha)$, we have that $p(x)=(x-\alpha_{1})q(x)$ by proposition 303 , so $[F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}):F(\alpha)] \leq n-1$. The above argument generalizes trivially to show that $[F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{i+1}):F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{i})] \leq n-i$.

Now, by the general form of theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$), we have 

$$
\begin{gathered}\\
[E:F]=\\
&[F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}):F]=\\
&\prod_{i=0}^{n-1} [F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{i+1}):F(\alpha_{1}, \dots \alpha_{i})] \leq\\
&\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(n-i)=\\
&&n!
\end{gathered}
$$

Which completes the proof.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Another corollary of theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$) is given below:

>[!corollary]
>
>If $E$ is an extension of $F$ and $\alpha \in E$ is algebraic over $F$, and $\beta \in F(\alpha)$, then $\text{deg}(\beta, F) \mid \text{deg}(\alpha, F)$.
 

**Proof.** Clearly $F(\alpha)$ is a finite extension over $F$ so $\text{deg}(\alpha, F)=[F(\alpha):F]$.  Also, $F(\beta)$ is a finite extension over $F$ so $\deg(\beta, F)=[F(\beta):F]$. Since $\beta \in F(\alpha)$ then clearly $F(\beta) \subseteq F(\alpha)$ by definition of a simple extension so $F(\alpha)$ is a finite extension over $F(\beta)$, so by the theorem $\text{deg}(\alpha, F)=[F(\alpha):F]=[F(\alpha):F(\beta)][F(\beta):F]=[F(\alpha):F(\beta)]\text{deg}(\beta, F)$, so by defition $\text{deg}(\beta, F) \mid \text{deg}(\alpha, F)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div> 

The above corollary plays an important role in determining whether certain numbers are constructible or not, and in answering classical construction problems that deal with constructing geometric shapes using a straightedge and a compass. The corollary also lets us prove that certain polynomials have no solution over a field, as illustrated below.

>[!example] $x^{3}-2$ has no zero in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$
>
>We prove by contraposition. Suppose $\beta$ is a zero of $x^{3}-2$ in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$. Note that $\deg(\sqrt{2}, \mathbb{Q})=2$. Observe that $x^{3}-2$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ so by theorem 318  it is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, so $\text{deg}(\beta, \mathbb{Q})=3$. But, $2 \not \mid 3$, so $\beta \notin \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ by corollary 421 , so $x^{3}-2$ has no zero in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$.


>[!theorem]
>
>Let $E$ be an algebraic extesion of a field $F$, then $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ for some $S=\{\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}\}$ if and only if $E$ is a finite extension of $F$.


**Proof.** 

$\implies$Suppose $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$. Consider the following iterative process:
1. Set $E_{0}=F, i=0$
2. Let $E_{i+1}=E_{i}(\alpha_{i+1})$ (such simple extension must exist since by supposition $E$ is an algebraic extension of $F$), if $\alpha_{i+1} \in E_{i}$ then obviously $E_{i}(\alpha_{i+1})=E_{i}$, so $[E_{i+1}:E_{i}]=1$ by proposition 416 . Otherwise, by corollary 382 ($\text{dim}(F(\alpha))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)$) it follows that $E_{i+1}$ is a finite extension of $E_{i}$, and by transitivity of $F$.
3. Set $i = i+1$ and repeat step $2$ until $i=n$

We now have $E_{n}=F(\alpha_{1})(\dots)(\alpha_{n})$ and by step $(2)$, $E_{n}$ is a finite extension of $F$. By theorem 386 , we have that $F(\alpha_{1})(\dots)(\alpha_{n})=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})=E$, so $E$ is a finite extension of $F$.

$\impliedby$ Suppose $E$ is a finite extension of $F$, then by theorem 417 (finite extensions are algebraic), $E$ is algebraic over $F$. If $E=F$, then we're done. Otherwise, $E \neq F$, so since $E$ is a finite extension then $1 \lt [E:F]$, then $\exists \alpha_{1} \in E \setminus F$, so $F(\alpha_{1})$ has dimension greater than $1$ since $F(\alpha_{1}) \neq F$ so by proposition 416  it follows that $1 \lt [F(\alpha_{1}):F]$. IF $E=F(\alpha_{1})$, then we're done, otherwise we pick some $\alpha_{2} \notin F(\alpha_{1})$. By theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$) we have $[F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}):F]=[F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}):F(\alpha_{1})][F(\alpha_{1}):F]$. Since both terms on the LHS are greater than $1$, then $[F(\alpha_{1}:F] \lt [F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}):F]$. If $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})=E$ then we're done, otherwise we continue this construction and since for every $n$, $[F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}):F] \lt [F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}, \alpha_{n+1}):F]$ as argued above, and $[E:F]$ is finite, then eventually we find some $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{m})$ contained in $E$ and also with same degree as $E$ over $F$, so $E=F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{m})$.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We conclude with a theorem which identifies necessary conditions for a field adjoined set $F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$ to be identical to a simple extension $F(\beta)$ for some $\beta \in F(\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n})$.

>[!theorem] primitive element theorem (Steinitz, 1910)
>
>If $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$, and $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ are algebraic over $F$, then $\exists \beta \in F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$ s.t. $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})=F(\beta)$. We say that $\beta$ is a *primitive element* of $F(\beta)$.
>


**Proof.** 

Let $\beta=\alpha_{1}+d\alpha_{2}$. There are two cases:

1. $\beta$ is a primitive element of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$, i.e. $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})=F(\beta)$, then we are done.
2. $\beta$ is not a primitive element of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$, so it follows that $\alpha_{2} \notin F(\beta)$, otherwise we have $\beta - d\alpha_{2} = \alpha_{1} \in F(\beta)$, so $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}) = F(\beta)$ by definition.

So, suppose $\beta$ is not a primitive element of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$ and also $\alpha_{2} \notin F(\beta)$.

Let $p(x)=\text{irr}(\alpha_{1}, F(\beta))$ and $q(x)=\text{irr}(\alpha_{2}, F(\beta))$, and let $K$ be a splitting field for $p(x), q(x)$ (which exists by theorem 390 (splitting field existence theorem)). If $\text{deg}(q(x))=1$, then by proposition 416  it follows that $F(\beta)=F(\beta)(\alpha_{2})=F(\beta, \alpha_{2})$ and therefore $\beta$ is a primitive element of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$, so suppose $1 \lt \text{deg}(q(x))$, then $q(x)$ has at least two zeros counting multiplicity, but since $\text{char}(F)=0$ then by theorem 411 (irreducible polynomials over a perfect field are separable) then all zeros of $q(x)$ over $K$ must be simple. By choice of $q(x)$, we know that $\alpha_{2}$ is a zero of $q(x)$, so $\exists \alpha_{3} \in K$ s.t. $\alpha_{3}$ is a zero of $q(x)$ and $\alpha_{3} \neq \alpha_{2}$.

By corollary 385 , $\exists \phi: F(\beta)(\alpha_{2}) \to F(\beta)( \alpha_{3})$ a ring isomorphism that fixes all elements of $F(\beta)$ and takes $\alpha_{2}$ to $\alpha_{3}$ which can be extended to an automorphism $\phi_{K}: K \to K$, s.t. $\phi_{K}(\beta)=\beta$, i.e. $\phi_{K}(\alpha_{1}+d\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{1}+d\alpha_{2}$, but also $\phi_{K}(\alpha_{1}+d\alpha_{2})=\phi_{K}(\alpha_{1})+d\alpha_{3}$. Solving for $d$, we find that $d=(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{2})^{-1}(\phi_{K}(\alpha_{1})-\alpha_{1})$.

Since $\phi_{K}(\alpha_{1})$ must map $\alpha_{1}$ to one of the other zeros of $p(x)$ over $K$, and $\alpha_{3}$ must be a zero of $q(x)$ over $K$, and there are at most $\text{deg}(p(x)), \text{deg}(q(x))$ such zeros respectively, then we find that there are finitely many candidates for $d \in F$ s.t. $\beta$ is not a primitive element of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$. Since $F$ has characteristic $0$ it is not finite, so there are infinitely many possible values for $d$, so $\exists \beta$ of the form $\alpha_{1}+d\alpha_{2}$ where $d \in F$, s.t. $d$ is not of the form $(a-\alpha_{2})(b-\alpha_{1})$ where $a, b$ are zeros of $\text{irr}(\alpha_{2}, F), \text{irr}(\alpha_{1}, F)$ respectively and $a \neq \alpha_{2}, b \neq \alpha_{1}$. By the above argument, it follows that all argument that satisfy this condition will be primitive elements of $F(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2})$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The primitive element theorem extends naturally by induction to any finite algebraic extension over a separable field (note that this is a stronger statement than the one shown above for field with characteristic $0$, working through this augmented base case is left as an exercise, since a separable field can have prime characteristic).

# Galois Theory

The motivation for Galois theory is the study of solutions to complex polynomials, then is, to $p(x) \in \mathbb{C}[x]$. To achieve this, Galois theory, named after French mathematician Evariste Galois, establishes a connection between field theory and group theory. Using Galois theory, one can prove the insolvability of the quintic by radicals (that is, that there's no closed formula in terms of the coefficients of a polynomial of degree $5$ and in fact, any other higher degree, in $\mathbb{C}[x]$ and thus in $\mathbb{R}[x]$), as well as the fundamental theorem of algebra. Our goal for this section will be to derive these two results.

In this section, we also adopt a new notation - instead of writing *$E$ is an extension of the field $F$*, we write $E \diagup F$. Furthermore, we may refer to the zeros of a polynomial as the *roots* of a polynomial.

### Galois Group

>[!definition] $F$-fixing automorphism group
>
>Let $E \diagup F$ be an extension field, then we define $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ to be the set of automorphisms on $E$ that fix $F$ pointwise, i.e. $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)=\{\sigma \in \text{Aut}(F)| \forall f \in F(\sigma(f)=f))\}$.

We say $F$ is a *fixed field* under $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$.

>[!theorem] $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a group
>
>$\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a group under composition.

**Proof.** First, recall from group theory that the automorphisms of a group form a group and function composition. Our proof for this theorem (found in proposition 92 ) can trivially be generalized to show that the automorphisms of a field form a group under composition, so by the one-step subgroup test, since $\text{id}: E \to E$ is trivially in $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, we find that $\forall \sigma, \tau \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, $(\sigma \circ \tau^{-1})(x)=\sigma(\tau^{-1}(x))$, and for $x \in F$ since $\sigma, \tau \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ and hence fix $F$, we find that $\sigma(\tau^{-1}(x))=\sigma(x)=x$, hence $\sigma \circ \tau^{-1} \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, hence it forms a group under composition. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Some writers call $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ the *Galois group* of $E \diagup F$. We reserve this term for the case where $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|=[E:F]$ (in which case we will call $E \diagup F$ a *Galois extension*).

At this point it is worth appreciating that we have already dealt with elements of $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$. For example, we found in corollary 385  that if $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F[x]$ and $\alpha, \beta$ are zeros of $p(x)$ in a splitting field $E \diagup F$, then there exists a field automorphism $\phi: E \to E$ s.t. $\phi_{|F}=\text{id}_{F}$, and also $\phi(\alpha)=\beta$.

>[!theorem] $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a splitting field of a polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$, and and $f(\alpha)=0$, then $\forall \sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, $f(\sigma(\alpha))=0$.


**Proof.** Note that $\sigma(0)=0$, and also $\sigma(\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\alpha^{i})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(\sigma(\alpha))^{i}$ (here we used the properties of $\sigma$ as a ring homomorphism, followed by the fact that $F$ is fixed under $\sigma$), and clearly the RHS is $f(\sigma(\alpha))$ for $\sigma(\alpha) \in E$, so $\sigma(\alpha)$ is a zero of $f(x)$ over $E$, so $\sigma$ sends zeros of $f(x)$ over $E$ to zeros of $f(x)$ over $E$, so by definition it defines a permutation on those zeroes. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ does not exchange zeros of irreducible factors of reducible polynomials in perfect field
>
>If $F$ is a perfect field and $E \diagup F$ is a splitting field of $f(x) \in F[x]$, and $f(x)$ is reducible in $F[x]$, then if $\alpha$ is a zero of an irreducible factor $p(x)$ of $f(x)$, then $\text{orb}_{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}(\alpha)$ is exactly the set of zeros of $p(x)$.

**Proof.** Let $h(x)$ be another irreducible factor of $f(x)$. There must be such $h(x)$ with degree at least $1$ since $f(x)$ is reducible by supposition. Suppose by contradiction that $\exists \sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ s.t. $h(\sigma(\alpha))=0$ for $\alpha \in E$ s.t. $p(\alpha)=0$. By theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$) ), we have that $p(\sigma(\alpha))=0$, so it follows that $h(x), p(x)$ have a shared linear factor by proposition 303 , but then $f(x)$ has a multiple zero. By theorem 406 (multiple zeros of an irreducible), $f(x)$ has no multiple zeros since $F$ is a perfect field, a contradiction, so $\forall \sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ and $\forall f(x) \in F[x]$ s.t. $p(x) \in F[x]$ is an irreducible factor of $f(x)$, $\sigma$ sends zeros of $p(x)$ to zeros of $p(x)$, and to zeros of $p(x)$ only (and not to other factors of $f(x)$). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We define a *Galois group* as a particular type of group of $F$-fixing automorphism:

>[!definition] Galois group
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a field extension over $F$, then $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is called a *Galois group* if $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|=[E:F]$. In this case, we denote the automorphism group by $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$.


>[!definition] Galois extension
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a finite field extension, then $E \diagup F$ is a *Galois extension* if $[E:F]=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$, i.e. if $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group.

The next few definitions and theorems provide us with a better understanding of Galois groups.

Given an extension field $E \diagup F$, we naturally find a group $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ corresponding to this extension field. Here, we demonstrate this relation in the other direction: given a subgroup of the automorphism $\text{Aut}(E)$, we can find a field $K$ s.t. $K$ consists of all elements fixed by $\text{Aut}(E)$.

>[!definition] fixed field of $\text{Aut}(E)$
>
>Let $G \leqslant \text{Aut}(E)$, then $\{a \in E | \forall \sigma \in G(\sigma(a)=a)\}$ is called the *fixed field* of $G$, and is denoted by $E^{G}$.


>[!theorem] $E^{G}$ is a subfield of $E$
>
>IF $G \leqslant \text{Aut}(E)$, then $E^{G}$ is a subfield of $E$, justifying calling it a fixed *field*.


**Proof.** Clearly $0 \in E^{G}$ since every automorphism must fix $0$ (and also $1$), so $E^{G} \neq \emptyset$. We proceed by theorem 215 (subring test). Let $a, b \in E^{G}$, and let $\sigma \in G$, then $\sigma(a)=a, \sigma(b)=b$, so $\sigma(a)\sigma(b)=ab$, but since $\sigma$ is a homomorphism then $\sigma(ab)=\sigma(a)\sigma(b)$, so $ab \in E^{G}$, so it is closed under multiplication. Similarly, $\sigma(a-b)=\sigma(a)-\sigma(b)=a-b$, so it is closed under subtraction, so it is a subring of $E$. Commutativity of multiplication in $E^{G}$ follows from commutativity of multiplication in $E$, so it remains to show that if $a \in E^{G}, a \neq 0$, then $a^{-1} \in E^{G}$. Again by the properties of a homomorphism, $\sigma(a^{-1})=\sigma(a)^{-1}=a^{-1}$, so $a^{-1} \in E^{G}$, so $E^{G}$ has the inverse property for every nonzero element, so it is a subfield of $E$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Next, we consider the relation between $[E:E^{G}]$ and $|G|$ (where $G$ is a subgroup of the automorphisms of $E$).

>[!lemma]
>
>Any finite subgroup $G$ of $\text{Aut}(E)$ is linearly independent, i.e. the only solution to $\sum_{\sigma_{i} \in G}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(x)=0$ for all $x \in E$, is the trivial solution (all $a_{i}$ coefficients are zero).


**Proof.** Let $|G|=n$. Suppose by contradiction $G$ is linearly dependent, and let $m$ be minimal s.t. $\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(x)=0$ for all $x \in E$ and for $a_{i} \in E$ not all zero (where $\sigma_{1}, \dots, \sigma_{m}$ is a subset of $G$). Clearly $1 \lt m$ because for every $\sigma \in G$, $a\sigma(x)=0 \iff a=0 \lor \sigma(x)=0$, and the latter is true if and only if $x=0$ since $\sigma(0)=0$ and $\sigma$ is injective, so it must be that $a=0$, so $1 \lt m$. We will show that it is possible to remove $\sigma_{m}$ and still have a linearly dependent set, this time with $m-1$ automorphisms, violating minimality of $m$, proving that $G$ is linearly independent.

Let $x_{0} \in E$ be an element on which $\sigma_{m}$ disagrees with all other $\sigma_{1}, \dots, \sigma_{m-1}$ (one must exist otherwise $\sigma_{m}$ is not distinct from the other functions). Clearly, $x_{0} \neq 0$ since an automorphism must send $0$ to $0$. Let $y \in E$, since $\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(x)=0$ for every $x \in E$, then it is zero for $x=x_{0}y$, so:

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(x_{0}y)=0\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)\sigma_{i}(x_{0})=0\\
\end{aligned}
$$


Where we used the fact that $\sigma_{i}$ is a homomorphism. Again since the initial sum is zero for every $x$, then it is $0$ for $y$ in particular, so $\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)=0$. Multiplying both sides by $\sigma_{m}(x_{0})$, we have $\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)\sigma_{m}(x_{0})=0$. Subtracting the last equation from lemma 433 , and recalling $\sigma_{i}(x_{0}) = \sigma_{m}(x_{0})$ if and only if $i=m$, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)(\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))=0\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)(\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))+a_{m}\sigma_{m}(y)(\sigma_{m}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))=0\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)(\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))=0\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}(\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))\sigma_{i}(y)=0
\end{aligned}
$$

Denote $b_{i}=a_{i}(\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}))$ Note that $\sigma_{i}(x_{0})-\sigma_{m}(x_{0}) \neq 0$ for $i \in [1, m-1]$ since $x_{0} \neq 0$ and $\sigma_{i}$ and $\sigma_{m}$ disagree on $x_{0}$ by choice of $x_{0}$, so $b_{i}=0 \iff a_{i}=0$ (since $E$ is a field so has no zero divisors). So, we have $\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}b_{i}\sigma_{i}(y)=0$ for any $y \in E$, so we have an $m-1$ linear combination of elements of $G$ that are linearly dependent, violating minimality of $m$, so $G$ is linearly independent.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use the lemma to prove the following:

>[!theorem] $[E:E^{G}]=|G|$
>
>Let $E$ be a field and $G\leqslant \text{Aut}(E)$, then $[E:E^{G}]=|G|$.


**Proof.** We prove by contradiction. Suppose $[E:E^{G}] \neq |G|$, then either $[E:E^{G}] \lt |G|$, or $[E:E^{G}] \gt |G|$. Denote $[E:E^{G}]=m, |G|=n$.

Suppose $[E:E^{G}] \lt |G|$, i.e. $m \lt n$. Let $e=\{e_{1}, \dots, e_{m}\}$ be a basis of $E$ over $E^{G}$, and consider the system of equations given below in matrix form:

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\sigma_{1}(e_{1}) & \sigma_{2}(e_{1}) & \dots & \sigma_{n}(e_{2})\\
\sigma_{1}(e_{2}) & \sigma_{2}(e_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{n}(e_{2})\\
\dots & \dots & \dots & \dots \\
\sigma_{1}(e_{m}) & \sigma_{2}(e_{m}) & \dots & \sigma_{n}(e_{m})\\
\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}
x_{1}\\
x_{2} \\
\dots\\
x_{n}
\end{pmatrix}
=
0
$$
Where the column vector $\vec{x}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\ \dots \\ x_{n} \end{pmatrix} \in E^{n}$ (an $n$-tuple of elements of $E$).
Note that the system has $n$ variables and $m$ equations, and by supposition $m \lt n$, so from linear algebra we know that it has a nontrivial solution, i.e. a solution s.t. $\vec{x} \neq 0$. Let $\vec{x}$ be this solution vector. Now, let $a \in E$, then since $e$ is a basis for $E$ over $E^{G}$, then $a=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}e_{i}$ for scalars $\lambda_{i} \in E^{G}$.

By writing out each equation in the system explicitly, and multiplying the $i^{\text{th}}$ equation by $\lambda_{i}$, and then summing all equations, we get

$$
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\sigma_{j}(e_{i})x_{j}=0
$$

Note that since $E^{G}$ is the field fixed by $G$, then $\lambda_{i}=\sigma_{j}(\lambda_{i})$ for every $\sigma_{j} \in G$, so $\lambda_{i}\sigma_{j}(e_{i})=\sigma_{j}(\lambda_{i}e_{i})$ (we also used the fact that $\sigma_{j}$ preserves multiplication). Grouping theorem 434 ($[E:E^{G}]=|G|$) ) by $x_{j}$, and using the relation we mentioned, and the fact that $\sigma_{j}$ preserves addition as well, we get

$$
\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\sigma_{j}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\Big)=0
$$
But, $\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}e_{i}=a$ which was an arbitrary element of $E$, so we have $\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\sigma_{j}(a)=0$ for every $a \in E$, we coefficients $x_{j}$ not all $0$ since $\vec{x}$ is a nontrivial solution to the system, so we have found that $G$ is linearly dependent, which contradicts lemma 433 , so, $[E:E^{G}] \not \lt |G|$.

Suppose then $[E:E^{G}] \gt |G|$, i.e. $m \gt n$. Since $m \gt n$, we can find $n+1$ linearly independent vectors $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n+1} \in E \diagup E^{G}$. Consider the homogeneous system of linear equations expressed in matrix form below:

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\sigma_{1}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{1}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{1}(a_{n+1}) \\
\sigma_{2}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{2}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{2}(a_{n+1}) \\
\dots & \dots & \dots & \dots \\
\sigma_{n}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{n}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{n}(a_{n+1}) \\
\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}
x_{1} \\
x_{2} \\
\dots \\
x_{n+1}
\end{pmatrix}
=
0
$$
It is a system of $n$ equations in $n+1$ variables, so it has a non-trivial solution $\vec{x}$. Of these non-trivial solutions, let $\vec{\beta}$ be the solution with least nonzero elements (one must exist by the existence of a non-trivial solution and the well-ordering principle). Suppose this least amount is $k$, so, via a reordering, we can rewrite the equation as

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\sigma_{1}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{1}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{1}(a_{k}) \\
\sigma_{2}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{2}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{2}(a_{k}) \\
\dots & \dots & \dots & \dots \\
\sigma_{n}(a_{1}) & \sigma_{n}(a_{2}) & \dots & \sigma_{n}(a_{k}) \\
\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}
\beta_{1} \\
\beta_{2} \\
\dots \\
\beta_{k}
\end{pmatrix}
=
0
$$

Finally, since each $\beta_{i}$ is non-zero by our choice, and $\beta_{i} \in E$, then since $E$ is a field $\exists \beta_{i}^{-1} \in E$, so we can multiply the equation by $\beta_{k}^{-1}$ and get a new solution with $k$ non-zero values s.t. the $k^{\text{th}}$ value is $1$. Via abuse of notation, we refer to these solution by $\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{k}$ as well, where $\beta_{k}=1$.

From here, we proceed in a similar manner to the proof for lemma 433 , following a similar chain of manipulations, to find a solution with only $k-1$ non-zero values, contradicting minimality of $k$, which will show that $m \not \gt n$ by contradiction.

Consider $\vec{\beta}=\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{k}, 0, \dots$ where we pad $\vec{\beta}$ with $0$ to make it an $n+1$-tuple . Each $i^{\text{th}}$ equation in the (original) system is $\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})\beta_{j}=0$. Since $G \leqslant \text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})$, then $\text{id}_{E} \in G$, so $\sigma_{i}=\text{id}_{E}$ for some $i$, so for this $i$ the equation becomes $\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}a_{j}\beta_{j}=0$, i.e. it is an $n+1$ linear combination of $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n+1}$ with not all zero coefficients $\beta_{1}, \dots$ s.t. its sum is $0$. If $\beta_{1}, \dots, \beta_{k}$ are all in $E^{G}$, this will contradict linear independence of $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n+1}$. It follows that $\exists \beta_{j} \in \vec{\beta}$ s.t. $\beta_{j} \notin E^{G}$. For this $\beta_{j}$, we must find some $\sigma_{t} \in G$ s.t. $\sigma_{t}(\beta_{j}) \neq \beta_{j}$, otherwise $\beta_{j}$ is fixed by $G$ so it is a part of $E^{G}$ by definition of $E^{G}$. w.l.o.g let this $\beta_{j}$ be $\beta_{1}$ and denote the matching automorphism by $\sigma$.

The key fact we will use in the next part of the proof is that $\sigma(\beta_{1})-\beta_{1} \neq 0$ for every $\sigma_{i} \in G$ s.t. $\sigma_{i} \neq \sigma$.

First, we act on every equation in the system by $\sigma$, so every equation becomes $\sigma(\sum_{j=1}^{k} \sigma_{i}(a_{j})\beta_{j})=\sigma(0)$. By the properties of a homomorphism, this becomes $\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\sigma \star \sigma_{i})(a_{j})\sigma(\beta_{j})=0$. Next, we use the fact that theorem 164 (group actions are equivalent to group homomorphism to symmetric group), so $\sigma$ acting on $G$ is a permutation of $G$, so in fact $\{\sigma_{1}, \dots, \sigma_{n}\}=\{\sigma \star \sigma_{1}, \dots, \sigma \star \sigma_{n}\}$. Since all equations differ only in $\sigma_{i}$, and since $\sigma$ acts as a permutation on $G$, then we can identify each $i^{\text{th}}$ equation in the original system with an equation of the form $\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})\sigma(\beta_{j})=0$ in the new system (acquired by acting on each equation by $\sigma$). Now, subtracting the corresponding equations, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})\sigma(\beta_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})\beta_{j}=0 \\
&\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})(\sigma(\beta_{j})-\beta_{j})=0\\
&\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})(\sigma(\beta_{j})-\beta_{j})+\sigma_{i}(a_{k})(\sigma(\beta_{k})-\beta_{k})=0
\end{aligned}
$$

Recall that $\beta_{k}=1$ in our solution, so $\sigma$ as a field automorphism must send $1$ to itself, so the above equation becomes

$$
\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}(a_{j})(\sigma(\beta_{j})-\beta_{j})=0
$$

For every $i$ in $[1, n]$. Note that, by construction, $\sigma(\beta_{1}) \neq \beta_{1}$, so we have a new solution vector with only $k-1$ elements that is not the trivial solution (each $j^{\text{th}}$ element of this new solution is given by $\sigma(\beta_{j})-\beta_{j}$), so $k$ is not minimal, a contradiction, so, $m \not \gt n$.

It follows that it must be that $[E:E^{G}]=|G|$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now consider $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ again.

>[!corollary] $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$
>
>Let $E$ be a finite extension field over $F$, then $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$.


**Proof.** Suppose $E$ is an extension over $F$, then by definition $F$ is fixed by $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$. Let $K=E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}$ be the field fixed by $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, then $F \subseteq K \subseteq E$. Since $E$ is a finite extension over $F$ then so is $K$, and by theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$) $[E:F]=[E:K][K:E]$. Now, by theorem 434 ($[E:E^{G}]=|G|$), $[E:K]=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$, and $[E:K] \leq [E:F]$, so $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G}) = G$
>
>If $G \subseteq \text{Aut}(E)$, and $E^{G}$ is the field fixed by $G$, then $\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})=G$.


**Proof.** We will show $\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})=G$ by mutual inclusion. First, suppose $\sigma \in G$, then $\sigma$ fixes $E^{G}$ by definition of $E^{G}$, so $\sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})$, so $G \subseteq \text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})$. Next, by corollary 435 ($|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$) we have $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})| \leq [E:E^{G}]$. By theorem 434 ($[E:E^{G}]=|G|$), $[E:E^{G}]=|G|$, so $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})| \leq |G|$, and also $|G| \leq |\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})|$, so we conclude $|G|=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})|$ and since $G$ is a subset of $\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})$, it follows that $\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G})=G$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

The following corollary provides an equivalent

>[!corollary] equivalent definition of Galois group
>
>$\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group if and only if $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$.


**Proof.** $\implies$ Suppose $G=\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group, and let $E^{G}$ be the field fixed by $G$, then by theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$), $[E:F]=[E:E^{G}][E^{G}:F]$. Since $G$ is Galois, then by definition 429 (Galois group), $[E:F]=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|=|G|$. By theorem 434 ($[E:E^{G}]=|G|$), $[E:E^{G}]=|G|$, so we have $|G|=|G| \space [E^{G}:F]$, so by proposition 416 , $E^{G}=F$, so $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$.

$\impliedby$Suppose $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$. By definition, by theorem 434 ($[E:E^{G}]=|G|$), $[E:F]=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$, so by definition 429 (Galois group), $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is Galois.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Consider $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$. Although it is true that, by definition, $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ fixes $F$, it is not immediately obvious that $F$ is the field fixed by $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ as defined in definition 431 (fixed field of $\text{Aut}(E)$): clearly $F \subseteq E^{\text{Aug}(E \diagup F)}$, but in general it can be a proper extension of $F$. Below we see that if $E$ is the splitting field of a separable polynomial in $F[x]$, then $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$.


>[!theorem] splitting field of separable polyonmial admits a Galois group
>
>If $f(x) \in F[x]$ is separable, and $E$ is a splitting field of $f(x)$, then $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group.


**Proof.** We prove by induction on $[E:F]$. If $[E:F]=1$ then $E=F$ by proposition 416 , so $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)=\{\text{id}_{E}\}$, so $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|=[E:F]$, so $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group.

Let $p(x)$ be the minimal polynomial whose zeros are all the zeros of $f(x)$ s.t. they are not in $F$. If no such zeros exist, then $f(x)$ splits over $F$ so we have $E=F$, which we handled above, so there must exists such $p(x)$. This $p(x)$ is a factor of $f(x)$ and so it is also separable (since $f(x)$ is separable), and so it also splits over $E$. Let $d=\text{deg}(p(x))$ (so $1 \lt d$ otherwise $p(x)$ splits in $F$), and let $\alpha \in E \setminus F$ be a zero of $p(x)$ (i.e. $p(\alpha)=0$).

By theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$) ], we have $[E:F]=[E:F(\alpha)][F(\alpha):F]$. Since by choice $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)=p(x)$, then $[F(\alpha):F]=\deg(p(x))=d$ by definition, so we have $[E:F]=d \space [E:F(\alpha)]$. Since $1 \lt d$ then $[E:F(\alpha)] \lt [E:F]$, so by the induction hypothesis $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))$ is a Galois group, i.e. there are exactly $[E:F(\alpha)]$ automorphisms on $E$ that fix $F(\alpha)$. We will show that every $\sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))$ admits exactly $\text{deg}(p(x))=d$ distinct $F$-fixing automorphisms on $E$, which will then yield $[E:F]=d \space [E:F(\alpha)]=d \space |\text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))| \leq |\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$.

Let $\sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))$, then $\sigma: E \to E$ fixes $F(\alpha)$ pointwise, so in particular $\sigma$ fixes $F$ pointwise. Suppose $\beta$ is a zero of $p(x)$ in $E \setminus F$. Since $p(x)$ is irreducible over $F$ and separable, there are exactly $\text{deg}(p(x))=d$ such zeros. By lemma 392 , there exists a unique isomorphism $\psi: F(\alpha) \to F(\beta)$ s.t. $\psi_{|F}=\text{id}_{F}$ and $\psi(\alpha)=\beta$. This isomorphism naturally expands to an $F$ preserving automorphism on $E$, $\overline{\psi}: E \to E$ by theorem 393 (splitting fields of polynomials under isomorphism are isomorphic).

Observe that $\overline{\psi} \circ \sigma: E \to E$ is a composition of automorphisms so it is an automorphism and it fixes $F$ pointwise since $\overline{\psi}, \sigma$ both fix $F$ pointwise, so $\overline{\psi} \circ \sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))$, and also it has the property that it sends $\alpha$ to $\beta$, i.e. it is uniquely determined by our choice of $\sigma$ and our choice of $\beta$. Since $p(x)$ is separable irreducible over $F$ with degree $d$, there are $d$ choices for $\beta$, so in turn $d$ distinct extensions $\overline{\psi} \circ \sigma$ for every $\sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha))$, so $d \space | \text{Aut}(E \diagup F(\alpha)| \leq |\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$ .

We have $[E:F] \leq |\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$ by the argument presented earlier. Since $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$ by corollary 435 ($|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)| \leq [E:F]$), then $[E:F]=|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|$, so $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group.

The key idea in this proof is illustrated below, where $\beta, \gamma, \delta$ are zeros of $p(x)$ in $E \setminus F$.

<TikzSvg7208b65f0e232d0f884593f18631f7eb style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>


<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary]
>
>If $p(x) \in F[x]$ is separable and $E \diagup F$ is a splitting field for $p(x)$, then $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$.

**Proof.** By theorem 438 (splitting field of separable polyonmial admits a Galois group) we know that $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois group, and by corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group) this is true if and only if $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$, hence the corollary. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] finite Galois extension is finite, normal and separable
>
>Let $E \diagup F$ be a finite Galois extension, then it is a finite, normal and separable extension.
>


**Proof.** Let $E \diagup F$ be a finite Galois extension, so $[E:F]=|\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)|$.  We denote $G=\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$.

First, we show $E \diagup F$ is normal.

Let $p(x) \in F[x]$ be irreducible over $F$. If $\exists \alpha \in E$ s.t. $p(\alpha)=0$, then we want $p(x)$ splits in $E$. w.l.o.g. suppose $p(x)$ is monic (otherwise we simply divide all coefficients by the leading coefficient to retrieve a monic polynomial).

By theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$), then $\text{orb}_{G}(\alpha)$ is a set of zeros of $p(x)$. Let $f(x)=\prod_{a_{i} \in \text{orb}_{G}(\alpha)}(x-a_{i})$. Clearly, $\text{deg}(f(x)) \leq \text{deg}(p(x))$ since $f(x)$ is a separable polynomial whose zeros are all zeros of $p(x)$.

Let $\sigma \in G$. Consider $\sigma$ naturally extended to an automorphism $\overline{\sigma}: E[x] \to E[x], \sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}x^{i} \mapsto \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sigma(b_{i})x^{i}$. Consider $f(x)$ under $\overline{\sigma}$, since $\overline{\sigma}$ is a homomorphism, then we get $\overline{\sigma}(f(x))=\prod_{a_{i} \in \text{orb}_{G}(x)}(x-\sigma(a_{i}))$, but  since $\sigma$ is a permutation on $\text{orb}_{G}(\alpha)$ then $f(x)$ is fixed under $\overline{\sigma}$ (since $\overline{\sigma}$ simply reorders the product $(x-a_{1})(x-a_{2})\dots(x-a_{n}))$, so all coefficients of $f(x)$ written as a formal power series are fixed by $\sigma$, and this holds for all $\sigma \in G$, so all coefficients are fixed by $G$, and since $G$ is a Galois group of $E$ over $F$ then by corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group) this means that all coefficients are in $F$, so $f(x) \in F[x]$.

Since $f(x) \in F[x]$ and also $f(x)$ has $\alpha$ as a zero and $p(x)$ is by construction $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$, then $\text{deg}(p(x)) \leq \text{deg}(f(x))$, and we have also seen that $\text{deg}(f(x)) \leq \text{deg}(p(x))$ by construction of $p(x)$, hence $p(x)=f(x)$.

It follows that if $p(x) \in F[x]$ is irreducible in $F$, and $p(x)$ has a zero in $E$, then $p(x)$ splits over $E$. So, $E \diagup F$ is a *normal extension* by definition 396 (normal extension).

Next, we show $E \diagup F$ is separable. Let $\alpha \in E$. Since $E \diagup F$ is finite by supposition, then by theorem 417 (finite extensions are algebraic), the minimal polynomial for $\alpha$ in $F$ exists. There are two cases:
1. $\alpha \in F$, in which case the minimal polynomial is simply $x-\alpha$, which is clearly separable.
2. $\alpha \notin F$, in which case by the above argument we have found that $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ is separable in $E$.

So $E \diagup F$ is separable, and we conclude that if $E \diagup F$ is a finite Galois extension, then it is a finite, normal, separable extension.

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a finite normal and separable extension, then $E \diagup F$ is the splitting field of a separable polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$.


**Proof.** Suppose $E \diagup F$ is a finite, normal and separable extension. By the general form of theorem 424 (primitive element theorem (Steinitz, 1910)) (here we use the fact that $E \diagup F$ is finite hence algebraic, and also the fact that it is a separable extension), we have that $E=F(\alpha)$ for some $\alpha \in E$. Since $E \diagup F$ is finite, it is also algebraic by theorem 417 (finite extensions are algebraic), so its minimal polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$ exists. Since $E \diagup F$ is normal and $\alpha \in E$ is a zero of $f(x)$, then $f(x)$ splits in $E \diagup F$, and also $F(\alpha)$ is the smallest extension of $F$ containing $\alpha$, so it is a splitting field for $f(x)$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Putting the last few theorems together, we get a nice set of equivalent definitions for a Galois group:

>[!theorem] equivalent definitions for Galois group
>
>Let $E \diagup F$ be a finite field extension, TFAE:
>1. $E^{\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)}=F$
>2. $|\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)|=[E:F]$
>3. $E \diagup F$ is a finite, normal and separable extension
>4. $E \diagup F$ is the splitting field of a separable polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$


**Proof.** 

1. We have seen $1 \iff 2$ in corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group).
2. We have seen $2 \implies 3$ in theorem 440 (finite Galois extension is finite, normal and separable).
3. We have seen $3 \implies 4$ in theorem 441 .
4. We have seen $4 \implies 1$ in theorem 438 (splitting field of separable polyonmial admits a Galois group).
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] Galois group is isomorphic to subgroup of $S_{n}$
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, and $[E: F]=n$, then its Galois group $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of $S_{n}$ the symmetric group on $n$ letters.
	

**Proof.** Suppose $[E :F]=1$, then $E=F$ by proposition 416 , in which case $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)=\{\text{id}\}$, which is the trivial group (definition 26 (trivial group)) up to an isomorphism, and so is $S_{1}$ (since $|S_{n}|=n!$ and for $n=1$ we have $|S_{1}|=1$), so the proposition holds.

Suppose $[E : F] \neq 1$, then since $E \diagup F$ is finite, then it is the splitting field of a separable polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$ by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group) (since $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension by supposition). Let $p(x)$ be an irreducible factor for $f(x) \in F[x]$ and $\alpha \in E$ be a zero of $p(x)$, then $\text{deg}(p(x))=\text{deg}(\alpha, F)=[E:F]=n$. We know that elements of $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ are uniquely defined by their action on the zeros of $p(x)$.

Consider an enumeration $\Omega=\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}$ on the zeros of $p(x)$, and consider the mapping $\phi: \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \to S_{\Omega}, \sigma \mapsto \sigma_{|\Omega}$. Clearly $\sigma$ restricted to $\Omega$ is a mapping $\Omega \to \Omega$ since theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$), so $\sigma_{|\Omega} \in S_{\Omega}$, so $\phi$ is well-defined. Also, since each $\sigma$ is defined by its action on $\Omega$ (see proof of theorem 393 (splitting fields of polynomials under isomorphism are isomorphic)), then this $\phi$ is injective. Next, we verify $\phi$ is a group homomorphism:

Let $\sigma, \tau \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then $\phi(\sigma) \circ \phi(\tau)=\sigma_{|\Omega} \circ \tau_{|\Omega}=(\sigma \circ \tau)_{\Omega}=\phi(\sigma \circ \tau)$, so $\phi$ is a group homomorphism. Since $\phi$ is injective, its kernel is exactly $\text{id}$, so by theorem 125 (first isomorphism theorem), $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \{\text{id}\} \cong \phi(\text{Gal}(E \diagup F))$, which by theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism) is a subgroup of $\text{Domain}(\phi)=S_{\Omega}$, which itself is isomorphic to $S_{|\Omega|}=S_{n}$. By corollary 122 , $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \cong \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \{\text{id}\}$. Putting it all together, we get that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of $S_{n}$, where $n=[E : F]$.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $|\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)| \leq n!$
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension of degree $n$, then the order of its Galois group is at most $n!$

**Proof.** Immediately from corollary 443 (Galois group is isomorphic to subgroup of $S_{n}$), since $|S_{n}|=n!$ <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

## Fundamental Theorem of Galois Theory

We have seen that Galois extensions/Galois groups form a correspondence between automorphism subgroups, and fields. Let $E \diagup F$ be an extension and let $G$ be a subgroup of $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$, then we know by theorem 432 ($E^{G}$ is a subfield of $E$) that $F \subseteq E^{G} \subseteq E$ and that $E^{G}$ is a subfield of $E$. If $E \diagup F$ is Galois, the fundamental theorem of Galois theory tells us that the above expends to a one-to-one correspondence between all subfields of $E \diagup F$ to all subgroups of $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$.

>[!theorem] fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)
>
>If $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, and $K$ is a subfield of $E$ s.t. $F \subseteq K \subseteq E$, then:
>1. (subgroups and subfields are in one-to-one correspondence) Let $A$ be the set of all such subfields $K$ (i.e. all finite extensions from $F$ to $E$ and $B$ be the set of all subgroups of $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then $\phi: A \to B, K \mapsto \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ is a bijection.
>2. $[E:K]=|\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)|$ (i.e. $E$ is also a Galois extension over $K$), and also $[K:F]=[\text{Gal}(E \diagup F):\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)]$ (where the LHS is the degree of the extension and the RHS is the index of the subgroup).
>3. (inclusion-inverting) $F \subset K \subset L \subset E \iff \text{id} \subset \text{Gal}(E \diagup L) \subset \text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \subset \text{Gak}(E \diagup F)$.
>4. $K \diagup F$ is a Galois extension of $F$ $\iff$ $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, and in this case $\text{Gal}(K \diagup F) \cong \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$.


**Proof.** 

$1$. We will show $\phi: K \mapsto \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ is bijective by showing it is injective and surjective. First, we show $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ is well defined for every extension $F \subseteq K \subseteq E$: clearly the identity fixes $K$, so $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ consists of at least the identity, so it exists for every sub-extension $K$ between $F$ and $E$.

For injectivity, suppose $\phi(K)=\phi(L)$, i.e. $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup L)$. By definition, $E^{\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)}=K$, and also $E^{\text{Gal}(E \diagup L)}=L$, and since $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup L)$, then we have $K=L$, so $\phi$ is injective.

For surjectivity, let $G \in B$, so $G \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, so $G \leqslant \text{Aut}(E)$, so by corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group) we have $E^{G}$ is a Galois extension, so $G=\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, so $E^{G}=K$, so $\phi(E^{G})=G$.

We have found that $\phi$ is injective and surjective, so it is a bijection.

$2$. $[E:K]=|\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)|$ since by $(1)$ we have that $K$ is a Galois extension for every sub-extension $K$ between $F$ and $E$. By theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$), we have that $[E:F]=[E:K][K:F]$, so $[K:F]=\frac{[E:F]}{[E:K]}=\frac{|\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)|}{|\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)|}$. Since $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then the RHS is the index of $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ in $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ (recall definition 102 (index of a subgroup)), hence $[E:F]=[\text{Gal}(E \diagup F):\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)]$.

$3$. Recall that $\phi: K \mapsto \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ is a bijection from the subfields $K$ of $E \diagup F$ s.t. $K$ is an extension of $F$, to the subgroups of $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. Since $\phi$ is bijective, it suffices to show the argument in one direction, and the argument in the other direction follows by a symmetric argument. We choose to prove $\implies$: suppose $F \subset K \subset L \subset E$. Clearly, $\phi(E)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup E)=\{\text{id}_{E}\}$. For $L$, $\phi(L)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup L)$, which obviously contains the identity since it is a group, so $\{\text{id}_{E}\} \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup L)$. For $K$, $\phi(K)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$.
