Let $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup L)$, then since $\sigma$ fixes $L$ and $K \subset L$, then obviously $\sigma$ fixes $L$, so $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, so $\text{Gal}(E \diagup L) \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$. By an identical argument, we find that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$.

The following diagram illustrates the proof of this proposition, and should also clarify why we say that this relation is *inclusion-inverting*.

<TikzSvgaac6ddd8df8d5cc0ff132052dbfd21a0 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

If we were to properly define the lattice of a group/field via a partial order defined as inclusion of subgroups/subfields, it will immediately follow from $(3)$ that the FTGT states that if $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, then the lattices of $E \diagup F$ and $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ are the same, but inverted. We will show an example of this later.

$4$. $\implies$ Suppose $K \diagup F$ is a Galois extension over $F$ for $F \subseteq K \subseteq E$. We want to show that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. We already know that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ since every automorphism on $E$ that fixes $K$ must also fix $F$. To show it is a normal subgroup, we proceed by proposition 111 . Let $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ and let $\psi \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. We will show that $\tau=\psi^{-1} \circ \sigma \circ \psi \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$. 

Clearly $\tau \in \text{Aut}(E)$ since it is a composition of automorphisms of $E$, so it remains to show that $\tau$ fixes $K$. Let $\alpha \in K$, then $\tau(\alpha)=\psi^{-1}(\sigma(\psi(\alpha)))$. If $\alpha \in F$, then $\psi$ and $\sigma$ both fix $\alpha$, so $\tau(\alpha)=\alpha$. Suppose $\alpha \in K \setminus F$. Since $K \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, then it is a normal, separable extension (by theorem 440 (finite Galois extension is finite, normal and separable)) and so $\text{irr}(\alpha, F)$ exists in $F[x]$ (since $K \diagup F$ is separable), denote it $p(x)$. Since $\psi \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then $\psi(\alpha)$ is also a zero of $F[x]$ by theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$). Since $K \diagup F$ is normal, then this zero is also in $K$, so $\psi(\alpha) \in K$. Since $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, then $\sigma$ fixes $\psi(\alpha)$, so $\tau(\alpha)=\psi^{-1}(\sigma(\psi(\alpha)))=\psi^{-1}(\psi(\alpha))=\alpha$, so $\tau$ fixes $\alpha$. We conclude that $\tau$ fixes $K$, so $\tau \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, hence $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$.

$\impliedby$Suppose $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. We want to show $K \diagup F$ is a Galois extension. To do that, we will show that $K^{\text{Aut}(K \diagup F)}=F$ (by corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group)).

Let $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ and $\psi \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then since $\text{Gal}(E \diagup K) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, by definition 106 (normal subgroup) it follows that $\exists \overline{\psi} \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ s.t. $\sigma \circ \overline{\psi} = \psi \circ \sigma$. Let $\alpha \in K$, then $\overline{\psi}(\sigma(\alpha))=\overline{\psi}(\alpha)$, so $\sigma(\overline{\psi}(\alpha))=\psi(\alpha)$, hence $(\sigma \circ \overline{\psi})_{|K}=\psi_{|K}$ is an element on $\text{Aut}(K \diagup F)$ (since $\psi$ is an automorphism on $E$ that fixes $F$).

Let $\beta \in K^{\text{Aut}(K \diagup F)}$, then $\beta$ is fixed by all automorphisms in $\text{Aut}(K \diagup F)$ and in particular, it is fixed by $\psi_{|K}$, but this is true for every $\psi \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, and since $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension then it must be that $\psi_{|K}(\beta) \in F$, so $K^{\text{Aut}(K \diagup F)}=F$, so $K \diagup F$ is a Galois extension over $F$.

Finally, it remains to show that if either of these equivalent conditions holds, then $\text{Gal}(K \diagup F) \cong \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$.

Let $\sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then $\sigma_{|K} \in \text{Gal}(K \diagup F)$ by the above (in the $\impliedby$ direction). Consider the mapping $\phi: \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \to \text{Gal}(K \diagup F), \sigma \mapsto \sigma_{|K}$. As we have just argued the domain and codomain of $\phi$ are properly defined. Let $\tau \in \text{Ker}(\phi)$, then $\phi(\tau)=\text{id}_{K}$, so $\tau$ must fix $K$ pointwise, so $\text{Ker}(\phi)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$, so by theorem 262 (first isomorphism theorem for rings) we have $\text{Im}(\phi) \cong \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$. It remains to show $\text{Im}(\phi)=\text{Gal}(K \diagup F)$.

We already know $\text{Im}(\phi) \leqslant \text{Gal}(K \diagup F)$. By $(2)$, we know that $|\text{Gal}(K \diagup F)| = [K:F]=[\text{Gal}(E \diagup F):\text{Gal}(E \diagup K)]$, and the RHS is by definition the order of the factor group $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup K)$ which by the first isomorphism theorem is isomorphic to $\text{Im}(\phi)$, so by theorem 90 (properties of a group isomorphism), we have that $|\text{Im}(\phi)|=|\text{Gal}(K \diagup F)|$, so since it is a subgroup of $\text{Gal}(K \diagup F)$ with the same order as the group it must be the group itself, so we have $\text{Im}(\phi)=\text{Gal}(K \diagup F)$, which completes the proof.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

By recalling that a finite Galois extension is a separable and normal extension, proposition $(4)$ in theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)) should provide better motivation for why named the property described in definition 396 (normal extension), *normal*.

The correspondence we see between subfields of a Galois extension $E \diagup F$ and subgroups of its Galois field $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is an example of a more general property, called a [Galois correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galois_correspondence).

Consider $f(x)=x^{3}-2 \in \mathbb{Q}$, first we analyze its Galois extension.

>[!example] Analyzing Galois extension of $f(x)=x^{3}-2$
>
>Consider $f(x)=x^{3}-2 \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$. Let $E \diagup \mathbb{Q}$ be a splitting field for $f(x)$. Since $E \diagup \mathbb{Q}$ is a finite extension of $f(x)$, by theorem 417 (finite extensions are algebraic) it is algebraic, and since $\text{char}(\mathbb{Q})=0$ then by $\mathbb{Q}$ is a perfect field and by theorem 414 (algebraic extension of a perfect field is separable), hence $f(x)$ is separable, so $E \diagup \mathbb{Q}$ is a Galois extension by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group).
>
>Denote the zeros of $f(x)$ by $\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2} \omega, \sqrt[3]{2} \omega^{2}$ where $\omega=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}$ the third root of unity, then $E=F(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega ^{2})$
>
>Consider $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$. In this extension field, we find $f(x)=(x-\sqrt[3]{2})h(x)$ where $\deg(h(x))=2$ (by proposition 297 (degree equation for polynomial ring of integer domain)). We know that $h(x)$ is irreducible in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ since the other two zeros of $f(x)$ are complex and $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ is isomorphic to a subfield of $\mathbb{R}$ (see example 397 ($\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$ is not a normal extension of $\mathbb{Q}$)).
>
>Consider $E \diagup \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$. We know that complex conjugation $\sigma: a \mapsto a^{*}$ is an automorphism on $E$ and also clearly it preserves $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})$, so $\sigma \in \text{Aut}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}))$, so by theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$) it defines a permutation on the zeros of $h(x)$ in $E$, so $\sqrt[3]{2}\omega, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega^{2}$ are conjugates. Since complex conjugation is achieved via the normal field operations, then this means that $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega)=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega^{2})=E$.
>
>By theorem 419 ($[K:F]=[K:E][E:F]$) , we have
>
>$$
>[E:\mathbb{Q}]=[\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega):\mathbb{Q}]=[\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega):\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})][\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}):\mathbb{Q}]
>$$ 
>
>Since $\text{irr}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \mathbb{Q})=f(x)=x^{3}-2$, then $[\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}):\mathbb{Q}]=3$. Also, $\text{irr}(\sqrt[3]{2} \omega, \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}))=h(x)$ which has degree $2$, so $[\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \sqrt[3]{2}\omega):\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2})]=2$.
>
>It follows that $[E:\mathbb{Q}]=6$. Also, we have seen that $[E:\mathbb{Q}]$ is generated by two cyclic subgroups of order $2$ (corresponding to complex conjugation) and $3$ (corresponding to all possible mappings from $\sqrt{3}[2]$ to the other zeros of the polynomial in its splitting field), so $[E:\mathbb{Q}] \cong S_{3}$ the symmetric group on $3$ letters.
>
>since $\mathbb{R}$ has characteristic $0$, then it is a perfect field and by theorem 414 (algebraic extension of a perfect field is separable) the splitting


Let's see theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)) in action. First, consider the (inverted) lattice diagram for $S_{3}$, where the numbers on the arrows indicate the index of the subgroup (upper end of the arrow) in the group (lower end of the arrow):

<TikzSvg65aecf80b2d141ff0f387795f4e9fd2a style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>


Now, consider the following lattice, which presents all sub-extensions from $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt[3]{2}, \omega)$, as broadly discussed in example 446 (Analyzing Galois extension of $f(x)=x^{3}-2$), where the numbers are the degree of the extension:

<TikzSvg6e9ba55dca2c75d0f298140503f2ace2 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

Notice that this is *exactly* the inverted lattice diagram for $S_{3}$, which is to be expected by the Galois correspondence between Galois extensions and Galois groups, which we have derived in theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)).

### Roots of Unity


>[!definition] root of unity
>
>An $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity is a solution to the equation $x^{n}=1$, i.e. it is a zero of the polynomial $f(x)=x^{n}-1$ over some field $F$.

>[!theorem] roots of unity form a cyclic abelian group
>
>If $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$, then the $n^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity in of $x^{n}-1 \in F[x]$ form a cyclic abelian group under multiplication.


**Proof.** Let $E$ be a splitting field for $x^{n}-1$ over $F$, then since $E$ has characteristic zero find that $x^{n}-1$ has $n$ distinct zeros in $E$ (by theorem 404 (criterion for multiple zeros), since $(x^{n}-1)'=nx^{n-1}$ which does not share a common factor with $x^{n}-1$ (since such factor would need to have nonzero constant coefficient))
, denote them $\Omega=\{\alpha_{1}, \dots, \alpha_{n}\}$. Consider the multiplicative group of $E$, which is $E \setminus \{0\}$, then $\Omega$ is a subset of this multiplicative group since trivially $0^{n} \neq 1$ so $0 \notin \Omega$. We will show $\Omega$ is a group by theorem 38 (one-step subgroup test for finite group):

1. Clearly, $|\Omega|=n$ so it is non-empty.
2. Let $\alpha_{i}, \alpha_{j} \in \Omega$, then $(\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j})^{n}=\alpha_{i}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}$ by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) since the multiplicative group of $F$ is abelian (since multiplication in a field commutes), and by supposition $\alpha_{i}^{n}=1, \alpha_{j}^{n}=1$, and since $1$ is the identity then $\alpha_{i}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}=1$, so $\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j} \in \Omega$, so $\Omega$ is closed under multiplication.

By the one-step subgroup test, $\Omega$ is a group, and also abelian since it is a subgroup of an abelian group. 

Let $d \mid n$, then $x^{d}-1$ has at most $d$ zeros by proposition 305 (a polynomial of degree $n$ has at most $n$ zeros), so for every such $d$ there are at most $d$ elements $\alpha \in \Omega$ s.t. $\alpha^{d}=1$, so by theorem 56 (sufficient condition for finite group to be cyclic) we have that $\Omega$ is cyclic.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We call a generator of this group a *primitive* $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity:

>[!definition] primitive $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity
>
>If $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$, then a generator  of the $n^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity of $x^{n}-1 \in F[x]$ is called a *primitive $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity*, and denoted $\omega$.

theorem 448 (roots of unity form a cyclic abelian group) promises the existence of such primitive root for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

The next corollary deals with the zeros of a polynomial of the form $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ where $\text{char}(F)=0$.

>[!corollary] zeros of $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ with field characteristic $0$
>
>If $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$ and $a \in F, a \neq 0$, and $\alpha$ is a zero of the polynomial $x^{n}-a$ in a splitting field $E \diagup F$, then the zeros of $x^{n}-a$ are exactly $\{\alpha\omega^{0}, \alpha \omega^{1}, \dots, \alpha \omega^{n-1}\}$ where $\omega$ is a primitive $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity.


**Proof.** Since the formal derivative of $x^{n}-a$ is $nx^{n-1}$ and $a \neq 0$ then it cannot share a factor with $x^{n}-a$ hence by theorem 404 (criterion for multiple zeros), $x^{n}-a$ has no multiple zeros, hence it has exactly $n$ distinct zeros in $E \diagup F$. Since $\omega$ generates the $n^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity, then $|\omega|=n$, hence all of $\{\omega^{0}, \omega^{1}, \dots, \omega^{n-1}\}$ are distinct, so $\{\alpha \omega^{0}, \dots, \alpha \omega^{n-1}\}$ are also distinct and consists of exactly $n$ elements, so it remains to show that every element of the form $\alpha \omega^{k}$ for $k \in [0, n-1]$ are zeros of $x^{n}-a$. Since $F$ is a field, $(ab)^{n}=a^{n}b^{n}$ for every $a, b \in F$ (the multiplicative group of $F \setminus \{0\}$ is abelian), we have $(\alpha \omega^{k})^{n}=\alpha^{n}(\omega^{k})^{n}$. By theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)) we have $(\omega^{k})^{n}=(\omega^{n})^{k}=1^{k}=1$, and also $\alpha^{n}=a$ since $\alpha$ is a zero of $x^{n}-a$ by supposition, hence $\alpha\omega^{k}$ is a zero of $x^{n}-a$, which completes the proof. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

### Radical Extension

We work our way towards one of our main results - the insolvability of the quintic by radicals. First, a definition of what it means for a polynomial to be "solvable by radicals" is in order. 

As an motivating example, let us consider a general second degree polynomial over a field $F$ with characteristic $0$, this polynomial is of the form $ax^{2}+bx+c=0$, where $a \neq 0$. Recall that in our general overview of abstract algebra, we presented a derivation of the quadratic formula. Let us study this derivation, presented in its entirety below:

>[!note]
>
>
In this example we abuse notation and use $x$ both as the indeterminate and as a proper element of the splitting field of the polynomial, hopefully context helps the reader avoid confusion.

$$
\begin{gathered}
ax^{2}+bx + c = 0 \\
x^{2}+\frac{b}{a}x + \frac{c}{a} = 0 \\
x^{2} + \frac{b}{a}x = -\frac{c}{a} \\
x^{2} + \frac{b}{a}x + \frac{b^{2}}{4a^{2}} = -\frac{c}{a} + \frac{b^{2}}{4a^{2}} \\
(x+\frac{b}{2a})^{2} = \frac{b^{2}-4ac}{4a^{2}} \\
x + \frac{b}{2a} = \pm \frac{\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}\\
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}
\end{gathered}
$$

Since we consider $ax^{2}+bx+c \in F[x]$ then $a, b, c \in F[x]$, and since by supposition $a \neq 0$ (otherwise we don't have a quadratic), then we know that $a^{-1} \in F$. The first step is simply claiming that, for a zero $x$ of $f(x)=ax^{2}+bx+c$ in a splitting field $E \diagup F$, we have that $f$ evaluated at this $x$ is $0$, which is the definition of $x$ being a zero of the polynomial. Then, we multiply both sides by $a^{-1}$ and proceed according to ring arithmetic, until eventually we have an expression of the form $(x+\frac{b}{2a})^{2}=\frac{b^{2}-4ac}{4a^{2}}$. Since $a, b, c \in F$ and $a \neq 0$, then the RHS is also an element of $F$, so $(x+\frac{b}{2a})^{2} \in F$ as well (since they are the same), but, it is not necessarily true that $x+\frac{b}{2a} \in F$ (since $x \in E \diagup F$ but not necessarily in $F$. For example, if we consider the case where $F=\mathbb{Q}$ and $f(x)=x^{2}-2$, then we already know that the zeros of this polynomial are $\pm \sqrt{2}$ which are irrational so not in $\mathbb{Q}$. 

The next step of the derivation requires just that - that we can take the second root (any second root, but when dealing with $\mathbb{R}$ by convention we take this to be the non-negative root) of $b^{2}-4ac$ (since $2a \in F$ (here $2a$ is $a$ acted on by $2 \in \mathbb{Z}$) and multiplication in a field commutes then $(2a)^{2}=4a^{2}$ so we do have a square root for the denomiator), i.e. we want that $\exists y \in E$ s.t. $y^{2}=b^{2}-4ac$, which we equivalently denote by $y=\sqrt[2]{b^{2}-4ac}$. Consider an extension $K=F(\sqrt[2]{b^{2}-4ac})$, i.e. $F$ adjoined with a square root of $b^{2}-4ac$, then in this field we can continue our derivation of the quadratic formula, and arrive at the end result.

We call such $F(\sqrt[2]{b^{2}-4ac})$ a *radical extension*.

>[!definition] radical extension
>
>If $F$ is a field, then $E$ is a *radical extension* over $F$ if $E=F(\sqrt[k]{\alpha})$ for some $\alpha \in F, k \in \mathbb{N}$. Equivalently, $E$ is a radical extension over $F$ if $E$ is $F$ adjoined with an element $\beta$ s.t. $\beta^{k} \in F$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$.

>[!definition] radical series
>
>If $F$ is a field, and $E$ is an extension of $F$, and there exists a finite series of extensions $F=F_{0} \subset F_{1} \subset F_{2} \subset \dots \subset F_{n}=E$ s.t. $F_{i+1}=F_{i}(\sqrt[k_{i}]{\alpha_{i}})$ for some $k_{i} \in \mathbb{N}, \alpha_{i} \in F_{i}$ (i.e. each $F_{i+1}$ is a radical extension over $F_{i}$), then we say that $F_{0} \subset F_{1} \subset \dots \subset F_{n}$ is a *radical series* and we say that $E$ is an *extension by radicals* over $F$.

In our example of the quadratic, we found that for $f(x)=ax^{2}+bx+c \in F[x]$, the field $E=F(\sqrt[2]{b^{2}-4ac})$ is a radical extension over $F$, and also since we can derive the quadratic formula in $F$, then clearly $f(x)$ splits in $E$, i.e. if $K$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$ then by definition and by uniqueness (corollary 394 (uniqueness of splitting field)), $K \subseteq E$ via an subfield isomorphism. This motivates the following definition:

>[!definition] solvability by radicals
>
>If $F$ is a field and $f(x) \in F[x]$, then we say that $f(x)$ is *solvable by radicals* if there exists an extension by radicals $E$ over $F$ s.t. the splitting field $K$ for $f(x)$ is contained (via subfield isomorphism) in $E$.

Practically, solvability by radicals means that the zeros of the polynomial can be found by manipulating the coefficients of the polynomial, allowing for a *finite sequence* of:
1. Multiplication and subtraction.
2. Division by the leading coefficient.
3. Root taking (which led to radical extensions)

## Insolvability of the Quintic

Recall that Galois theory deals with connecting fields and groups, letting us reduce questions about fields to questions about groups. So, while our end goal is to answer the question of when is a polynomial solvable by radicals, we will first define and examine a few properties of a class of groups called *solvable groups*. As the name hints, our main theorem in this section will be that if a polynomial is solvable by radicals, then its Galois group is solvable. We will then see why quadratic polynomials are not generally solvable by radicals.

>[!definition] solvable group
>
>A group $G$ is *solvable* if 
>1. there exists a normal series $\{e\} =H_{0} \unlhd H_{1} \unlhd H_{2} \unlhd \dots \unlhd H_{n}=G$, and
>2. Each factor group $H_{i+1} \diagup H_{i}$ in this normal series is abelian

>[!theorem] subgroup of solvable group are solvable
>
>If $G$ is a solvable group and $H \leqslant G$, then $H$ is solvable.


**Proof.** Suppose $G$ is a solvable group and $H \leqslant G$. Since $G$ is solvable, then we can consider a normal series $\{e\}=G_{0} \unlhd G_{1} \unlhd \dots \unlhd G_{n}=G$. So, by theorem 157 (second isomorphism theorem),

$$
(H \cap G_{i})G_{i-1} \diagup G_{i-1} \cong H \cap G_{i} \diagup H \cap G_{i-1}
$$
(here we used the fact the the normalizer of a normal group is the group (theorem 154 (properties of the normalizer)), and also that since $G_{i-1} \unlhd G_{i}$ then $(H \cap G_{i}) \cap G_{i-1}=H \cap G_{i-1}$) 

In particular, we find that $H \cap G_{i-1} \unlhd H \cap G_{i}$. Note that:
1. $H \cap G_{0}=H \cap \{e\}=\{e\}$
2. $H \cap G_{n}=H \cap G = H$ (since $H \leqslant G$)

So, letting $H_{i}=H \cap G_{i}$, we have $H_{0} \unlhd H_{1} \unlhd \dots \unlhd H_{n}=H$ a normal series of $H$. We will show that $H_{i+1} \diagup H_{i}$ is abelian. By the fourth isomorphism theorem (which we have't covered here), we have $(H \cap G_{i+1}) \diagup (H \cap G_{i}) \leqslant G_{i+1} \diagup G_{i}$, hence $H_{i+1} \diagup H_{i} \leqslant G_{i+1} \diagup G_{i}$. Since $G$ is solvable with normal sequence $G_{0} \unlhd \dots \unlhd G_{n}$, then $G_{i+1} \diagup G_{i}$ is abelian, so $H_{i+1} \diagup H_{i}$ is abelian as a subgroup of an abelian group.

We conclude that $H$ is solvable.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] simple and solvable iff abelian
>
>Suppose $G$ is a simple group, then it is solvable if and only if it is abelian.


**Proof.** Suppose $G$ is simple, the by definition 109 (simple group) the only normal subgroups of $G$ are $G$ and $\{e\}$. Since $G \diagup \{e\} \cong G$ by corollary 122  then if $G$ is solvable, it must be that $G$ is abelian, and if $G$ is abelian then $G$ must be solvable by definition. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!corollary] $A_{n}$ is not solvable for $5 \leq n$
>
>The alternating group on $n$ letters $A_{n}$ for $n$ greater than $5$ is not solvable.


**Proof.** Let $5 \leq n$. By theorem 114 ($A_{5 \leq n}$ is simple), $A_{n}$ is simple. By proposition 457 (simple and solvable iff abelian), if $A_{n}$ is solvable then it is abelian, yet by proposition 61 ($\text{Sym}(A)$ non-abelian if $2 \lt |A|$), $A_{n}$ is non-abelian since $5 \leq n$ hence $2 \lt n$, so $A_{n}$ is not solvable. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>  

>[!corollary] $S_{n}$ is not solvable for $5 \leq n$
>
>The symmetric group on $n$ letters $S_{n}$ for $n$ greater than $5$ is not solvable.


**Proof.** Let $n \geq 5$ and suppose by contradiction $S_{n}$ is solvable, so since $A_{n} \leqslant S_{n}$ the n by theorem 456 (subgroup of solvable group are solvable) it follows that $A_{n}$ is solvable, but by corollary 458 ($A_{n}$ is not solvable for $5 \leq n$) we know such $A_{n}$ is not solvable, a contradiction, hence $S_{n}$ is not solvable for $n \geq 5$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition] abelian groups are solvable
>
>If $G$ is abelian, then it is solvable.


**Proof.** Suppose $G$ is abelian, then by corollary 122  we have that $\{e\} \unlhd G$ is a normal series of $G$ and also $G \diagup \{e\} \cong G$, and since $G$ is abelian then so is $G \diagup \{e\}$, hence $G$ is solvable by definition. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] Galois group of splitting field for $x^{n}-a$ is solvable
>
>If $F$ is a field of characteristic zero and $E$ is a splitting field for the polynomial $x^{n}-a$, then $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is solvable (and is well-defined, i.e. $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ is indeed a Galois group).


**Proof.** By corollary 450 (zeros of $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ with field characteristic $0$), we have that $F \subseteq F(\omega) \subseteq E$ where $\omega$ is a primitive $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity. If $F=F(\omega)=E$ then $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)=\{\text{id}\}$ which is the trivial group and clearly it is abelian and solvable, so suppose $x^{n}-a$ does not split in $F$.

There are two cases: suppose $\omega \in F$, i.e. $F=F(\omega)$, and let $\alpha \in E$ be a zero of $x^{n}-a$, then $\alpha \notin F(\omega)$ otherwise by corollary 450 (zeros of $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ with field characteristic $0$) we would find that $x^{n}-a$ splits in $F$ which is a contardiction to our supposition. So, $E$ is the splitting field of an irreducible polynomial in $F$ which has characteristic $0$ so this polynomial is separable, so by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group) $\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)=\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. By theorem 427 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup F)$ defines a permutation on the zeros of a polynomial from $F[x]$), we have that the automorphisms on $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ are uniquely determined by their action on $\alpha$. Let $\sigma, \tau \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, then by corollary 450 (zeros of $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ with field characteristic $0$), $\sigma(\alpha)=\alpha \omega^{k}, \tau(\alpha)=\alpha \omega^{t}$ for some $k, t \in \mathbb{N}$. Since $\omega \in F$ by supposition, then all $n^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity are in $F$, and since $\sigma, \tau$ are homomorphisms that preserve $F$, then:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\sigma \circ \tau)(\alpha)=\\
&\sigma(\tau(\alpha))=\sigma(\alpha \omega^{t})=\sigma(\alpha)\omega^{t}=\\
&\alpha\omega^{k}\omega^{t}=\alpha \omega^{t}\omega^{k}=\\
&\tau(\alpha)\tau(\omega^{k})=\tau(\alpha \omega^{k})=\tau(\sigma(\alpha))=\\
&&(\tau \circ \sigma)(\alpha)
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $\sigma \circ \tau$ is defined by its action on $\sigma$, we find that $\sigma \circ \tau = \tau \circ \sigma$, i.e.$\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is abelian, hence by proposition 460 (abelian groups are solvable), $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is solvable.

Othewise, $\omega \notin F$, then $F \subset F(\omega) \subset E$, so $x^{n}-a$ must have an irreducible factor in $F$ since it can have at most one zero in $F$. Since $F$ has characteristic $0$, and $E$ is a splitting field for this factor, then $E \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, hence $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is a Galois extension, and by theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)) we have that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F(\omega)) \leqslant \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. Also, since $F(\omega)$ is the splitting field for $x^{n}-1$ which is separable, then $F(\omega) \diagup F$ is a Galois extension, so again by FTGT, we have $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F(\omega)) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, and also $\text{Gal}(F(\omega) \diagup F) \cong \text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup F(\omega))$, so we have a normal series $\{\text{id}\} \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F(\omega)) \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$, and it remains to show $\text{Gal}(F(\omega) \diagup F)$ is abelian. The proof is analogous to the one presented in the previous case: every $\sigma, \tau \in \text{Gal}(F(\omega)\ \diagup F)$ is uniquely defined by its action on $\omega$ and it must send $\omega$ to another $n^{\text{th}}$ root of unity, so $\exists t, k \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $\sigma(\omega)=\omega^{k}, \tau(\omega)=\omega^{t}$, and $\sigma \circ \tau, \tau \circ \sigma$ are uniquely defined by their action on $\omega$ as well, which agree via computation analogous to the one from the previous case, hence they are the same, hence $\text{Gal}(F(\omega) \diagup F)$ is abelian, hence $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is solvable
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem]
>
>If $F$ is a field of characteristic $0$, and $E$ is an extension by radicals of $F$, then there exists a normal series of radical extensions $F=K_{0} \subseteq K_{1} \subseteq \dots \subseteq K_{n}=K$, s.t. $E \subseteq K$.


**Proof.** Since $E$ is an extension by radical of $F$, then by definition there exists a series $F=F_{0} \subseteq F_{1} \subseteq F_{2} \dots \subseteq F_{n}=E$, s.t. $F_{i+1}=F_{i}(\sqrt[k_{i}]{\alpha_{i}})$ for some $\alpha_{i} \in F_{i}$ and some $k_{i} \in \mathbb{N}$.

First, let $F_{0}=K_{0}$. For each $F_{i+1}$, we will find a matching $K_{i+1}$ s.t. $K_{i+1}$ is a normal extension over $K_{i}$ by taking $K_{i+1}$ to be a splitting field for $f_{i}(x)=x^{k_{i}}-\alpha_{i}$. Let $\omega_{i}$ be a generator of the $k_{i}^{\text{th}}$ roots of unity (such $\omega_{i}$ exists by theorem 448 (roots of unity form a cyclic abelian group)), then by corollary 450 (zeros of $x^{n}-a \in F[x]$ with field characteristic $0$) we find that for some zero $\beta_{i}$ of $f_{i}(x)$, we have that $F_{i}(\beta_{i}\omega_{i})$ is a splitting field for $x^{k_{i}}-\alpha_{i}$, so it is a normal extension of $F_{i}$ (by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group)). For $i=1$, we let $K_{1}=F_{1}(\beta_{1}\omega_{1})$, clearly $K_{1}$ is a normal extension of $K_{0}$ as well. Continuing along this fashion, we find a series of normal extensions $K_{0} \subseteq K_{1} \subseteq \dots \subseteq K_{n}=K$, where $K$ itself is an extension (not necessarily proper) of $E$, which completes the proof.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!theorem] solvability by radicals implies Galois group is solvable
>
>If $F$ is a field with characteristic $0$ and $f(x) \in F[x]$ is solvable by radicals, then its Galois group is solvable.


**Proof.** Suppose $f(x)$ is solvable by radicals, and let $E$ be its splitting field, then $E$ is an extension by radicals over $F$ by definition, so by theorem 462  there exists a normal series of radical extensions $F=F_{0} \subseteq F_{1} \subseteq \dots \subseteq F_{n}=K$ s.t. $E \subseteq K$. Since $F$ has characteristic $0$ and $E$ is a splitting field for $f(x) \in F[x]$, then $E \diagup F$ admits a Galois extension by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group) hence it is also normal over $F$, so we can consider a normal series $F=F_{0} \subseteq F_{1} \subseteq \dots \subseteq F_{n}=E$.

Since this is a normal series, each $F_{i+1}$ is a normal extension of $F_{i}$ hence $F_{i+1} \diagup F_{i}$ is a Galois extension (since $\text{char}(F)=0$), so by theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)) we have a normal series $\{\text{id}\} \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F_{n-1}) \unlhd \dots \unlhd \text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$. Again by theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)), each $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F_{i}) \diagup \text{Gal}(E \diagup F_{i-1}) \cong \text{Gal}(F_{i-1} \diagup F_{i})$. 

Recall that in our construction of the normal radical extensions $F_{i} \diagup F_{i-1}$ in theorem 462  we constructed $F_{i}$ to be the splitting field for a polynomial of the form $x^{k_{i}}-\alpha_{i} \in F_{i-1}[x]$ for some $k_{i} \in \mathbb{N}, \alpha_{i} \in F_{i-1}$, then by theorem 461 (Galois group of splitting field for $x^{n}-a$ is solvable) $\text{Gal}(F_{i-1} \diagup F_{i})$ is solvable, hence $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ is solvable, which completes the proof. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

In fact, a stronger version of the theorem exists, which we will state without proof (the proof does not require any new machinery not covered here):

>[!theorem] solvable by radicals iff Glaois group is solvable
>
>If $F$ is a field and $\text{char}(F)=0$, and $f(x) \in F[x]$, then $f(x)$ is solvable by radicals if and only if its Galois group is solvable.


theorem 463 (solvability by radicals implies Galois group is solvable) proves the $\implies$ direction. We do not make use of this theorem to demonstrate insolvability of the quintic, so the proof for the other direction is omitted. We do, however, use it below, to provide a nice proof for the solvability of polynomials of degree less than $5$ that does not make use of the general formulas themselves:

>[!corollary] polynomials of degree less than $5$ are solvable by radicals
>
>If $F$ is a field and $\text{char}(F)=0$, and $f(x) \in F[x]$ has $\text{deg}(f(x)) \lt 5$, then $f(x)$ is solvable by radicals.


**Proof.** By corollary 443 (Galois group is isomorphic to subgroup of $S_{n}$) and theorem 456 (subgroup of solvable group are solvable), it suffices to show that $S_{n}$ is solvable for $n=\text{deg}(f(x))$, then by theorem 464 (solvable by radicals iff Glaois group is solvable) it would follow that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ the Galois group of $f(x)$ is solavble.

Note that $S_{1}, S_{2}$ are simply the trivial group and the group on two elements $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ under an isomorphism, both of which abelian, hence by proposition 460 (abelian groups are solvable) both are solvable.

For the cases where $n=3, n=4$, recall theorem 131 ($S_{n} \diagup A_{n} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{2}$). Since $A_{n} \unlhd S_{n}$ and also $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ is abelian, then if $A_{n}$ is solvable then we would have that $S_{n}$ is also solvable.

For $n=3$, we have that the alternating group on $3$ letters $A_{3}$ consists of exactly $3$ elements (verify this by listing our $S_{3}$ explicitly, to find that $A_{3}=\{(1), (1 \space 2 \space 3)=(1 \space 3)(1 \space 2), (1 \space 3 \space 2)=(1 \space 2)(1 \space 3)\}$). Since $3$ is prime there exists one group of order $3$ up to an isomorphism, so $A_{3} \cong \mathbb{Z}_{3}$, whose Cayley table we have already studied and we have found that $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$ is abelian, so it is solvable, so as we argued above $S_{3}$ is solvable (from solvability of $A_{3}$).

For $n=4$, we find that $|A_{4}|=12$, so it suffices to find a normal subgroup of $A_{4}$ of order $4$, since then the order of their quotient would be $12 \div 4 = 3$, so their quotient will be isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}_{3}$ which is abelian hence solvable, so it would follow that $A_{4}$ is solvable, hence $S_{4}$ is solvable. Indeed, $H=\{(1), (1 \space 2)(3 \space 4), (1 \space 3)(2 \space 4), (1 \space 4)(2 \space 3)\}$ is a normal subgroup of $A_{4}$ (this can be verified by direct calculation) and has order $4$, so $A_{4}$ is solvable, so $S_{4}$ is solvable.

We have demonstrated that $S_{1}, S_{2}, S_{3}, S_{4}$ are all solvable, so polynomial with coefficients from a field of characteristic $0$ with degree up to $5$ are solvable by radicals.

Note that this line of reasoning fails for $5 \leq n$, since we relied on showing that $A_{n}$ is solvable to show that $S_{n}$ is solvable, yet by corollary 458 ($A_{n}$ is not solvable for $5 \leq n$), $A_{n}$ is not solvable in those cases.
<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!proposition]
>
>If $F$ is a field of characteristic $0$ and $f(x) \in F[x]$ is a polynomial of degree at least $5$, and the Galois field for $f(x)$ is isomorphic to $S_{\text{deg}(f(x))}$, then $f(x)$ is insolvable by radicals.


**Proof.** Suppose by contradiction $f(x)$ is solvable by radicals, then by theorem 463 (solvability by radicals implies Galois group is solvable), its Galois group is solvable, so $S_{\deg(f(x))}$ is solvable (since by supposition the two are isomorphic, and it is left as an exercise to the reader to demonstrate that solvability of a group is preserved under group isomorphism), which contradicts corollary 459 ($S_{n}$ is not solvable for $5 \leq n$), hence $f(x)$ is insolvable by radicals. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We use this proposition as a guideline for finding a quintic $f(x)$ that is insolvable by radicals: first, recall that since $5$ is prime then by theorem 82  it suffices to find that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F)$ contains a transposition on the zeros of $f(x)$ and a cycle of order $5$ on the zeros of $f(x)$, since then we would have by the theorem that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup F) \cong S_{5}$, so by proposition 466  $f(x)$ is insolvable by radicals. 

A transposition is easily achievable by taking a polynomial $f(x) \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ s.t. $f(x)$ has a complex root, then since complex conjugation preserves $\mathbb{Q}$ and is an automorphism on its splitting field $E$, it would follow that $\text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ has a transposition (which exchanges the two complex roots of the polynomial). Next, if $f(x)$ is *irreducible* in $\mathbb{Q}$, and $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is a real root for $f(x)$, then $[\mathbb{Q}(\alpha):\mathbb{Q}]=5$ (note that this is *not* a Galois extension since $f(x)$ doesn't split in $f(x)$), and since $[E:\mathbb{Q}]=[E:\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)][\mathbb{Q}(\alpha):\mathbb{Q}]$, then $[E:\mathbb{Q}]$ must be divisible by $5$, hence $\text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ is a finite group with order divisible by $5$. By theorem 185 (Cauchy's theorem) since $5$ is prime and $5 \mid \text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$, then $\exists \tau \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ that has order $5$, so $\tau$ is a permutation on all $5$ distinct zeros of $f(x)$, so $\tau$ corresponds to a cycle of order $5$ (see also proposition 71 (order of a cycle)). 

The following example brings it all together:

>[!example] $x^{5}-x-1 \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ is insolvable by radicals
>
>By theorem 318  with $p=5$ we find that $x^{5}-x-1 \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$. From real analysis, we know $f(x)=x^{5}-x-1 \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ is continuous, and since $f(0)=-1$ and $f(2)=2^{5}-2-1=29$, by the intermediate value theorem we know that $\exists \alpha$ in the real interval $[0, 2]$ s.t. $f(\alpha)=0$ (since $f(0)=-1, f(2)=29$ and $-1 \lt 0 \lt 29$). It follows that $f(x)$ has a real zero $\alpha$, hence $[\mathbb{Q}(\alpha):\mathbb{Q}]=5$.
>
>Again from real analysis, we find that $f'(x)=5x^{4}-1$ which has two zeros (this can be proven by corollary 465 (polynomials of degree less than $5$ are solvable by radicals) by substituting $x^{2}$ for $y$, then we have the quadratic $5y^{2}-1=0$), which by Rolle's theorem means that there are at most two extremas of the function in $\mathbb{R}$, so there are at most $3$ real solutions (this is also a result from real analysis that follows from the IVT and Rolle's theorem). Since $\mathbb{Q}$ has characteristic $0$ and $x^{5}-x-1$, then it has $5$ distinct zeros by theorem 404 (criterion for multiple zeros). By the fundamental theorem of algebra, which we will soon prove independently of this example, $x^{5}-x-1$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$, hence the two remaining results are complex.
>
>Let $E \diagup \mathbb{Q}$ be the splitting field for $x^{5}-x-1$, then by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group) it is a Galois extension, and as argued above $\exists \sigma \in \text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ which is complex conjugation since $x^{5}-x-1$ has complex zeros, so $\text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ has a transposition on the zeros of the polynomial. Also, since $[\mathbb{Q}(\alpha):\mathbb{Q}]=5$, then as argued before the example $\text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q})$ contains a $5$-cycle, hence by theorem 82 , $\text{Gal}(E \diagup \mathbb{Q}) \cong S_{5}$, so by proposition 466 , $x^{5}-x-1$ is insolvable by radicals.

# Fundamental Theorem of Algebra

We end our discussion, fittingly, with a proof of the fundamental theorem of algebra. First, we collect a result from real analysis, using the IVT:

>[!lemma] every polynomial of odd degree with real coefficients has a zero in $\mathbb{R}$
>
>If $f(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ is a polynomial then if $f(x)$ has odd degree, it has a zero in $\mathbb{R}$.


**Proof.** Let $f(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ have odd degree. From limit arithmetic we know that the limit of $f(x)$ as $x \to \pm \infty$ is dominated by its leading term $a_{n}x^{n}$. Since $n$ is odd, then for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have $a_{n}x^{n}=-a_{n}(-x)^{n}$. Suppose w.l.o.g that $a_{n}$ is positive (otherwise we prove the same result for $-f(x)$ which will have positive leading coefficient, and every zero of $-f(x)$ is also a zero of $f(x)$ since they decompose into the same factors up to the sign), then $f(x) \lim_{x \to \infty} \infty$, and $f(x) \lim_{x \to -\infty}-\infty$ (since $a_{n}x^{n}$ is odd for $n$ odd), so for large enough $x$ we have that $f(x) \gt 0, f(-x) \lt 0$. Since every polynomial is continuous then from the IVT, we know that for every $y \in [f(-x), f(x)]$, in particular for $y=0$, $\exists x_{0} \in [x, -x]$ which is an interval in $\mathbb{R}$, s.t. $f(x_{0})=y=0$, hence $f(x)$ has a zero in $\mathbb{R}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

Essentially, the above result stems from the completeness of $\mathbb{R}$.

>[!lemma] $ax^{2}+bx+c \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$
>
>Let $f(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ s.t. $\text{deg}(f(x))=2$, then $f(x)$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$.


**Proof.** Let $f(x) \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ be a polynomial of degree $2$, then $f(x)=ax^{2}+bx+c$ for some $a, b, c \in \mathbb{R}[x]$, then by the quadratic formula the zeros of this equation are $\frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a}$. Consider $b^{2}-4ac$. If $b^{2}-4ac$ is non-zero then its root is in $\mathbb{R}$ by completeness of $\mathbb{R}$ hence $f(x)$ splits in $\mathbb{R}$ so it obviously splits in $\mathbb{C}$. Suppose $b^{2}-4ac \lt 0$, then $0 \lt 4ac-b^{2}$ and $(-1)(4ac-b^{2})=b^{2}-4ac$. Since $4ac-b^{2}$ is greater than zero then $\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}} \in \mathbb{R}$ again by completeness so $\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}} \in \mathbb{C}$, and also $i \in \mathbb{C}$ satisfies $i^{2}=-1$, hence $i \sqrt{4ac-b^{2}} \in \mathbb{C}$, which squares to $b^{2}-4ac$, hence $\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac} \in \mathbb{C}$, hence $f(x)$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!lemma] $ax^{2}+bx+c \in \mathbb{C}[x]$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$
>
>If $f(x) \in \mathbb{C}[x]$ is a polynomial of degree $2$, then it splits in $\mathbb{C}$.


**Proof.** Let $f(x)=ax^{2}+bx+c \in \mathbb{C}[x]$. Following a similar outline to that of lemma 469 ($ax^{2}+bx+c \in \mathbb{R}[x]$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$), it suffices to show that every complex number has a square root in $\mathbb{C}$, hence there exists an element $\alpha \in \mathbb{C}$ s.t. $\alpha=\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}$, so by the quadratic formula $f(x)$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$. Consider a general element in $\mathbb{C}$ expressed in polar coordinates in exponent form as $re^{i \theta}$ for some $r \in \mathbb{R}^{+} \cup \{0\}, \theta \in [-\pi, \pi]$. Consider the element $\sqrt{r}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}}$, since $\mathbb{C}$ is abelian then by theorem 15 (exponent laws (power rules)), $(\sqrt{r}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}})^{2}=(\sqrt{r})^{2}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}2}=re^{i \theta}$. From completeness of $\mathbb{R}$, it follows that $\sqrt{r} \in \mathbb{R}$ so also in $\sqrt{r}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}} \in \mathbb{C}$, so we can take the square root of a number in $\mathbb{C}$, so we can carry the quadratic formula in $\mathbb{C}$ for every polynomial in $\mathbb{C}$, which completes the proof. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

We now return to Galois theory to prove the following lemma:

>[!lemma]
>
>Let $K$ be a field. If $\forall f(x) \in K[x]$ of odd degree, $f(x)$ has a zero $\alpha \in K$, and $L \diagup K$ is a finite proper Galois extension of $K$, then $\text{Gal}(L \diagup K)$ is a $2$-group. (i.e. has order divisible by $2$)


**Proof.** Let $K$ be a field and let $L \diagup K$ be a finite Galois extension of $K$, and suppose $L \neq K$ (otherwise $L \diagup K$ is not a proper extension), then by theorem 424 (primitive element theorem (Steinitz, 1910)) there exists $\beta \in L$ s.t. $L=K(\beta)$. Since any finite extension is algebraic (theorem 417 (finite extensions are algebraic)) then there exists a minimal polynomial $f(x) \in K[x]$ s.t. $f(\beta)=0$, and $\text{deg}(f(x))=[K(\beta):K]$. Since by supposition every polynomial of odd degree with coefficients in $K$ has a zero in $K$, then $\text{deg}(f(x))$ is even, hence $2 \mid [K(\beta):K]$, hence $[L:K]=2^{n}q$ for some $n, q \in \mathbb{N}$ s.t. $q$ is odd.

Since $L \diagup K$ is a Galois extension, then by theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)), $\text{Gal}(L \diagup K)$ has order $2^{n}q$ as well, denote it $G$. By theorem 187 (Sylow's first theorem) it has a $2$-subgroup $H \leqslant G$. By theorem 188 (Sylow's third theorem), $[G:H]=q$, i.e. the index of $H$ in $G$ is odd.

Let $E^{H}$ be the field fixed by $H$, again by theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)) we have $K \subseteq E^{H} \subseteq L$ and $[E^{H}:K]=[\text{Gal}(L \diagup K):\text{Gal}(L \diagup E^{H})]$, and by corollary 436 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G}) = G$), $\text{Gal}(L \diagup E^{H})=H$ (the subgroup of $\text{Gal}(L \diagup K)$ that fixes $H$) hence $[E^{H}:K]=[G:H]=q$, so $[E^{H}:K]$ is odd. Suppose $E^{H} \neq K$, then since $E^{H} \diagup K$ is also a finite Galois extension (by FTGT), then it is finite and separable hence by the primitive element theorem, $E^{H}=K(\gamma)$ for some $\gamma$ algebraic over $K$, so $[E^{H}:K]=\text{deg}(g(x))$ where $\text{irr}(\gamma, K)=g(x)$, but then we have irreducible polynomial of odd degree, which is a contradition, so $E^{H}=K$, so $q=1$.

Hence, we have that $|\text{Gal}(L \diagup K)|=2^{n}$, so it is a $2$-group by definition.

The following diagram illustrates the relations between the fields and groups in the proof:


<TikzSvg6e9ba55dca2c75d0f298140503f2ace21 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>

>[!definition] algebraic closure
>
>A field $F$ is called *algebraically closes* if every non-constant polynomial in $F[x]$ has a zero in $F$. An extension $E \diagup F$ is called an *algebraic closure* of $F$ if every non-constant polynomial in $F[x]$ has a zero in $E$.


>[!proposition] no finite field is algebraically closed
>
>If $F$ is a finite field, then $F$ is not algebraically closed

**Proof.** Let $F$ be a finite field and consider an enumeration $a_{1}, \dots, a_{n}$ on its elements, then consider the polynomial $f(x)=\prod_{a_{i} \in F}(x-a_{i})+1 \in F[x]$. Suppose $f(x)$ has a zero $\alpha \in F$, then $(x-\alpha)$ is one of $x-a_{i}$ for some $i$, so $f(x)$ is in the ideal generated by $(x-a_{i})$, so $x-a_{i}$ divides $f(x)$, but evidently the remainder of this division is $1$ by construction of $f(x)$, a contradiction, hence $f(x)$ is irreducible in $F$. It follows that $F$ is not algebraically closed. <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>


>[!theorem] fundamental theorem of algebra
>
>$\mathbb{C}[x]$ is algebraically closed.


**Proof.** It suffices to show that there are no irreducible non-constant polynomials in $\mathbb{C}[x]$. Suppose by contradiction $\exists f(x) \in \mathbb{C}$ s.t. $f(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{C}$, and let $L$ be a splitting field for $f(x)$ over $\mathbb{C}$ ($L$ exists by theorem 390 (splitting field existence theorem)), then $L \diagup \mathbb{C}$ is a Galois extension (since $f(x)$ is separable since $\text{char}(\mathbb{C})=0$ and $L$ is a splitting field for $f(x)$). Note that this implies $\mathbb{C} \subset L$ and in particular $\mathbb{C} \neq L$.

 Since $\mathbb{R} \subset \mathbb{C} \subset L$ and $\mathbb{C}$ is a splitting field for $x^{2}+1$ over $\mathbb{R}$, then $L$ is a splitting field for $(1+x^{2})f(x)$ hence $L \diagup \mathbb{R}$ is also a Galois extension since it the splitting field of a separable polynomial (separable since $\text{char}(\mathbb{R})=0$). By lemma 468 (every polynomial of odd degree with real coefficients has a zero in $\mathbb{R}$), $\mathbb{R}$ qualifies for lemma 471 , so we have that $\text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{R})$ is a Sylow $2$-group.

By theorem 445 (fundamental theorem of Galois theory (FTGT)), $\text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{C}) \leqslant \text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{R})$, so its order must divide the order of $\text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{R})$ by theorem 100 (Lagrange's theorem), so it is also a $2$-group. Since $\text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{C})$ is a $2$-group then in particular $2 \mid |\text{Gal}(L \diagup \mathbb{C})|$, so it has a subgroup of order exactly $2$ by theorem 185 (Cauchy's theorem).

Let $K$ be that subgroup, and let $E^{K}$ be the field fixed by $K$, by corollary 436 ($\text{Aut}(E \diagup E^{G}) = G$) and corollary 437 (equivalent definition of Galois group), $K=\text{Gal}(E^{K} \diagup \mathbb{C})$, so $E^{K} \diagup \mathbb{C}$ is a finite Galois extension and by FTGT $[E^{K}:\mathbb{C}]=|K|=2$, so by theorem 442 (equivalent definitions for Galois group), $E^{K}$ is the splitting field of an irreducible polynomial of degree $2$ in $\mathbb{C}$, but by lemma 470 ($ax^{2}+bx+c \in \mathbb{C}[x]$ splits in $\mathbb{C}$), there is no such polynomial, a contradiction, so $L=\mathbb{C}$, another contradiction, hence $\mathbb{C}[x]$ is algebraically closed.

The following diagram illustrates the Galois correspondences and structures involved in the proof:


<TikzSvg4abc1bfe19c865c94bdf4bfb80423253 style={{display: "block", margin: "auto"}}/>

<div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
Which also immediately provides the following weaker statement:

>[!corollary] $\mathbb{C}$ is an algebraic closure of $\mathbb{R}$
>
>$\mathbb{C}$ is an algebraic closure of $\mathbb{R}$.

**Proof.** Immediately from theorem 474 (fundamental theorem of algebra) and definition 472 (algebraic closure). <div class="proof-end">$\square$</div>
